Lucio Russo

A course of study on

THE SCIENTIFIC WORKS OF GOETHE

by Rudolf Steiner

(Fratelli Melita Editori, Genova 1988)

Warning

Our friend Roberto Marcelli, to whom we already owe the transcription of the course of study on The Philosophy of Freedom published on this site, has now kindly sent us the transcription of another course, also taught by Lucio Russo (from September 2000 to November 2001), on The Scientific Works of Goethe by Rudolf Steiner (Fratelli Melita Editori, Genova 1988).

This consists of fifty-two meetings which, hoping to be of service to the friends of our site, we publish, after having (with the author's help) appropriately reworked them.

The Scientific Works of Goethe (1)


---


Let us begin by saying that this is not a book written by Steiner, nor one of those in which his lectures are published. The seventeen chapters that compose it are, in fact, nothing other than his introductions to as many of Goethe's scientific writings.

Steiner dealt with these writings on two different occasions: first, at twenty-four years of age (1885), when, on the occasion of the publication of Goethe's Complete Works (by the "Kürschner Deutsche National-Literatur"), he was entrusted with the task of curating its scientific section and, a second time, five years later, when he was called to perform the same task (in Weimar) for that edition of the same Work known as "the Grand Duchess Sophie edition."

But why, at such a short distance in time, was another edition made? Because Goethe's nephew, who died in the meantime, had bequeathed to Grand Duchess Sophie of Saxony-Weimar several unpublished writings by his grandfather. It therefore seemed appropriate to expand the Complete Works, inserting the new materials.

Goethe, in fact, had not given a systematic order to his scientific work, which lasted almost fifty years and was written, for the most part, in the form of articles, notes, outlines, notes and fragments (with the natural exception of the Theory of Colors and The Metamorphosis of Plants). It was often his friends who urged him to give his writings a form that would allow their publication.

When Steiner was entrusted with their curation, he collected, ordered and published these works, preceding them with introductions of varying length and substance.

These were then extracted from Goethe's Complete Works to be collected and published in this volume, entitled precisely: The Scientific Works of Goethe.

We will therefore deal with a book that will treat geology, meteorology, botany, zoology, osteology, colors, etc...

There will therefore be various subjects, but only one, on the contrary, will be the way in which Goethe approaches them; and the "how," that is, the way in which he approaches them, will be for us more important than the "what." Steiner rightly said: "What matters is not so much what Goethe dealt with, but the way in which he dealt with it."

Varied, therefore, is the matter, but not the spirit that observes it. Goethe, deeply loving life, empirical reality, things and phenomena, has in fact succeeded in translating his love into knowledge: that is, he has succeeded in knowing because he has loved and has succeeded in loving because he has known.

"If you wish to proceed into the infinite," he states for example, "move in every direction in the finite."

Reading his writings, one immediately notes with what scruple, with what caution, with what respect,


---


1
if not indeed devotion, Goethe goes in search of the objective reality of the phenomenon, and how great and constant is his fear of letting himself go to hasty judgments.

He did not want to override reality, but rather to allow it to reveal itself, to speak to him, to inspire him. Whoever is incapable of assuming an equally humble and devoted (that is, "scientific") attitude ends up, willy-nilly, by putting reality at his own service: that is, at the service of his personal opinions, his subjective interpretations, of what in short serves him, and not the world.

One more thing.

See this small botany manual? Well, open it and you will see that it begins by illustrating, not plants, but the plant cell (that is, the cytoplasm, the nucleus and the cell wall), and that only towards the end does it begin to speak of "seed plants" (of "spermatophytes").

It does not therefore start from the plant, that is, from the whole, to arrive at the parts, but starts from the parts to arrive at the plant; and this, because today we are convinced that the whole is formed accidentally, by virtue of the casual assembling of the parts: that is, of molecules, atoms or elementary particles.

But this presupposition, or better said prejudice, is absolutely foreign to Goethe. As a good "phenomenologist," he did not in fact start, philosophically, either from atomism or from holism, but only from what his senses directly presented to him.

Before undertaking the reading, it will also be appropriate to remember that Steiner, between 1885, the year in which he began his first work on Goethe's scientific works, and 1897, the year in which he finished the second, published Fundamental Lines of an Epistemology of the Goethean Worldview (1885), Truth and Science (1891) and The Philosophy of Freedom (1894): that is, three of his fundamental works (which were followed by Friedrich Nietzsche, Fighter Against His Time, in 1895, and The Goethean Worldview, in 1897).

Let us therefore begin to read the introduction.


---


"On August 18, 1787, Goethe wrote from Italy to Knebel: 'After what I have seen of plants and fish, near Naples and in Sicily, I would be very tempted, if I were ten years younger, to make a journey to India, not to discover new things, but to contemplate in my own way those already discovered.' In these words is indicated the point of view from which we must consider Goethe's scientific works. In his case, it is never a matter of discovering new facts, but of adopting a new point of view, a determined way of observing nature. It is true that Goethe made a series of important individual discoveries, such as that of the intermaxillary bone and the vertebral theory of the skull, in osteology, and, in the field of botany, that of the identity of all plant organs with the caulinary leaf; etc. But as an animating breath of these particulars, we must consider a grandiose conception of nature, by which they are all sustained; and above all we must see in the theory of organisms a grand discovery, such as to overshadow everything else: that of the essence of the organism itself. Goethe has set forth the principle by which an organism is what it manifests of itself, the cause of which the phenomena of life appear to us as the consequence, and all the questions of principle that we must raise in this regard" (p.1).

What Steiner calls here a "new point of view" is in reality a different and superior degree of consciousness. A degree of consciousness that lived in Goethe, but of which Goethe himself was not at first aware; he became so only later when he began to collaborate and exchange his ideas with Schiller.

Regarding "points of view," we could recall the so-called "ambiguous figures" (although they pertain only to the physical plane). For example, the well-known one that represents, together, a vase and two profile faces. Someone, observing it, could say: "I see the vase, but not the faces." What could we do


---


2
Well then? We could help him change his "point of view" by pointing out to him the lines of the forehead, the nose, or the chin, so that at a certain point he would be able to exclaim: "Ah, there, now I see them!".
The faces were therefore right there, together with the vase, but he was not initially able to see them, just as we are not initially able to see, together with their physical body, the (living) etheric body of plants, the etheric body and the astral (soul) body of animals, and the etheric body, the astral body, and the (spiritual) I of human beings.
We too — it is true — could let ourselves be helped (to open the spiritual eyes), but we usually, and lazily, prefer to hold to what we see (with our physical eyes), denying everything else.
Consider, just to give one example, the so-called vegetal "geotropism". According to the botanists, a "negative" geotropism would direct the plant's roots toward the center of the earth, while a "positive" geotropism would direct its stem toward the sky.
Yet phenomena of this kind could tell us a great deal more, not only about the nature of the forces that form the roots and the stem in the plant, but also about those that correspond to them in the human being; or is it perhaps enough to think of "positive" geotropism to explain the upright posture? The same could be said, all the more so, of the color and the scent of flowers. Plants, in fact, speak to us and reveal their essence through their forces and their qualities.
Today's materialistic science has, however, decreed that, since forces are physical magnitudes and colors and scents are human sensations, there is no reality that speaks or reveals itself to man — there is only man, who speaks and reveals himself to himself.
As is well known, it was Kant who conferred philosophical, or epistemological, dignity upon this point of view. It was he, in fact, who distinguished the "phenomenon" from the "noumenon" and who asserted that human consciousness can never grasp the latter. Kant was thus convinced that the phenomenon is a limit or a barrier, since it hides or conceals the noumenon — and not, as Goethe held, that the phenomenon manifests the noumenon, or that "an organism is precisely what it manifests of itself to us".

Answer to a question
The phenomenon is what is perceptible to the senses: that is, the perceptual image. For Kant, this is nothing but a subjective representation.


---


But let us return to the so-called "ambiguous figures". When we succeed in seeing what we previously did not see, it can happen that we say: "But how is it I did not see it before?". Well, we could say the same thing also at the opening of that spiritual eye which, beyond the sensible substances, allows us to perceive supersensible life (the etheric forces or processes).
You see, what Steiner calls the "etheric body" could also be called the "functional body". But what does materialism do? It naively identifies the anatomical (physical) body with the functional (etheric) one, and therefore imagines that functions are carried out by the organs, and not that they take place within the organs — as is made evident, among other things, by the so-called functional alterations (to which no organic ones correspond), but above all by the fact that, at the moment of death, although the organs are still present, their function ceases (inasmuch as the etheric body separates from the physical one).
You have heard me say, on the other hand, that materialism is the imagination of those who have no imagination: that is, of those who, of everything that it would be possible to see and understand, see and understand one thing only.
Imagine, for example, a person who, with closed eyes, sees nothing. Well, it will be enough for him to open them slightly to begin to make out, if only hazily, that, beyond the darkness, something exists. He will not yet be able to distinguish what is there, but we can be sure that from that moment on he will no longer give credence to anyone who tries to convince him that beyond the darkness (matter) there is nothing.
As soon as another way of seeing opens up within us — that is to say, another level of consciousness — the world becomes richer, for it takes on life, depth, and meaning. It is therefore our ordinary mind, or our ordinary consciousness, and not the world or life, that is wretched and disappointing (or, as it is sometimes said, not elegantly, a "rip-off").
This is why Steiner concerned himself so deeply with Goethe's scientific works. In the author of Faust he discovered precisely a man who, animated by a pure scientific spirit, showed himself capable of observing the phenomena of nature in a new and unusual way. In a manner different, for example, from the way Linnaeus had observed them in the botanical field.

"Before him, — Steiner observes, — natural science did not know the essence of the phenomena of life and studied organisms simply according to the composition of their parts and their exterior characteristics, as inorganic objects are also studied: it was therefore often induced to interpret the details erroneously, and to place them in a false light. Naturally, such an error cannot be detected from the details as such; we recognize it only when we understand the organism; for the details, considered in isolation, do not carry within themselves their principle of explanation. Only the nature of the whole explains them, for it is the whole that gives them essence and meaning" (p.2).


---


You probably remember that in Friuli, in 1976, there was a terrible earthquake which, among its many gravest damages, also razed Venzone to the ground, destroying, along with the houses, its beautiful Cathedral.
Well, how did those in charge of its reconstruction act? They adopted a method, called "anastylosis", consisting of the re-placement in situ of the original parts recovered in the vicinity of the collapsed building: that is, they gathered and numbered one by one all the pieces that were found, and then, on the basis of previous photographs, they put them back together, thereby reassembling the Cathedral.
Let us imagine, however, that the pieces had been there but not the photographs of the building before the collapse. By what criterion would they then have been assembled? Tom might have reassembled them in one way, and we would have had one Cathedral; Dick might have reassembled them in another, and we would have had another Cathedral; and Harry might have done likewise. Which goes to show that, in the absence of an objective vision of the whole, it would have been possible to reconstruct as many Cathedrals as there were possible combinations among the pieces.
"The details considered in isolation — Steiner says — do not carry within themselves the principle of explanation". In our example, in fact, the pieces lying on the ground do not carry within themselves the principle of explanation; it is the photograph that carries it: that is to say, the vision of the whole.
The details, therefore, are one thing; the relation among the details is another. But what is the relation among the details?
Think of an automobile. It is impossible to touch it with your hand, because with the hand you can touch only the bodywork, the seats, the steering wheel, or the engine: that is, its individual components. But does the automobile that cannot be touched exist or not exist? For the nominalists it does not exist, since they consider it merely a name; for the realists, on the other hand, it exists, and it is an idea: an idea that manifests itself precisely (or "unfolds", as Hegel would say) as the relation among the parts.
In organisms, the level of this relation among the parts is not yet that of the idea in itself, but the level at which it is possible to grasp its living, or etheric, manifestation. In organisms, the substance of the relation is thus life, time, or movement, while the essence of the relation is concept, idea, or, as Goethe prefers to say, "type" (Typus).

Answer to a question
Think, for example, of gravity. The existence of gravity does not depend on man, but the existence of the law of gravity does. Without man, the relation or the law operating objectively in the phenomenon would in fact never come to consciousness. Consider, moreover, that it is the law of gravity, on the plane of qualitative reality, that governs the force of gravity, on the plane of energetic or dynamic reality.


---


4
The relation, however, must not only be thought (abstractly); it must also be perceived. Even if many people seem unwilling to understand this, one must always remember that anthroposophy is a science, and not a philosophy, of the spirit. And why is it so? Because its objective is the direct perception or experience of the essence of the spirit (which Steiner calls, in the Anthroposophical Leading Thoughts, the "divine-spiritual Entity") and of the various grades of its manifestation (which Steiner calls, again in the Leading Thoughts, "manifestation", "working effect", and "completed work").
To make the relation (thinking) become real before our eyes means, therefore, to take the first step of the path that leads first to the reality of the idea and then to that of the I.
It is difficult, on the other hand, to imagine that a real (perceived) relation could be the manifestation of an unreal idea.
As we have said (but as we shall see better later on), Goethe calls the concept, or the idea, "type" (Typus): a type which, as the whole, is present and lives in all the parts.
This is the difference between inorganic and organic reality: in the former, the idea is present in the relation among the parts, but not in the parts themselves (inasmuch as the etheric body does not interpenetrate the physical body); in the latter, the idea lives both in the relation among the parts and in the parts themselves (since the etheric body interpenetrates the physical body). This is why it is not possible to add to, or subtract from, an organism a single part without altering the whole.
We mentioned Linnaeus earlier, the founder of modern systematic botany. To him goes the merit of having carried out a drastic reform of nomenclature (introducing the "binomial nomenclature", still in use today) and of having classified and ordered plants according to the structure of their reproductive apparatuses and in accordance with five precise criteria: genus, species, family, order, and variety. Proceeding in this way, he gave rise to a system that makes it possible to distinguish one plant from another clearly.
Linnaeus thus acted in an analytic manner, whereas Goethe, building upon this precedent, acted in a synthetic one ("only he who knows how to divide — he asserted — can unite").
You know that, from the psychological point of view, asthenic (or neurasthenic) characters incline toward analysis, while sthenic (or hysteric) characters incline toward synthesis. But true knowledge — which is precisely why it can only be a conquest — is obtained only where the limitations, or the one-sidednesses, of personal nature are overcome. Only the I, in fact, is capable of realizing a correct and dynamic equilibrium between the process of analysis and that of synthesis.
In reality, the relation between these two processes should be regulated by a rhythm similar to that which governs, for example, inhalation and exhalation, or diastole and systole. When this rhythm is altered in the direction of analysis, the result is indeed mechanism; when it is altered in the direction of synthesis, the result is instead mysticism.


---


Steiner writes: "The most significant element of the metamorphosis of plants is not, for example, the discovery of the individual fact that leaf, calyx, corolla, etc., are identical organs, but rather the grandiose construction of thought that springs from it — a living complex of interacting formative laws, which by its own force determines the particulars, the individual stages of development. The greatness of this thought, which Goethe later sought to extend to the animal world as well, becomes manifest to us only if we try to make it come alive within us, if we undertake to think it ourselves. We then realize that it is the nature of the plant itself, translated into idea, which lives in our spirit as it lives in the object" (p.3).

Thus, the plant as idea (as entity), and the idea as plant (as the life of the entity).
But the idea can also present itself as mineral. There exist, in fact, different planes of manifestation of the idea. On the vegetable plane it lives, develops and reproduces itself; on the mineral plane it is instead given in fixed or stable form, just as, in fixed or stable form ("clear" and "distinct," Descartes would say), our representations are given within us.
                                                     5
If representations within us correspond to precipitates or to calculations, what corresponds instead to the vegetable — or, better, to vegetating? It is easily said: that life or that movement of thinking which ever weaves, within us, the relations between the things thought.

As one can see, thanks to the viewpoint of Goethe and Steiner, we can begin to re-stitch the relationship between man and world; we can begin to understand, that is, why, in truly knowing the world, we truly know ourselves, and, in truly knowing ourselves, we truly know the world.

What presents itself incarnate, living and animated outside us, as nature, is in fact not only present within us, but, thanks to us, can become conscious of itself.

L.R.
Rome, September 5, 2000

6
The Scientific Works of Goethe (2)

Goethe was born in Frankfurt on August 28, 1749; from 1765 to 1768 he attended the University of Leipzig, where he cultivated, alongside his legal studies, artistic and literary interests. After an interruption caused by a serious illness, he resumed his studies in Strasbourg, where courses in medicine and the natural sciences attracted him more than the law lectures. He obtained his doctorate in law in 1771 and, having returned to Frankfurt, practiced law with the help of his father. In 1775, Grand Duke Karl August (1757-1828) summoned him to Weimar as a counselor, where he subsequently held ever more important offices (including that of minister).


---


Steiner observes on this point, at the beginning of the first chapter, entitled: The Genesis of the Doctrine of Metamorphosis: "It is generally held that Goethe's scientific thinking began after his arrival in Weimar. Yet one must go back still further, if one does not wish to leave the whole spirit of his conceptions unexplained: for already in his earliest youth there shows itself the vivifying power that guided his studies in the direction we shall go on to expound. When Goethe came to the University of Leipzig, there still reigned in natural studies that spirit, characteristic of a great part of the eighteenth century, which split all of science into two extremes, and felt no need at all to reconcile them. On one side stood the philosophy of Christian Wolf (1679-1754), which moved in an entirely abstract sphere; on the other, the individual branches of science, which lost themselves in the external description of endless particulars, while they absolutely lacked the aspiration to seek in the world of their objects a higher principle. That philosophy could not manage to find the passage from the sphere of its general concepts to the realm of immediate reality, of individual existence. The most obvious things were treated there with the utmost meticulousness; it was taught there that the thing is a quid containing no contradiction within itself, that finite substances and infinite substances exist, etc. But when one approached the things themselves with such generic assertions, in order to understand their action and life, one did not know where to begin, and one could not manage to apply those concepts to the world in which we live and which we wish to understand. As for the things themselves, they were described in a rather arbitrary manner, without principles, solely according to appearance and external characteristics. There thus stood facing each other, with no possibility of reconciliation, a doctrine of principles, which lacked living content and loving adherence to immediate reality, and a science without principles, devoid of ideal content: each was barren for the other" (pp. 5-6).


---


We must regretfully recognize that what was true yesterday is true still today. Philosophy, as a "doctrine of principles," continues to be barren for science, just as science "without principles" continues to be barren for philosophy; and when it is not so — when philosophy and science actually manage to fecundate one another — things go even worse, for their unions have so far brought to light nothing but a science that "no longer adheres to immediate reality" and a philosophy "without principles, devoid of ideal content."

The fact is that, without the succor of anthroposophy, one will never manage to reconcile philosophy with science or, more generally, humanistic culture with scientific culture.

Consider, for example, The Philosophy of Freedom: what does its subtitle say? "Results of soul-observation according to the method of the natural sciences."

"That motto — Steiner explains — was directed above all against the tendency of a world-conception which I esteemed, up to a certain degree, very highly: against the conception of Eduard von Hartmann, whose Philosophy of the Unconscious bore the motto: 'Speculative results according to the inductive method of the natural sciences.' 'Speculative results' — here was something that seemed to me to contradict in essence the true and proper meaning of a real spiritual and human knowledge; for with speculative results, with speculative thought-contents, one can only grasp what results when, by means of an abstract logic, something imperceptible is deduced from what is perceived — that is, when, by means of deductions, one arrives at something unknown that can be reached precisely only by means of deductions of thought, not by means of perception. Against this whole way of thinking I had to point out that whatever must be, for human beings, knowledge and content of life in every direction, should also enter in some manner into immediate observation, into perception. Just as external scientific phenomena place themselves before consciousness and can be observed, so too must psychic-spiritual contents place themselves before consciousness and consequently be accessible to observation" (The Cognitive Bases and Fruits of Anthroposophy — Antroposofica, Milan 1968, pp. 40-41).

Hence, then, the necessity of elaborating a science of the soul and of the spirit as an "ascending metamorphosis" of natural science, taking its start from the latter as it had been framed and (in part) developed by Goethe.

It does not take much, moreover, to note how philosophical knowledge tends to privilege the concept (the content of thought) at the expense of the percept (the content of perception), and how scientific knowledge tends, in contrast, to privilege the percept at the expense of the concept.

The philosopher thus ends up losing contact with sensible reality, while the scientist ends up losing contact with spiritual reality. No one, in fact, has ever become wiser merely for having observed or perceived more things (Mikhail Lermontov (1814-1841) maintained precisely that with two eyes one can become a superman, while with a hundred eyes one can remain a fly).

Philosophy should therefore learn to dirty its hands with perception, and science to clean them with thought. Toward the data furnished by ever more evolved and refined instruments of inquiry, present-day science brings indeed a thought so naive, flat, and coarse that one can hardly believe there once existed a Kant, a Fichte, a Schelling, a Hegel or — to stay in Italy — a Croce or a Gentile.

But let us return to Linnaeus.


---


"Linnaeus — Steiner writes — had aimed to bring a systematic clarity into the knowledge of plants. The task was to find a certain order within which every organism would have a determinate place, so that it could always be easily identified and, more generally, so that one would have a means of orientation in the boundless congeries of particulars. To this end, living beings had to be examined and grouped according to the degrees of their affinity. Since it was essentially a matter of recognizing each individual plant, so as easily to find again its place in the system, what had to be taken into account above all were the characteristics that distinguish plants from one another; thus, to render confusion between one plant and another impossible, the distinctive characters were brought into prominence (…) In this way the plants were indeed arrayed in an order, but in a manner that could have been applied to inorganic bodies as well: according to characters drawn from external experience, not from the inner nature of the plant. Such characters displayed themselves in an outward contiguity, without any necessary inner connection" (pp. 11-12).

In effect, just as we can distinguish, in a plant, the roots, the stem, the leaf, the flower, or the fruit, so can we distinguish, in an engine, the connecting rod, the piston, the valves, or the spark plugs. From an external point of view, an organism no less than an aggregate has parts. Their difference therefore cannot lie in this. In what, then? It lies, as we said last time, in the character of the relation that holds between the parts. In the machine, in fact, the parts are brought into relation with one another by man — that is, by an external factor — while in the plant the parts are brought into relation with one another by the plant itself — that is, by an internal factor.

8
Steiner observes precisely: "In the organism one must keep an eye above all on the fact that in it the outer manifestation is dominated by an inner principle, and that in every organ the whole is at work" (p. 6).

And what is this "whole" that is at work in every organ? We have said it: it is the type, the idea, or the species.

It is important to keep this in mind if one does not wish to be led, in speaking of the "whole," into some mystical temptation.

The idea of a "principle" that "interpenetrates all the particulars" — Steiner rightly notes — "is not at once applied to a single organism (…) But this quasi-mystical way of contemplating the world represents only a passing episode in Goethe's evolution, and soon yields the field to a healthier and more objective conception" (p. 8).

Before discovering the one (the singular) in the whole (in the universal), it would therefore be prudent to seek the whole in the one: that is, the idea in the individual thing. The true problem, in fact, lies in grasping the idea in the thing and the thing in the idea.

Listen, in this regard, to these verses of the Russian poet Fyodor Ivanovich Tyutchev (1803-1873):


---


It is not what you think nature is,
                                It is not a mold, not a face without a soul:
                                In it there is soul, in it there is freedom,
                                In it there is love, in it there is speech.

Reflect. Things stand before the eyes of each person: yet there are those who see in them the whole (the idea or the species) and those who see nothing. Seeing the whole or nothing in them does not therefore depend on things, but on us; and one can be certain that whoever sees the whole in themselves (the I), sees it also in things (as precisely idea or species), and that whoever does not see it in themselves, does not see it even in things.
Remember what the Spirit of the Earth says to Faust? "You resemble the spirit you comprehend, not me!"

And Tjutčev still sings:

They do not see and do not hear,
                                They live in this world, as in darkness
                                They do not aspire to know the sun,
                                And there is no life in the waves of the sea;
                                The rays have not descended into their soul,
                                Spring has not flowered in their breasts,
                                Before them the woods have not spoken,
                                And the night was mute of stars;
                                And with non-earthly tongues
                                Stirring rivers and woods
                                In the night it did not confer with them
                                In a loving colloquy the storm...

Paul-Henri Holbach (1723-1789), for example, not seeing in himself the I (the idea of man in man), did not see the idea of things in things.


---


Steiner writes on the matter: "While such defined conceptions were developing in Goethe's spirit, in Strasbourg there fell into his hands a book in which a conception of the world exactly opposite to his own was propounded: Holbach's Système de la nature. If until then Goethe had found only the fact that the living was described as a mechanical agglomerate of single things to criticize, now in Holbach there appeared to him a philosopher who really considered the living as a mechanism. What there was born only from the incapacity to know life at its root, here led to a dogma that killed life itself. Goethe thus speaks of it in Poetry and Truth: 'A matter should exist from eternity, and from eternity be in motion, and with such motion should without doubt produce, to the right, to the left and in every direction, the infinite phenomena of existence? We might perhaps have accepted all this, if from his matter in motion the author had really made the world arise before our eyes. But of nature he knows as much as we do: for, after having planted there some general concepts, he abandons them immediately, to transform what is superior to nature (or that at least appears, as superior nature, in nature), into a material nature, heavy, in motion, yes, but without direction, nor form: and with this he believes he has made a great step'" (p. 9).

"What there was born only from the incapacity to know life at its root," says Steiner, "here led to a dogma that killed life itself."
And also as proof of the fact that the "vivifying power" of Goethe's spirit had given sign of itself already before his arrival in Weimar, he reports the following and significant passage from a letter of Goethe, "of July 14, 1770, where he speaks of a butterfly": "The poor beast trembles in the net, strips itself of its most beautiful colors; and even if one succeeds in taking it unharmed, in the end there it is, rigid and inanimate; the corpse is not the whole animal, something is missing, a principal part is missing which in this as in every other case is essential: life..." (p. 7).


---


If the mystic therefore loses himself in the light of the whole (or of the One), the mechanist or the atomist instead loses himself in the darkness of the manifold: of a material (or submaterial) manifold in motion, but, as it is devoid of ideas, "without direction, nor form."
The idea, in fact, is not only "species," but also "form"; and it is forms that govern forces, just as it is forces that govern substances.
As we have said, the plane of reality on which the idea gives itself as force, activity, movement or life is the same on which, in our soul, thinking gives itself. Not by chance, the key to The Philosophy of Freedom is constituted by thinking as verb: that is, precisely as force, activity, movement or life.
Between the aggregate and the organism there is therefore a qualitative leap: the same that exists, for example, on the geometric plane, between the square and the circle or between the segment and the line.
We could in fact compare the aggregate to a sum of segments and the organism to a line, since the continuity of the latter is analogous to that of the living. Try to construct a line with multiple segments and you will see that, even by aligning infinite ones, you will never obtain it. Since every segment presents a beginning and an end, one will at most obtain the imitation of a line, but not a line. What will thus be obtained not only, therefore, will not tend – as is commonly said – to infinity, but will not even transform itself – as Steiner has indicated on several occasions – into a circle: that is, into the circle that precisely characterizes the unfolding of life.
Think of the becoming of a plant. One starts from the seed and, through the roots, the stem, the leaf, the flower and the fruit, one returns to the seed: here is the circle, here is the continuity!
A continuity that would also be proper to the nature of time if, to measure it, we did not fraction and break it up, thus rendering it discrete. But time – as they say – flows, runs and passes. We can stop the clock, but not time.
Whoever experiences thinking therefore experiences that life of the idea (in the human being, of the I) that comes to meet him, in nature, as a vegetal process.


---


10
As you see, whoever makes the science of the spirit his own runs no risk of becoming a mystic, a dreamer or a man who tends to escape from reality; he rather learns to love it, since he discovers that there is nothing more extraordinary, if not divine, than reality. The art lies in seeing it as it is, and not as one would like to have others believe it to be.
Listen, for example, to how Edoardo Boncinelli presents living beings: "Today we know that living beings are essentially motors – mechanical, thermal, chemical or electrochemical – that take good quality energy from the surrounding environment and give it back degraded. The active balance of this transformation is used to sustain their incessant activity, most of which is aimed at keeping themselves alive, a certain portion at multiplying and another portion at transforming, more or less noticeably, the surrounding environment" (E. Boncinelli: The brain, the mind and the soul – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 9).
Thinking of this, and paraphrasing what someone (a Russian) said, in the years when controversies raged over the presumed descent of man from the ape (Darwin's Origin of Species is from 1859), we could therefore say: "Men are essentially motors – mechanical, thermal, chemical or electrochemical –, ergo let us love one another."

L.R.
Rome, September 12, 2000

11
Goethe's Scientific Works (3)

In Weimar, I am finally granted – Goethe confesses – "to change the close and city air for an atmosphere of countryside, of wood and of garden."
And Steiner observes: "We must consider, as an immediate incentive to the study of plants, the work that the poet then undertakes in the garden given to him by Duke Charles Augustus. Goethe takes possession of it on April 21, 1776, and the Diary published by Keil henceforth makes frequent mention of Goethe's works in that garden, which become one of his dearest occupations" (p. 11).


---


This "practical" attitude of Goethe's is the same one that informs the science of the spirit (Practical Education of Thinking is, for example, the title of a lecture Steiner gave in January 1909). To pass from the experience of ordinary representative thinking to that of imaginative thinking is, in fact, to pass from the exercise of a thinking that is practical (expert) in what is dead (for example, in what is technical) to a thinking that is practical in what is alive. And let us not forget that it is precisely the experience (the perception) of thinking as a living or dynamic reality that prepares, at two different and successive levels, both that of the concept or the idea and that of the I, as essential or spiritual realities.
Our task (the task of the conscious soul) is therefore not to think the spirit abstractly (as philosophy does), but rather to perceive it.
As you know, we could not experience "singularity" if we did not perceive things through the (physical) senses. To this concretely perceived singularity, however, we are accustomed to oppose an abstractly thought universality, whereas we would be called upon to develop the psychic-spiritual senses in order to integrate the (physical) perception of the former with the (spiritual) perception of the latter.
But let us return to ourselves.
Goethe began working in his garden between the ages of twenty-six and twenty-seven and, with the intention of deepening his experiences, not long afterwards he took up the study of Linnaeus.
Steiner writes on this subject: "In the Linnaean system one never sought the essence of the plant. Goethe, on the other hand, could not help asking himself: what is the quid that makes a given natural being a plant? He had to recognize that that quid is found equally in all plants, and yet there was also the whole infinite variety of individual beings, which demanded an explanation. How does it come about that that one manifests itself in such varied forms? Such could have been the problems Goethe was posing to himself as he read the writings of Linnaeus, for he himself said of himself: 'What he, Linnaeus, strove at all costs to keep separate had, according to the inner necessity of my being, to tend toward unification'" (p. 12).


---


"To tend toward unification": that is, toward synthesis. But a synthesis is sound only if it follows analysis; otherwise it is a "mystical" synthesis, and not a scientific one. The mystic anchors himself, through feeling, to the one and shies away from passing to the many, for he fears losing himself in it. As we now know, however, one can lose oneself in the many just as much as in the one. Certainly, it is not easy to realize the one beyond the many, for in order to do so one must not only renounce privileging the one at the expense of the other, but also untie the knot of their relationship.
It is rather easy to foresee that many of our contemporaries will smile or take scandal at the question Goethe "could not help" posing to himself. Who indeed asks themselves today what the essence of a thing consists in? Not by chance do many present-day scientists like to hark back (in their own way) to Kant: precisely to the one, that is, who held the "noumenon" or the essence to be ungraspable and unknowable.
                                                   12
This means, however, that today we have a mineralogy that does not know what the mineral is, a botany that does not know what the plant is, a zoology that does not know what the animal is and, needless to say, an anthropology that does not know what the human being is.
All this is ignored, yet in the end one behaves as if one knew it. Edoardo Boncinelli, for example, first asserts: "What it means to stay alive is not yet entirely clear. To this day a rigorous definition of life and of the living being is lacking," but then he does not scruple to declare — as you heard last time — that "living beings are essentially motors."
But let us return to Goethe. He therefore asks himself: "What is the quid that makes a given natural being a plant?"
Steiner writes: during his studies, "he came to realize ever more clearly that it is precisely a single fundamental form that appears in the infinite multiplicity of individual plant organisms, and this fundamental form itself became ever more perspicuous to him; he recognized, moreover, that in this fundamental form resides the possibility of infinite variations, so that multiplicity derives from unity. On 9 July 1786 he writes to Frau von Stein: 'One comes to perceive the form with which nature plays, so to speak, continually, and in playing produces manifold life'" (p. 15).
Note that Goethe here says "to perceive," and not, say, "to hypothesize," "to presume," or "to conjecture." He is thus on the point of having (extrasensory) experience of the "type" and of the relationship that holds between this entity and the external environment.
To discover this relationship — Steiner notes, however — "Goethe had hitherto investigated too narrow a field. He needed to be able to study one and the same plant in different conditions and under different influences" (p. 15).
And so it happens that the journey to Italy offers him numerous occasions to carry out the observations he needed.


---


In Venice, for example, "he discovers various plants that show him properties which only the ancient salt of the sandy soil, and still more the salt air, could have conferred upon them. There he finds a plant that seems to him similar to the 'harmless coltsfoot,' but which there is armed with sharp defenses, with a leathery leaf, and likewise the follicles and the stems; everything massive and fleshy. Goethe was thus able to establish that all the exterior characters of the plant, all that appears of it to the eye, is inconstant, variable, and he draws from this the consequence that the essence of the plant does not consist in these properties, but must be sought more deeply" (pp. 15-16).
This essence can therefore develop, in one environment into form A, in another into form B, in yet another into form C, and so on. Which means — as everyone now knows — that the environment conditions its development.
But in what way does it condition it? This is the point!
For the most part one thinks (in a mechanical way) that the exterior characters of the plant are determined directly by the environment; this, however, is not true, for the latter acts upon the inner essence of the plant, which reacts, adapting or conforming its exterior characters to it.
Everything of the plant that "appears to the eye," and is "inconstant" or "variable," is therefore determined by the plant itself in response to the stimuli exerted upon it by the environment.
"The Darwinian conception — Steiner precisely observes — supposes that external influences act upon the nature of an organism as mechanical causes, and as such modify it. For Goethe,
                                                    13
on the other hand, the individual modifications are different outward manifestations of the primordial organism, which has within itself the capacity to take on multiple aspects, and in a given case assumes the one that proves most appropriate to the environmental conditions" (p. 19).
By "primordial organism" we must naturally understand the Urpflanze: that is, the type-plant, the archetypal plant, or the archetype of the plant.
One should not believe, however, that Goethe's inquiry and Darwin's are in conflict with each other; seen properly, they are in fact complementary. Darwin was indeed chiefly attentive to what acts from the external environment, while Goethe was chiefly attentive to what reacts, from within, to the environmental stimuli.
As you see, when one has a thinking of broad sweep at one's disposal, there is no need to take sides for one truth or the other (as, for example, the creationists and the Darwinians or neo-Darwinians do in this specific case), for one is able to receive and harmonize them all.


---


"Goethe — Steiner in fact explains — sets himself the goal of developing the constant element, while Darwin strives to investigate and to set out in detail the causes" of variability. "These two attitudes are both necessary and complement one another. One is greatly mistaken if one makes Goethe's greatness consist solely in the fact that he was a precursor of Darwin. The Goethean conception is much broader and comprises two aspects: 1) the type, that is, the law that manifests itself in the organism, the animality in the animal, the life that unfolds from itself and possesses the strength and capacity to develop, thanks to the possibilities inherent in it, into multiple exterior forms (genera, species); 2) the reciprocal action between organism and inorganic nature, as well as between the various organisms (adaptation and struggle for existence). Darwin developed only this latter aspect of the science of organisms; one cannot therefore assert that the Darwinian theory is the development of Goethe's fundamental ideas; it is the development of only one of their aspects, of one part of them. That theory contemplates only the facts whereby the organic world develops in a certain way, but does not consider that quid upon which such facts act in a determining manner (...) A simple comparison will make the matter clearer. Take a piece of lead, melt it with heat, then pour it into cold water. The lead passes through two successive states of aggregation: the first is obtained by means of the higher temperature, the second by means of the lower one. Now, the formation of these two states does not depend only on the nature of the heat, but essentially also on that of the lead: a different substance, placed in the same conditions, will show an altogether different behavior. Organisms too allow themselves to be influenced by the environment that surrounds them, they too assume, under the action of the environment, different conditions, and precisely in a manner corresponding to their essential nature, to that quid which makes them organisms in the first place. This their essence is precisely what we find again in Goethe's ideas. Only one endowed with understanding for this essence of organisms will be capable of comprehending why they respond to given stimuli precisely in that given way and no other; and will be able to form for himself correct representations of the variability of organic forms and of the laws of adaptation and of the struggle for existence connected with them" (pp. 16-17).
We could say, in short: show me how you react and I will tell you who you are!
But let us proceed in order.
The type is therefore "law," just as the concept or the idea is law, insofar as — as Hegel would say — it is "determinate being," "essence," or "quality."
"What is called an idea — Goethe himself moreover reiterates — is that which always manifests itself and therefore appears to us as the law of all phenomena" (Maxims and Reflections — TEA, Rome 1988, p. 219).


---


Think, for example, of a musical note, say a C. The C has an essence or a quality different from that, let us say, of an E, of a D, or of an F. The law of the C is therefore nothing other than the mode of being of the C.
Or think of the colors. Red can only be red: that is its law, its necessity.
This is why, in nature, there can be no freedom. Just as the C can only be the C, and red can only be red, so a poppy can only be a poppy, an elephant can only be an elephant, and so on.
Man, on the other hand, is free insofar as he is an I: an I which, being beyond or above the world of concepts, ideas, or laws, has the faculty to maneuver among the multiple necessities.
Consider, for example, a painter. To create a painting, he uses various colors. Now, although each color constitutes a necessity, he has the faculty to create a something that has absolutely nothing necessary about it.
This could help us understand that man should actualize his freedom precisely by moving, as an I, among the different necessities (concepts, ideas, laws), wisely balancing each of their intrinsic one-sidedness with the opposite one.
Steiner — as we have just seen — gives the example of lead. Today, however, one knows the "properties," and not the "qualities," of metals: that is, one knows what the metal has, and not what the metal is. And why? Because even at this level being has been (in an Ahrimanic way) replaced by having. Even in this way, however, one does not escape the problem: who, in fact, is the owner of these properties?
For Goethe — as we have seen — this owner is (in the botanical field) the "typical plant" (Urpflanze).
Of this, he writes on April 17, 1787: "It must exist; how otherwise could I recognize that this or that formation is a plant, if they were not all formed according to one single model?" And Steiner observes: "Goethe means to speak of the complex of formative laws that organizes the plant, and makes it what it is; that whereby, in the presence of a given object of nature, we become aware that it is a plant: this is what the typical plant is. As such, it is an ideal quid, graspable only in thought; but it takes on figure, it takes on a certain form, size, color, number of organs, etc." (p. 18).
Let it therefore be clear that the "typical plant" is not perceived with the physical eyes, but with the eyes of thought (with inspired consciousness). With the former, it cannot in fact be seen, since on earth it incarnates always and only in the ways that environmental conditions allow it, and never therefore fully.
As you can see, this is a discovery (supersensible), and not an invention: the discovery, that is, of that essence or entity which governs the living, sensible or spatial manifestation of the plant.


---


Indeed, Steiner concludes: "The living being is a whole complete in itself, which derives from itself its own modes of existing. Both in the spatial connection of the organs, and in the temporal succession of the stages of a living being, there exists a play of reciprocal relationships that does not appear to be conditioned by the sensible characteristics of the organs, nor by a mechanical-causal nexus between a preceding stage and a subsequent one; on the contrary, it is dominated by a higher principle that raises itself above the individual organs and the individual stages. It depends on the nature of the whole that a given stage is posited as the first and another as the last; and likewise the succession of the intermediate stages is already included in the idea of the whole organism. The earlier depends on the later and vice versa; in short, in the living organism there is a development of one element out of another, a passing over of the different stages into one another; not a finished, concluded existence of the individual, but a continuous becoming" (p. 19).

L.R. Rome, September 19, 2000
                                                  15
Le opere scientifiche di Goethe (4) [Goethe's Scientific Works (4)]


---


At the end of our last meeting, we read a passage in which Steiner says: "Both in the spatial connection of the organs, and in the temporal succession of the stages of a living being, there exists a play of reciprocal relationships that does not appear to be conditioned by the sensible characteristics of the organs, nor by a mechanical-causal nexus between a preceding stage and a subsequent one; on the contrary, it is dominated by a higher principle that raises itself above the individual organs and the individual stages."
For the science of the spirit, this "higher principle that raises itself above the individual organs and the individual stages" is constituted (at a first level) by the etheric body (that is, by a field of morphogenetic forces).
And for official science? Boncinelli tells us (to whom we are delegating precisely the burden of representing its opinions): "In living beings there exists an organization that maintains itself beyond and above the continual turnover of molecules. What this particular form of organization that concerns the molecules of living matter is, today we all know. It is in fact nothing other than the fruit of the continual consultation of a series of instructions that each cell carries enclosed in its nucleus, and which takes the name of genetic heritage or genome. The instructions are written in a particular language and are recorded on a particular material substrate, called DNA" (La mente, il cervello e l'anima — Mondadori, Milan 2000, pp. 27-28).
As one can see, official science too recognizes the existence of "an organization that maintains itself beyond and above the continual turnover of molecules."
What, then, is the problem? That both the science of the spirit and official science admit the existence of a reality (a "higher principle" or an "organization") that stands "beyond or above" the sensible one (the "individual organs and the individual stages" or the "continual turnover of molecules"), but while the former places it precisely in the supersensible, the latter places it, on the contrary, in the subsensible.
And why does it place it in the subsensible? Because it declares, yes, that this reality is found "beyond or above" the sensible one, but then ends up representing it (not knowing how to do otherwise) just as one is used to representing the sensible.
But anyone who represents a supersensible reality as if it were sensible (or, which is the same thing, thinks the supersensible with the same type of thought with which he thinks the sensible) fatally arrives — as The Philosophy of Freedom teaches — at "metaphysical realism."
Or is there perhaps someone able to show us with our eyes those pieces of information or "instructions" that would be "written in a particular language" and would be found "recorded on a particular material substrate, called DNA"?
Think of the child: around the age of seven, he changes his teeth, without anyone, from outside, taking care to extract the old ones and implant the new ones. Who, then, is it that provides, from within, for their replacement? Precisely that etheric body or "higher principle that raises itself above the individual organs and the individual stages," which will also take care, toward the end of the second seven-year period, of making him pubescent.
That evolutionary processes are determined from within, and unfold in phases or stages, could moreover also be demonstrated by the results reached, in the psychodynamic field, by the research of Freud and Jung.
According to Freud, the autonomous development of libido would in fact begin with the "oral" phase and, continuing with the "anal" and the "urethral" ones, would conclude with the "genital" phase; according to Jung, the equally autonomous "process of individuation" would instead begin (as illustrated by Erich Neumann in his The Origins and History of Consciousness — Astrolabio, Rome,


---


16
1978) with the "archetypal" stage of the "Uroboros" and, through those of the "Great Mother" and of the "Hero," would be completed with the stage of "Transformation" or of "Osiridization."
Certainly, the former moved on the biological or, more precisely, "psychosexual" plane, while the latter on the "archetypal" one; both, however, could not help but observe a process that originates from within and that, from outside, can be favored or hindered, but never created.
Let us return, in any case, to Goethe.

Steiner notes: "On May 17, 1787, Goethe writes to Herder: 'I had realized that in that organ of the plant which we usually call the leaf there hides the true Proteus, capable of concealing and manifesting itself under the most diverse appearances. In whatever direction one considers the plant, it is always only leaf, and so inseparably united with the future germ that one cannot think the one without the other.' While in the animal that higher principle which dominates each single specimen presents itself to us concretely, as that which moves the various organs, uses them in a manner conforming to its needs, etc., the plant is still devoid of such a real life-principle" (p. 20).

What does it mean that the plant "is still devoid of such a real life-principle," that is, of an essence?
To understand it, we must remember that the essence (the idea) is one thing, and the relation in which the essence stands to the substance (the "thing") is another, and that the plant-essence stands to the plant-substance in a relation different from that in which the animal-essence stands to the animal-substance.
In the vegetable world, this relation has a "transcendent" character; in the animal world, an "immanent" one.
To the science of the spirit, it in fact results that the astral body (the body of essences) is incarnated in animals, but not in plants.
Precisely for this reason, plants can grow and reproduce, but cannot uproot themselves and move. Unlike animals, they have in fact not severed the umbilical cord that binds them to the mother-earth.
It will also be well to remember that every essence (every "in-itself"), in order to manifest itself, must go out of itself ("out of itself") and become existence. First it manifests itself in time, then in space: in the latter, however, it dies, transforming itself into state. The essence, therefore, first flows, streams, or lives in time, and then coagulates, halts, or dies in space.
To truly think life, one must therefore truly think movement. But how to think it without arresting it, by representing it to oneself? To this question, I have already answered on other occasions thus: the true thought of movement is the true movement of thought.
If the I could succeed (thanks to inner exercise) in experiencing itself in the act or in the movement of thought, the secret of becoming, of time, and of life would thereby be unveiled.


---


Answer to a Question

The relation that the I has to imaginative thinking is different from the one it has to representative thinking. In the former, one experiences oneself as active; in the latter, as passive: to the point of being led to believe, as naive realism maintains, that the inner representation is not a production of the subject, but rather a re-production of the external object.

But let us return to our subject.

Steiner writes: "The plant is therefore a being that develops, in succession, a series of organs all connected to one another and to the whole organism by one and the same formative idea. Every plant is a harmonious ensemble of plants. Once this idea had been made clear, Goethe had nothing left to do but the individual observations suited to demonstrating, part by part, the various stages of development that the plant expresses from its own bosom"; and in a footnote he adds: "We shall have occasion more than once to clarify the nature of this relation of the single parts to the whole. If we wished to borrow from contemporary science a concept applicable to such a grouping of animate beings cooperating within a whole, we might perhaps resort to that of the beehive in zoology. It is a kind of 'State' of living beings, an individual constituted in turn of autonomous individuals, an individual of a higher order" (p. 20).


---


This "formative idea" or "individual of a higher order" is once again the essence. With regard to it, however, the following alternative arises: either ordinary thinking is raised up onto the plane of the essence, or the essence is brought down onto the plane of ordinary thinking (thereby ending up — as we have seen — below that very plane).

But how does one bring the essence down? It is simple: by making the inessential essential; that is, by projecting the essence onto existence in general, as naive realism and materialism do, or onto some particular entity or existent, as, for example, Linnaeus did.

From this point of view, Linnaeus appears, in spite of himself, as a kind of "Freud of the vegetable world." Just as the latter was convinced that the sexual activity was the essential activity of man, so Linnaeus was convinced that it was the essential activity of plants (inasmuch as it was the cause of "fructification").

"The Linnaean system — Giulio Barsanti aptly observes — was absolutely unprecedented in this respect, and the eighteenth-century botanists were not deceived by those lists: given that Linnaeus assumed full paternity of the sexual system, some went so far as to affirm that such a system, being founded on a single character, was in fact an artificial system. For zoological classification the Linnaean system met with less resistance because it was not founded solely on the structure of the reproductive apparatus, and thus took the form of a more traditional system" (introduction to C. Linnaeus: I fondamenti della botanica — Theoria, Rome-Naples 1985, p. 17).

When one is incapable of seeing the part in the whole and the whole in the part, one can end up reducing the whole to the part, thereby promoting the inessential (perceptible, if one is a naive realist; imperceptible, if one is a metaphysical realist) to the rank of the essential.

Since there is no escaping reality, and since the essence is precisely a reality (an ideal or spiritual one), one would have to make up one's mind: either accept it as it is, or engage some parvenu (physical or metaphysical) to play its part.

You see, the German physicist Wilhelm Röntgen (1845-1923) discovered some "rays" (for which he was awarded the Nobel Prize in 1901) which he had the good sense to call "X" precisely because their nature was not understood (M. Giroud: Marie Curie — Fabbri, Milan 2000, p. 71).

If only the same were done with the essence of that "organization which maintains itself — as Boncinelli says — beyond and above the continual turnover of molecules"!

But let us return to Goethe.

For him, every part is worth as much as any other, since the "type" does not lend itself to being identified with any one of the organs that constitute the whole (all the more so as those privileged by Linnaeus, the male genitals or "stamens" and the female genitals or "pistils," were, for Goethe, nothing but metamorphosed leaves).


---


Steiner writes on this subject: "If we consider the Goethean doctrine of metamorphosis as we find it formulated in the year 1790, we see that for Goethe this concept is that of an alternating expansion and contraction. In the seed, the formation of the plant is contracted (concentrated) to the highest degree. With the leaves there then follows the first development, the first expansion of the formative forces. What in the seed is concentrated in a point separates, expands spatially in the
18
leaves. In the calyx the forces contract again toward an axial point; the corolla is the result of the subsequent expansion; the stamens and the pistil, of the subsequent contraction; the fruit, of the last (third) expansion; after which the whole vital force of the plant (this principle of entelechy) hides itself in the seed, in the condition of maximum contraction" (p. 21).


---


Hegel, however, while greatly admiring Goethe, holds that metamorphosis is nothing other than "a fleeting breath of forms" (V. Verra: Letture hegeliane — Il Mulino, Bologna 1992, p. 108).

And for what reason? Because between the "forms" or essences of things and the things themselves (perceived in space by means of the senses) time interposes itself: that becoming, breath, or indeed "alito," of the forms or essences which, as it is spent, transmutes them into things.

Let us imagine that we are standing before two plants: say, a rose and a gardenia. The essence of the one is different from that of the other: different indeed are their forms, their colors, and their scents. We should not stop there, however. Both the rose and the gardenia are in fact plants, and as such they share, beyond what makes them different, one and the same essence: namely, that same quid or that same "type" which makes a plant precisely a plant.

This means that essences interpenetrate, standing within one another, in the quality of subordinated or superordinated ones.

Think, for example, of the concepts "cat," "feline," "carnivore," "mammal," and "animal": do they not stand within one another? Is not the first subordinate to the second, the second to the third, the third to the fourth, and the fourth to the fifth? And is not the kitty we may happen to be stroking, all at once, a cat, a feline, a carnivore, a mammal, and an animal?

As you see, these are concepts to be placed not one beside the other (as the analytic intellect is wont to do), but one within the other, like concentric circumferences of different diameter.

It is therefore possible to distinguish the essence of one plant from that of another, but it is also possible to distinguish, at a higher level, the essence of the plant (Goethe's Urpflanze) from that of the animal.

The doctrine of metamorphosis thus concerns becoming. But the becoming of what? Of a quid that lies beyond time and space. Plants live, animals live, and human beings live as well: there is therefore a living something that is indeed other than the non-living, but which does not yet account, by itself, for qualitative differences.

According to Hegel — Verra aptly observes — "metamorphosis is only 'one side' of the process, which does not exhaust the totality, inasmuch as one must also attend to the 'distinction of the constructs'" (ibid., p. 106).


---
