3	equal to, if not superior to, those of Linnaeus?
4	Well then, listen to what Barsanti says again: "As it soon became clear that in spite of certain
5	premises it (the Linnaean philosophia – ed.) led to an artificial system, so it was
6	simultaneously just as clear that this constituted a great advantage for botanical science,
7	and perhaps the most important advantage. Gilibert (Jean-Emmanuel Gilibert, botanist, 1741-
8	1814 – ed.) realized this with great clarity: although Tournefort's (Joseph Pitton de
9	Tournefort, French physician and botanist, 1656-1740 – ed.) was more natural, Linnaeus's system
10	was indisputably to be preferred to it because it was "more uniform in its articulation", that is,
11	it avoided providing too many criteria for classifying bodies (which disorients the naturalist) and
12	provided, much more effectively, a single parameter, the key to finding one's way through the labyrinth of nature. The
13	sexual system was certainly an artificial system (because sexuality is only one aspect of
14	living nature) but it was to be preferred precisely for this reason: because by selecting one character it was
15	able to compare objects that, being very different in other characters, would have been difficult to
16	compare. Like other Linnaeans, Gilibert thus appreciates, in this operation, its misleading
17	character: and, in particular, the fact that thanks to it nature seems a much more uniform reality
18	than it is, as if to move within it one needed, at least to achieve certain
19	objectives, to transfigure it" (op. cit., pp. 19-20).
20	If Goethe's fame and authority are not equal, if not superior, to those of Linnaeus, it is therefore
21	because ordinary consciousness has preferred to "upset" or "transfigure" the order of
22	nature rather than to develop and modify itself.
23
24	L.R.
25	Rome, 26 September 2000
26
27	20
28	\fThe Scientific Works of Goethe (5)


---


1	Tonight we begin the second chapter, entitled: The genesis of Goethe's ideas on the formation
2	of animals.
3	Having spoken of the plant "type", we shall therefore speak of the animal "type" (of the "Urtier" or
4	of the idea of the animal).
5	Let us keep in mind, however, that these entities also operate in man: the I as I is in fact a human
6	being, while the I as quality (soul) is an animal, as time (life) is a plant, and as
7	space (physical body) is a mineral.
8	In observing and knowing nature, the I therefore does not observe and know itself, but rather what
9	is within it; the I is in fact always the knowing subject, and never the known object.
10	Whoever is familiar with the science of the spirit, and in particular with the evolution described by Steiner in
11	Occult Science in its General Lines, knows that minerals, plants and animals are, so to speak,
12	indirect creatures of God and direct creatures of man. God in fact created man (the "firstborn" —
13	this is precisely what Herbert Fritsche called him — cf. H. Fritsche: The Firstborn – Bompiani, 1946) and
14	from man the kingdoms of nature then sprang.
15	But let us come to ourselves.
16
17	Goethe – Steiner writes – "arrived at the point of view that the constitution (structure) of man,
18	considered as a totality, forms the basis for the highest manifestations of his life, and that in the
19	peculiarity of this totality lies the condition that places man at the apex of creation. What
20	we must keep foremost in mind is that Goethe seeks the figure of the animal in the perfected figure of
21	man; only, in the former there come to the fore the organs that serve the
22	animal functions, as it were the point toward which the whole formation converges and which it serves, while
23	human formation especially perfects those organs that serve the spiritual functions. Already
24	here we find that the animal organism Goethe sees before him is no longer something
25	sensibly real, but an ideal quid that in animals develops in a lower direction,
26	in man in a higher one. Here already lies the germ of what Goethe later called the "type",
27	by which he meant to designate not "some individual animal", but the "idea" of the
28	animal. Moreover, there is
29	already found here a hint of a law he later enunciated, most important for its
30	consequences, namely that "the variety of forms springs from the fact that this or that part has
31	been granted a preponderance over the others"" (p. 26).
32
33	I think you know, in this connection, that Steiner speaks of birds as animals in which the
34	cephalic organization is prevalent, of felines as animals in which the rhythmic
35	organization is prevalent, and of bovines as animals in which the metabolic
36	organization is prevalent.
37	Which means that birds, felines and bovines anatomically possess all three
38	organizations, but that in the first, the functions of the head prevail over the other two; in the second, the middle ones; and in the third, the visceral ones. These differentiations have in the first place a
39	qualitative (ideal) nature, in the second place a functional (energetic) one, and lastly an anatomical (sensible) one.


---


1	Answer to a Question
2	Your difficulty in considering birds as animals in which the organization of the head prevails
3	stems from the fact that this prevalence is to be grasped above all on the etheric plane, which, as such, is in
4	relationship with the so-called "elements": fire, air, water and earth (as the
5	"pre-Socratics" understood them). Birds have air as their habitat: not only because they have wings and fly, but
6	more still, because their bones are "pneumatic", that is, provided with internal cavities full of air.
7	Air, however, is also the (astral) habitat of thoughts. In the lectures collected in the book entitled:
8	                                                    21
9	\fMan, Harmonic Synthesis of Universal Creative Activities (Antroposofica, Milan 1968), Steiner has
10	illustrated, in this connection, the subtle relationship existing between man's thoughts and the plumage
11	of birds.
12
13	It is therefore necessary to start from the observation of external forms in order to ascend, by virtue of the processes or
14	functions that take place in them, to essences or qualities: that is, one must start from the sensible
15	form to ascend to the ideal form (to the idea), without however forgetting — as we have said and
16	repeated in dealing with The Philosophy of Freedom — that the idea (or concept) is form, but has no
17	form.
18	The "mystic chorus" sings, in Faust: "All that is transitory is but a symbol". And why? Because everything
19	that is perishable (the "phenomenon"), that is to say, everything that the (physical) senses perceive, is the expression,
20	manifestation or revelation of the essence (of the "noumenon").
21	Certainly, one must possess an (imaginative) eye for forms and an (inspirative) ear for
22	qualities, if one does not want to end up, like Kant, believing the "noumenon" unknowable merely because one
23	is incapable of understanding the language of the "phenomenon".
24	Knowing that the variety of forms springs — as Goethe says — "from the fact that this or that
25	part has been granted a preponderance over the others" proves particularly useful in the choice
26	of medicinal plants. There are, for example, plants that display a greater root development than
27	leaf development, and others in which the opposite occurs. This means that the former convey forces
28	qualitatively opposite to those conveyed by the latter, and can therefore be of help (if
29	appropriately prepared) to those persons in whom the corresponding forces appear, on the contrary,
30	deficient.
31	But let us return to the animals.
32
33	Steiner writes: "Already here the difference between man and animal is therefore sought in the fact that an
34	ideal figure perfects itself in two different directions, and that each time one system of organs gains the
35	upper hand, and from this the whole creature receives its own character" (p. 21).


---


Whenever "a system of organs gains the upper hand," there comes into being, so to speak, a "specialized" creature; and animals are, in truth, nothing but "specialists."
Man, however, is not; and why? Because in order to be free, one must not be specialized.
If in a man, say, the lower (metabolic) system were to gain the upper hand (as happens in bovines) over the higher (cephalic) system and the middle (rhythmic) system, the law prevailing in that system would in fact impose itself; and it is precisely for this reason that bovines can "do" only bovines, and nothing else.
How, then, is freedom realized? By creating "systems of organs" which, neutralizing one another, find themselves in dynamic and constant equilibrium. Each of these — it is true — would tend to impose its own law on the whole organism, but it does not succeed (except by engendering pathologies), because it finds itself counterbalanced by the other two.
This is made particularly clear by "temperament." The very term "temperament" in fact alludes to a moderating or conciliating activity: moderating or conciliating what? It is obvious: the differing and conflicting dispositions of the four (Hippocratic) "humors": the choleric, the sanguine, the phlegmatic, and the melancholic.
Where one of these humors takes a clear upper hand, man in fact regresses from individuality to type or species: that is, to something collective, necessary, and predictable.
When instead things go as they could and should go — that is, when the I "does" the I — the humors, neutralizing one another, no longer impose themselves and are scarcely felt at all.


---


22
But is this not precisely the "state of health"? Is it not common experience that when all is well we barely notice we have a body, whereas a slight headache suffices to remind us that we have a head?
Health is thus "unconsciousness" or "higher consciousness," while that which stands between the two — that is, that which is no longer unconsciousness, but is not yet higher consciousness — is ordinary intellectual consciousness, which Hegel calls "unhappy." He writes: "The unhappy consciousness is consciousness of itself as of the duplicated essence, still entirely implicated in contradiction"; it is consciousness "split within itself" (G.W.F. Hegel: Phenomenology of Spirit — La Nuova Italia, Scandicci (Fi) 1996, pp. 131-132).
Unconsciousness and higher consciousness in fact resemble each other: so much so that the latter (by virtue of its immediacy) could even be called a "conscious unconsciousness" or an "unconscious consciousness."
Christ indeed says: "Let the children come to me, for to those who are like them belongs the kingdom of God. Whoever does not receive the kingdom of God like a child will not enter it" (Lk 18:16).
The "kingdom of God" will belong, then, to those who will be capable of conquering, on the conscious plane, that same freshness, spontaneity, or immediacy that belongs to childhood, and therefore to unconsciousness.
What we are saying — it is worth repeating — holds for man as he could and should be (or become), not for man as he exists today. The I in fact comes into the world with a constitution, a temperament, and a character that are karmically determined: it comes into the world (as an ego) almost as an "animal." There is therefore no cause for wonder that all those who are ignorant of its true essence are currently convinced that man is nothing but an "intelligent animal."
But let us return to Goethe.
Steiner writes: in 1776, "Goethe gains clarity about the point from which one must proceed when one wishes to study the figure of the animal organism. He recognized that the bones are the foundation of the structure; and he maintained this thought also later, when in his anatomical works he took his start from osteology (…) 'It will already have been observed,' he says for instance, 'that I regard the skeletal system as the fundamental drawing of man; the skull as the foundation of that drawing, and every fleshy part almost only as the coloring of that drawing'" (p. 27).


---


We have spoken of constitution; it presents, however, two aspects: one morphological, the other physiological. The second, however, is no longer merely physical. "Physiology" means in fact functionality, processuality, life, or movement: that is, everything that depends on the etheric body.
When, for example, one distinguishes — as the Italian physician and chemist Giacinto Viola (1870-1943) does — the "longilinear" type, the "normolinear" type, and the "brevilinear" type, one performs a classification that considers constitutions from a purely morphological point of view. When instead one distinguishes — as the French physician Claude Sigaud (1862-1921) does — the "respiratory" type, the "digestive" type, the "cerebral" type, and the "muscular" type, then the morphological (physical) element comes to unite and merge with the functional (etheric) one.
Of the pure physical body one must speak in morphological terms: that is, in the terms of that discipline which studies — as is well known — the external forms and internal structures of living organisms and minerals.
To observe such forms and structures with an "artistic" eye — that is, with an eye that, being imaginative, transcends the ordinary separation between art and science — means, however, to understand such forms and structures as images, symbols, or — in Goethe's words — "drawings."
He asserts — as we have just seen — that "every fleshy part" is almost only "the coloring" of such drawings. Well, do we not often say of someone in desperate condition that he has been reduced to "skin and bones"? In anthroposophic terms, however, we could also say that he has been reduced to "Lucifer and Ahriman": that is, that human being has failed in him who stands precisely,
                                                  23
as the third, between the luciferic skin and the ahrimanic bones, just as, on Golgotha, the cross of Christ stands, as the third, between those of the two "thieves."
Only that which stands at the center — precisely as that "tertium" which, according to binary logic (governing computers), would, curiously enough, be "non datum" — is therefore able to humanize both the skin and the bones.
Our task is therefore not to flay ourselves or de-bone ourselves, but to place these parts in the service of the I: that is, of that which, in us, is properly human.
But let us resume our discourse.
Goethe — Steiner observes — "does nothing other than seek the animal within man, that is, the simpler within the more complex, as he would later (1795) say explicitly" (p. 27).
We have already said, in fact, that essences interpenetrate, and that the simpler (subordinate) ones lie within the more complex (superordinate) ones, in the same way that concentric circles of smaller diameter lie within circles of larger diameter.


---


Steiner further observes: "Goethe considered man absolutely in conformity with the rest of nature. Ever more alive in him grows the thought, already noted by us above, that the figure of man, like that of the animal, is governed by a fundamental form, which however in the former rises to such a perfection as to allow it to become the vehicle of a free spiritual being."
Goethe, he continues, "was in active relation with Herder, who in 1783 was beginning to outline his Ideas toward a philosophy of the history of humanity (J.G. Herder: Ideas toward a Philosophy of the History of Humanity — Laterza, Rome-Bari 1992 — nda). That work sprang almost from the conversations between the two, and certainly more than one of those ideas could be traced back to Goethe. The thoughts expressed in it are often wholly Goethean, only uttered in Herder's manner, so that from them we can draw sure conclusions about Goethe's thoughts at that time" (pp. 29-30).
I do not think I am mistaken in saying that hardly anyone any longer takes an interest in the text of Johann Gottfried Herder (1744-1803) cited by Steiner. Yet it is very sad that this is so, because we would instead have an urgent need to rediscover that "cultural movement" to which Steiner gives the name of "Goetheanism" (cf. in particular: The Study of Historical Symptoms — Antroposofica, Milan 1961).
"Goetheanism," however, is something more than what we today abstractly mean by a "cultural movement": it is rather an atmosphere of soul, an ideal tension, or a spiritual humus, nourished in the highest degree by a profound human element. Alongside Goethe and Herder, we should at the very least count Schiller (1759-1805), Fichte (1762-1814), Hegel (1770-1831), Novalis (1772-1801), and Schelling (1775-1854).
Do you remember when we spoke of the "circle" of life, and of the fact that, in the plant, the seed lies at the beginning as much as at the end of its becoming?
Well, here too we can observe that what emerges at the end of a developmental process is nothing other than what stands at its beginning ("In the beginning was the Word…").
Science today speaks, for example, of "emergent properties" ("they emerge — Boncinelli explains — only at a certain level of aggregation, while they are absent in the preceding ones" — The Brain, the Mind, and the Soul — Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 21), but it has not at all realized that these can "emerge" from substance, "at a certain level of aggregation," only insofar as they were previously "immersed" in it.
This holds, however, not only for the properties or qualities (of things), but also for the being of man. The I has in fact immersed itself in the astral (soul) body, in the etheric (living) body, and in the physical body, precisely in order to one day be able, by the grace of the Logos, to re-emerge from them.


---


24
Reflect: could the "Son of Man" ever "emerge" from our souls if the "Son of God" had not first been "immersed" in them, becoming "flesh"?
From this point of view, it is moreover well understood how man does not at all descend from the ape, but how, from a single original (supersensible) being, the ape derived, by descending evolution, and man, by ascending (and not yet completed) evolution.
Steiner writes: "Herder, in the first part of that work (Ideas toward a Philosophy of the History of Humanity — nda), has the following conception of the essence of the world. One must presuppose a principal form that passes through all beings and realizes itself in various ways. 'From stone to crystal, from crystal to metals, from these to the creation of plants, from plants to the animal, from this to man, we saw the form of organization ascend, and with it the forces and impulses of nature become more manifold, in order to reunite at the end in the figure of man, as far as this figure could comprise them.' The thought is entirely clear: an ideal, typical form, which as such has no sensible reality, realizes itself in an infinite number of beings, spatially distinct and differing in their qualities, right up to man" (p. 30).
But what is the "organizing" idea or form that ascends in this way? It is the I itself which, like an artist, while realizing its works, realizes itself.
Steiner continues, however: "This thought acted very fruitfully upon later German philosophy. Let there be quoted here, for greater clarity, the exposition of the same idea made later by Oken (Lorenz Ockenfuss, physician and naturalist, 1779-1851 — nda). He says in fact, in the Treatise on the Philosophy of Nature: 'The animal kingdom is one single animal; that is to say, it is the manifestation of animality with all its organs, each of which is in itself a whole. An individual animal appears when an individual organ detaches itself from the general animal body and nevertheless exercises the essential animal functions. The animal kingdom is the highest animal — man — in fragmented form. There is one single human tribe, one single human race, one single human genus, precisely because it is the entire animal kingdom'" (p. 30).


---


Imagine, by way of absurdity, that your liver detaches itself from you and starts wandering about on its own. It could naturally not preserve the form it had while it was within you and part of you, but would have to assume another one, thereby transforming itself into an animal. If all human organs did the same, the phenomenon would obviously multiply, thus generating an entire animal kingdom: generating, that is, the many from the one.

Certainly, all of this is difficult to believe at first sight, since it belongs to man, in realizing his own humanity, to prove and bear witness to its truth.

Think what would happen if all the animals merely vegetated, falling perhaps into an endless hibernation. In all likelihood, we would begin to mistake them for plants. What the animals do not do, however, men do: and it is precisely for this reason that we have come to mistake them for animals.

But while the animal can become nothing other than an animal, man, precisely because he is free, can become either a "man" or a "beast" or, as many today wish, a "robot."

We have deliberately said "beast," since animality, in animals, is physiological and innocent, whereas in man, whenever it gains the upper hand over the I (over the human essence) or replaces it, it is pathological, and precisely "bestial."

Today, everyone wants to "realize themselves." But in order truly to do so, they would first have to discover what they really are or, better said, who they really are.

We have a great responsibility toward nature. Only a man who is a man can in fact nourish an authentic sense of love or compassion for the creatures of the animal world,

25

of the vegetable world and of the mineral world; not by chance, a man who fails to realize his own humanity will end up making these creatures victims of his bestiality.

Like "sorcerers' apprentices," we are currently violating and destroying ourselves and nature. But who is a "sorcerer's apprentice"? One who deludes himself that he can with impunity make use of forces whose nature he neither knows nor masters.

Goethe himself has said: "Man's knowledge, of whatever kind it may be, determines his conduct; hence nothing is more terrible than to see ignorance at work" (J.W. Goethe: Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1983, pp. 131-132).

As is well known, Martin Heidegger declared: "Only a God can save us"; he too, then, was waiting for the Messiah, not realizing that by now it is, conversely, the Messiah who is waiting for us.

But let us take up, in conclusion, our discourse.

Steiner writes: "In human formation, all the organs and systems of organs constitute themselves in such a way that each leaves the other sufficient space for a free development, and that each restrains itself within those limits that appear necessary so that all the others may equally assert themselves" (p. 31).


---


On closer consideration, this statement holds not only for the physical-etheric (bio-constitutional) reality, but also for the soul reality, if not indeed for the social one. Were one to realize — as Steiner hoped — a "triarticulation of the social organism," would the cultural or spiritual apparatus, the juridical or political apparatus, and the economic one not in fact come to constitute themselves "in such a way that each leaves the other sufficient space for a free development, and that each restrains itself within those limits that appear necessary so that all the others may equally assert themselves"?

L.R.
Rome, October 3, 2000

26

The Scientific Works of Goethe (6)


---


This evening, before resuming the reading, I would like to offer you a few reflections. We said, last time, that "Goetheanism" (regarded by Steiner as a modern "universitas litterarum" that "prepares the ground for the reception of the science of the spirit") was a "cultural movement" characterized by "a soul atmosphere, an ideal tension or a spiritual humus, nourished to the highest degree by a profound human element": by something that nourishes the culture of the present, and especially its scientific culture, very little indeed.

In this regard, we have repeatedly stressed that one thing is the data gathered by scientific research, another the theories that are cobbled together by putting those data in relation to one another.

One who follows the science of the spirit should always accept the former, but should never fail to examine the latter carefully, to verify whether or not they are mortgaged to preconceptions or prejudices of a philosophical character, if not indeed to interests of a political or economic nature.

When, for example, one declares oneself certain — as Rita Levi Montalcini does — that thanks to the progress of the neurological and cognitive sciences it will not be long before "the essence of the human species" is discovered (la Repubblica, October 10, 2000), one takes for granted that an "essence" can have a sensory or subsensory nature. But what would Montalcini herself say if someone maintained, say, that thanks to the progress of acoustics it will not be long before the essence of colors is discovered? Before that of the human species, would it not therefore be better to seek the essence of essence? Would it not in fact be advisable to have some idea of what one is looking for, above all when it is a matter — as in this case — of forming an idea of the idea?

Going precisely in search of the essence of plants and animals, Goethe discovered — as we have said — the "type."

But the reality of the "type," being essential, is qualitative; and here is where the shoe pinches, for today we have eyes for quantity, but not for quality; we are even convinced — and we declare — that qualitative reality cannot and must not concern science: that science which, reducing everything to the quantitative, ends up, however, giving always and only numbers.

We have already said it: essence is not a word, but a reality. It is not enough, therefore, to pronounce its name or picture it abstractly: one must perceive it.

We know that the first stage of the "path of knowledge" is represented by "study." Study opens the way, but in order to pass from mere thinking (from abstract thinking) to thinking-perceiving (to concrete thinking), one must then integrate it with inner exercise or discipline. Only thus can one in fact develop that imaginative consciousness which — as Goethe bears witness — is at once a thinking-seeing or a seeing-thinking.

Highly significant, in this regard, is the following well-known episode, as related by Steiner: "Goethe had worked out within himself the representation of a plastic-ideal form, such as can reveal itself to a spirit able to encompass with its gaze the multiplicity of plant figures and to note their common element. Schiller contemplated the image of this entity which should live not in a single plant but in all of them, and said, shaking his head in disapproval: 'This is not experience, this is an idea.' To Goethe those words seemed to come from an alien world. He knew, consciously, that he had obtained his symbolic figure through the same kind of spontaneous perception by which one arrives at the representation of a thing that can be seen with the eyes and grasped with the hands. In the symbolic or primordial plant he saw a being as objective as the individual plant. And he believed he owed it not to arbitrary speculation, but to unprejudiced and impartial observation. Hence he could reply only this: 'It can give me great pleasure if I have ideas without knowing it, and can even see them with my eyes'" (R. Steiner: Goethe's Conception of the World – Tilopa, Rome 1991, pp. 17-18).


---


27

In any case, also with regard to the discovery of the "intermaxillary bone," Steiner shows himself interested less in the discovery itself than in the manner in which Goethe arrived at it: that is, in the reasoning that prepared and enabled it.

To proceed by following a line of reasoning (a process of thought) is in fact something quite different from proceeding blindly, through trial and error.

And what was, in this case, Goethe's reasoning? Roughly the following: "If at the basis of the evolution of organisms there is the process of metamorphosis, and therefore an essential continuity, then in man too that intermaxillary bone present in all the animals must exist."

He then set about looking for it, and he found it.

As you know, science, in observing and studying phenomena, formulates hypotheses, which experimental verification can then promote or reject.

At the beginning and at the end of its procedure there are thus two facts of experience: the natural fact (called by Goethe the "empirical phenomenon") and the experimental one (called by Goethe the "scientific phenomenon"); and in between, what is there? There is the hypothesis: that is, not a fact, but a process of thought which can have as its outcome idea A, idea B, idea C, and so on.

Well then, what would we think of a process of thought that always arrived at idea A ("fixed" in the scientist's head), and never at idea B, or at idea C, and so on (which objectively animate the phenomenon)?

We would judge it, at the very least, monotonous or monochord, and for that very reason incapable of measuring itself against the variety, the richness, and the fantasy of reality ("hypotheses," Goethe indeed says, "are lullabies with which the teacher lulls his pupils to sleep" – Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 138). And we would be right to do so, since the moment of the hypothesis is precisely the moment in which the "nobility" (the morality) of the scientist reveals itself: the moment in which it becomes apparent, that is, whether the scientist loves reality more than himself, or does not.

But today is there perhaps a process of thought that invariably arrives at idea A? Of course there is: it is precisely that (materialistic) process which does nothing but reduce quality to quantity.


---


Answer to a question

You see, the process of knowledge includes both induction, which goes from the particular (from the percept) to the universal (to the concept), and deduction, which goes, conversely, from the universal (from the concept) to the particular (to the percept).

Today, however, little value is given to the deductive process, because people are accustomed to philosophical or logical deduction (which goes from concept to concept) and not to scientific deduction (which goes from the concept to the percept). What does the philosopher in fact do? Starting from one or more principles (or from one or more categories), he deduces a whole series of things that follow from them logically, to be sure, but which need not for that reason correspond to reality. From the standpoint of the science of the spirit, this way of proceeding is characteristic of the "rational or feeling soul," which is indeed more attentive to the concept (to the content of thought) than, like the "conscious soul," to the percept (to the content of perception).

Steiner observes in this regard: "Whoever asks 'what relationship is there between the idea and the sense-world outside man' is posing the question badly, for outside man a sense-world (nature) devoid of ideas does not exist. Only man, on his own, can separate the idea from the world of the senses, and thus represent to himself a nature without ideas" (R. Steiner: Goethe's Conception of the World, p. 23).

Precisely by observing nature, Goethe "induces" the universal (the type) by observing the particular (the plant or the animal) and "deduces" the particular by observing the universal. He is therefore able to see the ideas in nature and nature in the ideas, whereas the philosopher sees the ideas outside nature and the (materialist) scientist sees nature outside the ideas.


---


28

The task, then, is to pass from the realism of things to the realism of ideas; to do so, however, one must cross the dark wood of a culture, such as the contemporary one, that actually takes pleasure in having dissolved both the reality of the subject and that of the object.

Psychology took charge of the first dissolution, physics of the second. Psychology indeed opened its doors to instincts, drives, and affective, emotional or archetypal impulses, while closing them at the same time to the I; physics opened them to subatomic particles while closing them to objects as the senses perceive them ("By itself — Goethe asserts instead — and insofar as he makes use of his whole senses, man is the greatest and most precise physical instrument that can exist"; the senses in fact "do not deceive; it is judgment that deceives" — Maxims and Reflections, pp. 160 and 227).

In this way one manages to know that facts or events "happen," but not who makes them happen nor to whom they happen; it is held, in fact, that subject and object are "indeterminable," and that it is therefore possible to know only their "interaction."

Yes, but whoever observes this interaction — does he interact with the phenomenon or not? Here there are only two possibilities: either he interacts with the interaction, which then means that this phenomenon too is indeterminable; or he does not interact with the interaction, which then means that this phenomenon, being determinable, does not exclude the possibility of objective knowledge.

It is very important to keep these things in mind, because almost all those who fall out of the (Ahrimanic) "frying pan" of naive realism (the realism of things) generally end up in the (Luciferic) "fire" of relativism (or of what John R. Searle calls "antirealism").

Do you recall, for instance, what Boncinelli says about living beings? "Living beings," he says, "are essentially engines — mechanical, thermal, chemical or electrochemical — that take in energy of good quality from the surrounding environment and give it back degraded. The active balance of this transformation is used to sustain their unceasing activity, the bulk of which is aimed at keeping themselves alive, a certain portion at multiplying, and another portion at transforming, more or less appreciably, the surrounding environment" (E. Boncinelli: The Brain, the Mind and the Soul — Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 9).

Now, if Ahriman "keeps watch" over those who are content to use part of this "active balance" to eat and sleep ("keep themselves alive"), to reproduce ("multiply") and to work ("transform the surrounding environment more or less appreciably"), Lucifer instead lies in wait for those who, not content with all this, dream of a better life or a better world, yet do not know how or want to think them in a better way (that is, by developing their consciousness).

The former (with satisfaction) and the latter (with regret) are nonetheless both convinced that the only way of doing science is the one currently in force, since they do not realize that a menacing regression has long been under way in cultural life — from the "conscious soul" to the "rational or feeling soul" (if not, indeed, to the "sentient soul") or, which is the same thing, from (Galilean) science to scientistic and materialistic metaphysics.

Physics and psychology, precisely, have pushed themselves, respectively, beyond the sensible and beyond consciousness, deluding themselves that they had thereby reached something higher (exemplary, with regard to the first, is The Tao of Physics by Fritjof Capra — Adelphi, Milan 1994), whereas, given the uncritical way they have proceeded, they have in fact descended into the subsensible and the subconscious.

The misunderstanding, you will agree, is no small one.

In reality, scientific knowledge of the natural world ought to have prepared the way for scientific knowledge of the spiritual world. Once that preparatory phase was exhausted (toward the end of the nineteenth century), science did not, however, turn to the supersensible, but to the subsensible, penetrating this sphere without the faintest idea of the quality of the forces that dwell and operate there.

Just as the sensible had been quantified or mathematized, people went on quantifying or mathematizing the subsensible (to the point of launching a "quantum" mechanics), without even


---


29

asking itself whether such a way of proceeding, tested with (relative) success in the knowledge of the former, was fit to comprehend the reality of the latter.

What, in fact, is the quality of quantity? That is a question only a science of the spirit (or of spirits) could answer.

But it is time to return to our text and take up again the discussion of the intermaxillary bone.

Steiner writes: "Goethe, given his spiritual orientation, could only think that an intermaxillary bone had to exist in man too. It was merely a matter of demonstrating it empirically, by seeking what form it takes in man and how far it fits into the totality of the organism. He succeeded in this demonstration only in the spring of 1784, in collaboration with Loder (Justus Christian Loder, anatomist and professor at Jena — note), with whom he compared human and animal skulls in Jena. Goethe announced the matter on March 27, both to Frau von Stein and to Herder" (pp. 33-34).

What Steiner here calls "spiritual orientation" we might also call a "guiding idea."

Think, for example, of the labyrinth in which the Minotaur dwelt. How did Theseus manage to get out of it? Everyone knows: thanks to the thread given him by Ariadne. But this, precisely, is in image the function of the guiding idea: that of guaranteeing, that is, a luminous continuity to thinking, and therefore — as we say — to the "thread" of the reasoning or of the discourse.

The guiding idea in itself might instead be represented by that star or comet that led the Magi to the cradle of the child Jesus.

But pay attention: the guiding idea must be actively, lucidly and critically conquered. One thing, in fact, is an idea we possess and by which we let ourselves be consciously and freely guided; quite another is an idea that possesses us and drags us along, making us dogmatic, fanatical and intolerant.

Before concluding, however, let us return for a moment to the "thread."

When we do the concentration exercise, we unfold a more or less long series of representations. But what confers order and coherence upon them? Precisely that thread of reasoning which we unwind, inspired by the guiding idea or, in this specific case, by the theme of the exercise (the button, the pin, the pencil, etc.).

By virtue of this exercise we are thus enabled to realize that the nature of representations is discrete, whereas that of reasoning is continuous (like that — we already know — of the flow of time, of life, of thinking, or of memory).

But does this have anything to do, perhaps, with the discovery of the intermaxillary bone? Well then, listen to what Steiner says here:


---


"Goethe never thought of the organism as a dead and rigid structure, but as continually flowing from its inner formative forces (...) The discovery of the intermaxillary bone is therefore merely a consequence of those great conceptions. It was bound to remain incomprehensible to those who did not share them, for in this way the one natural-historical characteristic suited to distinguishing man from the animals was taken away from them. They had not the faintest idea of those thoughts that were seething in Goethe, concerning the elements which, distributed among the animals, are united in harmony in the single human figure, and thus, despite the identity of every particular, found the difference in the totality that assigns to man his high rank in the series of beings. Their way of observing was not an ideal comparison but an extrinsic one, according to which the intermaxillary bone did not exist in man. The seeing with the eyes of the spirit, which Goethe demanded, was an object of very little understanding for them, and this determined the difference of judgment between them and Goethe" (p. 35).

Not only that, but in Goethe's Conception of the World he adds: "When Goethe observes it, nature itself carries the ideas toward him (...) He must let the river of world events flow perpetually through himself. He will then feel that the world of ideas is the active creative potency of nature, and nothing else. And he will not linger to meditate above things and upon things, but will want to penetrate into their depths in order to extract what lives and acts in them" (p. 37).

L.R.
Rome, October 10, 2000

31

Goethe's Scientific Works (7)

42	warm quality? And that a morbid state arises every time that, in the human being, the cold
43	(saline) processes of the upper organization interfere with the warm (sulfurous) ones of the lower
44	organization, and vice versa?

48	"ivory tower") to immerse themselves in objects given by experimental-sensible research, while
49	scientists (materialists) immerse themselves without love in objects given by experimental-
50	sensible research, because they experience no ideal tension ("Few indeed are those – Goethe
51	complained – who become enthusiastic about what appears only to the spirit" – Formation and

53	Still regarding Goethe's "unfinished" work, Steiner adds:


---


"Objective immersion in the observed objects causes the spirit to dissolve completely into them; so that Goethe's theories do not appear to us as if a spirit were abstracting them from objects, but rather as if they formed the objects themselves in a spirit that, during observation, forgot itself. This extremely rigorous objectivity would have made Goethe's work the most perfect work of natural science; it would be an ideal to which every natural scientist should aspire; it would be for the philosopher a typical example of research into the laws of objective observation of the world. One may suppose that the theory of knowledge that now arises everywhere as a fundamental philosophical science will be fruitful only when it takes its starting point from the Goethean manner of thinking and observing" (pp. 36-37).


---


It is worth noting that this judgment by Steiner on the "objectivity" of Goethe's thought coincides with that expressed (in 1822) by Johann Christian August Heinroth (1773-1843), professor of psychiatry at Leipzig, and thus commented by Goethe himself: "In his Anthropology, a work to which we will have occasion to return, Dr. Heinroth speaks benevolently of me and my works, and defines my way of proceeding as genial, consisting – he writes – in the fact that my thought works objectively or, in other terms, does not separate itself from objects, but the elements of these, their sensible images, are absorbed and intimately penetrated by it; that my seeing is already a thinking, my thinking a seeing – a procedure to which he cannot deny his applause (Decisive impulse of a single acute judgment in Works, vol. V, p. 57).

I think you know, in this regard, the definition of truth given by Thomas Aquinas: "Veritas est adaequatio rei et intellectus."

This definition can however mean – as Heidegger rightly notes (On the Essence of Truth – Armando, Rome 1999, p. 37) – both that truth is adaequatio intellectus ad rem (adequation of thinking or knowing to the thing) and that it is adaequatio rei ad intellectum (adequation of the thing to thinking or knowing).

Well, there is no doubt that the first of these two formulas fits perfectly with Goethe's way of proceeding (and with the science of the spirit), while the second fits no less perfectly with the way of proceeding of that science which "adapts" precisely every thing to the thinking or knowing (quantitative) of the intellect or, as one hears said more and more often today, of the "computational mind."

To understand the significance of this difference, try to imagine a person who, in the hope of being able to overcome their anxiety, anguish or despair, turns to a psychometrist: that is, to a psychologist expert in the measurement of psychic phenomena, obtained through the administration of "mental tests."

Well, what is likely to happen? That the first tries to speak and open their soul to the second, but the latter pays little or no attention, entirely taken by the (protocol) requirement to administer tests.

Certainly, such a situation is perhaps paradoxical; nevertheless it exemplifies quite faithfully the way science behaves toward nature.

Even the "naturometrists" (to call them thus) do not in fact listen to that soul which reveals itself through natural beings and phenomena, entirely taken by the (protocol) requirement to weigh and measure them, thus transforming them into numbers.

The fact is that only one who takes seriously, and therefore listens to, their own soul can take seriously, and therefore listen to, the soul of the world (Anima mundi) (let us not forget, in this regard, that Lucifer is a soul without spirit, while Ahriman is precisely a spirit without soul).

You see, almost everywhere today it is taught (once again following Kant) that science has nothing to do with morality, because science is one thing and morality another. But this is not true. Between the truth that science deals with (or should deal with) and the good that morality deals with (or should


---


1	35
concern itself) there exists instead a subtle relationship, in that it is the good that presents itself to
thinking as "truth" and it is truth that presents itself to the will as "good".
It is a question, therefore, of the experience not of two different realities, but of one and the
same reality (that of the I am, or of the Logos) at two different levels.
But what follows from this? Like it or not, this: that a science which declares it has nothing
to do with the good has nothing to do with truth either (nor with "beauty", as the "splendor of the
true").

L.R.
Rome, 17 October 2000

36
Goethe's Scientific Works (8)

We said, last time, that to "research" equipped with an ideal criterion (a guiding idea) which
allows one to reasonably count on the confirmation of facts is quite a different thing from
proceeding blindly, like a traveler devoid of maps or compass.
Listen to what Steiner writes, in commentary on the exposition given by Johann Wilhelm
Josephi (1763-1845), Privatdozent at the University of Göttingen, of the discovery of the
intermaxillary bone:

"This is certainly a complete exposition of Goethe's discovery, but not derived from a
coherent application of the idea of the type, rather as the expression of an immediately
ascertainable fact. When one relies solely on similar cases, then it depends only on fortuitous
circumstances whether one manages to find precisely those specimens in which the thing is clearly
visible. If instead one conceives the matter in Goethe's ideal manner, these special specimens serve
only to confirm the idea, that is, to make manifest what nature usually conceals; but the idea itself
can be followed in any specimen; each one always shows a particular aspect of it. Precisely when one
possesses the idea, one is able to find just those circumstances in which it especially expresses
itself. Without it, instead, one is subject to chance" (p. 40).


---


1	Well, today (making a virtue of necessity) one has gone so far as to theorize and sanction the
evolutionary function of chance, attributing to it the role of the Deus ex machina. Listen, indeed, to
what Jacques Monod (1910-1976) writes: "The elementary initial events that open the way of
evolution to the profoundly conservative systems represented by living beings are microscopic,
fortuitous and without any relationship to the effects they may produce in teleonomic functions
(...) Selection acts upon the products of chance and can feed on nothing else; it operates, however,
within a field of rigorous necessities from which chance is banished. From these necessities, and
not from chance, evolution has drawn its generally ascending orientations, its successive conquests,
the ordered unwinding of which it apparently offers the image" (J. Monod: Chance and Necessity -
Mondadori, Milan 1999, pp. 110-111).
So then: "In the beginning was chance...".
We are convinced, however, that theorizations of this kind derive from nothing other than a
"projection" of the randomness governing the way of proceeding of the subject, in this case of the
researcher, onto the object, that is, onto nature or the world.
For what reason, after all, should a researcher devoid of a guiding idea, accustomed therefore
to moving at random, not be led to believe that nature does likewise?
But what is chance? Nothing else – Voltaire (1694-1778) would say – than the "unknown
cause" of a "known effect": that is, a "scotoma", a "black point" which conceals and usurps the role
of the spirit or, if you will, an empty idea or a concept devoid of concept, in roughly the same
sense in which – as we have seen in quoting Hegel – quantity is "a quality devoid of quality".
Is it not, on the other hand, emblematic that the mechanists, precisely those in whom the
quantitative or "computational" form mentis predominates, elect chance as their God?
But let us move on.


---


Steiner writes: "Just as, among the infinite plant forms, he sought the primordial plant (…), so in relation to animals and man, Goethe did everything he could to 'discover ideal characters' that were fully conformable to the laws of nature. Immediately after his return from Italy, we learn that he was working diligently in Anatomicis, and in 1789 he wrote to Herder: 'I have to set forth a harmony of nature newly discovered.' At that time, this must have been part of the vertebral theory of the skull, whose completion, however, falls in 1790. What he already knew from then on was that all the bones forming the occiput are three modified vertebrae. Goethe imagined this fact in the following way: the brain is nothing but a spinal cord perfected to the highest degree; while in the spinal cord the nerves that serve especially the lower organic functions terminate and branch off, in the brain the nerves that serve the higher spiritual functions terminate and branch off, especially the sensory nerves. In the brain there appears in developed form what is indicated in potentiality in the spinal cord. The brain is a completely perfected marrow; the spinal cord, a brain not yet arrived at perfect development. Now the vertebrae of the spine are completely adapted to the parts of the spinal cord, of which they are the protective organs. It therefore appears very likely that, if the brain is a spinal cord at a higher potency, the bones that protect it are also merely more perfect vertebrae" (pp. 42-43).


---


As you can see, in order to grasp the reality of these processes or metamorphoses, it is necessary to set thought in motion. It is not a matter of merely establishing that A is A and B is B, that C is C and D is D (as analytic logic does, in deference to the principle of identity), but rather of understanding how A changes into B and how C changes into D: that is, how the marrow becomes the brain, and how the vertebrae become the cranial bones.

By invoking becoming, one naturally also invokes time. Which is older, for example, the marrow or the brain? Undoubtedly the brain, because before being a brain it was a marrow. The brain — to put it in Jung's terms — is thus a "Senex," while the marrow is a "Puer"; and the same holds for the cranial bones and the vertebrae.

What matters, however, is that these processes are not directly observable by the (physical) senses. With the eyes of the body, we can indeed see the spinal cord and the brain, the vertebrae and the cranial bones, but not the former transforming into the latter, nor the third transforming into the fourth: in short, we can see the things, but not the movement by virtue of which they metamorphose.

Today we rightly pride ourselves on being evolutionists. But evolution is a process, not a thing that can be seen with the eyes; with these, one can indeed see "evolved" or "involved" things, but not "evolution," since the latter can be "seen" only by thought: that is, it can only be "imagined." But is it possible to imagine it as it objectively is, without possessing an adequate level of consciousness (namely, the imaginative one)? No, it is not possible. All the errors and crudeness of Darwinism and neo-Darwinism (responsible, moreover, for having given renewed breath to "creationism") derive essentially from having naively brought the static reality of the intellect face to face with the dynamic reality of evolution.

Remember what we said, in its time, about those properties that science today calls "emergent"? Well, we have here an example of how things really stand. We have just seen, in fact, that the forces active at a lower level (in the marrow and the vertebrae) convey different properties or qualities at a higher level (in the brain and the cranial bones).

Properties or qualities, then, "emerge" (are actualized) — it is true — "only at a certain level of aggregation" of matter or substance, but they can do so insofar as, at the previous levels, they are not "absent" but rather "immersed" (potentially present), and insofar as it is by virtue of their own activity that matter or substance reaches a higher "level of aggregation."

Whoever fails to grasp the reality of these processes ends up reasoning in the same way as someone who, seeing a diver emerge from the water, but not having seen him plunge into it, took to maintaining that the diver, insofar as he "emerges" from the water, was created or produced by the water.

In any case, of the discovery that "the whole head appears already preformed in the lower bodily organs," Steiner says:


---


"It was a discovery of the broadest significance; with it, it was demonstrated that all the elements of an organic complex are ideally identical; that organically 'internally unformed' masses manifest themselves outwardly in different ways, and that the same thing discloses itself at a lower level as a spinal nerve, at a higher one as a nerve of specific sense, to then open out into organs of sense, capable of perceiving, grasping, comprehending the exterior world. All that lives was thereby described in its plastic and formative force, unfolding from within; it was now, for the first time, understood as truly living" (p. 43).

That is, as that "living" which, if you remember what Boncinelli asserted, "is not yet entirely clear" what it is, and of which "a rigorous definition is still lacking to this day."

But such a state of affairs depends only on the fact — as we shall never tire of repeating — that "it is not yet entirely clear" what the movement of thinking is. Only by grasping the nature of this movement is it in fact possible to grasp the nature of the living.

Augustine said: "Noli foras ire, in te ipsum redi. In interiore homine habitat veritas" — Do not go outward; return within yourself. In the inner man dwells truth (The True Religion — Rusconi, Milan 1997, p. 128).

The "veritas" of life (of the living, or of the etheric body), however, is called upon to mediate between that of the soul (of the astral body) and that of the spirit (of the I), which dwell precisely "in interiore homine," and the "veritas" of death, which dwells instead "in exteriore homine" (in the physical body).

Whoever does not wish to limit himself to a mystical reading of this famous statement must therefore learn to distinguish these levels and to develop, together with thinking, the degrees of consciousness that correspond to them (the imaginative one for life, the inspirational one for the soul, the intuitive one for the spirit).

Freud, for example, declares the "leap from mind to body" "mysterious" precisely because he ignores the reality of these levels and, in particular, the mediating reality of the living (which Goethe, not by chance, called "sensible-supersensible").

At this point, since we still have a bit of time, I would propose to begin the third chapter, entitled: The Essence and Significance of Goethe's Writings on Organic Formation.


---


Steiner writes: "The lofty significance of Goethe's morphological works is to be sought in the fact that in them the theoretical foundations and the method for the study of organic natures are laid down, which is a scientific fact of the first order. If one wishes to assess it rightly, one must above all keep in mind the great difference that obtains between the phenomena of inorganic nature and those of organic nature. A phenomenon of the former is, for example, the collision of two elastic spheres. If one of the spheres is at rest and is struck by the other in a given direction and at a given velocity, the other in turn receives a definite direction of motion and a definite velocity. If, then, it is a matter of comprehending such a phenomenon, this can be achieved only if what is immediately given to the senses is translated into concepts. We must succeed in this to the point that nothing of the sensory real subsists without our having conceptually penetrated it. We see one of the spheres arrive, strike the second, and the latter move onward. We have comprehended this phenomenon when we know how to deduce, from the mass, direction, and velocity of the first and from the mass of the second, the velocity and direction of the latter; when we recognize that, under the given conditions, that phenomenon must necessarily occur." This, however, means nothing other than that what offers itself to our senses must appear as a necessary consequence of what we must presuppose ideally. If this comes about, we can say that concept and phenomenon coincide. There is nothing in the concept that is not also in the phenomenon, and nothing in the phenomenon that is not also in the concept (p. 45).


---


But what does it mean that in the phenomena (or objects) of the inorganic world "there is nothing in the concept that is not also in the phenomenon, and nothing in the phenomenon that is not also in the concept"?

Consider, for example, a bicycle. If we disassembled it, so as to have before us all the parts that compose it, and then reassembled it, we could be absolutely certain of having embraced and mastered the entire object in thought. The reassembled bicycle would in fact be identical to the disassembled one, and would also work in the same way.

It would certainly not be so if, in place of a bicycle, we "disassembled" and then "reassembled" a plant or an animal. The reassembled plant and animal would not in fact be identical to the disassembled ones, nor would they "work" in the same way (not to mention that they would not work at all).

Remember, in this regard, what Goethe said (in a passage we have read a few times before) about a butterfly caught in a net: "The poor creature trembles in the net, shedding its most beautiful colors; and even if one manages to catch it unharmed, in the end there it lies, rigid and lifeless; the corpse is not the whole animal, something is missing from it, a principal part is missing which in this as in every other case is essential: life...".

Thus nothing is missing from the (reassembled) bicycle, whereas from the (killed) butterfly an "essential" part of its nature is missing: namely, life. In the first case we can therefore say that "concept and phenomenon coincide," inasmuch as, so to speak, nothing remains outside; in the second case, instead, they do not coincide, inasmuch as something remains outside (indeed something "principal").

But for what reason is this so? Because the concept (or law) of the bicycle refers back to that of its human inventor or builder, whereas the concept (or law) of the butterfly refers back to that of its cosmic creator, and thereby to a level of consciousness that man could approach and attain only by moving beyond the ordinary level that binds him (representatively) to the sensible world.

Steiner continues: "A body is heated and therefore increases in volume; both the cause and the effect belong to the world of the senses. We thus have no need, in order to comprehend such processes, to step outside the world of the senses. We simply deduce one phenomenon from another within its bosom. When, therefore, we explain such a phenomenon, that is, when we want to penetrate it conceptually, we need not admit into the concept any other element beyond those that are also manifestly perceptible with our senses. We can perceive everything we wish to understand. And in this consists the coinciding of perception (phenomenon) with the concept" (p. 46).


---


It is worth emphasizing that it is precisely this coincidence of perception with the concept that makes the science of dead or inorganic reality admirable, not to say "exact."

Yet it must not be forgotten that this science is a (posthumous) fruit of the incarnation of the Logos; it is not granted to the dead to observe and know death, but rather to the living, or, better said, to the risen.

We owe, therefore, to "bearers of the Logos," such as, for example, Copernicus (1473-1543), Galileo (1564-1642), or Kepler (1571-1630), the birth of the conscious soul and of "modernity," as well as the hard-won affirmation of freedom of thought that followed from it.

And in what did the latter consist? Precisely in the freedom to make the concept coincide with the phenomenon (with sense perception), setting aside everything that Aristotle, Scripture, or religious authority asserted about it.

Today, however, the obstacle is different. In the name of the coincidence of the concept with sense perception, not only do people (materialistically) refuse to enliven, develop, and expand the concept so that it may coincide (like the Goethean concept of the "type") with the perception of supersensible reality (of the living), but they also strive to bring back and reduce every concept of a supersensible order (such as those of life, soul, and spirit) to real perceptions of a sensible order, or to more or less virtual perceptions of a subsensible order.

When we speak of plants, animals, or human beings just as we would speak of "bicycles," we do not exhaust the phenomenon, but rather our own capacities; not knowing how to think otherwise, we in fact do nothing but perform an adaequatio rei ad intellectum.

Let us nonetheless hold firmly, in conclusion, that for every inorganic phenomenon two conditions must always hold: a cause and an effect must be given; and both must be perceivable by the (physical) senses.

When, therefore, one asserts, just to give an example, that the brain is the sensible cause of thinking, feeling, and willing — which are supersensible — one steps out of the field of science and into that of metaphysics.

L.R.
Rome, 24 October 2000

41

Le opere scientifiche di Goethe (9)


---


Tonight, before resuming our reading of the text, it will be well to return to reflect on the difference between organic and inorganic reality: a difference that contemporary science (materialistically oriented) mostly tends to neglect, when it does not outright annul it.

Jacques Monod, for example, explicitly declares that between living and non-living reality there are only quantitative differences, since the former would simply be more "informed" than the latter.

One would be tempted to ask (which Monod does not) whether reality is "living" because it is more informed, or, conversely, whether it is more "informed" because it is living.

For our reflection, I would in any case propose to take up again the following statement of Steiner concerning the phenomena of inorganic nature: "There is nothing in the concept that is not also in the phenomenon, and nothing in the phenomenon that is not also in the concept."

We have already said that in this case, "the accounts — as the saying goes — balance," since there is full correspondence between the character of the phenomenon and that of our perceiving and our thinking.

One might observe, however: as regards perceiving, there is correspondence, since the phenomenon is "physical" and the organs by which we perceive it are "physical"; but what of thinking? If the nature of thinking — according to what the science of the spirit teaches — is "etheric," how can there be correspondence between that and the "physical" nature of the phenomenon?

It is true, the nature of thinking is etheric; no less true, however, is that we think "physical" phenomena with the intellect. And what does it mean that we think them with the intellect? It means that we think them with the brain, and therefore not with etheric thinking, but with its extinguished physical reflection.

The dead reality of the physical world would never have opened up to us cognitively had we not been given the intellect (the mind): that is, the possibility of using, by means of the physical body and, more precisely, the cortical "mirror," a thinking that is no longer direct and alive (imaginative), but indirect and dead (representative). "In the imaginative world — Steiner observes — everything speaks to man as if it were intelligent in a direct way, whereas in the physical world even intelligence can manifest itself only by way of the physical body" (The Stages of Higher Knowledge in On the Path of Initiation — Antroposofica, Milan 1977, p. 31).

There is thus full concordance between knowledge and the reality of the physical world, since we develop the former on the basis of perceptions and thoughts mediated by the physical body.

Regarding this concordance, we have already recalled the definition of truth given by Thomas Aquinas: "Veritas est adaequatio rei et intellectus." The intellect, however, is capable of adapting itself to the reality of "things" or of quantity, but not to those of "processes" and "qualities": in short, it is capable of adapting itself to a part of reality, but not to all of reality. We should not therefore ask of it what does not fall within its capacities, and which it therefore cannot give. "The azure — as a verse of Aleksandr Blok (1880-1921) rightly says — is not measured with the mind."

The intellect is not consciousness, but a part of consciousness: precisely that "intellectual" part which Steiner also calls "representative," "objective," or "material."

Just as the intellectual form exists, so indeed other and higher forms of consciousness exist; and the I is all the freer the more it is able, according to what reality requires, to move among them without identifying itself with any of them.

But the I still identifies itself today with the intellect or the mind (cogito, ergo sum), and to the same degree rejects even the idea of a development of the ordinary level of consciousness.

Pay attention, however: the I rejects this possibility not so much out of love for intellectual consciousness or the computational mind (digito, ergo sum), as out of love for itself; since it is identified with that level of consciousness, it has the deceptive sensation that, by moving beyond number and calculation (and thereby beyond its own existence in the form of an "ego"), it would lose itself.

42

Answer to a question


---


You see, whoever understands the science of the spirit appreciates, perhaps more than many others, the benefits brought to humanity by the intellect, and the advances for which we moderns are indebted to science and technology.

Moreover, criticizing the intellect would make no sense, since it would be like criticizing, say, sight; it would, however, make sense to criticize the latter, were it to advance the titanic pretension of also taking over the functions assigned to the other senses.

But while no sense organ, fortunately, advances such a pretension, intellectual consciousness has long been claiming to deal also with life, soul, and spirit, thereby usurping, in effect, the functions that would instead belong, in order, to imaginative consciousness, inspirative consciousness, and intuitive consciousness.

Since we are at it, allow me to try to bring to light a further aspect of the question.

Let us imagine we had all lost our hearing. What could we do? We might try to make up for it by using some contrivance: that is, some "prosthesis." From that moment on, we would surely consider it a sure "index of progress" to begin investing resources in the research and production of ever more perfect and efficient prostheses.

How can we fail to recognize, however, that such progress would be useless, were we granted the possibility of recovering our hearing? Who tells us, in fact, that the forces used to build and perfect prostheses could not instead be used to recover hearing? Who tells us, that is, (to step out of the metaphor) that the forces we employ to give an intellectual (quantitative) account of life, soul, and spirit, could not instead (and more usefully) be employed to develop higher degrees of consciousness?

One thing, on the other hand, is the healthy intellect (which "keeps its feet on the ground"), another is intellectualistic elephantiasis (which "climbs up mirrors"); just as one thing is science, another is scientistic metaphysics (Schopenhauer said, with his usual "amiability": "To hear the hoarse sing, or to see the lame dance, is a painful thing, but to hear a limited brain philosophize is unbearable" — The Philosophy of the Universities — Adelphi, Milan 1992, p. 48).

It is told, for example, that peasants used to observe ash trees and oaks with attention, because if the leaves sprouted first on the ash trees a fine season was announced, whereas if they sprouted first on the oaks, on the contrary, a rainy one was announced.

Today we wait for satellites to give us this information: that is, extremely costly and sophisticated instruments we could, however, very well do without, were we to return to learning, with humility, the mute language of nature.

Do you remember the Vajont tragedy? In October 1963, a part of Mount Toc collapsed into the Vajont lake, raising an immense wave which, plunging down the valley, swept away and obliterated everything it found in its path.

Well then, listen to what Mauro Corona writes, recalling (as witness, storyteller, and poet) those events: "One day Mount Toc woke up with a start. It woke up because the water of the dam had shoved it roughly. It noticed with astonishment that it had become a little smaller. It had slipped a few meters downward. Then it called the Borgà, the mountain friend of his that stands facing it, and said: 'Listen, the water here is taking away my feet and that crowd of presumptuous technicians, engineers and geologists notices nothing. I am sure I am about to fall down into that damned lake they have built and I don't know how to warn them. For many days I have felt weak and I have tried to make them understand. I have even tilted the trees toward the ground so that my movements would be noticed, but they, obtuse as they are, do not realize. Please help me, put them on alert, warn them yourself, since they pay no heed to me.' The Borgà, which is much older than the Toc and therefore wiser, replied with sadness: 'I can do nothing about it. Those are insensitive people and they do not understand. They are merely arid technicians who cannot interpret our messages: those of the plants, of the water, of the sounds. They are children of presumption, and therefore they develop and put into practice a science that goes against nature...'" (The Flight of the Marten — Vivalda, Turin 1999, pp. 168-169).

The fact is that the intellect, which can comprehend and master only a quarter of reality (and that is no small thing), but which nonetheless has to live within the whole of reality, contrives to realize, as best it can, certain "prostheses" (or, if you prefer, certain "as ifs" or "approximations") that give it the illusion of being able somehow to make up for its own incapacity to comprehend life, soul, and spirit.

As we have seen, the quarter of reality that the intellect comprehends and masters is the physical one; and a physical body is possessed by minerals, plants, animals, and human beings; with the difference, however (as brought to light by the science of the spirit), that this body is essential in minerals, but not in plants, in which (although a physical body is present) the etheric body is essential; not in animals, in which (although a physical body and an etheric body are present) the astral body is essential; and not in human beings, in which (although a physical body, an etheric body, and an astral body are present) the I is essential.

This therefore signifies that the conclusions reached by scientific investigations based on the intellect are, the further they move away from the mineral, the less able they are to grasp — to put it with Jacob Böhme (1575-1642) — the "signatura rerum": the intimate and true nature, that is, of things.

But let us now resume the text.


---


Steiner writes: "It must be conceded that, in a living being, all sensible relations do not appear as a consequence of other relations perceivable by the senses, as is the case for inorganic nature. All sensible qualities appear here rather as consequences of a condition that is no longer perceivable by the senses. They appear as the consequence of a higher unity hovering above the sensible processes. The figure of the root does not determine that of the stem, nor does the latter determine that of the leaf, and so on; rather, all these forms are determined by something that stands above them and that, in itself, no longer has a form visible to the senses. They exist, indeed, for one another, but not one as a consequence of the other. They do not determine one another mutually, but are all determined by an other" (p. 47).

This means that neither is the figure of the root the cause of that of the stem, nor is the figure of the stem the effect of that of the root, but that both (like those of the leaves, the flowers, and the fruits) are "determined by an other": namely, by a "type" or by an idea that "is no longer perceivable by the senses."

It would seem obvious, but it is not so. Listen, in fact, to what Edoardo Boncinelli writes: "When the brain of man could no longer expand materially, because this would have endangered his life or his capacity to reproduce, his abstract correlates — that is, the mind and thought — expanded" (Il cervello, la mente e l'anima – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 289).

The brain, had it continued to expand materially, would thus have put "its life or its capacity to reproduce" at risk. And why? Because it would probably have been crushed by the cranial vault, or would have broken through it (on this point, consult the note by Francesco Giorgi, "Fat brains" and "lean brains," of July 4, 2004 — ed.).

As you can see, whoever asserts this holds that, in man, the brain and the cranial vault do not develop in concert, as both "determined" by the I, but independently and separately (if not indeed antagonistically).


---


In a note, still regarding the difference between the organism and the machine, Steiner adds:

"In the machine everything is a collaboration of the parts, and in it nothing real exists apart from this exchange of action. The unitary principle that governs the common action of those parts is absent from the object itself, and stands outside it, like the design in the mind of the builder. Only the most extreme
                                                    44
myopia can deny that precisely herein lies the difference between organism and mechanism, and that the principle that brings about the collaboration of the parts exists, in the latter, only outside (abstract), while in the former it attains its true existence within the thing itself. Likewise, the conditions of the organism perceivable by the senses do not appear as a mere consequence of one another, but as governed by that inner principle, as the consequence of something that is no longer sensibly perceivable. In this sense it is just as little sensible as the design in the mind of the builder, which likewise exists only for the spirit; indeed it is essentially that very design itself; only that here it has penetrated into the interior of the being and no longer works through the mediation of a third party — that builder — but carries out its actions directly from itself" (p. 47).


---


The "design," the project or the idea of the mechanism, resides in the thought of the builder. It has its law, then, outside itself (ex sé), while the organism has it within itself (in sé).

For anyone who might find these concepts too "abstract" or too "Hegelian," I would like not only to recall that it is really the way we are accustomed to thinking of them that is "abstract," but also to read what Aleksandr Herzen (1812-1870) wrote in his time about Hegel: "There is nothing more ridiculous than the Germans considering Hegel a dry logician, despite the fact that every one of his works is imbued with profound poetry" ("There is nothing more ridiculous than the Germans…considering Hegel a dry logician…, even though each of his works is imbued with powerful poetry" – A. Belyi: Anthroposophy and Russia – St. George Publications, Spring Valley, New York, 1983, p. 31 — our translation).

We could therefore say (in the light of anthroposophy) that minerals are ex sé, since in them the I, the astral body, and the etheric body, standing outside the physical body, are transcendent; that plants are instead ex sé-in sé, since in them the I and the astral body, standing outside the physical body, are transcendent, while the etheric body, residing within it, is immanent; and that animals are finally in sé, since in them (the individual I remaining outside the physical body, and thus transcendent) the astral body, bearer of the collective (group or species) I, and the etheric body stand within the physical body, and are therefore immanent ("The animal subject — Hegel indeed observes — is figure insofar as it is a whole, which is in relation only with itself. It represents the concept, in its developed determinations and insofar as they exist in it" — Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences — Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989, p. 345).

The I, or the essence, thus manifests itself in minerals (specifically in crystals) as a "stable structure," in plants as a "living form," and in animals as a "(collective) model of behavior" (and not yet, therefore, as freedom).

As can be seen, the I, or the essence, by secretly elaborating substance, gradually opens its own passage toward existence, manifestation, or revelation.

We would therefore do well, thinking of the Word that, in opening such a passage, finally manifested itself in the human being (or that finally "emerged" — as some would prefer to say — from the human being), to return to meditating on the following verses of the prologue of the Gospel of John: "In the beginning was the Word (the Logos — ed.), and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. He was in the beginning with God. All things were made through him, and without him not one of the things created was made" (Jn 1:1-4).

L.R.
Rome, October 31, 2000

45
Goethe's Scientific Works (10)


---


We said, last time, that the intellect can comprehend and master only a quarter of reality; that the quarter of reality it comprehends and masters is the physical one; that the physical body is essential only in minerals; and that, for this reason, scientific investigations based on the intellect are all the less capable of grasping the intimate and true nature of things, the further they move away from the mineral kingdom.

Listen, for example, to what Jacques Monod says: "Living beings are strange objects. Men of every age must have known this, more or less confusedly. The development of the natural sciences from the seventeenth century onward, and their explosion from the nineteenth century onward, have contributed to making this impression of strangeness more acute rather than erasing it.
