With regard to the physical laws that govern macroscopic systems, the very existence of living beings seemed a paradox, a violation of certain fundamental principles upon which modern science rests" (Chance and Necessity — Mondadori, Milan 1999, p. 22).

As you can see, what does not seem "strange" and "paradoxical" is the wish to comprehend living beings as "objects" on the basis of physical laws alone; rather, living beings themselves seem to be "strange objects," and their existence a "paradox."

Of the living, Steiner instead says: "Here we cannot derive what we perceive with the senses from relations that are in turn perceivable with the senses; rather, we must admit into the concept of the processes elements that do not belong to the world of the senses, we must go beyond the world of the senses. Observation no longer suffices; we must grasp the unity conceptually, if we wish to explain the phenomena. With this, however, a separation arises between observation and concept; they no longer appear to coincide; the concept hovers above the observation" (p. 48).


---


Why does the concept "hover above observation"? Because, being real and living (not reflected), it "hovers above" the brain, without giving rise to any "representation."
As we know (thanks, in particular, to The Philosophy of Freedom), the reality of the concept is one thing, and that of the representation is another.
Listen to what Hegel says on this point: "Feelings, intuitions, appetitions, volitions, etc., insofar as one is conscious of them, are generally termed representations: one may say, therefore, that philosophy puts, in place of representations, thoughts, categories and, more properly, concepts. Representations in general may be regarded as metaphors of thoughts and concepts (…) In our ordinary consciousness, thoughts are clothed and united with the customary material, both sensible and spiritual, and in our re-thinking, reflecting and reasoning we mingle feelings, intuitions and representations with thoughts" (Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989, pp. 6-7).
And then hear what he adds: "To consciousness it seems as if, with the removal of the mode of representation, the ground is taken away from it, which was its firm and habitual support. When it is transported into the pure region of concepts, it no longer knows what world it is in" (ibid., p. 7).
This is, obviously, the very same consciousness that no longer knows "what world it is in" when it finds itself in the presence of those strange and paradoxical "living objects" of which Monod speaks.
The fact is that unless one educates oneself to "transport" consciousness "into the pure region of concepts" (that is, to the inspired or intuitive level), one will never grasp the reality of that "type" or of that "entelechy" which – as Steiner says – "hovers above observation."
We have already seen, for example, that Montalcini declares herself confident that the cognitive sciences, together with the neurosciences, will not be long in "deciphering the essence of the human species." But what do the cognitive sciences deal with? With "mental representations," considering them (according to the various orientations) "reproductions" (of the external environment), "mental codes," or
                                                  46
"abstract characterizations" – and not, therefore, the "fruits" (as explained in The Philosophy of Freedom) of the encounter and union, in the soul, of the percept (the content of perception, drawn in through the body) with the concept (the content of thinking, drawn in through the spirit).
Hegel writes again, indeed: "Representation begins from intuition and from the found material of intuition"; representation is therefore "the midpoint between the immediate determinate finding-itself of intelligence, and intelligence itself in its freedom, which is thought" (Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989, p. 441).
Just as water is born from the union of hydrogen and oxygen, or as colors were born – according to Goethe – from the union of light and darkness, so representation is born from the union of concept and percept.
How, then, can we expect sciences that have so far failed to decipher even the essence of representation – that is, of their very own subject of inquiry – to decipher "the essence of the human species"?
In any case, the "how" a representation forms is one thing; the "why" is another. And why does it form? Because the concept, being a living entity, cannot know a dead thing except precisely by extinguishing itself and transforming itself into representation.
Representation is, in short, a "cut flower" or, more properly, the shed skin of the concept; and it is precisely for this reason that it enables us to know the inorganic world. What else is a mineral, indeed, if not a "shed skin": that is to say, an "object" into which life, soul and spirit have not entered, or out of which they have on the contrary departed?
It should be clear, therefore, that when it comes to knowing, no longer dead things, but living beings, one must, proceeding in reverse, ascend from representation back to concept.


---


"While in inorganic nature – Steiner observes on this point – concept and reality were one single thing, here they seem to divide from one another and to belong properly to two different worlds" (p. 48).

Kant indeed divided them, placing the former in the noumenal world, the world of things as they are in themselves, and the latter in the phenomenal world, the world of things as they are for us.

Steiner continues, however: "The view that offers itself immediately to the senses no longer seems to carry within itself its ground, its essence. The object no longer appears explicable from itself, because its concept is taken not from its own self but from something else. Since the object does not appear governed by the laws of the world of the senses, and yet is present to the senses and appears to them, it is as if in nature there were an insoluble contradiction, as if there were an abyss between the inorganic phenomena, comprehensible in themselves, and the organic beings, for whom a violation of the laws of nature takes place, for whom, all at once, the laws of universal validity would be broken" (p. 48).

Here, then, is the true reason for the "strangenesses," the "paradoxes," and even the "violations" of which Monod speaks.
All of this, however, derives solely from the fact that the relationship that essence (the noumenon) and existence (the phenomenon) have in organisms is different from the one they have in mechanisms or in any other product of technology.
To Kant must therefore be recognized the merit of having brought this difference to light, but also the fault of having – as the saying goes – "put it in a cast": of not having realized, in other words, that what is not attainable and knowable by ordinary intellectual consciousness can be attained and known by higher degrees of consciousness (which man, however, ought to develop).

47
Steiner writes precisely: "According to Kant, we should limit ourselves to seeing that such a relation exists; but one could not, with regard to organisms, satisfy the logical requirement of recognizing how the general concept, the idea, steps out of itself and manifests itself as sensible reality. Rather, we should have to admit that concept and reality here stand immediately opposite one another, and that both have been actualized thanks to an influence existing outside them, in the same way as man, on the basis of an idea he has conceived, builds any composite object: for example, a machine. With this, the possibility of an explanation of the organic world would be denied – indeed, its impossibility would apparently be demonstrated" (p. 49).


---


Just as it would be senseless, if not indeed cruel, to reproach the mute for not speaking, or the blind for not seeing, so it would be equally senseless, if not indeed cruel, to reproach today's "intellectuals" (who, as such, cannot be – whether they admit it or not – anything other than materialists) for not recognizing the reality of the idea and that of the process by which it steps out of itself and manifests itself as sensible reality.
The intellectual and materialistic has by now become, as it were, a "physiological" limit.
Not without reason does Steiner warn us against thinking that materialism is merely a mistaken theory that can be rationally or dialectically refuted.
What does this theory maintain, for example? That thinking, feeling, and willing depend toto coelo on the physical body (on the brain). Well, it is true: not because – note well – it has always been so and always will be, but because it is so today: because today, that is, human souls have really become so dependent on their physical body that they can no longer even recall a past in which they depended on the (transcendent) spirit, or imagine a future in which, if they so wished, they could realize themselves (thanks to the I and the Logos that dwells within it) in full freedom.
Materialism, however, does not limit itself to registering this contingent (historical or evolutionary) truth; rather, it hypostatizes it, building upon this foundation a culture which, by preventing souls from knowing their authentic history or their authentic past, takes away from them both the possibility of giving a just sense to the present and that of looking to the future with hope and confidence.
Materialism is therefore an obstacle not because it "photographs," so to speak, the condition of a "fallen," "sick," or "alienated" humanity, but because it prevents this humanity from redeeming itself, from healing, or from finding itself again.
"The superstition of the materialists – Steiner states precisely – hinders the true progress of humanity, by failing to provide for the development of the faculties latent in man" (The Degrees of Higher Knowledge in On the Path of Initiation – Antroposofica, Milan 1977, p. 44).
Is it not significant, for example, that a culture that prides itself (rightly) on being historicist and evolutionary has so far failed to give birth to a history of the soul that does not systematically reduce itself to that of the body? And that it therefore continues, irresponsibly, to ignore the (ontogenetic and phylogenetic) becoming of the soul through the "sentient" phase, the "rational or affective" phase, and the "conscious" or, better, "self-conscious" phase?
As you know, it is in this last phase (called precisely by Steiner that of the "consciousness soul") that "modernity" historically comes to light (1413). Well, what happens when a fact of this kind is ignored? It happens that some – as Stephen Toulmin recalls – date the birth of modernity
"to the year 1436, when Gutenberg adopted movable type; some to 1520, with Luther's rebellion against the authority of the Church; others to 1648, with the end of the Thirty Years' War; still others to the American Revolution of 1776 or the French Revolution of 1789; while only for a few do modern times begin in 1895, with Freud's The Interpretation of Dreams and the rise of 'modernism' in the figurative arts and literature" (Cosmopolis – Rizzoli, Milan 1991, p. 18).
What happens, in short, is that these (material) events are considered as the "causes" of modernity, and not – as would be right – as its symptomatic or emblematic "effects."
                                                  48
But let us return to Kant.
He distinguishes – as we have said – the noumenon from the phenomenon, then entrusting to a third (to a superhuman "intellectus archetypus") the burden of placing the former in relation with the latter, precisely because, conceiving the noumenon abstractly, he cannot manage to imagine how the idea, as entelechy, possesses the force to animate and enliven the phenomenon, or "how – in Steiner's words – the general concept, the idea, steps out of itself and manifests itself as sensible reality."
But the idea that "steps out of itself," flows or – as Hegel likes to say – "unfolds," manifests itself, before it does so as physical reality, as etheric reality. It is from this "source" ("light of life") that there springs, indeed, that current of life (that "life of light") which enlivens all organisms.
No wonder, then, that whoever cannot conceive a reality of this kind ends up thinking – like Kant – that just as man, as a third party, first has the idea of the machine and then builds it, so God, as a third party, first has the idea of the plant and then builds it (and keeps it alive).
At this point, Baruch Spinoza (1632-1677) enters the scene.


---


"Goethe – Steiner writes indeed – first approached Spinoza in the spring of 1774; and he speaks of this first acquaintance with the philosopher (in Poetry and Truth) in the following way: 'After having vainly busied myself all over the world to find a means of educating my strange being, I finally came upon the Ethics of this man'" (p. 49).

What interested Goethe most was the fact that Spinoza admitted – contrary to Kant – the possibility of a knowledge of the essence of things (of the "things in themselves"). He distinguished, indeed, three kinds of knowledge, explaining how the third and highest of these was "that – according to what Steiner further writes – by which, from an adequate representation of the true essence of certain attributes of God, we progress to an adequate knowledge of the essence of things. Spinoza calls this kind of knowledge scientia intuitiva. It was to this last, supreme kind of knowledge that Goethe aspired" (p. 51).

But what does it mean, as Spinoza says, "that things must come to be known in such a way that we recognize in their essence certain attributes of God"?

That God – Steiner always answers – "is the ideal content of the world, the moving principle that drives, sustains, and guides everything"; but he immediately adds: "Now this can be represented in such a way as to presuppose it as an independent being, existing for itself, separate from finite beings, which has these finite things beside it and dominates them and sets them in mutual interaction. Or one represents this being as poured out into finite things, so as to exist no longer above or beside them, but only in them. This conception does not at all deny that primordial principle; it fully recognizes it, but considers it as poured out into the world. The first of the two conceptions regards the finite world as a manifestation of the infinite, but this infinite remains subsisting in its own being, giving away nothing of itself. It does not step out of itself; it remains what it was before its manifestation. The second conception likewise regards the finite world as a manifestation of the infinite; it only presupposes that this infinite, in its manifesting, has completely stepped out of itself, has laid down itself, its own essence and life, in its creation, so that henceforth it exists only in this. Then, since knowing is manifestly a discovering of the essence of things, while this essence can only consist in that part which a finite being has of the primordial principle of all things, to know means to discover that infinite in things" (p. 51).

In the first case, then, we have a "transcendent" conception (of God); in the second, an "immanent" one.
One must be careful, however, not to identify immanentism with "pantheism."


---


49

Since God is present in the physical world (as body or space), in the etheric world (as life or time), in the astral world (as quality or essence), and in that of the I (as Being or Logos), who then is a "pantheist"? The one who recognizes only the living God in the etheric sphere (as generally do, in a more or less "paganizing" way, the naturalists or the vitalists).

Who instead is a "polytheist"? The one who recognizes only the Gods who populate the astral sphere, that is to say, the entities that form the so-called "world of ideas."

And who, finally, is a "monotheist"? The one who recognizes only the God of the I — but of an I, pay close attention, reflected by the physical body in the form of ego.

Let me say, in this regard, that hearing people speak, as is often done, of a "Judeo-Christian" conception is truly dismaying. The Christian conception is, in fact, a "trinitarian" one, or more precisely a "triunitarian" one, which has very little to do either with the Jewish monotheistic conception or with the Islamic monotheistic one.

Answer to a question

Spinoza distinguishes "natura naturans," or creative nature, from "natura naturata," or created nature. The whole problem, as you see, lies in the way the relationship between the one and the other is conceived. Whoever conceives it in a transcendent way thinks that natura naturans stands above natura naturata or beside it; whoever conceives it in an immanent way thinks instead that natura naturans stands within natura naturata, and that the latter therefore manifests or reveals it.

One does not do science, in any case, by merely asserting that "God is in everything"; one does it only if one is capable of distinguishing, within the whole, the different levels, the different modes or the different qualities of the divine presence. Goethe states precisely: "'I believe in a God!' That is a fine, commendable phrase; but to recognize God where and in what form He manifests Himself — that is in truth bliss on earth" (Maxims and Reflections — TEA, Rome 1988, pp. 176-177).

To know means, in fact, to discover not only the essence of things (the Hegelian "determinate being"), but also what these essences have in common (the quality of "essence"), and therefore the Being of all essences (the Hegelian "indeterminate being").


---


Steiner writes: "Before Goethe (...) organic nature was explained according to the former conception (the transcendent one — note), inorganic nature according to the other" (the immanent one — note); and he adds: "Concerning the book by Jacobi (Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi, 1743-1819 — note), published in 1811, under the title Of Divine Things and Their Manifestation, Goethe observes: 'How could a book by a beloved friend be welcome to me, in which I had to see maintained the thesis that nature hides God? With my pure, deep, innate, tested conception, which had taught me to see God infallibly in nature, and nature in God, so that this manner of representation formed the very basis of my existence, how could such a strange, one-sidedly limited expression not estrange me forever, in spirit, from the noblest man whose heart I devotedly loved?'" (p. 52).

It is not in fact nature that hides God; it is we who do so.

You see, we are accustomed to using a fine expression: the "given." But what is a given if not something that does not hide itself, precisely because it gives itself, offers itself, bestows itself? And on what does its unveiling or concealing then depend? Solely on our understanding or lack of understanding.

Let us keep in mind, in any case, that it is also possible to unveil one thing while concealing another. To modern man, for instance, the "death of God" or the "dead God" has been unveiled: that is to say, the God of the physical or inorganic world; his (representative) eyes have thereby been opened for the world in which God is dead, but not those (imaginative) for the world in which God is alive: precisely those eyes, that is, which ancient man (the man of the sentient soul or of myth) still had open.

50

It is nonetheless true, on the other hand, that without passing through this experience of death (and of blindness), we would not have made ourselves free, and therefore capable of taking upon ourselves, individually, the responsibility of gradually reopening the eyes (of the spirit) for the risen God.

L.R.
Rome, November 7, 2000

51

Goethe's Scientific Works (11)

Let us begin reading at once.


---


Suppose, Steiner writes, "that the causal process consists of the elements m, d and v (mass, direction and velocity of an elastic sphere in motion), and the produced process of the elements m', d' and v'; then for given m, d and v, m', d' and v' will be determined by them. If I now want to comprehend the process, I must unite in a common concept the totality of the process, which is composed of cause and effect. This concept, however, is not such that it can reside within the process itself and determine it. It merely unites two processes in a common expression. It effects and determines nothing. Only the objects of the world of the senses determine themselves. The elements m, d and v are perceptible to the outer senses as well. The concept appears only to serve the spirit as a summarizing means; it expresses something that is neither ideal nor conceptual, but sensibly real. And that something which it expresses is the sensible object. Our knowledge of inorganic nature rests on the possibility of grasping the outer world by means of the senses and of expressing their mutual interactions by means of concepts" (p. 53).

Let us therefore observe the sensible phenomenon A ( constituted of the elements m, d and v) and the sensible phenomenon B (constituted of the elements m', d' and v'), and then, by thinking, place them in a relation of "cause and effect" with one another.

This is what the intellect does, and we must acknowledge that it does it admirably.

It is by its virtue, in fact, that thinking, moving in a linear and discrete way, relates cause A with effect B, cause B with effect C, cause C with effect D, and so on.

When one observes and thinks about an organism, however, thinking must move differently: that is, no longer in a linear and discrete way, but in a circular and continuous one.

In this case, in fact, it is as if the phenomena A, B, C and D were ideally situated along a circumference, and depended therefore no longer one upon the other, but all upon its center; that center, then, would be the single invisible cause of multiple visible effects.

Kant realized this, but he pronounced: "Man will never be able to know such an invisible cause, because the human intellect is not capable of such knowledge."

He was not wrong in saying so, since the knowledge of invisible causes does not pertain to intellectual thinking. We could — it is true — educate it, develop it and transform it, so as to allow it to cross this limit, but it is unfortunately difficult, if not rare, to find anyone willing to do so (let it always be borne in mind that anthroposophy pursues, at one and the same time, the evolution of the human being through knowledge, and knowledge through the evolution of the human being).

Kant himself, for instance, not only did not do so, but actually hypostasized those limits, entrusting the burden of managing what transcends them to the divine intellect: that is, to the intellect of the Creator, and not of the creature.


---


Answer to a question

The center of the circumference of which I have spoken is in reality the "type," the essence, or the idea.

Let us remember that we have on the one side the I and the astral body, on the other the etheric body and the physical body, and that between the former and the latter lies what Steiner calls the "threshold": that is, the boundary line dividing the sphere of being (and of essences) from that of existing (of time and space).

I know well that those who have made Albert Einstein's teaching their own no longer consider time and space as two distinct realities (as Newton, for instance, conceived them), but speak of a single "space-time" dimension, just as they no longer distinguish energy from mass.

52

One must be very careful, however, about the level at which these syntheses are carried out.

On this side of the "threshold," in fact — that is, in the sphere of existing or of the etheric-physical world — both time and space, and energy and mass, are (qualitatively) different realities.

Just think of man. What do space and mass correspond to? To his physical body (dead or anatomical); and what, on the other hand, do time and energy correspond to? To his etheric body (living or physiological).

To unify below the threshold (indeed in the subsensible) both time and space, and energy and mass, does not therefore help to clarify ideas; it helps rather to decompose or break up those few and (not coincidentally so-called) "solid" certainties that the intellect derives from its experience of the sense-world.

To mix and confuse these elements, ignoring what truly unifies them (beyond the threshold), therefore means regressing — that is, descending — below the "natural light" into the subnatural darkness, and not progressing — that is, ascending — above the "natural light" to the spiritual light: it means, in short, chaotizing, and not synthesizing.

But let us return to our subject. We have said that in order to grasp the essence of an organism a different movement of thinking or a different faculty of judgment is required.

Or rather, Steiner explains: "A faculty of judgment that can confer upon a thought also other substance than that obtained solely by means of the outer senses, that can comprehend not only the sensible, but what is purely ideal, by itself, separate from the sense-world"; and he adds: "A concept that is not derived by abstraction from the world of the senses, but has a content springing only from itself, can be called an intuitive concept, and its knowledge intuitive. What follows from this is clear: an organism can be comprehended only in the intuitive concept; and Goethe demonstrates through facts that this kind of knowledge is granted to man" (p. 54).


---


But what is an "intuitive concept"? It is a concept grasped by intuitive consciousness, and thereby at last understood for what it is. Only intuitive consciousness can in fact grasp the (spiritual) essence of the concept, since ordinary representative consciousness can grasp only its dead (physical) body, imaginative consciousness only its living (etheric) body, and inspirative consciousness only its soul (astral) body.

Answer to a question

As we have said so many times, one thing is the concept, another the representative consciousness of it that we normally have, just as one thing, for instance, is a living being, another its portrait or its photograph.

The Zen masters say: "The moon reflected in the pond is not the moon." Well then, the concept reflected in the brain (as a representation) is not the concept either.

But pay attention: it is true that the concept reflected in the brain is not the concept; yet it is precisely by virtue of its reflection that we are enabled to discover, by raising our gaze upward, the real concept.

This is the reason why, around reflected reality (the reality of appearance or of non-being), a bitter "spiritual struggle" rages between the (Michaelic) entities, which urge man to make use of it to raise his gaze toward his spiritual being, and the (Ahrimanic) ones, which push him instead to make use of it to lower his gaze toward his supposed material or submaterial being.

It is in this second direction that present-day science moves, which, to put it in Zen terms, does nothing but methodically dredge the pond, in the illusion of finding there the reality of the moon reflected in it.

"To raise the gaze," however, means "to raise thinking," beginning by carrying it from the ordinary static or representative level to the dynamic or imaginative one. Between the indirect experience of the reality of the concept and the direct one is placed, in fact, the living experience of thinking (as force, movement or activity).

53

Answer to a question

You see, the empiricists maintain that the concept is derived or abstracted from perception; this holds, however, not for the concept (in itself), but for our consciousness of the concept.

It is by virtue of perception, in fact, that we manage to gain a first (abstract or nominalistic) consciousness of the concept. But this we should then develop, until we pass from the perception of the object (which gives us the concept) to the perception of the concept (which gives us the object), thereby (dynamically) unifying those opposite points of view which, if adopted unilaterally, give rise respectively to empiricism and rationalism. Let us not forget, in fact, that for Hegel (but also for Steiner) reality is precisely the unity or synthesis of essence and manifestation. In this sense, we could even say that essence is nothing but manifestation seen from within (from the spirit), and that manifestation is nothing but essence seen from without (from the physical body).

Steiner writes in this regard: "This self-determining element we can call, with Goethe, an entelechy. Entelechy is thus the force that, by virtue of its own power, calls itself into life. What appears in the phenomenon has sensible life too, but this is determined by the force of the entelechy" (p. 54).

The entelechy — we have said — is (potential) form, but has no (actual) form. It can therefore take on any form, and thus appear in way A in habitat A, in way B in habitat B, in way C in habitat C, and so on. It is these different modes of appearing of the entelechy that present themselves to our (physical) senses, and it is from these, therefore, that anyone who wishes to ascend, by thinking, back to it must set out.

Steiner observes precisely: "Here human reason intervenes and forms in the idea an organism which corresponds not to the influences of the outer world, but only to that principle. Every chance influence that has nothing to do with the organic as such is thereby eliminated. Now, this idea, which in the organism corresponds purely to the organic, is the idea of the primordial organism, of Goethe's type. From this one also sees the high legitimacy of this idea of the type. It is not a mere logical concept; it is what in every organism is the truly organic element, without which it would not be an organism at all" (p. 55).


---


Steiner writes in this regard: "This self-determining element we can call, with Goethe, an entelechy. Entelechy is thus the force that, by virtue of its own power, calls itself into life. What appears in the phenomenon has sensible life too, but this is determined by the force of the entelechy" (p. 54).

The entelechy — we have said — is (potential) form, but has no (actual) form. It can therefore take on any form, and thus appear in way A in habitat A, in way B in habitat B, in way C in habitat C, and so on. It is these different modes of appearing of the entelechy that present themselves to our (physical) senses, and it is from these, therefore, that anyone who wishes to ascend, by thinking, back to it must set out.

Steiner observes precisely: "Here human reason intervenes and forms in the idea an organism which corresponds not to the influences of the outer world, but only to that principle. Every chance influence that has nothing to do with the organic as such is thereby eliminated. Now, this idea, which in the organism corresponds purely to the organic, is the idea of the primordial organism, of Goethe's type. From this one also sees the high legitimacy of this idea of the type. It is not a mere logical concept; it is what in every organism is the truly organic element, without which it would not be an organism at all" (p. 55).


---


The need to pass from representing to imagining becomes very clear here. Representation, in fact, insofar as it is static and bound to sensible perception, can only grasp one of the multiple forms assumed by the entelechy: that is, one of its (determinate) states, but not its becoming.

Think, for example (to take up the title of a well-known book by the Russian painter Wassily Kandinsky), of Point and Line to Plane (Adelphi, Milan 1968). From the standpoint of analytic logic and the principle of identity, these are three different realities (A, B, C), juxtaposed: the point is in fact the point (A=A), just as the line is the line (B=B) and the surface is the surface (C=C).

According to this logic, then, these are realities that have nothing in common (apart from the fact of all three being geometrical), and that still less could be born one from another. The matter changes, however, if one integrates this point of view with that of imaginative logic. What does this logic in fact do? It introduces movement into the consideration of things and of their mutual relations. And does one perhaps discover something if one introduces movement into the consideration of the point, the line and the surface? Very much indeed! One discovers, nothing less, that the line is generated by the movement of the point, and that the surface is generated, in its turn, by the movement of the line.

Shall we give another example?

Try to imagine drawing a triangle mentally. You may draw it right-angled, obtuse-angled or acute-angled, equilateral, isosceles or scalene, but you will always and in any case be drawing one particular triangle.

54

Now imagine that someone says he has instead drawn the "universal triangle." Would you believe him or not? And if you did believe him, would you not have had recourse to an act of faith?

But why have recourse to faith, and not to imagination? Thanks to the latter, in fact, you could directly observe, beyond the physical (representative) one, the etheric manifestation of the "universal triangle" (which, as such, lies beyond the "threshold"): its manifestation, that is, at that level (called by Goethe and Steiner "sensible-supersensible") whose task is precisely to mediate, to serve as a trait d'union, between that of the so-called "immanence" (sensible) and that of the so-called "transcendence" (supersensible).

Suppose — Steiner gives this example — that we draw a triangle mentally, in such a way as to allow its sides, hinged at a vertex, to move with different velocities and in every direction; by moving and completing a whole turn (as the hands of a clock roughly do), these sides will give form, instant after instant, to all possible triangles.

This is certainly inconvenient, he adds, "since one must carry out movements in one's thoughts. But in this way one really does obtain the universal thought of the triangle; whereas it is not attained if one stops at a single triangle. The universal thought 'triangle' is there when the thought is kept in continuous movement, when it is mobile" (Cosmic Thinking — Basaia, Rome 1985, p. 10).

One "stops at a single triangle," and hence at a representation of it, every time this "continuous movement" is arrested (rather as is done today with the "freeze-frame" of video recorders).


---


Answer to a question

It can happen, in fact, that the intellect, accustomed to dealing with dead things in proper detachment, finding itself before a living thing — a plant or an animal, say — falls in love with it: that is, proves unable to preserve the coolness and lucidity that are its own, and thus ends up giving its relation with it a sentimental, if not indeed sensual, character.

One thing, moreover, are the feelings generated (from above downward) by spiritual thoughts; another the (natural) feelings that generate (from below upward) thoughts.

Only elevated thoughts can in fact open the way to elevated feelings: that is, to that living, vibrant and deep feeling that has nothing to do with the sentimentalism or sensualism of the so-called "nature lovers."

Think, in this regard, of the epoch of the sentient soul (3564-747 B.C.). Then thinking stood within willing, rather as today the intelligence of animals stands within their instincts. Then came the epoch of the rational or feeling soul (747 B.C.-A.D. 1413). What changed? Thinking moved within feeling, manifesting itself no longer in mythical form, but in conceptual and philosophical form. Now, in the epoch of the conscious soul (which begins precisely in A.D. 1413), where does thinking stand? It is simple: within itself.

After thinking within willing and thinking within feeling, we have therefore thinking within thinking (the living and pure thinking).

It is this thinking, reflecting itself — as we have seen — in the cerebral mirror, that makes possible the birth of individual consciousness (of self-consciousness), of freedom and of modernity. Everyone can in fact begin to think whatever he wishes, since thinking now no longer carries the compelling feeling and willing of the spiritual world.

This process has, however, a reverse side that must absolutely not be ignored.

What does living thinking give us? Living universality. And what happens when thinking instead reflects itself in the cerebral mirror? Universality becomes abstract, and as a consequence feeling and willing move away from it, placing themselves respectively at the service of particularity and singularity: that is to say, of egohood.


---


55

Whoever is sincere will not be slow to admit, in fact, that natural feeling and willing always and only lead us back to ourselves: the former chiefly through vanity; the latter chiefly through utility or self-interest.

And what can be done, then, to overcome such a state of affairs (such a "human condition")? Only one thing can be done: to lead feeling and willing gradually back within thinking.

Let us clarify — to avoid misunderstandings — that leading feeling and willing back within thinking does not mean leading them back to universality at the expense of particularity and singularity, nor does it in any way imply that, in order to give to God what is God's, one must no longer give to Caesar what is Caesar's.

It means, rather, restoring to God what, while belonging to God, was previously being given, in surplus, to Caesar.

Steiner states, for instance: "In willing, freedom is exercised; in feeling, it is experienced; in thinking, it is recognized" (The Story of My Life — Antroposofica, Milan 1992, p. 135).

What it comes down to, in the end, is redistributing the forces equitably, so that feeling and willing the universal (conquered through thinking) may bestow new light and new impulses upon morality, and that feeling and willing the particular or the singular, freed of every plethora, may at last see themselves restored to their sane and human sobriety.

L.R.
Rome, November 14, 2000

56

Goethe's Scientific Works (12)


---


I would like to begin, this evening, by reading you several passages from a lecture given by the celebrated German physicist (Nobel laureate in 1932) Werner Heisenberg (1901-1976) at the general assembly of the Goethe Society, on May 21, 1967 in Weimar.

"According to Goethe," Heisenberg says, "the observation and knowledge of nature begins with the immediate sensory impression; therefore not with the observation of a single phenomenon by means of instruments, so to speak, torn away from nature, but with the natural event directly perceivable by our senses (...) But Goethe does not stop at immediate observation. He knows well that only with the guidance of a connection, which at first is merely presumed but subsequently becomes certain, can the immediate sensation become knowledge. I cite as an example a point from the preface to the Theory of Colours: 'The mere looking at a thing does not permit us to progress. Every looking turns into a considering, every considering into a reflecting, every reflecting into a connecting. One can therefore say that we already theorize in every attentive glance directed at the world. If, however, the abstraction we fear is to be neutralized, and if the result of experience, which we hope for, is to turn out genuinely vital and useful, we must know how to carry out these transitions with consciousness, self-awareness, freedom and — to use a bold expression — with irony.'"

"The abstraction we fear." Here we already have a precise indication of the point at which Goethe's road diverges from that of official science. Goethe knows that all knowledge needs images, relations, interpretive structures. Without these, knowledge would be impossible. But the road that leads to these structures leads inevitably to abstraction." "Contemporary natural science" in fact employs abstraction (mathematical in the first place), whereas Goethe, on arriving "at the frontier with abstraction, was afraid (...) The frontiers of abstraction were not to be violated. Where the limit of observation had been reached, one was not to go further and replace observation with abstract thought" (Goethe's Conception of Nature and the World of Science and Technology, in Beyond the Frontiers of Science — Editori Riuniti, Rome 1984, pp. 150-151).

So let us reflect: first it is asserted that Goethe "does not stop at immediate observation," since "he knows well that only with the guidance of a connection (...) can the immediate sensation become knowledge" (that is, he does not stop at perception, since he knows well that this can become knowledge only under the guidance of thinking); then it is maintained that Goethe, on arriving "at the frontier with abstraction, was afraid," and, so as not to violate it and not to replace observation with abstract thought, went no further.

But if one acknowledges that Goethe "does not stop at immediate observation," how can one then say that, having reached the limit of observation, he went no further (thereby renouncing the "images," the "relations," or the "interpretive structures")?

The fact of the matter is that one should not identify abstracting with thinking. It is not that Goethe, in order not to replace observation with abstract thought, renounced thinking; it is that he no longer thought in an intellectual manner, but in an imaginative one.

Goethe's road diverges from that of "official science," then, not because the latter abstracts insofar as it thinks and the former does not abstract insofar as he does not think, but because Goethe succeeds in thinking without abstracting.

But how can one think without abstracting? It is quickly said: by constantly adapting the mode of thought to the quality of the phenomenon.

We have seen, in this regard, that ordinary representative thought is adequate to inorganic reality, but not to organic reality.

And yet — we might ask ourselves — how is it that an "abstract" thought shows itself adequate to a "concrete" (physical) reality? Could it not be that the reality we believe "concrete" is in fact abstract?

57

Well, it is so; it is not easy, however, to explain in what sense inorganic reality is "abstract."

Think, for example, of a puppet and an actor: the former is moved from without; the latter from within. We can in fact regard the actor's I as a kind of "puppeteer" who moves his character (his "puppet") from within. We have therefore two puppeteers: the one, "transcendent," moves his puppet from without; the other, "immanent," moves it instead from within.

Now, consulting any dictionary of the Italian language, we discover that "astrarre" (Lat. abstrahere: composed of abs "from" and trahere "to draw") is equivalent to "removing," "separating" or "detaching." In this sense, the puppet, which is precisely removed, separated or detached from the subject (the puppeteer) that moves it, is therefore abstract.

What has been said of the puppet holds, however, for the whole inorganic world: this too is in fact abstract, since it is separated (like a corpse) from life, from soul and from spirit (from the I).

Every reality that has outside itself its own essence, or, so to speak, its own "reason for being," must therefore be judged "abstract."

But let us take up our reading again.


---


Steiner writes: "For the inorganic, what is to be observed as essential is the fact that the phenomenon, in its variety, is not identical with the law that explains it, but merely points to that law as to something external. Perception, the material element of knowledge, which is given to us through the outer senses, and the concept, the formal element through which we recognize the perception as necessary, confront each other as two elements that in truth objectively postulate one another, but in such a way that the concept does not reside in the individual members of one and the same series of phenomena, but in the relation of these to one another. This relation, which gathers the variety into a unitary whole, is grounded in the individual parts of the given, but in its totality (as unity) does not rise to real, concrete manifestation. Only the members of this relation attain outward existence — in the object. The unity, the concept, manifests as such only in our intellect" (pp. 55-56).

We might even say, if we wished, that the law stands to the inorganic phenomenon as the Kantian "categorical imperative" stands to the human being. As the former resides outside or above the phenomenon, in the mind of man, so the latter resides outside or above man, in the mind of God.

Let us think again, in any case, of the automobile. What, properly speaking, is an automobile? An assemblage of parts: parts which, precisely insofar as they are assembled or placed in relation to one another, present themselves as a unity. This unity (the concept) does not, however, dwell in the automobile, but in the head of the subject who conceived and built it.

"In organic nature," Steiner goes on, "the parts of one and the same being do not stand in such an external relation to one another. The unity is realized in the perceived object simultaneously with the variety and as identical with it. The relation among the individual members of a complex of phenomena (the organism) has become something real. It no longer rises to concrete manifestation only in our intellect, but in the object itself, which unfolds the variety by drawing it out of itself. The concept no longer has simply the function of a sum, of a summary that has its object outside itself; with this it has become completely unified. What we see is no longer different from that by means of which we think what is seen; we see the concept itself as idea (...) The idea by means of which we comprehend the organic is therefore essentially different from the concept by means of which we explain the inorganic; it does not simply gather up, like a sum, a given multiplicity, but unfolds from itself its own content" (p. 56).


---


58

This explains why Goethe asserted, in his Prose Sayings: "The concept is a sum; the idea is the result of experience; the intellect is needed to accomplish the former, reason is required to grasp the latter" (p. 55).

On the difference between intellect (concept) and reason (idea), Steiner writes: "A concept is the single thought as it is fixed by the intellect. If I set in motion, in a living flowing, a plurality of such single thoughts, so that they interpenetrate and interconnect, there arise configurations of thought accessible only to reason, unreachable by the intellect. For reason, the creatures of the intellect renounce their separate existences and continue to live only as parts of a whole. We shall call these configurations created by reason, ideas"; the intellect is thus analytic, whereas reason is not only synthetic, but is also capable "of perceiving the ideas" (Fundamentals of a Theory of Knowledge of the Goethean World View, in Philosophical Essays — Antroposofica, Milan 1974, pp. 63-64).

Reason could not, however, perceive the ideas if these were not real. We have already mentioned, one evening, the name of John Searle (b. 1932), one of the greatest contemporary American philosophers. He distinguishes, clearly, the followers (like himself) of "realism" from those of "antirealism" (cf. Mind, Language and Society — Cortina, Milan 2000).

His is, however, a realism of "things," and not, like that of Goethe and Steiner, a realism of "ideas." I think you will recall that this type of realism is described, in The Philosophy of Freedom, as "naive" or "primitive," precisely because, not having realized (critically) that things are ideas, it believes (uncritically) that ideas are things.

The point is not, in any case, to substitute one unilateral conviction with an opposite and equally unilateral one; the point is rather to integrate the first with the second, not indeed abstractly, but by realizing that if ideas, from the standpoint of sensible perception, are things, then things, from the standpoint of spiritual perception, are ideas.

Answer to a question

It is true: ordinary thinking makes it possible to know not only machines, conceived and built by man, but also — as you say — crystals, which have been neither conceived nor built by man.

But our propensity to regard as equally "dead" both what was once alive (like plants, animals and human beings) and what was never alive (like precisely the minerals) depends precisely on the fact that the intellect finds itself perfectly at ease with both.


---


Of the idea, or of the entelechy, Steiner says further: "The entelechy grounded in itself comprises a number of sensible formative configurations, one of which must be the first and another the last; and the one can follow the other always and only in a determinate way. The ideal unity unfolds from itself a series of sensible organs in temporal sequence and spatial contiguity, and withdraws in a wholly determinate manner from the rest of nature. It generates its various states from its own inwardness. These are therefore comprehensible only to one who follows the configuring of successive states, proceeding from an ideal unity; and this means that an organic being is comprehensible only in its becoming, in its development" (p. 58).

This last point could also be put as follows: an organic being is comprehensible only in time.

Not because — note well — such a being becomes in time, but because its becoming and its life are its time (its etheric body). This also explains why an inorganic being, insofar as it has become, is a state (physical body), and can instead be comprehended only in space.

Steiner writes precisely: "The inorganic body is closed, rigid, excitable only from without, but inwardly immobile. The organism is restlessness itself, perpetually changing,

59

transforming itself from within outward, in continuous metamamorphosis. To this the following expressions of Goethe refer: 'Reason is directed to what is becoming, the intellect to what has become; the former does not ask: what for?; the latter does not ask: whence? The former delights in development; the latter wants to consolidate everything so that it may be of use' (Prose Sayings)" (p. 58).


---


Well then, with regard to "what for?," to "being of use," or, in a word, to "utility" — do you recall what Giulio Barsanti says about Linnaeus's system? That although it was, in many respects, "artificial" (so much so that it even "transfigured" nature), it was preferred to the "more natural" one of Joseph Pitton de Tournefort because, its artificiality constituting "a great advantage for botanical science," it proved precisely more useful.

To the same point, Werner Heisenberg, in a short essay in which he compares Goethe's theory of colours with Newton's, acknowledges that Newton's theory has proved practically more useful, but at the same time asks whether it is really correct to consider "truer" what is simply "more useful."

One must be careful, however, not to oppose a "useful" lie with a "useless" truth.

One should rather ask: to whom is the lie useful? And to whom is the truth useful? By proceeding thus, one could in fact come to understand that the lie is useful to those who place themselves at the service of only the material part of man, passing it off as the whole man, whereas the truth is useful to those who place themselves effectively at the service of the whole man, and therefore also of his material part.

Even today many delude themselves that they can set against the brute practicality of the modern technical-scientific culture the abstract (and, all things considered, narcissistic) meditativeness or contemplativeness of the classical (humanistic) culture, whereas what would be urgent instead would be to set against the exclusively material and instrumental practicality of the former a higher and richer practicality: that is, a (quasi-"artistic") practicality that would be useful (like that of the science of the spirit) not only to the body, but also to the soul and the spirit.

But let us return to our subject.

Steiner writes: "The organism comes to meet us in nature in two principal forms: as plant and as animal; in both, in a different way. The plant is distinguished from the animal by the lack of a real inner life. In the animal this arises as sensation, as free movement, etc. The plant has no such soul principle. It still unfolds itself entirely in its outwardness, in its figure" (p. 58).


---


We have said and repeated that inorganic reality has its reason for being outside itself, whereas organic reality has it within itself. This latter, however, is present as spirit (as individual I) in man, as soul (as astral body or collective I) in the animal, and as life (as etheric body) in the plant.

This has the consequence that the plant can do nothing but bestow itself upon its own environment and, pouring out everything it has within itself outside itself, thus grant the world the "gratia plena" of its forms, its colours, its scents, its flowers, its fruits, and even its therapeutic virtues.

It would be important to meditate on this reality, since an exercise of this kind could carry one very, very far.

Let it suffice here to hint at the fact that the plant pours out everything it has within itself precisely because, having no astral body, and therefore no sensations and cravings, it is pure, immaculate or chaste.

Moving from the earth toward the heavens, and having no will of its own (no ego) to impose or assert, it is as if it echoed the Virgin, saying: "Ecce ancilla Domini; Fiat mihi secundum verbum tuum" — "Behold the handmaid of the Lord; be it done unto me according to thy word."

L.R. Rome, November 21, 2000

60

Goethe's Scientific Works (13)


---


You will recall that Werner Heisenberg, in a lecture from which we read several passages last week, asserts: "According to Goethe, the observation and knowledge of nature begins with the immediate sensory impression; therefore not with the observation of a single phenomenon by means of instruments, so to speak, torn away from nature, but with the natural event directly perceivable by our senses."

Goethe is in fact convinced — as we have already seen — that "by itself and insofar as it makes use of its whole senses, man is the greatest and most precise physical instrument that can exist," and that it is therefore not the senses that deceive him, but judgment; a judgment that can deceive him to such a point as to persuade him to place more trust in an artificial instrument than in his own natural senses, and therefore in himself. Goethe notes precisely: "The greatest evil of recent physics is that the experiments are separated from man himself, and that nature is seen only in what the artificial instruments show: that is, they want to prove her by these, and with them they limit the understanding of her" (quoted in W. Heisenberg: Beyond the Frontiers of Science — Editori Riuniti, Rome 1984, p. 153).

This mistrust has, however, distant roots. Steiner recalls: "At an unpropitious moment, suspicion toward the human sense-organs took hold of a Greek thinker, and he came to believe that those organs did not transmit truth to man but deceived him; he thus lost faith in what spontaneous and unbiased observation offers, and convinced himself that regarding the true essence of things thought sends messages different from those of experience" (Goethe's Conception of the World — Tilopa, Rome 1991, p. 19).

It is difficult to say who that "Greek thinker" was, but it is a fact that this mistrust is already encountered in the Eleatic philosophical school (whose greatest representative, and real founder, was Parmenides — beginning of the fifth century B.C.), and it reaches almost its culmination in that of Plato (427-347 B.C.).

We are faced, in any case, with an apparent paradox.

Last time, we said in fact that "the lie is useful to those who place themselves at the service of only the material part of man, passing it off as the whole man, whereas the truth is useful to those who place themselves effectively at the service of the whole man, and therefore also of his material part."

But how to explain, then, that precisely those who care only for the material part of man, passing it off as the whole man, have no faith in the physical senses and in their experiences? How to explain, that is, that it is precisely the materialists who have no faith in the material body of man?

The answer is simple: because they regard it as equal to the physical body of a mineral, completely ignoring the fact that man's, unlike the mineral's, is a physical body that harbours and conveys an etheric body (a life), an astral body (a soul) and an I (a spirit).

Whoever thought it exaggerated to say that the materialists regard the human body as qualitatively equal to that of a mineral (though quantitatively different, being more "informed") should know that Jacques Monod explicitly invites us to ask "whether the internal forces that confer upon living beings their microscopic structure might not perchance have the same nature as the microscopic interactions of crystalline morphologies" (Chance and Necessity — Mondadori, Milan 1997, p. 16).

In reality, people believe they can avoid, wholly or in part, what is taken to be the deception of the natural senses by having recourse to the use of artificial instruments (that is, to the use of "things" which it is still and always the natural senses that must observe) only because they are incapable of distinguishing the sense-organs (physical) from the activity (etheric) that is carried out through them, and they ignore, as a consequence, that sense-experience is a living experience carried out by means of dead organs (we refer here, naturally, to the classic five exteroceptive senses).

And to think that the Buddha already drew attention to the fact that it is not the eyes that see, but that it is we who see through the eyes!

61

Goethe is therefore right: it is not the senses that deceive, but judgment. But why can it deceive? Why can it judge, say, white black and black white?

There is only one answer: because otherwise man would not be free. It is for this sole end that the possibility of error was given to him, not, however, without the possibility of recognizing error as such and of being able thus freely to seek the truth: precisely that truth which once imposed itself upon man, just as it still today imposes itself upon all the beings of nature.

Truth, no longer imposed by the transcendent spirit (by the Father), can therefore be posited (thought, felt and willed) by man, through love or by the grace of the immanent spirit (of the Son who dwells in the I).

But let us return to the text.

We have so far dealt with the archetypal plant (the Urpflanze), and we have seen how this entity (the "type") generates, in order to adapt itself to the environment, the infinite variety of vegetal forms.

We are accustomed, however, to distinguish not only one plant from another, but also, within one and the same plant, the root from the stem, the stem from the leaf, the leaf from the flower, and so on.

For just as there is a variety of plants, so there is also a variety within plants.


---


Steiner writes in this regard: "The question therefore arises: what produces that phenomenal variety of the plant's organs, which, according to the inner principle, are identical? How is it possible for the formative laws, all operating according to a single formative principle, to produce at one time a leaf, at another a sepal (one of the small leaves that form the calyx — note)? The variety in the life of the plant, which unfolds entirely within the inwardness, can likewise rest only upon external, that is, spatial elements. Now Goethe regards as such an alternating expansion and contraction. The principle of the entelechy, which operates from the standpoint of the life of plants, on entering existence manifests itself as spatial; the formative forces operate in space; they generate organs of determinate spatial form. These forces either concentrate, tending toward a single point: and this is the stage of contraction; or they extend, widen, strive to move away from one another: and this is the stage of expansion" (p. 59).

It will be well to recall, in this connection, the distinction of the ethers carried out by the science of the spirit. In the etheric world there are in fact active, in relation to the so-called "elements" (fire, air, water and earth), four corresponding species of ethers: the warmth ether; the light ether; the chemical or sound ether; the life ether.

What matters to underline here, however, is that the former two (the warmth ether and the light ether) have a centrifugal nature, and therefore tend to "expansion," whereas the latter two (the chemical or sound ether and the life ether) have a centripetal nature, and therefore tend to "contraction."

Goethe, though ignorant of this, nonetheless had a presentiment, in the plant, of the secret working of forces producing not only an "alternating expansion and contraction," but also an "ascending metamorphosis": that is to say, for example, the process that leads, in a particular specimen, from the root to the flower or fruit, and, in the vegetal kingdom, from the lower cryptogams (algae, fungi, lichens, etc.) to the higher phanerogams.


---


"In the total life of the plant," Steiner writes in this regard, "three expansions and three contractions alternate (...) At first the whole plant, in latent state, stands contracted in a point, in the seed. From here it emerges, unfolds, expands in the formation of the leaf. The formative forces push each other ever further apart; hence the lower leaves still appear coarse, compact; the higher one goes, the more they become veined, serrated. The elements, which before were still crowding close upon one another, now move apart. What previously stood at successive intervals appears again, in the formation of the calyx, concentrated at a point of the stem. This constitutes the second contraction. In the corolla there appears once more an unfolding, a widening. The petals are finer, more tender in comparison with the sepals; which

62

can depend only on a lesser intensity at a point, and therefore on a greater extension of the formative forces. In the sexual organs (stamens and pistils) the next contraction takes place, after which a new expansion occurs in the formation of the fruit. In the seed issuing from the fruit the whole essence of the plant appears once more, contracted into a point." And (in the footnote) he adds: "The life of the plant differentiates into a final organ, the true fruit, and into the seeds; in the former all the phenomenal elements are, as it were, united; it is pure phenomenon, it estranges itself from life, becomes a dead product. In the seed all the inward essential elements of the life of plants are concentrated. From it a new plant arises. It has become almost wholly ideal; the phenomenal in it is reduced to a minimum" (pp. 59-60).


---


The seed is thus potency, the fruit act; the seed is a becoming, the fruit a become.

But do we not speak also of a "fruit" of reasoning or of discourse? And what is this fruit, if not precisely the end or conclusion of the reasoning or the discourse, and therefore something that is no longer in becoming because it has by now become?

But is the become perhaps being? No, it is state (that is, dead or represented being).

I regard it as very important to keep this in mind, because otherwise it would be hard to reconcile the idea of being with that of becoming ("If the eternal exists, becoming cannot exist," Emanuele Severino declares precisely — P. Coda, E. Severino: Truth and Nothingness — SANPAOLO, Cinisello Balsamo (Mi) 2000, p. 31).

You will recall, for instance, that Parmenides is par excellence the "philosopher of being that is" (and of becoming that is not), just as Heraclitus, by contrast, is par excellence the "philosopher of becoming that is" (and of being that is not).

Well, this dichotomy has run through the centuries, and even today it sees arrayed, on the side of being, the conservatives, the anti-historicists, the creationists or, in short, the opponents of "modernity," and, on the side of becoming, the progressives, the historicists, the evolutionists or, in short, the champions of "modernity."

What, then, is the problem? It is that, in a wholly unnoticed way, we represent being in a "spatial" key and becoming in a "temporal" one: that is, we represent them as if they resided on this side of the "threshold," and therefore fell within the sphere of existing, and not within that of being.

But the space and the time we experience on this side of the threshold are in reality nothing but the reflection or counter-image of that being and that becoming which, beyond the threshold, are a living unity — since being becomes, and becoming is.

Do you remember the first verses of the Prologue to the Gospel of John? "In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. He was in the beginning with God. All things were made through him, and without him was made nothing that has been made" (Jn 1:1-3).

Well then, I would like to suggest that you try to meditate on this paraphrase of it: "In the beginning was the Becoming, and the Becoming was with Being, and the Becoming was Being. The Becoming was in the beginning with Being. All things have become through the Becoming, and without him has become nothing that has become."


---


Answer to a question

The become is the thing, the object, matter or dead spirit.

You see, spirit is fire, spirit is air, spirit is water and spirit is earth. All is spirit, but one must then see at what level and in what form spirit manifests itself: that of earth, for instance, is the lowest of its levels of manifestation. Nevertheless, it is precisely at this level (called by Steiner, in his Anthroposophical Maxims, that of the "completed work") that spirit dies and, in man, consciousness of spirit (self-consciousness) is born. But it is born precisely as consciousness of dead spirit (of dead thought), or as a dead consciousness of spirit (of thought).

63

Each of us, in imitatio of what Christ did with Lazarus, is therefore called to make the spirit, or the consciousness of the spirit, rise again.

How necessary — to return to our subject — such a resurrection of thinking is, the following words of Steiner prove, were further proof still needed: "Objection has been raised especially against the Goethean concept of an alternating contraction and expansion. All the criticisms in this regard derive, however, from a misunderstanding. It is believed that these concepts could be valid only if a physical cause were found for them, and if one succeeded in indicating a mode of action of the laws operating in the plant, of which such an expansion and contraction would be the consequence. This shows that one is starting from the top instead of from the base. Nothing is to be presupposed that occasions the expansion and contraction; on the contrary, everything else is a consequence of them, which effect a continuous metamorphosis from step to step. One is incapable of representing the concept simply in its own proper, intuitive form; one demands that it represent the result of an external process. One manages to think expansion and contraction only as products, not as producing" (p. 61).

As the expansion and contraction of the lungs are produced by our breathing (by the breathing of the I), so the expansion and contraction of the plant's organs are produced by the breathing of the entelechy. And as it would therefore be senseless to seek the external cause of the alternating movement of our lungs, so it is senseless to seek the external cause of the expanding and contracting of the plant's organs.


---


Steiner continues: "The life of plants is sustained by material metabolism. With regard to this, an essential diversity shows itself between those organs that are nearer to the roots, that is, that provide for the absorption of nourishment from the earth, and those that receive the nutritive substances already passed through other organs. The former appear immediately dependent on their external inorganic surroundings; the latter, however, on the organic parts that precede them. Each subsequent organ therefore receives a nourishment, as it were, prepared for it by what precedes it. Nature proceeds from the seed to the fruit in a sequence of positions, so that the following appears as the result of the preceding. And this proceeding is called by Goethe a proceeding upon a spiritual ladder" (p. 62).

The organs that are nearer to the roots thus grasp the nourishment just as it is, while those farther away receive it already worked up, filtered or distilled. We too, moreover, do not let the food reach the stomach just as it is, but first take care to chew it and moisten it with saliva in the mouth. From the topical point of view, however, we do it in reverse: that is, we send downward ("down") what we have worked up above, whereas the plant sends upward what it has worked up below. Whoever knows the science of the spirit knows well that a difference of this kind carries great significance.

Steiner writes: "In animal life something different certainly takes place. Here life does not lose itself in outwardness, but rather separates itself, distinguishes itself from corporeality, and employs bodily manifestation merely as its own instrument (...) The animal appears as a world closed within itself, a microcosm in a far higher sense than the plant. It has a center to which every organ serves (...) In the animals, every organ appears as deriving from that center; the center forms all the organs in conformity with its own being. The figure of the animal is therefore the foundation of its outward existence. But it is determined from within (...) If all animal beings were conformed solely to the principles inherent in the primordial animal, they would all be alike" (pp. 62-63).

The mineral has a physical body, but not yet an etheric body (a life), an astral body (a soul) and an I (a spirit); the vegetal has, instead, a physical body and an etheric body, but not


---


64

an astral body and an I yet; the animal, finally, has a physical body, an etheric body, an astral body, but not yet an I (an individual one).

The advent of the astral body therefore marks the advent of a subjectivity (not of an "individuality") capable of using the etheric body and the physical body as its own instruments: capable, that is, of making the outwardness (the ex se) the means of expression (Lat. exprimere: to press — premere — in order to bring out — ex-) of the inwardness (the in se).

"Language" and behaviour are thus born. Since, however, existing animals are not all alike, in that they are not "conformed solely to the principles inherent in the primordial animal," their "language" (the chirping, the bleating, the neighing, the roaring, the trumpeting, etc.) and their behaviour (as the ethologists know well) become diversified, thereby giving expression to the (collective) soul of the various species.

By means of "language" and behaviour, the inner sensations of animals are thus revealed (in dogs, for instance, the sensation that gives rise to barking is different from those that give rise instead to yelping, howling, growling or whimpering).

But how are all these differentiations born?

"Here," Steiner answers, "adaptation, by virtue of which the organism is conformed according to the surrounding outward characteristics, and the struggle for existence, tending toward the survival of only those beings best adapted to the prevailing conditions, assert their rights. But adaptation and the struggle for life could effect nothing upon the organism if the principle that constitutes it were not such as to be able to assume the most varied forms while the inner unity remains constant. The connection between the outer formative forces and this principle, however, is not at all to be understood as though the forces in question acted upon it as one organic being acts upon another. The outer conditions are indeed the occasion for the type to unfold itself in a determinate form; this form itself, however, is not to be derived from the outer conditions, but from the inner principle" (p. 64).

The differences between species are therefore the result of the interaction between the environment and the Urpflanze (the "type" or archetypal plant), in the vegetal kingdom, and between the environment and the Urtier (the "type" or archetypal animal), in the animal kingdom.

Terrestrial plants and animals will therefore never be able to equal their respective "types," since to do so they would have to renounce their (sensible) existence; nor will they ever be able to equal the environment, since to do so they would have to renounce their (supersensible) essence.

If one grasps this "interlocutory" relation between type and environment, one then grasps that all those become "mechanicists" who ignore the type and look to the environment as the sole "cause" of the variety of vegetal and animal forms, and that all those instead become "neo-Darwinists" who — as we saw in due course — put "chance" in the place of the type.

L.R.
Rome, November 28, 2000

65

Goethe's Scientific Works (14)


---


Let us begin reading at once:

"The difference between plants and animals, established by Goethe through these conceptions, might appear to be of no importance in the face of the justified doubts that modern science raises about the existence of a definite boundary between plants and animals. But Goethe was already aware of the impossibility of erecting such a boundary. And yet he gives determinate definitions of plant and of animal. This is connected with his whole conception of nature. He attributes in general nothing constant, nothing fixed to the phenomenon, because in it all fluctuates in perpetual movement. But the essence of a thing, to be fixed in a concept, is not derived from the changing forms, but from certain mean stages in which it can be observed" (pp. 64-65).

What does it mean that "the essence of a thing, to be fixed in a concept, is not derived from the changing forms, but from certain mean stages in which it can be observed"?

It will be easier for us to understand this if we think, for example, of the relation between the soul and its faculties: thinking, feeling and willing.

We know that those of thinking and willing, although they are activities of the soul, are conditioned, above, by the spirit (by the I) and, below, by the body: we know that they are, that is, "changing forms" from which the essence of the soul, "to be fixed in a concept," is not "derived."

And from what can it then be derived? From the activity of feeling, which represents precisely a determinate "mean state."

Just as, in the soul, the activity of feeling is situated between that of thinking and that of willing, so, in the plant, the activity of the leaf is situated between that of the root and that of the flower (or fruit).

This is why Goethe, within the phenomenal realm, grasps precisely in the leaf the most reliable (probable) manifestation of the plant's essence.

Steiner observes: "Whereas Darwinism must presuppose a primordial organism, one can say of Goethe that he discovered the essence of that primordial organism"; and he then explains (in a note): "In the modern doctrine of nature, a primordial organism is commonly understood to mean a primordial cell (protocyte), that is, a simple being standing on the lowest rung of organic evolution. What is in view here is an absolutely determinate, real and sensible being. When, however, one speaks of a primordial organism in the Goethean sense, it is not a matter of something visible, but of that essence, that entelechial formative principle, which makes of that primordial cell an organism. This principle manifests itself in the simplest organism no less than in the most perfected, only in a different development" (p. 65).


---


That there is a relation between the "primordial cell" and life is beyond doubt; what remains to be established is whether one must explain, as the "modern doctrine of nature" does, the latter by means of the former, or, as Goethe and Steiner do, the former by means of the latter.

But why does that "doctrine" explain life by means of the (primordial) cell? It is obvious: because it sees the cell, whereas life it not only does not see, but does not even know what it is.

If one asserts, with Goethe and Steiner, that it is life that explains the cell, one must therefore be ready to explain what life is, thereby finding oneself grappling with the problem of how, or in what way, the extrasensible is to be thought.

But how many are there today who pose such a problem? In the general conviction that the good of the intellect (of the "natural light") is thinking as such, and not one particular way of thinking, it is more than rare, in fact, that

66

it occurs to anyone that intellectual (representative) thought is fit to think some things, but not others, and that, in order to think the others, a different and higher thought is required.

It is more than rare, in other words, to find anyone willing to admit that, in the soul-spiritual realm, an ascending series of degrees of consciousness holds sway, just as in the natural realm "an ascending series of developmental stages prevails."

Steiner writes precisely: "In the vegetal kingdom no less than in the animal kingdom, an ascending series of developmental stages prevails. Organisms divide into perfect and imperfect. How is this possible? The ideal form, the type of organisms, has precisely the characteristic of consisting of spatial and temporal elements. Hence it appeared to Goethe also as a sensible-supersensible form. It contains spatial and temporal forms as ideal (intuitive) observation. Now, when the real (no longer intuitive) sensible form manifests itself, it can correspond more or less completely to that ideal one; the type can attain its full development or not" (p. 67).

Since the "type" (containing in potential state "spatial and temporal forms") develops in form A in environment A, in form B in environment B, in form C in environment C, and so on, we might assert (as the "relativists" do in the ethical and noetic field) that these forms are equivalent, in that they all refer back to one and the same "type."

If we assert, then, that there is an "ascending series" (a hierarchy) of forms, and that one plant can therefore be more evolved than another, it is because we take into account the fact that, just as it is true, on the one hand, that the "type" never incarnates fully, so it is true, on the other, that there exist organisms in which this supersensible essence actualizes few of its potentialities, others in which it actualizes more, and others still in which it actualizes almost the totality.


---


Steiner goes on, in fact: "The lower organisms are such precisely because their phenomenal form does not fully correspond to the organic type. The more the phenomenon and the organic type coincide in a determinate being, the more perfect it is.
