This is the objective reason for an ascending series of development"; and shortly afterward he adds: "The cryptogams are precisely plants in which the primordial plant manifests itself in the most one-sided way; they represent the idea of the plant in a one-sided sensible form. They can be evaluated on the basis of the established idea; but this idea itself rises to complete manifestation only in the phanerogams" (p. 67).

And so we arrive at what Ernst Haeckel (1834-1919) calls the "fundamental biogenetic law." He writes precisely: "I had already pointed out in my General Morphology (at the end of the fifth book), as one of the most important concepts of transformism, the close causal nexus which, according to my conviction, exists between the two branches of the history of organic development, and had given it precise expression in several Theses on the causal nexus of biontic and phyletic evolution: 'Ontogenesis is a brief and rapid recapitulation of phylogenesis, determined by the physiological functions of heredity (reproduction) and adaptation (nutrition)'" (The Riddle of the Universe — UTET, Turin 1904, pp. 106-107).

"From Goethe's concepts," Steiner writes in this regard, "we also obtain an ideal explanation of the fact, found by Darwin and Haeckel, that the developmental history of the individual represents a repetition of the history of the species. For what Haeckel presents cannot be taken for more than an unexplained fact. It is the fact that every individual traverses, in abbreviated form, all the stages of development which palaeontology shows us as distinct organic forms. Haeckel and his followers explain it by the law of heredity. But this too is only an abbreviated expression for the fact cited. The explanation is that those forms, like every individual, are the phenomenal forms of one and the same primordial form, which in successive periods of time develops the

67

formative forces according to the possibilities lying within it. Every higher individual is more perfect precisely because the favourable influences of its environment have not prevented it from developing in full freedom according to its inner nature. If, instead, the individual is compelled, by various influences, to remain on a lower rung, then only some of its lower forces develop, and that becomes a whole for it which, for that more perfect individual, is only a part of the whole. In this way the higher organism, in its development, appears composed of the lower organisms, or the lower ones in their turn appear as parts of the higher one" (pp. 68-69).


---


As you see, in order for a faculty or property to "emerge" — as we say today — it is necessary that a series of presuppositions be created which thereby end up representing a kind of history of the manifestation of that faculty.

It is no wonder, then, that the "type," in order to allow one of its new and higher faculties to emerge, must retrace, albeit briefly, all the phases it had to traverse in order to allow that faculty to come to maturity.

The human organism is therefore superior to the animal one precisely in that it is composed, in its development, of the animal organisms, or in that the animal organisms appear, in their turn, as parts of the human one.

This means, however, that in man there is present and operative a principle (the I) that surpasses not only that which governs the mineral kingdom and the vegetal kingdom, but also that which governs the animal kingdom.

"Just as the physicist," Steiner observes, "is not content to state and describe the facts, but seeks out their laws, that is, seeks the concepts of the phenomena, so it cannot suffice one who wishes to penetrate into the nature of organic beings simply to adduce the facts of affinity, heredity, struggle for existence, etc.; he wants to know the ideas that lie at the basis of these. In Goethe we find this aspiration" (p. 69).

What does Steiner mean here? That affinity, heredity or the struggle for existence, representing the means by which the "type" pursues its ends (itself), can explain how the "type" pursues them, but not why it pursues them.

"It is maintained," he goes on, "that the concept of metamorphosis, in the Goethean sense, would be a mere image formed only in our intellect by way of abstraction (...) One overturns Goethe's conceptions if one posits as first and principal a sensible organ and then derives the rest from it in a manner accessible to the senses. He never meant to say that. For him, what is first in time is not at all first in the idea, in the principle. The stamens and the leaves are not today akin because the former were once leaves; rather, because they are ideally akin, according to their inner essence, they once appeared as true leaves. The sensible transformation is only the consequence of the ideal affinity, and not vice versa" (p. 69).


---


As you see, this is the same difficulty raised by the concept of "life": that of "metamorphosis" too is in fact the concept of a process (which lives in time), and not of a "thing" (which lies in space).

Which means that its living and uninterrupted flowing turns into a static "mere image formed only in our intellect by way of abstraction" only at the moment when we represent it to ourselves, just as a brook, a stream or a river would turn into an equally static "mere image" at the very moment we painted or photographed it.

The fact of the matter is that we think the real world by means of the ideal one, but we do not think the ideal world as real; and we do not think it such — we have said — because we do not perceive it.

We could, however, come to perceive it if we had the courage to change ourselves, devoting ourselves to study and to inner exercise.

When one considers, for example, the extraordinary things that jugglers or acrobats manage to do by virtue of constant physical exercise, one is led to think of the "extra-ordinary" things that all of us could do, by virtue of constant spiritual exercise, in the field of knowledge: such as managing, for instance, to think and know what is ordinarily held to be unthinkable or unknowable.

Let us not forget, on this precise point, that the science of the spirit is precisely a "practice" of thinking, and not an abstract speculation, and that this practice contemplates first and foremost the observation of thinking.

"The peculiar nature of thinking," Steiner explains, "consists in the fact that the thinker forgets the thinking while he carries it out. It is not thinking that occupies the thinker, but the object observed upon which he thinks." Since thinking is "the unobserved element of the ordinary life of our spirit," its observation is therefore "the most extraordinarily important of all" that it is possible to undertake (The Philosophy of Freedom — Antroposofica, Milan 1966, pp. 35 and 38).

Only one who manages to observe or perceive thinking (thanks to the exercise of "concentration") is in fact able to recognize it as an (etheric) reality.

The concept of "metamorphosis," to return to Goethe, is therefore not an abstract, brilliant or "dainty" idea, but rather a concept in which there is implied a movement or process having essentially the same nature as that thinking which, by imagining it, grasps it.

For Goethe, Steiner says, "what is first in time is not at all first in the idea, in the principle. The stamens and the leaves are not today akin because the former were once leaves; rather, because they are ideally akin, according to their inner essence, they once appeared as true leaves."

This means that the affinity between stamens and leaves derives not from the fact that the stamens, before becoming such, were leaves, but from the fact that the one no less than the other are forms of manifestation of one and the same essence.

Consider, for example, the relation in which a rough-hewn statue stands to the finished one, imagining however (since we are referring to organic reality) that the sculptor is inside the block of marble and therefore models it from within.

As one sees, the roughed-out statue is "first in time" with respect to the finished statue, but not with respect to the sculptor: that is, to the "type," the "idea" or the "principle."

The inner sculptor, that is, the "statue-concept," therefore precedes (on the plane of being) the roughed-out statue (on the plane of existing), just as the latter in its turn precedes (always on the plane of existing) the finished statue. What is "last" (omega) from the existential or sensible-perception point of view is therefore "first" (alpha) from the essential or spiritual-perception point of view.

"Only when the philosopher," Steiner rightly observes, "considers the absolutely last as his first, will he be able to reach his goal. And the absolutely last to which the evolution of the world has attained is thinking" (ibid., p. 44).

Considerations of this kind ought, moreover, to hold also for the origin of the human species. Who indeed has been, if not precisely the "man-concept" (the I), to pass through a series of stages in order then to metamorphose itself into that particular "man-percept" whom we call Homo sapiens (but which, to tell the truth, it would be more appropriate to call Homo intelligens or Homo cerebri)?

This modern "man-percept," or man of the ego, is, however, the "man-concept," or man of the I, "roughed-out," and not yet therefore "finished" or, better said, actualized or incarnated.

Nature has in fact brought him to this evolutionary stage (that of the conscious soul) so that, starting from it, he might consciously and voluntarily continue and complete his evolution.


---


69

One can therefore become man or not become man, since nature has arranged things in such a way that our evolution can reach its goal, and not in such a way that it must reach it.

But let us return to our subject.

Steiner writes: "Just as every newly discovered planet must, according to Kepler's laws, circle around its fixed star, so must it happen for every process in organic nature, according to the ideas of Goethe. The processes of the starry heavens were seen long before Kepler and Copernicus. These two first found their laws. Long before Goethe, the organic natural kingdom was being observed. Goethe discovered its laws. Goethe is the Copernicus and the Kepler of the organic world" (p. 70).

Today's materialistic science is, however, far from regarding him as such.

But this depends above all on the fact that the learning of that science requires more intelligence and applied diligence than an active and deepened commitment of thought (one involving, as such, the soul).

Let us be clear: it is not that one who attends school or University is not active, but he is active precisely in order to learn, to know and to remember (trusting, perhaps, in a future and profitable investment of the "intellectual capital" thus accumulated), but certainly not in order to educate and form himself, so far as to come, for example, to say to himself: "If I do not mature in thinking, I shall never be able to understand the reality that surrounds me"; "If I do not mature in feeling, I shall never be able to experience my own humanity"; "If I do not mature in willing, I shall never be able to act in freedom and with love."

Today, however, people look more and more to "information" and less and less to "formation."

But information too forms. And how does it form? What, that is, is the quality of the formation given by information? No one knows.

We therefore run the risk, by informing, of giving form unawares to something that could hold bitter surprises in store for us in the future.

L.R.
Rome, December 5, 2000

70

Goethe's Scientific Works (15)

Let us take up our reading again at once.


---


It is interesting to see — Steiner writes — "how Goethe himself, in the face of the empirical students of nature, enters the field for the rights of experimental research, and for the rigorous separation of idea and object. In 1796 Sömmerring (Samuel Thomas von Sömmerring, anatomist, 1755-1830 — note) sends him a book in which he makes the attempt to discover the seat of the soul. Goethe, in a letter addressed to Sömmerring on August 28, 1796, says that the latter had woven too much metaphysics into his views, and that an idea about the objects of experience has no justification whatever when it transcends them, and is not grounded in the essence of the objects themselves. The idea must be, in the face of the objects of experience, an organ for comprehending as necessary connection what would otherwise be purely perceived as blind relation of contiguity in space and succession in time. From the fact that the idea can add nothing new to the object, it follows that the latter itself is, according to its own essence, something ideal, and that empirical reality in general must have two sides: one by which it is particular, individual, the other by which it is ideal-universal" (p. 71).


---


If the idea were not an "organ" deputed to comprehend, beyond the sphere of space and time, the essence of objects and their "necessary connection," we could only assert that A stands next to B, that B stands next to C, that C stands next to D, etc., or else that A comes before B, that B comes before C, that C comes before D, etc., and we could never accede to that (qualitative) "necessary connection" that allows us, for example, to assert that A is the cause of B and that B is the effect of A.

There is therefore an abyssal difference between "getting — as we say — an idea" about things (and about their relations) and grasping the idea in things (and in their relations): that is, between "calling up," devising or inventing an idea, and discovering it.

Ideas are in fact part of reality, in that they are the other face of things.

Imagine observing an object — an armchair, say. With your eyes you will never see it whole, but will always and only see a part of it; suppose it were the front; in order to see the back, you would have to move either yourself or the armchair.

Well, this is a little what we should have to do if we wanted to see the other face of things, with the obvious difference that in this case we would have to carry out an inner movement: that is, to pass with our gaze or our attention from the material object (perceived by means of the physical senses) to the ideal one (perceived by means of thinking).

Let us keep well in mind, however, that between the thing and the idea of the thing (the idea in the thing) there interposes itself our personal (psycho-physical) nature, which hides reality from us, making us see, as in a mirror, always and only ourselves.

As you see, that of conquering or overcoming ourselves (our narcissism or egoism) is not a merely moral task, but also, and in the first place, a cognitive one. Who, on the other hand, could ever conquer or overcome himself on the plane of feeling and of willing, if he had not first conquered or overcome himself on that of thinking?

Nature, Goethe writes, "plays a spectacle; whether she herself sees it, we do not know; and yet she plays it for us, spectators seated in a corner" (Fragment on Nature, in Works — Sansoni, Florence 1961, vol. V, p. 21).

We know, however, that she does see it, but that she sees it only if the spectators for whom she plays it see it. And why do these not always see it? It is quickly said: because they are not "pure": because they have not carried themselves, that is, beyond their personal nature.

Christ says in fact: "Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God."

71

Paraphrasing, we could therefore say: "Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see the idea"; and they shall see it because the pure thought of the heart is not so much a "logical" fact as an act of the Logos: that is, an act of soul and of spirit or, in a word, of love.

But let us return to our subject.


---


After recalling that Goethe, as a result of the contacts he had with some philosophers of his own time (F. W. J. Schelling, 1775-1854; H. Steffens, 1773-1845; G. W. F. Hegel, 1770-1831), brought his reflections to fruition in a dozen short essays, Steiner writes: "These essays express the thought already indicated above, that every object has two sides: the immediate one of its appearing (phenomenal form), and the other, which contains its essence. Thus Goethe attained the only satisfying conception of nature, which establishes the only truly objective method. When a theory regards the idea as something alien to the object itself, as merely subjective, it cannot claim to be truly objective, even when it in general makes use of the idea. Goethe, on the other hand, can maintain that he adds nothing to the objects that is not already contained within them" (p. 72).

One thing, then, is "racking one's brains" to hypothesize, suppose, presume, opine or conjecture; another is to silence ourselves so as to be able to hear what the objects reveal.

From the psychodynamic point of view, "adding nothing to the objects that is not already contained in them" is equivalent to "projecting" nothing onto them; and psychotherapists know well that the so-called "withdrawal of projections" is an integral part of the process of healing.

It is precisely for this reason — as I hope you will recall — that I spoke, in due course, of The Philosophy of Freedom as a text of "logodynamics": as a text, that is, in which the relations between body, soul and spirit (between perception, representation and concept) are presented as a play of forces, and therefore in a dynamic manner analogous to that in which Freud, for example, presents those between the Id, the ego and the Super-ego.

In this regard, we spoke before of "purity," but we could also have spoken of "chastity": of that chastity which makes Paul say, "Not I, but Christ in me," and which could make Goethe say, "Not I, but the object or the phenomenon in me."

Think that Arturo Toscanini (if I remember rightly) used to speak of interpretive "chastity," alluding precisely to those performers or those conductors capable of placing all their talent at the service of the author, and not of themselves.

But what is — to return to our subject — that which is "already contained" in objects? It is the universal, "already contained," precisely, in the particular (or in the individual).


---


Steiner writes in fact: "The essence of the particular is not at all exhausted in this its particularity; in order to be comprehended, it presses toward something that is not particular, but universal. This ideal-universal is the true being — the essence — of any particular whatsoever, which has in its particularity only one side of its existence, while the other is the universal, the type (...) Thus one is to understand what is meant when one speaks of the particular as a form of the universal. Since, then, the true being, the content of the particular, is the ideal-universal, it is impossible that the latter should be derived from the particular, abstracted from it. Since it cannot borrow its content from anywhere, it must give it to itself from itself. With this, the universal-typical is that in which content and form are identical; in consequence it can be comprehended only as a whole, independent of the single instance" (p. 74).

Here "content" and "form" are spoken of, but we have grown accustomed to speak of "force" and "form."

In the "universal-typical," that is, in the concept, in the living idea, in the essence or in the entelechy, both content and form, and force and form, are in fact "identical" (a unity).


---


72

The philosophers, in this regard, have taught us to distinguish the "real" (which we perceive) from the "ideal" (which we think). But for what reason do we judge the real "real" and the ideal "ideal"? For one reason only: because we experience the former (perceiving it) with the will, whereas we experience the latter (representing it) with thinking. And there is undoubtedly no comparison between the living and corpulent experience of things made by the will and the bloodless and abstract one made instead by (ordinary) thinking.

This means, however, not that we perceive the real because it is "real," but that the real is "real" because we perceive it, experiencing it as a force.

If we did only this, however, we could never give form to that force: we could never, that is, determine or qualify it. We could know, for instance, that beside us there is something alive that gives off a certain smell, moves, paws the ground and perhaps neighs, but we could never determine and qualify it as a "horse." To do so — that is, to give form to that indeterminate force — we need in fact the concept, and therefore thinking. As a rule, however, we know nothing of the concept as a force capable — forgive the play on words — of giving form to a force. We know in fact only of the representation: that is, of a form that has by now exhausted the force.

One thing, then, is the concept; another the consciousness of the concept: and it is precisely the reflected, intellectual or cerebral consciousness of the concept that gives rise to the representation.

What, then, is the representation? The fruit of the dead consciousness of living reality, and therefore the non-being of being (of the concept or of the idea).

And what, then, is the task? To conquer, by educating, strengthening and developing thinking, a living consciousness of living reality, and therefore of the being of being.

Only thus shall we in fact be able to see the idea in reality and reality in the idea, thereby discovering that the ideal is the real and that the real is ideal.

I would say that at this point we should pass to the fourth chapter, entitled: Conclusion on Goethe's Morphological Conceptions. It is a brief chapter that stands in close relation to what we have just been dealing with.


---


Steiner writes: "Opinions on Goethe's scientific studies are divided into two camps. The representatives of modern monism, at their head Professor Haeckel, recognize in Goethe the prophet of Darwinism, who, in agreement with them, conceives the organic as governed by the same laws that are active in inorganic nature. What Goethe lacked, they say, was only the theory of selection, by means of which Darwin for the first time would have founded the monistic conception of the world and raised the theory of evolution to a scientific conviction. Opposed to this point of view stands another, which holds the Goethean idea of the type to be nothing but a general concept, an idea in the sense of Platonic philosophy. Goethe would indeed have made certain individual assertions that recall the theory of evolution; to this he would have come through the pantheism inherent in his nature; but he would have felt no necessity of advancing to its ultimate mechanical foundation. One could therefore not speak, in his case, of a theory of evolution in the modern sense of the word" (pp. 77-78).

As you see, the monism of the materialists and the mechanicists, which resolves the Cartesian dualism by reducing the "res cogitans" to the "res extensa," or by conceiving "the organic — as Steiner says — governed by the same laws that are active in inorganic nature," is a very different thing from Goethe's monism.

The latter (like Steiner's) is in fact a monism that we might call "organic," in that — to continue putting it with Descartes — it neither reduces (in the Ahrimanic way) the "res cogitans" to the "res extensa," nor reduces (in the Luciferic way) the "res extensa" to the "res cogitans," but contemplates a

73

higher and articulated principle (essence), of which the one no less than the other constitutes but two different forms of manifestation.

Goethe, Steiner observes precisely, "did indeed represent the universe to himself in a monistic way, as an indivisible unity — from which he by no means excluded man (cf. the correspondence between Goethe and F. H. Jacobi) — but he nevertheless recognized that within this unity there are to be distinguished steps which have their own proper laws. From his youth onward he rejected the tendencies that represent unity as uniformity, and that conceive the organic world, and, in general, everything that appears as higher nature within nature, as governed by laws active in the inorganic world (...) Goethe thinks the world as a circle of circles, each of which has its own explanatory principle. The modern monists know only one circle, that of the laws of inorganic nature" (p. 78).


---


To think the world as a "circle of circles," however, is equivalent to thinking the Logos as a "logic of logics": there is in fact the (static) logic of death, there is the (dynamic) logic of life, there is the (qualitative) logic of the soul, and there is finally the Logos as their sole foundation, or as precisely a "logic of logics."

Whoever thinks he can arrive at monism by merely reducing one of the terms of the dualism to the other, arrives therefore not at unity, but at one-sidedness, uniformity, flatness or — as psychologists might perhaps say — at "monoideism."

The difference between the ego and the I also becomes transparent here. The I as I (fruit of intuitive consciousness) can in fact, resting upon itself (sum, ergo cogito), move freely among the various logics, adopting each time the one corresponding to the reality of the objects or phenomena it encounters, whereas the I as ego (fruit of ordinary representative consciousness) can do nothing else, having identified itself ("cogito, ergo sum") with a single logic, than impose upon the reality of the objects or phenomena it encounters the logic of death which it has unconsciously made "its own."

But let us now come to that point of view which "holds the Goethean idea of the type to be nothing but a general concept, an idea in the sense of Platonic philosophy."

Steiner writes in this regard: "Since the representatives of this point of view posit as a postulate of science that organic nature be explained in just the same way as inorganic nature, and reject a priori a conception such as Goethe's, they even regard it as useless to concern themselves more closely with his studies" (p. 79).

As one sees, the former, by recognizing in Goethe a "monist," transform him into a "mechanicist," while the latter, by not recognizing him as a "mechanicist," transform him into a "dualist."

To conceive Goethe's "type" in the same way as Plato's idea means in fact to conceive it as sundered from sensible reality, from the empirical phenomenon or from perception: it means, that is, to conceive it in a way different from Goethe's.

The suspicion is therefore justified that the latter do not so much regard it as "useless to concern themselves more closely" with Goethe's scientific studies because they conceive the "type" in this way, as rather conceive the "type" in this way because they have regarded it as "useless to concern themselves more closely" with his studies.

Steiner concludes, in any case: "Hence Goethe's lofty principles did not succeed in asserting themselves fully, either here or there. And precisely these principles are the eminent fact of his studies; they are what, for one who realizes their whole depth, loses none of its value even when one must acknowledge that some of the particulars of Goethean research call for rectification. From this there follows, for one who seeks to bring Goethe's conceptions into prominence, the requirement of directing one's gaze, beyond the

74

critical judgment of the particulars found in this or that chapter of natural science, toward the center of his conception of nature" (p. 79).


---


Steiner acknowledges, then, whatever in Goethe's studies requires modification or correction, but stresses that it is a matter of particulars, and not of "the center of his conception of nature": particulars of which anyone competent could freely undertake (as he himself has done) the verification and, where necessary, the rectification.

One thing, however, is to verify or rectify; another is to reject, taking as a pretext the limits of Goethe's particular conceptions, his general conception of nature.

It is a sincere regret that it is precisely the "empiricists" — that is, those who, standing in closer contact with the particulars, collect and supply the data of research — who ignore or misunderstand Goethe's lesson (his way of relating those data to one another).

The fact of the matter is that only by starting from the "center" of his conception of nature, and thanks to the help of the empiricists, could a truly "human" science finally see the light.

Listen, to conclude, to what was said, for example (and in his own way), by Heisenberg some forty years ago: "In science and in art, the world since Goethe has taken a road against which he had warned us because he considered it dangerous. Art has moved away from immediate reality and has withdrawn into the interior of the human soul, and science has set out on the path of abstraction, has produced the immense expansion of modern technology, and has reached as far as the primary structures of biology, as far as the fundamental forms which in modern science correspond to the Platonic solids. At the same time the dangers have become imminent, as Goethe had foreseen. Think, for example, of alienation, of the depersonalization of work, of the absurdity of modern armaments, of the flight into irrationality, which has taken the form of a political movement. The devil is powerful. But the luminous realm of which we spoke before with regard to romantic music, and which Goethe was able to recognize in the whole of nature, has become visible also in modern science, where it shows us the grandiose unitary design of the world. Today we can still learn something from Goethe: we must not allow all our faculties to atrophy in favour of the single organ capable of rational analysis; we should rather seek to comprehend reality with all the senses that have been given us, trusting then that this reality reflects the essence of things, 'the one, the good, the true.' Let us hope that this way of seeing has greater success in the future than it has in our own day" (Goethe's Conception of Nature and the World of Science and Technology, in Beyond the Frontiers of Science — Editori Riuniti, Rome 1984, pp. 164-165).

L.R.
Rome, December 12, 2000

75

Goethe's Scientific Works (16)


---


This evening, before beginning the fifth chapter, Goethean Knowing, I should like to say something to which my reading of the book by Pavel Florensky (1882-1937), "Do not forget me" (Mondadori, Milan 2000), has given rise.

It collects the letters which this great mathematician, philosopher and priest (called the Russian "Leonardo da Vinci" or "Blaise Pascal") sent to his wife and children from the Stalinist Gulag, between 1933 and 1937, the year of his execution by firing squad.

Do you recall what we said when we spoke of "Goetheanism"? That it is not an abstract "cultural movement," but "a soul-atmosphere, an ideal tension or spiritual humus nourished in the highest degree by a profound human element," which characterized not only the work of Goethe and Herder, but also that, for instance, of Schiller, Fichte, Hegel, Novalis and Schelling.

Well, whoever thought that the soul of Goetheanism is a by-now dead and exclusively German soul (if not indeed "pan-Germanist"), and not therefore an always living and universally human soul, would do well to read these letters.

Listen, for instance, to what Florensky writes to his eldest son Vasily on November 23, 1933: "In studying nature, the most important thing is to have immediate impressions, which, if they are examined as far as possible in an impartial way and free of preconceptions, gradually compose themselves on their own into an overall picture; from the overall picture there is born the intuition of the types of structure of nature, and it is precisely this intuition that provides motives for profound conclusions (...) Goethe possessed, in the highest degree, this capacity of seeing the type of what he was studying; one must learn from Goethe the knowledge of nature" (ibid., pp. 74-75).

And listen also to what the two editors of the collection (Natalino Valentini and Lubomir Žák) add, in a note, citing a passage from Florensky's "memoirs" (To My Children): "My desire has always been to know the world precisely as something unknown, without seeking to force and violate its secret, but limiting myself to trying to smooth out its folds and to peek behind it. The symbol is precisely the expression and the result of this effort, in that through symbols the secret of the world is not buried and done away with, but rather discloses itself in its most authentic essence, that is, precisely, as arcanum and enigma (...) On the basis of these considerations now, looking backward, I am able to understand why, from my childhood, from the very moment, one may say, in which I learned to read, I always found myself with 'Goethe and endless Goethe' in my hands — Goethe directly, and not through the filter of the reading of him proposed by Du Bois-Reymond. He was constantly my mental nourishment. I had, of course, difficulty in understanding him rationally, and yet I had the precise sensation that his thought was something connatural with me. That toward which I tended was Goethe's Urphänomenon" (ibid., p. 82).

To understand these words better, it will be well to recall that, for Goethe, "all that is ephemeral is but a symbol"; and that, for Steiner, the imaginative "sense" is precisely a "symbolic" sense, and what discloses itself, "through symbols," "as arcanum and enigma," can be unveiled and resolved by the inspirative and intuitive senses.

How can one help thinking, then, of how different the destiny of humanity would have been if the "Central European" spirit of Goetheanism had been allowed to meet and wed (especially toward the end of the nineteenth century) the Russian soul, not only of Pavel Florensky, but also of Vladimir Solovyov (1853-1900)?

Steiner recalls, in fact (we are in 1919): "When the Russian revolution began, the followers of Soloviev appeared like comets. They hoped for a renewal of the deaf, crepuscular and paralyzed spiritual life upon which had fallen, like a soul-night, a spiritual death, the killing

76

of the soul with all its connections. These people, Kartashov, Samarin, who it seems were true disciples of Soloviev, wanted a liberation. From the first glittering rays of the revolution they wanted to kindle a spiritual movement in Russia. In place of this there is now realized, in Lenin, this bearer-of-corpses of all spiritual life, a frenzied destruction of every spiritual element, in a condition in which everything that had shown itself to the humanity of the East in the great figure of Soloviev is denied" (Answers of the Science of the Spirit to Social and Pedagogical Problems — Antroposofica, Milan 1974, pp. 232-233).

But now let us take up the new chapter.


---


Steiner writes: "Today we are very far from the way of thinking that was Goethe's own. It is true that in all fields of culture we have progress to register; but one cannot assert that these are progresses in depth. And for the worth of an epoch only progresses in depth are decisive. Our own, on the other hand, could be characterized by saying that it actually declares every progress in depth to be unattainable for man" (p. 82).

What worth, then, can an epoch have in which the extension or horizontality of thinking gains the upper hand over its intension or verticality, and whose progresses, in consequence, occur only on the surface, and not "in depth"?

Extension is indeed one thing, intension another; and if the intension of thinking could permit us (whatever Kant may say) to know things "in themselves," its extension instead allows us to know things "for us," and therefore to make use of them for the ends of our practical life (of our material needs).

We have said, in this regard, that thinking ought to discover the ideas in things, and not get ideas about things. Thinking that invents or devises is animated not by love, but rather by vanity or craving (for success, for power, for gain).

This happens above all in the field of the human sciences (where one ought to be concerned with the soul and the spirit), whereas in that of the natural sciences only what refers to the inorganic world stands out as a luminous exception.

In a context of this kind — that is, a context in which one starts from the presupposition that truth does not exist and that, even if it existed, it could not be reached by us — it is, however, inevitable that opinion ends up imposing itself.

But opinion, not having in itself the force of truth, how does it manage to impose itself? It is simple: by substituting for the spiritual force of truth the juridical force of the law or the economic force of money: in short, power.

Steiner continues: "We have become fainthearted in all fields; above all in those of thinking and willing. As for thinking, endless observations are made which accumulate without anyone having the courage to derive from them an overall scientific conception of reality, while German idealistic philosophy is accused of not being scientific precisely because it had that courage. Today one does not want to think, but only to look with the senses. All faith in thinking has been lost; thinking is not held capable of penetrating into the secrets of the world and of life; one even renounces every solution of the great enigmas of existence. The only thing held possible is to reduce the data of experience to a system" (p. 82).

Let us recall, in this regard, that courage stands at the center, between (Luciferic) rashness and (Ahrimanic) cowardice, and draws its force of warmth from the very same source as love.

Here Steiner speaks of "faintheartedness," and hence of "cowardice," but what is more interesting to note is that he refers it not so much, as is customary, to outward behaviour, as to thinking.


---


77

When we dealt with The Philosophy of Freedom, I spoke, in this regard, of a will-to-impotence, which gives rise to "depression" (just as a will-to-power gives rise, conversely, to "mania"). But does there exist, you will ask, a "depressed" thinking? Of course there does: to convince oneself of it, one need only consider, say, the "fallible" thinking of Karl Popper, the "weak" thinking of Gianni Vattimo, or, more generally, that of the agnostics or the relativists.

Let us be clear: the fault of these people lies not in regarding present-day thinking in such a way, but in having lost "all faith in thinking," and in believing, therefore, that it cannot in any way redeem itself from this its sorry state.

Steiner says that "today one does not want to think, but only to look with the senses": "looking," in fact, is a different thing from "observing" or from "seeing."

Think that precisely Florensky — that is, a man of assured scientific formation (even in the Gulag researches were entrusted to him) — writes, in another letter to his son Vasily: "It is astonishing how incapable people are of observing" (op. cit., p. 97).

The fact of the matter is that observation and thinking proceed hand in hand: when the former is superficial, the latter is superficial, just as, conversely, when the former is profound, the latter is profound.

Let us bear in mind, however, that the depth of observation is not at all assured or guaranteed — as we have seen — by the "instruments" one makes use of: by what, that is, one interposes, artificially, between ourselves and the objects or phenomena.

We have seen, moreover, in due course, that the rational or feeling soul (philosophical) is attentive first of all to the concept, whereas the conscious soul (scientific) is attentive first of all to the percept.

This transfer of attention from the content of thinking to that of perception (as the fruit of an evolutionary process) has, however, resolved itself, with the passage of time, into a new and no less pernicious prejudice: into that of believing, that is, that the burden of knowing falls to (sensible) perception, and that the burden instead of ordering or organizing what is learned from perception falls to thinking — or, as Steiner says, of "reducing the data of experience to a system."

One does not realize, then, that losing "all faith in thinking" is equivalent to losing all faith in man.

Steiner writes: "Experimental science simply forgets one thing: that at times thousands and thousands of men have passed before a sensible fact and have looked at it without observing anything noteworthy in it; then someone has come along who, in casting his gaze upon it, suddenly descried an important law. From what does this derive, if not from the fact that he was able to look at the same phenomenon in a different way, with other eyes than his predecessors? In looking, he had a given idea of how a fact is to be placed in relation with other facts, and of what in it is important or not. Thus, by thinking, he oriented himself and saw more than the others. He saw with the eyes of the spirit" (pp. 82-83).


---


In reality, everyone sees with the eyes of the spirit, but very few know it. Naturally, we enjoy intuition (to which we owe the concepts), but not "intuitive consciousness"; we enjoy inspiration (to which we owe judging), but not "inspirative consciousness"; we enjoy imagination (to which we owe those living images, reflected in the cerebral organ, that we know as extinguished representations), but not "imaginative consciousness."

We are therefore awake or vigilant on the plane of representation (since we enjoy precisely "representative consciousness"), whereas we dream on the plane of imagination, and sleep (at two different degrees of depth) on those of inspiration and intuition.

As we say, we are not therefore "inventing anything": we are rather attempting to realize, or to bring to consciousness, what we do unconsciously from morning to evening. "Anthroposophy," Steiner affirms, "is nothing but the stimulus to draw out the forces of knowledge lying in the

78

depths of souls" (Life from Death to New Birth — Libreria Editrice Psiche, Turin 1997, p. 87).

Steiner goes on: "Experimental science wanders perplexed through the vast realm of phenomena; the world of the senses becomes for it a confused and disconcerting multiplicity, because it lacks in thinking the energy to penetrate to the center. One speaks today of limits of knowledge because one ignores where the goal of thinking lies. One has no clear idea of what one wants to reach, and one doubts being able to reach it (...) Precisely the same happens with willing and with acting. We are incapable of setting our life tasks for the accomplishment of which our forces are suited. One dreams of indefinite and confused ideals and then weeps if one does not reach what one has not even a vague idea of, much less a clear one" (p. 83).


---


Whoever sows agnosticism in the field of thinking, then, reaps apathy in the field of feeling and abulia in that of willing.

Let us not be deceived, in this regard, by the frenzy of the world that surrounds us today. As Nietzsche saw well, action is one thing, agitation another; and today's world is a world that the more it agitates itself, the less it acts, and that dreams "indefinite and confused ideals" since it has precisely "not even a vague idea" of what it wants to reach.

One of the many forms assumed by agnosticism is that of "problematicism." We must, however, distinguish the "physiological" problematicism of those who seek the truth by making methodical use of doubt (and doubting, therefore, even doubt itself), from the "pathological" problematicism of those who, made instead slaves (obsessively) of doubt, flee the truth.

The former, in fact, go to meet the truth with their whole being, which is the truth of their being, whereas the latter go to meet it with the head alone: that is, with a part of the truth of their being which, in the same measure as it is absolutized ("cephalocentrically"), turns into a lie. This dreads the truth, and then strives to exorcise it by means of the most varied dialectical virtuosity.

Steiner writes: "Whoever recognizes in thinking the faculty of perceiving beyond what the senses can descry, must necessarily also attribute to it objects that stand beyond purely sensible reality. Now the objects of thinking are the ideas. Insofar as thinking takes possession of the idea, it fuses with the primordial ground of cosmic existence; what acts without, penetrates into the spirit of man; it becomes one with objective reality at its highest potency. The perception of the idea in reality is man's true communion" (p. 84).


---


Think, with regard to this last assertion, that the Russian philosopher Nikolai Alexandrovich Berdyaev (1874-1948) spoke of the act of knowing as a "conjugal act": not out of poetic whim, but because he was aware that in this act opposites are really "conjoined."

But which opposites? We already know: the concept, that is, the form (of thinking) which has a feminine character or quality (yin), and the percept, that is, the force (of the will) which has a masculine character or quality (yang).

The human soul is therefore the temple in which, by means of the union of thinking and willing (perceiving), the soul and spirit of man are conjoined with the soul and spirit of the world: and therefore the temple in which there takes place — as Steiner says — "man's true communion."

Perhaps you will recall that, in dealing with The Philosophy of Freedom, we spoke of the concept and the percept as the "betrothed," and compared the role of the obstructers to that assigned by Manzoni to Don Rodrigo, specifying that Lucifer obstructs their "nuptials" (The Chymical Wedding of Christian Rosenkreuz) starting from the feminine form, whereas Ahriman obstructs them starting from the masculine force.

79

Steiner writes precisely: "Thinking has, with respect to the ideas, the same importance that the eye has with respect to light and the ear with respect to sound. It is an organ of perception. This conception is capable of uniting two things that today are held to be wholly irreconcilable with each other: the empirical method and idealism as a scientific conception of the world. It is believed that admitting the former leads, as a necessary consequence, to repudiating the latter. But this is absolutely mistaken (...) The objective given does not at all coincide with the sensible given, as the mechanistic conception of the world believes. The sensible is only one half of the given. The other half are the ideas, which are likewise object of experience, though of a higher experience, having thinking as its organ. The ideas too are accessible to an inductive method" (p. 85).


---


We saw, in its time, that the perceptual image, the sensation, and the representation are
"subjective," while the concept is "objective" just as much as the percept (which constitutes
its other face).
The one and the other are in fact "world"; between the (spiritual) "world" of the former and
the (sensible) world of the latter, however, lies the world of man: and it is precisely in the
world of man (in his soul) that the world of the concept and that of the percept, divided by
man himself, can finally find each other again and reunite.
But this their re-union is, at the same time, a re-creation of the world and of man: a
re-creation that is redemption, resurrection, and salvation.
Emil Bock points out, in this regard, that the name Adam (Adamah: "the one who was formed
of earthly matter") does not designate the first "man," but rather the first "earthly man":
that is, he points out that man was created well before Adam.
"The history of the Earth – he explains – up to Adam is cosmic, from Adam to Abraham it is
mythical, and it is truly historical only starting from Moses" (Genesis – Rainbow, Oriago di
Mira (Ve) 2000, p. 24).
The re-creation of man is therefore the re-creation of Adam; it is no accident, then, that
Paul speaks of Christ as the "new Adam" (Rom 5:14).

L.R.
Rome, 19 December 2000

80
The Scientific Works of Goethe (17)


---


We find ourselves in the days between Christmas and Epiphany (in the period of the so-called
"twelve holy nights") and I know that some of you have wondered why we have not interrupted
our work to read, as others do, a lecture dedicated to these feasts.
Well, I will answer with the following words of Steiner: "There are many among us who,
listening to the teachings of anthroposophy, consider it on the same footing as external
science, and thus in their minds there is no sharp distinction between them. Anthroposophy
is understood correctly not when one grasps it only with the mind, but when in each of its
expressions it gives us enthusiasm, when it lives in us to such a degree that it finds the
passage from the nervous system to the blood system. When we manage to become enthusiastic
about the truths contained in anthroposophy, only then do we understand it. As long as we
take it only abstractly, studying it like a primer, a mathematics book, a service manual, or
a cookbook, we will not understand it. We will continue not to understand it by studying it
like chemistry or botany. We understand it only when it gives us warmth, when it fills us
with the life that reigns in it. Christ once said: 'I am with you all the days until the
end of the earth.' And He is in our midst not as a dead man, but as a living one, and He
reveals Himself continually. Only those who are so short-sighted as to fear such
revelations say that one should hold fast to what has always been believed. Whoever is not
fainthearted knows instead that Christ reveals Himself always. We must therefore welcome as
a real revelation of Christ what He manifests as anthroposophy. I have often been asked by
anthroposophists: 'How do I come into connection with the Christ?' That is a truly naive
question! For every effort of ours, every line we read of our anthroposophical science is a
connecting with the Christ. In a certain sense we do nothing else" (Cosmic Being and I –
Antroposofica, Milan 2000, p. 38).
Having said this, let us return to our text.
Before going forward, however, I would like to take up again the passage we read at the end
of last time, in order to make some further observations.
The passage is this: "Thinking has, with respect to the ideas, the same importance that the
eye has with respect to light and the ear with respect to sound. It is an organ of
perception. This conception is capable of uniting two things that today are considered
wholly irreconcilable with each other: the empirical method and idealism as a scientific
conception of the world. It is believed that admitting the former leads, as a necessary
consequence, to repudiating the latter. But this is absolutely wrong (...) The objective
datum does not at all coincide with the sensible datum, as the mechanistic conception of the
world believes. The sensible is only one half of the datum. The other half are the ideas,
which are also objects of experience, albeit of a superior experience, having thinking as
its organ. The ideas too are accessible to an inductive method."
But why are "the empirical method and idealism as a scientific conception of the world"
"considered wholly irreconcilable with each other"? For the simple reason that, given that
empiricism presents itself as a "philosophy," and not as a "method," and that idealism
presents itself as a "philosophy," and not as a "science," we find ourselves in the presence
of two conceptions of the world which place at their respective and antithetical foundation
the so-called "concrete" (material) reality and the so-called "abstract" (ideal) one.
We have seen, however, that the former is the reality of the percept or of (indeterminate)
force, which we experience through willing; that the latter is that of the concept or of
(determinate) form, which we grasp through thinking; and that the reason for their
difference lies, therefore, in us, and not in things.
If we therefore managed to carry ourselves beyond the level of consciousness at which we
ordinarily experience the one and grasp the other, we would discover that the form of the
concept is just as real or "concrete" as the force of the percept.
                                                  81
Where there was an (irreconcilable) duality, there would thus be a unity (in us, the I).
But why has there been duality? It is easily said: so that consciousness of the real could be
born. One thing, in fact, is the real; another is consciousness of the real: and the first
presupposition for the arising of the second is, precisely, that the unity of the real
present itself as a duality: first of all as that of "subject" and "object."
To realize, together, a true consciousness of the world and a true consciousness of the I,
it is therefore necessary to overcome this duality and to reconstitute on the spiritual
plane the original and lost natural unity.
This entails, however, the adoption of an "empirical idealism" which, uniting – as Steiner
says – "the empirical method and idealism as a scientific conception of the world," is
immune from the one-sidedness and the abstractions of both philosophical idealism and
philosophical empiricism.
Schelling says, for example, that man is a "whole composed of these three elements: body,
spirit, and soul" (Clara – Guerini, Milan 1987, p. 53).
He does not say, however, that spirit, soul, and body are respectively in relation to
thinking, feeling, and willing, nor, still less, that thinking, feeling, and willing are in
turn related, in that order, to the neuro-sensory system, to the rhythmic system, and to the
metabolic system: he does not let us know, in short, by virtue of which actual relations it
is possible to pass, through the soul, from the spirit to the body, and vice versa.
As you see, these are relations that can be discovered only by a science of the spirit
(namely an "empirical idealism"), and that are therefore unknown to empiricists and
idealists alike.
"The objective datum – Steiner says – does not at all coincide with the sensible datum, as
the mechanistic conception of the world believes," or – we may add – as naive realism
believes. This must be overcome, however – as we have often said – as "naive," and not as
"realism." Wherein does its "naivety" in fact consist? Precisely in believing that the
"objective datum" has a sensible, and not an ideal, nature.
Consider that Pavel Florensky (of whom we spoke last week), convinced of the
"indissoluble bond existing between idealism and realism," speaks, in this regard, of a
"joy of the concrete" ("do not forget me" – Mondadori, Milan 2000, pp. 19 and 138). This
"joy of the concrete" of his is no different, however, from the "joy of being" of which
Massimo Scaligero speaks (Practical Manual of Meditation – Tilopa, Rome 1984, p. 138), given
that the concrete is being and being is the concrete.
It will be worth stressing (to avoid misunderstandings) that whoever has the "joy of being"
also has the "joy of existing," while whoever lacks the former, however much he may strive
or despair in seeking it, has neither the latter. To separate existing from being is
equivalent to separating a plant from its own roots, thereby condemning it to withering,
fading, and death.
But now let us go forward.


---


Steiner writes: "Approaching Goethe with such views, we believe we penetrate into his true
being. We hold to idealism, placing however at the basis of its elaboration not Hegel's
dialectical method, but a higher, purified empiricism" (p. 85).
It is important, in fact, that the relation between the ideal and the real, or between the
idea and the individual phenomenon, never be lost sight of in the name of the "system" ("A
philosophizing without system – Hegel writes – cannot be anything scientific" –
Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989, p. 22).
To this end, however, what must be called into question is not so much Hegel's "dialectical
method" or some particular "system" (as, to give just one example, Schelling's System of
Transcendental Idealism – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1990), but rather the esprit de système of the
rational or affective soul.
                                                  82
Whoever works on the scientific plane of the conscious soul feels in fact the desire to
penetrate and comprehend the real, and precisely for this reason cares little about having
to give up the "system"; all the more so since, the real being the one and only true
"system," he knows he must find the way to enter into the "system" of the real himself, and
not to make the real enter into, or force it into, a "system" of his own.
Steiner continues nonetheless: "A similar attitude also lies at the basis of the philosophy
of Eduard von Hartmann. He seeks in nature the ideal unity that yields positively to a
thinking full of content. He rejects the purely mechanical conception of nature and the
hyper-Darwinism that clings to the external (...) Hartmann's philosophy differs from mine
only with regard to the question of pessimism and as to the metaphysical culmination of his
system in the 'unconscious'" (pp. 85-86).


---


In truth, the position of Eduard von Hartmann (1842-1906) differs from that of Steiner more
than these words let on: whoever knows The Philosophy of Freedom knows, for example, that
the former's "metaphysical realism" is a very different thing from the latter's "ethical
individualism" or – as was said a moment ago – "empirical idealism."
It must in any case be recognized, to von Hartmann's credit, that he distinguished the
reality of the idea from that of consciousness of the idea: that is, that he asserted that
one thing is the reality of the idea, another the relation, conscious or unconscious, in
which man stands to it. Indeed, he was one of the few, if not the only one, to admit the
possibility that there are ideas in the unconscious.
Freud and Jung, for example, while they could not exempt themselves from counting among the
precursors of the "psychology of the unconscious" the author of the Philosophy of the
Unconscious (Philosophy of the Unconscious – Routledge, London 2000), were very careful not
to follow him on that path.
In place of "ideas," Freud's psychoanalysis has in fact put the "instincts," and Jung's
analytical psychology the "archetypes": that is to say, two factors which no one really
knows what they are ("I am very far from knowing – Jung himself admits – what the spirit is
in itself, and equally far from knowing what the instincts are. The one is for me as much a
mystery as the other" – The Problem of the Unconscious in Modern Psychology – Einaudi,
Turin 1969, p. 56).
But let us continue, listening to what Steiner says about von Hartmann's pessimism:
"The reasons that Hartmann adduces in favor of pessimism, that is, of the opinion that
nothing in the world can fully satisfy us and that displeasure always exceeds pleasure, I
would like to designate outright as the good fortune of humanity. What he adduces is for me
only a further proof of how vain it is to aspire to happiness. We must precisely desist from
every such aspiration and seek our destination solely in unselfishly fulfilling those ideal
tasks that our reason marks out for us. And what else does this mean but that we must seek
our happiness only in working, in creating without pause? Only he who is active, and
precisely active without selfishness, seeking no compensation for his activity, fulfills his
mission" (p. 86).


---


Anyone who did not read this passage with due attention could well go around saying: "Steiner said that aspiring to happiness is futile!" You know better than I that this can unfortunately happen.

But is the Steiner who affirmed that it is "futile to aspire to happiness" not the very same one who affirmed that "we must seek our happiness only in working, in creating without cease"?

The problem, as we can see, is therefore not whether one should or should not aspire to happiness, but how one should or should not do so.

Not for nothing did we say a little earlier that one thing is to seek the joy of existing directly, another to seek it indirectly, through being; and we also said, last time, that whoever sows agnosticism in the field of thinking reaps apathy in the field of feeling and abulia in that of willing, ending up, sooner or later, no longer loving life.

                                                  83

Life must indeed be loved: yet it is rare that this happens; as a rule we content ourselves with "enjoying it" in the sphere of sensations (bound to the body).

But why not love it in its entirety, that is, "enjoying it" in the spirit, in the soul, and in the body?

It is customary to say that "too much ruins"; but could it not be that this "too much" consists precisely in the pretension that it is always and only the body that gives us those enjoyments which should instead be given to us by the soul and the spirit? And could it not be that it is precisely for this reason that it "ruins," spoiling for us even those of the body?

But why have we been reduced to asking of the body even what we should instead ask of the soul and the spirit? Because, since the horizon of our consciousness has narrowed, we pour all the force of our feeling and willing onto the material things alone that we are able to think.

"We must seek our happiness — Steiner says — only in working, in creating without cease." Indeed, it is the activity, the very becoming of our own being, that gives us joy or happiness. It is therefore illusory to think that joy or happiness are "objects" to be sought in the way one seeks all other things.

How, then, can one be happy? By becoming — to put it with Nietzsche — the Self that one is.

Steiner says further: "Only an action done out of love can be a moral action. In science our polar star must be the idea, in action, love. With this we have returned once more to Goethe: 'What matters to the man of action is to do what is right; whether what is right then comes to pass or not must not concern him'; 'All our art of living consists in renouncing our existence, in order to exist' (Prose Sayings)" (p. 86).


---


Let us recall, in this connection, that one thing is the ego, as the fruit of the bodily or spatial consciousness of the I, another is the I, and yet another is the Christ, as the "holy entelechy of humanity" (this is how Florensky names it).

The Being of love indwells the I, then, but not the ego ("The light shines in the darkness; but the darkness has not received it" — Jn 1:5). Before being able to meet this Being, and thus to be able to love, each of us must therefore overcome the ego and meet the I. For love wells up from the I in the very moment in which the I, by carrying itself beyond the ordinary bodily or spatial self-consciousness, begins truly to be itself.

The reality of the world, which one fondly imagines can be reached through craving, can thus be reached by the I: for love cancels the opposition between the ego and the non-ego, since it allows the subject to recognize itself in the object, and the object to recognize itself in the subject.

Scaligero says that "love is the spirit that wills the spirit in the other" (Dell'amore immortale — Tilopa, Rome 1982, p. 23), inasmuch as it sets out from being in order to arrive at being. In ordinary life (and all the more in times of materialism) it happens, however, that love, which cannot but set out (albeit unconsciously) from the spirit (from the I), halts at the body (at the surface) or, at best, at the "psyche," and does not reach the soul or the spirit (the depth) of the other. The result is that in the subject who believes he loves there arises the dark and painful sensation that something eludes him, leaving him unsatisfied. Hence precisely the adage (attributed, more or less rightly, to Aristotle): post coitum omne animal triste.

Only by setting out from the spirit and arriving at the spirit can craving (flamma urens) in fact be stilled and become love (flamma non urens): "The consummation of craving — Scaligero writes — is pure beatitude: bound to no sensation. For it is the substance of life that no sensation grasps, unless it be the sensation in which the spirit lives" (ibid., p. 87).

Goethe affirms — as we have seen — that "what matters to the man of action is to do what is right; whether what is right then comes to pass or not must not concern him." For this to occur, however, it is necessary that the "right" be not only thought, but also felt as such.

                                                  84

To assert, as is customary, that "between saying and doing lies the sea" is in fact to assert that between thinking and willing lies feeling, and that it is therefore improbable that a truth thought but not felt can translate itself into action.

How, on the other hand, could one hope to feel a truth that had not first been thought?

Steiner concludes: "I arrived at my conception of the world not only through the study of Goethe and of Hegelianism. I started from the mechanistic-naturalistic conception; but I recognized that, for intensive thinking, it is not possible to halt there. Proceeding rigorously according to the method of the natural sciences, I found in objective idealism the only satisfying conception of the world" (p. 86).


---


Steiner speaks here of his conception as an "objective idealism," whereas we have spoken of it as an "empirical idealism."

The two expressions are naturally equivalent. It would moreover be impossible to find one (with perhaps the sole exception of "logodynamics") that implies "objectivity" and "scientificity" without at the same time having a philosophical precedent: it is enough to consult any History of Philosophy to discover that, in addition to the "problematic" idealism of Descartes (1596-1650), the "dogmatic" or "subjective" idealism of Berkeley (1685-1753), the "formal," "critical," or "transcendental" idealism of Kant (1724-1804), the "ethical" idealism of Fichte, or the "absolute" idealism of Hegel, there exist both the "objective" idealism of Schelling and the "empirical" idealism of John Stuart Mill (1806-1873).

L.R.
Rome, 2 January 2001

                                                  85

Goethe's Scientific Works (18)

Tonight, if all goes well, we shall read and comment on two very brief chapters: the sixth, entitled: On the Order in Which Goethe's Scientific Works Have Been Arranged, and the seventh, entitled: From Art to Science.

Let us therefore begin with the sixth.

Steiner writes: "In editing the publication of Goethe's Scientific Works I was guided by the idea of enlivening the study of particulars by presenting the grand ideas that underlie them (…) It cannot be denied that many of his scientific treatises appear devoid of importance if we look at them from the standpoint of science, in the meantime so far advanced. But this is of no importance whatever. What matters is the significance that must be attributed to them within the scope of the Goethean conception itself. At the spiritual height where the poet stands, the scientific need is also stronger. Without a scientific need there is no science. What matters, then, is to ask ourselves: What questions does Goethe put to nature? Whether and how he then answered them is secondary" (p. 89).


---


An example of the fact that progress in the scientific knowledge of "particulars" does not necessarily go hand in hand with progress in the ideas that animate it is given, in spite of themselves, by today's "neurosciences."

How can one deny, in fact, that our knowledge of the brain has advanced enormously — not since Goethe's time, let us say, but since Freud's? But from what standpoint? From the anatomical-physiological one; and has there perhaps accompanied this progress an advance in the knowledge of, say, the relation between the brain and the human subject (the I), or between the brain and thinking, feeling, and willing? Not in the least.

Do you see this book? It is entitled: Il cervello e le sue meraviglie (The Brain and Its Wonders) (Rizzoli, Milan 1987). Its authors are Robert Ornstein (professor of Human Biology at Stanford University) and Richard F. Thompson (professor of Psychology and Human Biology at the same University). Well then, let us open it to page twelve, and read: "The largest part of the human encephalon is the brain proper (above all in common speech, the word 'brain' usually designates the entire encephalon). The brain is divided into two halves, or hemispheres, each of which controls the opposite side of the body. The hemispheres are connected to each other by a band formed of some three hundred million nerve fibers, called the corpus callosum. Each hemisphere is covered by a layer of nerve cells pleated in a very complex fashion, three millimeters thick, called the cerebral cortex. The cortex first appeared in our progenitors some two hundred million years ago, and it is what gives us our peculiar human quality. Thanks to the cortex we are able to organize, to remember, to communicate, to understand, to appreciate, and to create."

Granted; but just as it is observable that the brain is divided into two hemispheres and that each of these is covered by the cerebral cortex, is it equally observable that it is the cortex that gives us "our peculiar human quality"? And what if, on the contrary, it is the human being that gives the cortex its peculiar quality? And why say, then, that thanks to the cortex "we are able to organize, to remember, to communicate, to understand, to appreciate, and to create," rather than that, thanks to the cortex, we are able — not to do all these things, but to be aware of doing them?

Certainly, to answer these questions one must make use not of the microscope, of PET (positron emitting tomography), or of some other sophisticated contraption, but of thinking. But how can one make use of it if, by dint of trafficking with the infinitely small, thinking itself has been rendered "infinitely small"? Or is the idea that everything the human being thinks, feels, and wills is thought, felt, and willed by the brain perhaps a "grand" one?


---


Listen, for example, to what Edoardo Boncinelli says: once it has received the message transmitted to it by the "bodily sensation," our brain can "decide to act immediately or to hold off and reflect on what to do. If it takes the second decision, the signal will begin to wander in a seemingly erratic manner through the cerebral cortex, passing through cortical areas that are neither purely receptive nor purely motor, but rather associative, and which constitute the most substantial part of the cortex itself. Along this path, traveled moreover at great speed, the signal will fray into many different signals and undergo many manipulations; in particular, it may be put into memory, it may be suppressed as irrelevant, or it may lead to a slightly delayed response. In the end a decision will in any case be made and we shall pass to action. The signal will then converge toward the motor area, sometimes also called the primary motor area, of the cortex, from which the message will depart, this time centrifugal, carrying the decision to move some muscle, for example to flex the arm in question" (Il cervello, la mente e l'anima - Mondadori 2000, pp. 82-83).

So for Boncinelli too it is the brain, and not the man (the I), that does everything.

Well then, let us imagine a primitive who finds himself in front of a radio that is broadcasting a speech. What is he more likely to believe? That he is in the presence of an unknown and unsettling being that speaks, or of an object that is transmitting the speech of a human being? You will agree that he is more likely to believe the first thing, and that he will be all the more convinced of it when he observes, after striking it with a stone, that that being no longer speaks.

Yet the "logic" that leads the primitive to believe that it is the radio that speaks is not much different from the logic that leads today's neuroscientists to believe that it is the brain that does everything that Ornstein, Thompson, and Boncinelli tell us about.

If what they say were true, one would moreover have to ask: but who then wrote their books? Their brains? No, because by saying "their" brains, we would be presupposing, in addition to the reality of these, that of their owners (of the I's).

As you can see, in light of "their" (?) theses, we could only answer that such books were written by three different brains, to which other brains one day decided to assign the aforementioned names and surnames.

I hope this is enough to demonstrate – as we have said – that "the progress of scientific knowledge of the 'particulars' is not necessarily accompanied by progress in the ideas that animate it."

What television viewer has not, after all, noticed at least once the disconcerting disproportion that exists between the intelligence that gave birth to the television "instrument" and the intelligence that instead gives birth to its programs?

But let us return to our subject.


---


Steiner explains that "however attractive this may be," it would never have occurred to him to distribute Goethe's writings in the customary way: starting, that is, from "general points of view" to the "particular developments of fundamental ideas." "I could not thus have achieved – he writes – what I wanted, that is – to use Goethe's comparison – to make clear the design of the game, by means of the pieces moved boldly at the beginning" (for Goethe indeed says: "It happens with the opinions we dare to advance as with the pieces we move on the chessboard; they may indeed be lost, but they will nevertheless have served to begin a victorious game"); and he adds: "Nothing was less consonant with Goethe than wanting to take conscious departure from general concepts. He always starts from concrete facts, compares them, orders them. In so doing, the ideal foundation of the same arises before him" (p. 90).

Nothing was therefore "less consonant with Goethe" than a "deductive" method. It is indeed by virtue of induction (of "empirical idealism") that what is implicit in the phenomenon gradually comes to be made explicit, up to revealing the idea, the type or the entelechy.

"The method Goethe employs – Steiner further observes – remains always that based on pure experience, even where he rises to the idea. For he never allows any subjective ingredient to penetrate his investigation. He merely liberates phenomena from every accidental element, in order to penetrate their deeper foundations. His subject has no other task than that of arranging the object in such a way that it reveals its innermost being. 'The true is like the Divine: it does not appear immediately; we must divine it from its manifestations.' It is a matter of placing these manifestations in such a connection that the 'true' appears. In the facts we confront through observation, the true, the idea, is already contained; it is only for us to remove the covering that hides it. In displacing this covering lies the true scientific method" (p. 91).


---


Remember what Florensky said, in the passage from his memoirs that we read two weeks ago? From childhood – he said – "what I strove toward was Goethe's Urphänomenon." But what is the Urphänomenon? It is the "pure" ("primordial") phenomenon: that is, the phenomenon free of all the accidental or casual elements that always accompany it.

The "scientific method" thus lies in displacing the covering that hides the idea from us, but it will be well to observe that this is hidden from us as much by an external (natural) covering as by an internal (psychic) one.

To discover the idea, it is indeed necessary that pure thought move to meet the pure phenomenon: that is, a thought immune from any subjective mortgage (sympathies, antipathies, preconceptions, prejudices, etc.). "A merely subjective intellect – Goethe rightly observes – has very soon exhausted its petty little I" (G.P. Eckermann: Conversations with Goethe – Laterza, Bari 1912, vol. I, p. 176).

The researcher's sole concern should therefore be that of putting the idea in a position to reveal itself. This is why Goethe insisted so much on the necessity of an observation that is serene, patient, and made, above all, from multiple points of view.

Imagine, to take a banal example, photographing the front and the rear of an automobile. Well, someone unfamiliar with automobiles could not think, looking at such images, that they are two different objects?

There is also, in this regard, a small Zen story. One day, two farmers, while working, saw passing along the road that divided their fields from each other, a fellow wearing a hat with one side white and the other black. Well, it is said that the two spent the rest of their lives arguing and quarreling, because one was certain that that hat was white, and the other that it was black.

The fact is that to grasp the essence of a phenomenon we should perform, while observing it, a sort of "circumambulatio": a sort, that is, of rotation that allows it to appear to us in the most diverse perspectives.

But how can we manage to perform such an interior movement if our constitution, temperament, and character (our "nature") impose upon us a single, determinate, and personal point of view? Do we not often say, in fact: "in my opinion," "in my view," "as I see it," "from my perspective," or, precisely, "from my point of view"?

As you see, here one grasps, almost with one's hand, the necessity of liberating oneself, in the name of knowledge, from this mortgage that binds us to our point of view in the same measure that it binds us to our space (to our physical body).

Goethe says: "The true is like the Divine: it does not appear immediately; we must divine it from its manifestations."

Which amounts to saying that the "true" does not appear to (sensory) perception: this, insofar as bound to the hic et nunc, gives us in fact a "part" and a "moment" (of the manifestation) of the "true," but not the "true"; and it is precisely because the perceptual images of one and the same phenomenon are diverse and


---


multiple that the necessity arises of connecting them in such a way as to allow its essence to come to light.

We have thus arrived at the end of the sixth chapter; let us therefore begin the seventh: From art to science.

Steiner writes: "Goethe suffered greatly from the false opinion of his contemporaries who could not comprehend how artistic creation and scientific investigation could be united in one and the same spirit. Here it is first of all a matter of answering the question: What motives drove the great poet to science? Did his passing from art to science derive only from a subjective inclination of his, from a personal whim? Or was his artistic tendency such that it had of necessity to lead him to science?" (p. 93).

So, one of two things: either his "passing from art to science" was accidental, and we are then faced with a poet who, by coincidence, was also a thinker; or it was not accidental, and we are then faced with an "objective transition" from art to science, which presupposes, of necessity, not only a relationship between the two, but also a moment in which these touch each other.
