Steiner nonetheless recalls that it is the general conviction that "outside us, in objective existence, stands the origin of scientific laws; within us, in our individuality, that of aesthetic laws"; and that the latter, therefore, "have not the slightest cognitive value; they generate illusions without any factor of reality"; immediately adding, however, that "whoever takes the matter in this way will never arrive at clarity regarding the relation in which Goethe's art and Goethe's science stand to each other; and will thereby misunderstand them both. Goethe's universal historical importance lies precisely in the fact that his art springs directly from the primordial source of being and bears within it nothing illusory, nothing subjective, but appears as the revealer of those laws which the poet has gathered from the universal Spirit in the depths of nature's workings. At this level art becomes the interpreter of the mysteries of the universe, as science is, in another sense. For Goethe indeed always understood art in this way. It was for him one of the revelations of the primordial law of the world; science was the other. For him art and science sprang from a single source. While the scientist immerses himself in the depth of reality in order to express its propulsive forces in the form of ideas, the artist seeks to shape his material according to those same propulsive powers" (pp. 94-95).


---


Well then, listen to what Nadežda Mandel'štam, wife of the great Russian poet Osip Emil'evič Mandel'štam (1891-1938), writes on this subject: "It seems to me that for a poet, auditory hallucinations are a kind of occupational disease. That is how poetry begins. Many poets have said so, from the author of the Poem Without a Hero to Mandel'štam himself: an obsessively sounding musical phrase rings in their ears — first shapeless, then ever more defined, but still without words. I happened to see Mandel'štam trying to free himself from one of these refrains, to shake it off, to escape its power (…) Achmatova (Anna Andreevna Achmatova, 1889-1966 – ed.) recounted that when she was assailed by the melody of the Poem, she would have done anything to be rid of it: she even took to washing the dishes, but without result. At a certain moment, the words would suddenly force their way through the fabric of the musical phrase, and then her lips would begin to move. It is probable that the work of the composer and that of the poet have something in common, and that the appearance of the words marks the critical moment that distinguishes these two forms of creation from one another" (L'epoca e i lupi – Serra e Riva, Milan 1990, pp. 98-99).

But could it not be, then, that the work of the scientist also "has something in common" with that of the composer and the poet, and that the appearance of the idea "marks the critical moment" that distinguishes these three forms of creation from one another? And that precisely by virtue of Goethe's proximity to the


---


89

world of ideas, his poetic experience was very different from those of Mandel'štam and Achmatova?

Let us return, in any case, to the question of the "objectivity" of science and the "subjectivity" of art.

Bearing in mind that science is related primarily to thinking, art primarily to feeling, and religion primarily to willing, and that thinking and willing — as we saw when treating The Philosophy of Freedom — are "transitive" or, in Freud's words, "objectual," whereas feeling is "intransitive" or, again in Freud's words, "narcissistic," one could indeed pose this question: can feeling and art become "transitive," "objectual" or "objective"?

Certainly, but they can do so only through thinking.

Let us reflect: what does thinking give us? The concept; and what is the concept? The universal (spiritual); and what do feeling and willing instead (ordinarily) give us? The particular (psychic) and the individual (bodily). To make our feeling universal, and thereby "objective," we would therefore have to succeed in giving the concept as its "object": that is, we would have to succeed in feeling the concept.

What, on the other hand, is an "abstract" concept if not precisely a concept that is not felt (and therefore not willed)?

To bring the force of feeling (and that of willing) toward the form of thinking, or, to put it once again in Freud's words, to "invest" (besetzen) or "charge" the form of thinking with the force of feeling (and of willing), means, on the one hand, enlivening universality and, on the other, universalizing particularity (and individuality).

What is generally done instead? One thinks (and preaches), for example, universal love, but feels particular love (for a "group") and wills individual love (for oneself), thereby digging an unbridgeable gulf between the sphere of knowledge (of "pure reason") and that of morality (of "practical reason").

But let us return to our subject.

Steiner writes: "What matters is not what nature has created, but the principle according to which it created it. That principle must then be worked out in the manner that conforms to its own nature, and not as it has come to be configured in the individual natural formations dependent on a thousand chance contingencies. The artist must develop 'from the common, the sublime; from the formless, the beautiful.' Goethe and Schiller take art in all its depth. The beautiful is 'a manifestation of secret natural laws which, without its appearance, would have remained eternally concealed from us'" (p. 96).


---


It is not true, then, that "the beautiful is what pleases"; rather, it is true that what is beautiful should please us, and that to this end we should educate, develop, and refine our taste.

Beauty — Vladimir Solov'ëv affirms on this point — is "a transfiguration of matter through the embodiment within it of a different, trans-material principle": namely, "of an idea" (Beauty in Nature, in The Meaning of Love – La Casa di Matriona, Milan 1983, pp. 170-171 and 173).

Dostoevsky famously said: "Beauty will save the world"; it will be able to save it, however, only if it is the radiance of truth, the visibility of goodness or (as the Fathers said) "God outside of God" — and not, therefore, illusion or luciferic seduction.

L.R.
Rome, 8 January 2001

90

The Scientific Works of Goethe (19)

Tonight we begin the eighth chapter: The Goethean theory of knowledge.

Steiner writes: "In this chapter we shall concern ourselves with the Goethean theory of knowledge. Unfortunately, since Kant, there has arisen, with regard to what the task of the theory of knowledge is, a confusion to which we must here briefly allude before passing to Goethe's relation to this science. Kant believed that the philosophy preceding him had gone astray because it sought knowledge of the being of things without first asking how such knowledge was possible. He saw the fundamental error of all previous philosophizing in the fact that one pondered the nature of the object to be known before having examined knowing itself with regard to its suitability" (p. 100).


---


In contrast with the philosophy of being (ontology, metaphysics), modern philosophy is born, with Descartes (with the celebrated cogito, ergo sum), as a philosophy of knowing (as epistemology), and then reaches, with Kant, a kind of culmination: with the Critique of Pure Reason we have, according to a happy expression of Gustavo Bontadini (1903-1990), an "explosion of epistemologism."

To Kant, therefore, must be attributed the merit of having posed a question that current science has still not managed to resolve. Listen, for example, to how Edoardo Boncinelli concludes his book: "We have thus arrived at the end of the journey. We have proceeded at times at a gallop, at times at a walk, at times struggling, at times even groping. We have witnessed the most powerful assault yet launched by the human mind at resolving what Valentino Braitenberg in his time called the cognitive scotoma: the difficulty our mind encounters in attempting to understand itself and its own vital center. Some things proved obvious, others surprising; some utterly fascinating, others disappointing. The ground nevertheless seems ready for the final step: the experimental investigation of the ways in which our mind orders and categorizes the things of the world — the cognitive problem par excellence" (Il cervello, la mente e l'anima – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 291).

It is needless to observe that such an "experimental investigation" has very little hope of achieving this goal (whatever the members of today's "scientific community" may think): not — mark well — because it is "experimental," but because, in order to discover "the ways in which our mind orders and categorizes the things of the world," it investigates "experimentally" the brain, and not precisely the mind: because it investigates, that is, the sensible, and not the extrasensible or the supersensible.

Steiner says that "since Kant, there has arisen, with regard to what the task of the theory of knowledge is, a confusion." And for what reason? For the simple reason that the Kantian one — as we shall see — was indeed a "Copernican revolution," but an unfinished revolution that represents, on the one hand, a help and, on the other, an obstacle.

It represents a help because it overcomes the "naive realism" of those who believe that the knowing subject is simply deputed to reflect, like a mirror, the reality of things; it represents an obstacle because it postulates that this subject, precisely insofar as it is active, can know the object only as it appears to it (as phenomenon), but not as it is in itself (as noumenon).

The Kantian revolution thus ends by placing us before this alternative: either to renounce, by adopting naive realism, the subject for the sake of the object; or to renounce, by adopting critical idealism, the object for the sake of the subject.

As you see, this is an either-or that can be overcome only — as Steiner does in The Philosophy of Freedom — by discovering the object (the world) within the subject (within the human being): that is, not by opposing to "naive" realism some form of "anti-realism," but rather by bringing the Kantian revolution to completion, passing from the "naive" realism of things to the "critical" realism of ideas.

                                                  91

But let us proceed in order. We have seen that, according to Boncinelli (a great admirer, moreover, of Kant), "the experimental investigation of the ways in which our mind orders and categorizes the things of the world" constitutes "the cognitive problem par excellence."


---


"On closer examination, — Steiner nevertheless observes, — it turns out that the solution to this problem cannot be placed at the head of the theory of knowledge. If I am concerned to know the possibility of a thing, I must first have examined the thing itself. But... what if the concept of knowledge that is proper to Kant and his followers, and about which they ask whether it is possible or not, should itself prove untenable and incapable of withstanding a thorough critique? What if our process of knowing were something entirely different from what Kant defined? Then all the work would be null and void. Kant adopted the common concept of knowing and raised the question of its possibility. According to that concept, knowing would consist in the reproduction of states of existence situated outside consciousness and subsisting in themselves. But nothing can be decided about the possibility of knowing until the question of what knowing itself is has been resolved" (pp. 100-101).

In other words, one cannot place a theory of knowledge "at the head of the theory of knowledge." By stating that "Kant adopted the common concept of knowing," Steiner means that Kant did not start from a phenomenon, but rather from a theoretical presupposition or postulate: namely, from the conviction — typical of naive realism — that knowing consists "in the reproduction of states of existence situated outside consciousness and subsisting in themselves."

A "conviction" (a "common concept"), insofar as it is the effect of a judgment, and not of an experience, ought to be discussed, and not placed at the foundation of a reflection (which would moreover claim to be "critical").

Steiner observes precisely: "The forming of an individual judgment, the establishing of a fact or a series of facts — which, according to Kant, could already be called knowledge — is not yet knowing in the Goethean sense (...) This departure from the sensory world in its immediacy is characteristic of Goethe's view of true knowing. The immediately given is experience" (p. 101).


---


Since what is "mediated" is the fruit of thinking, while what is "immediate" is the fruit of perceiving, what is "immediately given" is then only the "percept": that is to say, the indeterminate content of perception.

To know "knowing," in the Goethean sense, it would therefore be necessary to start from the percept — which Kant does not do.

Listen, for example, to how the Critique of Pure Reason begins: "There can be no doubt that all our knowledge begins with experience; for how else could our faculty of knowledge be awakened to activity, if not by means of objects that strike our senses, which, on the one hand, give rise of themselves to representations, and on the other, set the activity of our intellect in motion to compare these representations, to combine or separate them, and thus to work up the raw material of sensory impressions into that knowledge of objects which is called experience?" (Critique of Pure Reason - Laterza, Bari 1966, vol. I, pp. 39-40).

As you can see, not only is it taken for granted (as naive realism does) that the "objects that strike our senses" "give rise of themselves to representations," and that the intellect merely, by comparing them, "combines or separates them," but confusion is also created regarding "experience": first it is said, indeed, that "all our knowledge begins with experience," and then it is said, instead, that the "knowledge" of objects "is called experience."

92
It remains true, in any case, that "all our knowledge begins with experience"; and precisely for this reason, even knowledge of "knowing" should begin in this way: but it could do so only if it started — as we have said — from the immediately given, from the percept.

One thing, in short, is experience (perceiving), another is knowing (perceiving and thinking).

Steiner rightly writes: "In knowing, we create from the immediately given an image that contains far more than the senses can supply, even though they are the medium of all experience" (p. 101).

What does this "image" contain, in fact? Nothing less than the determined form of the indeterminate force experienced through the senses: that is, that quid (the concept) which allows us to qualify as A, B, C, etc. that X or that indeterminate content which we have perceived.


---


Steiner writes on this point: "Mill's school (John Stuart Mill, 1806-1873 - ed.) holds that all that can be done with experience is merely to gather individual objects into groups which are then fixed in abstract concepts. This is not true knowing. For Mill's abstract concepts have no other task than to summarize what is offered to the senses, with all the qualities of immediate experience. True knowing must grant, however, that the immediate form of the given sensory world is not yet its essential form, for this reveals itself to us only in the process of thinking. Knowing must furnish us with what the experience of the senses does not give us, but which is nonetheless real. Mill's knowing is not true knowing, since it is only a developed sensory experience; it leaves things as the eyes and ears present them" (pp. 101-102).

But why does Mill think this way? For the simple reason that he considers inner representations (two-dimensional) to be mere reproductions of exterior perceptual images (three-dimensional), and attributes to the (abstract) concept only the ("bureaucratic") task of gathering them "into groups" and "summarizing" them.

This is therefore a standpoint that takes no account of Kant's discovery. What did Kant discover, in fact? That the representation is not a reproduction of the object, but rather a production of the subject (in response to the stimulus coming from the object or the "thing in itself"), and that it is therefore not the representation that derives from the perceptual image, but the perceptual image that derives from the representation.

Steiner says that Mill "leaves things as the eyes and ears present them": yet it was precisely Kant who demonstrated that what presents itself to the eyes and ears is the world as it appears to us (phenomenal), and not as it is in itself (noumenal).

It would be better, then, to acknowledge this truth — not in order to halt at it (as Kant did), but to go beyond, in search of the activity and the factors that allow the world, precisely, to appear to us, and us to imagine and represent it.

Only by discovering by virtue of what activity and what factors we manage to make use of these products could we in fact understand at what level and in what way we retain, beyond representations or subjective images, an objective relation with the world.

We know, however, that the activity is thinking and that the factors are the concepts: precisely those concepts which we have — coincidentally — been unable to bring into play either when speaking of the naive realists or when speaking of Kant.

Let it be clear: it is not that these thinkers do not speak of concepts; it is that they speak of them abstractly, and not therefore as of essences or entities to which we owe the fact of being able to give form both to representations and to perceptual images; or as of "objective" realities to which we owe the fact of being able to represent and imagine the world "subjectively."


---


93
It is only by virtue of the synthetic force of concepts that we are in fact able to give, at a first level, the unitary garb of the perceptual image to the unrelated multiplicity of sensory stimuli or inputs, and, at a second level, the unitary garb of our consciousness of the real to the unrelated multiplicity of individual perceptual images (or individual representations).

Let us keep this well in mind, because Steiner, both here and in The Philosophy of Freedom, conducts his inquiry above all at this second level.

In any case he writes: "How does the immediately experienced behave in relation to the image of experience that arises during the process of knowing? (...) Let us now take an example from the inorganic world, carefully separating what we perceive with the senses from what the process of knowing supplies us. We see a stone, hurled at a pane of glass, pierce it and then, after a certain time, fall to the ground. We ask: What is given to us here as immediate experience? A series of visual perceptions arising from the places successively traversed by the stone, a series of sound perceptions in the shattering of the pane, in the breaking and flying away of the glass splinters, etc. If one does not wish to deceive oneself, one must say: To immediate experience nothing else is given besides this incoherent aggregate of perceptual acts" (of "perceptual images" - ed.) (p. 102).

Which means that the "relations" between individual perceptual images (or individual representations) — as we have said — are not given to "immediate experience."

The fact is that what chemistry does with substances, epistemology ought to do with knowing. Just as the former, by analyzing water for example, discovered that it is constituted of two atoms of hydrogen and one of oxygen, so the latter, were it to analyze knowing, would discover that it is constituted of percept and concept, and that what is given to us in the waking state, in the form of an image, is nothing but the result of the combination of these two elements.

We have just said, returning to Steiner's example, that the relations between individual perceptual images (or individual representations) are not given to immediate experience. It will therefore be appropriate to specify that spatial and temporal relations (Kant's "sensible intuitions") are indeed given to it, but not yet those thanks to which it is possible to know or to do science.

If we were to make use only of spatial and temporal relations, we could in fact affirm that phenomenon A occurred here, while phenomenon B occurred there, that phenomenon A occurred before phenomenon B, while phenomenon B occurred after phenomenon A; but we could not assert that the first is the cause of the second or that the second is the effect of the first: we could not assert, that is, anything qualitative.


---


Steiner writes: "Volket (Johannes Volket, 1848-1930 - ed. note) truly takes an untenable position by asserting, first, that we must hold to what is given to us as immediate experience, and then making the presupposition (which cannot be given) that the world of experience is a world of representations" (p. 103).

We have said that Kant must be granted the merit of having discovered that it is not the representation that derives from the perceptual image, but the perceptual image that derives from the representation. "Perception — Steiner in fact asserts — is a representation carried over ("projected" - ed. note) into the external world" (Anthroposophy, Psychosophy, Pneumatosophy - Religio, Rome 1939, p. 83).

It is one thing, however, to maintain (as the Kantian Volket does) that the representation is the subjective reaction to the objective action of the "thing-in-itself"; it is another to maintain (as Steiner does) that it is the particular result of the encounter between the individual percept and the universal concept. The representation — he writes precisely — is nothing other than "a concept that was once conjoined with a perception and to which the relationship with that perception has remained": it is nothing other, that is, than "an individualized concept" (The Philosophy of Freedom - Anthroposophica, Milan 1966, p. 89). One thing, in short, is to assert that it is a (subjective) phenomenon concealing the noumenal (objective) reality of the world; another is to assert that it is a phenomenon that, on the contrary, reveals it.

Steiner observes in this regard: "The ideal connection of perceptions is not given by means of the senses, but is grasped by our spirit in an absolutely independent way. For a being endowed solely with the faculty of sense perception, all this work would simply not exist. The external world would remain for it only the disconnected chaos of perceptions that we have characterized as what appears to us at first. Thus the place where perceptual images appear in their ideal connection, and where this connection is set against them as their conceptual counterpart, is human consciousness. But although this conceptual connection (the law) is produced, as regards its substantial constitution, in consciousness, it does not at all follow that it is subjective also as regards its significance. Rather, with regard to its content, it derives from objectivity, just as, with regard to its conceptual form, it springs from consciousness. It is the necessary objective complement of the perceptual image" (p. 104).


---


You see, during waking life, we continually receive (sensory) stimuli that would remain just that (that is, unrelated and indeterminate) if we did not possess a thinking capable of discovering their connections, and of synthesizing them thus in the form of representations and perceptual images (as well as resolving them into concepts).

Well, those who hold that such syntheses have no objective foundation neglect to observe that the stimuli present themselves as separate only insofar as the subject's sensory organization parcellizes or analyzes the original, unitary input coming from the object.

One and the same table, for example, is one thing for sight, another for touch, yet another for smell, and so on. One thing, therefore, is the table; another the stimuli into which it is subdivided by the senses: the former is in fact one, while the latter are multiple (that is, as many as there are sense organs engaged in the act of perceiving).

It is well understood, in this light, that the task of knowing should then be to re-create the original synthesis or unity, not to invent it.

The fact is that, in us, there is a "place" (the physical body or, more precisely, the neuro-sensory system) in which reality gives itself in the form of stimuli, and a "place" (of soul and spirit) in which it gives itself instead in conceptual or ideal form.

We should therefore guard not only against projecting onto reality the dualism that characterizes our cognitive organization, but also against deeming "subjective" that part of reality that gives itself in conceptual or ideal form. One thing, in fact, is the "content" that is given; another the "place" in which it is given — just as one thing, for example, is the actor who performs, another the stage on which he performs.

Which is to say that not everything that is given within the subject is to be judged, for that reason alone, "subjective." In any case it is decisive to realize that perceptual images, sensations, and representations are subjective, whereas the concept is objective — and that without the concept we would not even have those.

Almost all of us are in fact convinced that we have perceptual images, sensations, and representations of the objects, whereas in reality we have, as a reaction to the stimuli of the objects, perceptual images, sensations, and representations of the concepts.

To ascertain a fact so contrary to common opinion, it will be necessary to consider the "external" (physical) world, the "internal" (soul) world, and the "external of the internal" (spiritual) world, and to realize that the "external of the internal" is nothing other than the "internal of the external": that is to say, the spiritual essence of the physical world.

L.R. Rome, January 16, 2001
                                                   95
Le opere scientifiche di Goethe (20) [The Scientific Works of Goethe (20)]


---


Last week we concluded our meeting by speaking of the "external" (physical) world, the "internal" (soul) world, and the "external of the internal" (spiritual) world, and we said that this last "is nothing other than the 'internal of the external': that is, the spiritual essence of the physical world."

Well, before resuming the reading, I would like to offer a few further considerations in this regard.

You see, with ordinary consciousness we move within two precise limits: an outer one, constituted by the perceptual image; an inner one, constituted by the representation. This leads us to believe that we are permitted to move only within the "soul-space" they delimit, and that it is not possible to discover either what lies, in the world, beyond the perceptual image, or what lies, in us, on this side of the representation: that it is not possible, that is, to discover their origin and the manner in which they take form.

Naive realists (also called "perceptionists") think to solve the problem by asserting that the perceptual image of the thing coincides with the thing, and that the representation is merely a reproduction (a "photograph") of the perceptual image; Kant thinks to solve it by asserting instead that beyond the perceptual image lies the substantial reality of the "thing-in-itself," while on this side of the representation lie the formal reality of the "categories" and the equally formal reality of the "transcendental I" (as their original unity).

To assert — as Steiner does — that we apprehend the "in-itself" of the thing (which lies precisely both beyond the perceptual image and on this side of the representation) by virtue of perceiving (of willing), as percept (as indeterminate force), and by virtue of thinking, as concept (as determined form), is therefore a very different thing from asserting — as Kant does — that of the "thing-in-itself" we can apprehend nothing, since what we apprehend depends toto coelo on our mode of apprehending, and not on the thing.

"I call transcendental — he in fact writes — all knowledge that is occupied not with objects, but with our mode of knowledge of objects insofar as this must be possible a priori" (Critique of Pure Reason - Laterza, Bari 1966, vol. I, p. 58).

As you see, both for the naive realists and for Kant there exist only the "external" world and the "internal" one: with the difference, however, that for the former the "internal" world is merely a (passive) reflection of the "external" one, whereas for the latter the "external" world is merely an (active) projection of the "internal" one.

But this happens because both ignore the reality of that "third" (spiritual) world that we have named (and not to play with words) the "external of the internal."

Since, however, the space comprised between the "Scylla" of the perceptual image and the "Charybdis" of the representation is a narrow one (and "anguish" has the same root as "anxiety" - Lat. angere: "to constrict"), we suffer (unconsciously) from its narrowness and feel (obscurely) the need to widen our ordinary horizon. But in order to widen it, we would have precisely to overcome the barriers that normally limit it, discovering that both beyond the perceptual image and on this side of the representation there lies one and the same reality (that of the essence of the object or of the phenomenon).

By restoring to the soul its true space, we would therefore restore the soul to the soul (ourselves to ourselves). Do you remember when we asserted that of someone who has been reduced to "skin and bones" one could also say (in anthroposophical terms) that he has been reduced to "Lucifer and Ahriman," since that human being who stands, as a third, between the luciferic skin and the ahrimanic bones has perished in him?

Well, it is the same "human being" who ordinarily finds himself constrained between the ahrimanic perceptual images and the luciferic representations.


---


96
Just as we then concluded that our task is not to skin ourselves or de-bone ourselves, but to place these parts in the service of the I, so we can now conclude that our task is not to avoid perceptual images (as those tend to do who stand, like the anti-realists, with their "head in the air") or representations (as those tend to do who stand, like the naive realists, with their "head on the ground"), but to make use of these manifestations to ascend, together, to the I and to the essence of the objects or of the phenomena.

But now let us resume reading.

Steiner writes: "If what is immediately given were sufficient unto itself, so that no problem sprang from it for us at any point, we would never have the need to transcend it. But perceptual images do not follow one another, nor do they derive from one another; they follow rather from something else, which is barred to sense perception. Conceptual comprehension now draws near to them and grasps also that side of reality which remains closed to the senses" (p. 104).

Reality reveals itself to us by two different paths: what we apprehend of it through the senses must therefore be reunited, in the soul, with what we apprehend of it through thinking. It is in the soul (where the I carries out the activity of "judging") that error can thus arise; Goethe in fact warns: "The senses do not deceive; it is judgment that deceives" (Maxims and Reflections - TEA, Rome 1988, p. 227).

When the I, in judging, re-unites in the soul the percept and the concept so as to spiritually re-create the original natural unity (broken by the I itself), we have truth; when any usurper of the I's role, in judging, unites them differently, we have instead error or falsehood.

Let us imagine, for example, that essence A gives itself to perceiving as a' and to thinking as a''. Well: if, in judging, we took the percept a' and re-united it with the concept a'', we would have A again; whereas if we took a' and united it, say, with b'', c'' or d'', we would instead have an error or a falsehood.

Do you remember when we spoke of the need to restore to the psyche (to Eve) the purity, chastity, or virginity of the soul (of the Ave)? At this point the reason should be even clearer: because only such a soul, being devoted to the world and to the I, is able not to condition and not to alter (with its cravings or with its sympathies and antipathies) the process of judging.

Let us keep in mind, in any case, that just as a pure soul is needed to rediscover truth, so truth is needed to purify the psyche and rediscover the soul.

To come upon falsehood means, therefore, to come upon a psyche (an "internal world") in which vanity, self-interest, and personal utility (by usurping precisely the I's role) have smothered the love for reality.


---


Answer to a Question

You see, truth has the power to evoke and kindle, in feeling, beauty and, in willing, goodness (morality). A world permeated by truth ("The truth shall make you free") would therefore be different and better. If only those who claim to fight for a "better world" without fighting for truth understood this!

Certainly, to speak of truth to someone immersed, like all of us, in error and lies, is a bit like explaining the physiology of the eyes, as myopes or hypermetropes, to other myopes or hypermetropes. But that is not what matters! What matters rather is realizing that our eyes could and should see otherwise, and that it is not true — as the obstructionists would have us believe — that by nature they can only be myopic or hypermetropic.

97

Steiner writes: "The most widespread opinion on this point is that the concept is only a means, belonging to consciousness, by which consciousness takes possession of the data of reality. The essence of reality would lie in the in-itself of the things themselves; so that even if we were able truly to arrive at the ground of things, we could nevertheless take possession only of a conceptual image of it, and never of the ground itself. Here, then, two wholly separate worlds are presupposed: the objective external world, which carries within itself its own essence, the grounds of its existence, and the subjective-ideal inner world, which would be a conceptual image of the external world. The latter is wholly indifferent to objectivity, is not demanded by it, and exists only for the knowing human being. The congruence of these two worlds would be the epistemological ideal of this fundamental opinion" (p. 105).

The two "presupposed" worlds are, of course, the "external" (objective) world of perceptual images and the "internal" (subjective) world of representations.

The third, the "external of the internal" world of concepts, insofar as it is reduced to a formal world, is in fact inserted into and comprehended within the ("internal") world of representations, and by that very fact subjectivized. "The congruence of these two worlds," says Steiner, "would be the epistemological ideal of this fundamental opinion." This ideal can be realized, however, by pursuing not the "congruence" of the "external" (physical) world with the "internal" (psychic) one, but rather the "congruence," within the "internal" (psychic) world, of the "external" (physical) world with the "external of the internal" (spiritual) world — this last, as we have said, being none other than the "internal of the external" world, and therefore the spiritual essence of the physical world.

The conceptual image — Steiner says again — "is wholly indifferent to objectivity, is not demanded by it, exists only for the knowing human being." But the "knowing human being" too is part of the world (of "objectivity") and is "world." If, therefore, one considers that the world has come to create a being through which it can become conscious of itself, it is truly hard to think that human knowing is "wholly indifferent to objectivity."


---


Thinking, Steiner again writes, "is an organ of perception no less than the eye and the ear. As the eye sees colors and the ear hears sounds, so thinking perceives ideas. Idealism is therefore perfectly reconcilable with the principle of empirical investigation" (p. 105).

But why does the eye allow us to perceive colors distinctly, and the ear sounds? Because, while we perceive colors and sounds, the one and the other do not make themselves perceived. By humbly stepping aside, they thereby demonstrate that they know how to do precisely what we are unable to do. If we had already acquired, in the soul, their own humility or purity, we would in fact perceive the ideas distinctly as well, and would re-unite them, without psychic interference, to their objects (Paul says exactly this: "For now we see, indeed, as through a mirror, dimly; but then we shall see face to face in God..." — 1 Cor 13:12).

Steiner continues: "The idea is not the content of subjective thinking, but is the result of investigation. Reality presents itself to us insofar as we place ourselves before it with open senses. It appears to us in a form that we cannot regard as its true form; this we reach only when we set our thinking in motion. To know means to add the perception of thinking to the half-reality of sense experience, so that its image may become complete" (p. 105).


---


As a "result of investigation," the idea is therefore the fruit of an observation, not of an invention. And there is a fine difference — you will agree — between the state of mind of one who thinks he must elaborate and formulate a theory of his own, and that of one who knows instead that the theory is in the things or in the world, and that his own, at most, could be only the discovery or the awareness of the theory. "The ideal," Goethe states precisely, "would be to understand that every real element is already theory. The blue of the sky reveals to us the fundamental law of chromatics. Above all, do not seek anything behind the phenomena: they themselves are the theory" (ibid., p. 137).

"To know," says Steiner, "means to add the perception of thinking to the half-reality of sense experience": it means, in short, "to judge." However, in the "perceptual judgment" (as Steiner calls it), by means of the copula (is), we do not join one concept to another concept, but rather we join to a percept (an undetermined subject) a concept (a determined predicate). Calling the first X and the second A, the perceptual judgment will in fact be: X is A.

In the very instant in which we think it as X (and not yet, therefore, as A), even the first term of this judgment thus resolves into a "concept" (into precisely that of "percept" or "undetermined subject"). How so? Because thinking is deputed to grasp the "thoughts" in the "things," and not (as is generally believed) the "things" — or, more precisely, because it is deputed to grasp the "concepts," not the "percepts" (so much so that it grasps, not the "percept," but rather the "concept of the percept").

But for what reason, then, does the "concept" of the "determined predicate" (A) present itself to us precisely as a "concept," while the "concept" of the "undetermined subject" (X) presents itself instead as a "percept"? For the simple reason — as we have already had occasion to stress — that we grasp the first in a reflected or indirect, but clear, manner, by means of thinking, while we grasp the second in a direct, but obscure, manner, by means of willing (of perceiving). Steiner will indeed say: "That which constitutes the singularity of an object cannot be comprehended, but only perceived" (p. 108).

As you see, empirical idealism is "idealism" because it succeeds in making explicit the concept (the universal) implied in the percept (in the individual or singular), and it is "empirical" because it starts from the percept (from the individual or singular) in which the concept (the universal) is implied.

98


---


Steiner writes: if "the most widespread opinion" held (that according to which the concept would be "only a means, belonging to consciousness, by which consciousness takes possession of the data of reality"), then upon seeing, for example, a triangle "I would have to follow in thought its contours, the size, the direction of its sides, etc., and form for myself a conceptual photograph of it. For another triangle I would have to do precisely the same, and so for every object of the world of the senses, whether external or internal. In this way, in my ideal image of the world, I would find again exactly every object, with its situation and its qualities. Now we must ask ourselves: Does this consequence correspond to the facts? Not at all. My concept of the triangle is a single concept that embraces all perceived triangles; and however many times I may represent it, it always remains the same. My various representations of triangles are all identical with one another. I have only one single concept of the triangle. In reality, every object presents itself to us as a particular 'this,' fully determined, to which there stand opposed the 'those,' equally determined and saturated with true reality. In the face of such multiplicity, the concept arises as a rigorous unity. In it there are no specifications, nor parts; it does not multiply and, though represented infinitely many times, it is always the same. What, then, is in reality the bearer of this identity of the concept? It cannot, in effect, be the form of its appearing as representation, because, as Berkeley rightly asserted, the representation of the tree which I have at this moment has absolutely nothing to do with that of the same tree which I have a moment later, if, between the one and the other, I keep my eyes closed; and equally little have the various representations of the same object in different individuals to do with one another. The identity can therefore reside solely in the content of the representation, that is, in its quid. The substance, the essential, must guarantee me the identity" (pp. 106-107).


---


Well then, let us imagine drawing three figures: an equilateral triangle, an isosceles triangle, and a scalene triangle, and then asking ourselves what they have in common. As pure perceptual images, they are each different from the others. Language could nonetheless help us, because — note this — we say we have drawn not an "equilateral," an "isosceles," and a "scalene," but rather an "equilateral triangle," an "isosceles triangle," and a "scalene triangle"; by repeating the word "triangle" each time, it is therefore as if we were saying that we had drawn one and the same "content" in three different "forms." The same thing would naturally have happened had we drawn, say, three dogs: a Maremma sheepdog, a setter, and a poodle. In this case too, we would have found ourselves before three different "forms" of one and the same "content": that is, three different "breeds" of one and the same "species," and therefore (to step out of the metaphor) three different "manifestations" of one and the same "essence," or three different "representations" of one and the same "concept."

Diversity (multiplicity) is thus given to the senses, while identity (unity) is given instead to thought: and the latter is no less real than the former. Certainly, the reality of the first is given to us by nature, while that of the second — as we well know — we must freely and actively conquer for ourselves, through study and inner exercise (concentration and meditation).

L.R.
Rome, January 23, 2001

100
The Scientific Works of Goethe (21)


---


We said last time that our habitual experience of the world is bounded, on the one hand, by the perceptual images (three-dimensional) we obtain by looking outward, and on the other by the representations (two-dimensional) we obtain by looking inward; and we also asserted that both beyond the former and on this side of the latter there hides that entelechy or unitary essence of the object or phenomenon which presents itself in the guise of a percept to perceiving (willing), and in the guise of a concept to thinking.

We might nonetheless ask ourselves: since we are normally unconscious of both the process of forming perceptual images and that of forming representations, why do we then proceed from the latter rather than from the former?

The answer is quick to give: so as not to commit the same naivety as those who believe, following Freud and Jung, that they can access the subconscious activity of feeling and the unconscious activity of willing without passing through the pre-conscious activity of thinking.

Since unconscious contents tend — as psychoanalysis itself teaches — to be projected (and hence alienated), it is only by bringing to light that pre-conscious activity of thinking which gives form to (conscious) representations that one can be sure of not projecting (and hence alienating) onto the life of feeling and onto that of willing (onto the life of a "not-I," such as for example Freud's Es or Jung's Self) what belongs to the life of thinking (to the life of the I).

"The notion of the unconscious," Massimo Scaligero observes, "must be drawn out of the equivocation of an unknowing transcendence. One must know what can legitimately be called unconscious, given that the activity capable of registering its phenomenology is its opposite, namely the inner activity which unfolds on condition of eliminating the unconscious. But the non-being of the unconscious, insofar as its place is taken by consciousness, is in effect its being for consciousness" (Psicoterapia: fondamenti esoterici — Perseo, Rome 1974, p. 15).

Which means that ordinary consciousness, in order to penetrate in a healthy way into the so-called "unconscious," must descend into the subconscious (dreaming) and unconscious (sleeping) spheres not directly, but indirectly, by ascending into the pre-conscious and super-conscious ones of imagination, inspiration, and intuition.

But let us now resume reading from the point where we had left off.


---


Steiner writes: "With this there also falls away the opinion that denies to the concept or the idea any autonomous content. This opinion holds that conceptual unity as such is entirely devoid of all content, and originates only from the fact that certain determinations are omitted in the objects of experience, while the common element is instead brought into relief and inserted into our intellect in order to conveniently compress the manifold objective reality, according to the principle that the spirit embraces the totality of experience by means of the smallest possible number of general units; thus according to the principle of the minimum expenditure of force (...) This opinion rests solely upon a complete failure to recognize, not only the substance of the concept, but also perception" (p. 107).

Sound — Edoardo Boncinelli asserts, for example — "consists of a vibration of the air molecules which propagates at a certain velocity, like a pressure wave" (Il cervello, la mente e l'anima — Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 16).

But it is not so. Sound is not "constituted" by a vibration of the air molecules; rather it is the cause of a vibration of the air molecules; and the nature of this effect depends not so much on that of the sound as on the nature of the medium it traverses: in air, sound produces

101

effects that depend on the nature of air, while, say, in water, it would produce effects that depend on the nature of water.

This moreover goes to show — as Steiner says — that in failing to recognize the content of the concept one also fails to recognize the content of perception. Is it not significant, moreover, that in order to indicate the latter we have been forced to resort to the term "percept": that is to say, to a term which is generally used to indicate the perceptual image, and not the content of which this is precisely nothing but the "image"?

Steiner continues: "To make this point clear we must go back to the reason why perception is opposed, as the particular, to the concept as the universal. We must ask ourselves: wherein does the characteristic of the particular truly consist? Can it be determined conceptually? Is it perhaps permissible to say that this conceptual unit must resolve itself into these or those particular perceptible multiplicities? No, is the very decided answer. The concept itself does not know particularity at all" (p. 107).


---


In this passage, Steiner sets the particularity (of the percept) against the universality (of the concept). Let us keep in mind, however, that "particularity" is used here as a synonym of "singularity," and not as a category to be interposed — logically — between that of universality and that of individuality (Steiner himself, after all, will shortly speak of "singularity").

Do you remember what we said last time? We said that thinking is charged with grasping the "thoughts" in "things" and not the "things," or that it is charged with apprehending "concepts" and not "percepts": that is, it is charged with grasping the universal and not the particular (the singular). We illustrated this by observing that with the (perceptual) judgment "X is A" one means (more or less consciously) that this percept X (particular or singular) is that concept A (universal). This is an experience of everyday life on which it would be extremely important to reflect in a truly thoroughgoing way.

"Here and now" (hic et nunc), I perceive something. I therefore know that something (an X) exists, but I do not yet know what the thing is that exists: I am not yet able, that is, to determine it, qualify it, identify it, or recognize it (as A). For how could one understand, in the absence of the concept, whether it is, say, a cat, a dog, or a horse? Whether it is, that is, a specimen of the first, the second, or the third species?

The fact is that to assert, say, "This is a dog" is equivalent to asserting: "This is a (single) specimen of the (universal) species dog."

That the "single" or the "this" (the X) belongs to the species "dog" can therefore be revealed to us only by thought (by means of the concept). But the concept — as Steiner says — "does not know particularity at all." Which means that thinking knows the dog as "concept," but not this dog as "percept."

And thanks to what do we then know this dog? We have said it: thanks to perceiving.

Whoever knows the (ontogenetic and phylogenetic) stages of the evolution of human consciousness, as illustrated by the science of the spirit, knows that it is a process which, by separating thinking from perceiving ever more, has brought modern consciousness to experience a thinking devoid of perceiving (a thinking — as Kant says — "empty") and a perceiving devoid of thinking (a perceiving — as Kant again says — "blind"), or, as we are wont to say, to experience a form devoid of force (Schopenhauer's "representation") and a force devoid of form (Schopenhauer's "will" again).

The separation or division of thinking from perceiving (willing) necessarily entails that of being from existing, and is the (mostly unconscious) motive both of the demotion of the former to non-being (due in particular to the nominalists) and of the promotion of the latter to being (due in particular to the existentialists).

102

When Steiner speaks — as you know — of the ancient "twilight" or "instinctive" clairvoyance, he alludes therefore to an experience in which thinking and perceiving, not yet having split apart, were very different from how we experience them today.

For our habitual consciousness they in fact constitute a duality or a polarity: that between the real sphere (based on perceiving) and the ideal sphere (based on thinking).

There was, however, a time, prior to that split, in which the real coincided with the ideal, and the ideal with the real.

Returning to our judgment "X is A": it is therefore important to understand that thinking gives us the "predicate" (the universal concept A), but it is no less important to understand that perceiving gives us the "subject" (the singular percept X).


---


Steiner writes precisely: "What constitutes the singularity of an object cannot be comprehended, but only perceived. Herein lies the reason for the necessary failure of every philosophy that wishes to derive (deduce) the whole of sensible reality, in its multiplicity of individual particulars, from the concept itself" (p. 108).

But for what reason was it necessary that thinking, which always and only gives us universality, should be divided from (sensible) perceiving, which always and only gives us singularity? Why, otherwise, would the individual consciousness of the I ever have been born?

(Sensible) perceiving is in fact a perceiving by means of the sense organs of that physical body which precisely divides and distinguishes us from all other beings.

It is well to remember, in this regard, that the split between thinking and perceiving supervened at the very moment in which the etheric body (which had previously served as the trait d'union between physical reality and the psychic-spiritual one), sinking ever deeper into the physical body, came to be entirely sheathed within it (above all in the neuro-sensory sphere).

It is important to keep this in mind, since only one who understands how modern individual self-consciousness was born (the bodily consciousness of the I as ego) can understand in what way we could, and should, make it grow, develop, and mature.

Answer to a question

As we know, modern individual self-consciousness, that is to say the consciousness soul, is born in 1413 and exhausts its first phase of "natural-scientific" development in 1879, the date marking the beginning of the new epoch or regency of the Archangel Michael (called by Steiner the "flaming prince of thought" — Anthroposophical Leading Thoughts [Massime antroposofiche] — Antroposofica, Milan 1969, p. 58). Once this phase, which we might call "Euclidean," "Galilean," or that of "classical physics," was exhausted, the "spiritual-scientific" one ought to have begun, prepared by Goethe and brought to perfection by Steiner.

The cultural world, having "fixed itself" at the intellectual or cerebral stage of the consciousness soul, has however opposed, and still opposes, a tenacious "resistance" to the new evolutionary impulses, without realizing that it thereby provokes a regression to the rational or sentient soul (if not to the sentient soul outright).

This is an extremely disquieting phenomenon, since the rational or sentient soul which (from 747 B.C. to A.D. 1413) conveyed the progressing forces of the spirit was one thing; quite another is the rational or sentient soul which, once that task has passed to the consciousness soul, conveys forces of an altogether different (moral) nature.

In any case Steiner proceeds: "What truly and essentially distinguishes perception from the idea is precisely this element which cannot be put into concepts but must be experienced. Thus concept and perception stand facing each other as aspects of the world which are substantially alike, and yet different" (p. 108).


---


103

This "element" (which we have called X) is the entity (the being): that is, the essence experienced in its force (as percept), but not yet determined in its form (as concept).
But what does it mean that this element "must be experienced"? It means that it must be encountered in a direct or immediate way, and not indirectly, mediated or reflected as (normally) happens with the concept.
Since one can have access to singularity only through (sensible) perceiving, and to universality only through thinking, and since knowing consists in reuniting the singular and the universal, all those will therefore find themselves in difficulty who lack enough courage, open-mindedness or love to overcome — the one, the Ahrimanic deception, by raising themselves to the supersensible; the other, the Luciferic illusion, by descending to the sensible ("In scholastic philosophy — Hegel says, on this latter point — man's eyes had been put out" — Lectures on the History of Philosophy — La Nuova Italia, Florence 1981, vol. 3, II, p. 7).

In its manifestation, Steiner precisely observes, universality "must first be found in the subject; for it can indeed be acquired by the subject in contact with the object, but not extracted from the object" (p. 108); and he adds: "It is customary to say that the object of experience is individual, a living vision, whereas the concept is abstract and, compared with experience full of content, poor, empty. But wherein is the richness of determinations sought here? In their number, which, given the infinity of space, can be infinitely large. But the concept is not on that account less fully determined. The number of determinations is here replaced by quality. But just as number, quantity, is not to be found in the concept, so perception lacks the qualitative-dynamic element of characteristics. The concept is just as individual, just as full of content as perception (…) It is empty and idle to say that the concept is the enemy of living perception. It is its essence, the true active and operative principle within it" (pp. 108-109).

Remember what we said last time, imagining that we draw three triangles: one equilateral, one isosceles and one scalene? We said that we would find ourselves before three perceptual images (and three representations) of one and the same concept. It would have been the same, naturally, if instead of three triangles we had imagined drawing thirty, three hundred or three thousand; and it would have been the same precisely because "in the concept — as Steiner says — number, quantity, is not to be found," just as "perception lacks the qualitative-dynamic element of characteristics."
How did we in fact express ourselves, a little while ago? "'Here and now' (hic et nunc), I perceive something. I therefore know that something (an X) exists, but I do not yet know what the thing that exists is": that is, I do not yet know what its quality is.


---


Steiner writes again: "Only in the way we have indicated does one arrive at a satisfying explanation of what experimental knowledge truly is. One would not see at all the necessity of proceeding to conceptual knowledge, if the concept added nothing new to the perception of the senses. Pure experimental knowledge would not take even one step beyond the millions of individual particulars that stand before us in perception" (p. 109).


---


As you know, Francis Bacon (Francesco Bacone, 1561-1626) is one of the most important masters of that "experimental knowledge" (or "empirical philosophy") which — Hegel writes — "considers experience as the only and true source of knowing" (ibid., p. 20), and which is therefore convinced that the concept — as Steiner states — adds "nothing new to the perception of the senses."
Allow me therefore to conclude this meeting of ours by reading you some passages of what Hegel again writes concerning this philosophy: "Bacon is still today extolled as the one who is supposed to have indicated to knowledge its true source, experience; he is in fact properly the forerunner and the representative of what in England is customarily called philosophy, and upon which the English have not yet managed to rise. For they seem to constitute in Europe the people which, confined to the intellect of 'reality,' is destined, like the shopkeepers and artisans in the State, to live forever immersed in matter, and to have as its object 'reality,' but not reason"; Bacon and "all the heroes of experience who came after him, who put into practice what he wanted, and who through observations, investigations and experiments believed they could obtain the thing itself in all its purity, could not do so without resorting to deductions and concepts; and their concepts and deductions were the worse, the more they believed they had nothing to do with concepts (…) We have already recalled how important it is to aim at content as the content of present reality: the rational, in fact, must have objective actuality. The reconciliation of spirit with the world, the sublimation of nature and of every reality, must not be a beyond, a 'then,' but must be accomplished now and here. This moment of the now and the here is that which in essence, by this path, comes to self-consciousness. Experiences, experiments, observations, however, do not know what in truth they do, namely that the only reason they take an interest in things is precisely the inner unconscious certainty that reason has of finding itself in reality; observations and experiments, when they are rightly instituted, arrive precisely at the result that only the concept is the objective. What escapes from the experiments' grasp is precisely the individual sensible, and it becomes a universal (…) The other formal error, common to all empiricists, is to believe they are holding to experience alone: they have no consciousness that in receiving these perceptions they are doing metaphysics. Man does not halt at the individual, nor can he halt there. He seeks the universal, and it consists of thoughts, if not of concepts. The most conspicuous form of thought is that of force: one speaks thus of an electric force, of a magnetic force, of a force of gravity. But force is a universal and not a perceptible; therefore in abandoning themselves to these determinations the empiricists operate in a wholly uncritical way and without awareness" (ibid., pp. 17-18 and 27-28).
The problem, therefore, is not whether one should or should not start from (sensible) perception, but rather whether one has or does not have consciousness of the processes and forces that allow it to rise to cognitive or scientific experience.


---


L.R.
Rome, 30 January 2001

105
Le opere scientifiche di Goethe (22)

Remember — we read it a few weeks ago — what Boncinelli says at the end of his book?
"We have witnessed — he says — the most powerful assault launched by the human mind upon the resolution of what Valentino Braitenberg in his time called the cognitive scotoma: the difficulty our mind encounters in attempting to comprehend itself and its own vital center (The Brain, the Mind and the Soul — Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 291).
Well, we concluded our last meeting by affirming that this problem will not be resolved for as long as we do not decide to become conscious of the (pre-conscious) processes and forces that allow (sensible) perception to rise to cognitive or scientific experience.
Which means, among other things, that (philosophical) conceptions such as — let us say — empiricism, materialism, rationalism or idealism constitute not so much "errors" (as the rational or affective soul would judge them), but rather "symptoms" of an insufficient degree of self-consciousness (as the conscious soul judges them).
What indeed is the paradox? The paradox is that we are normally unconscious of the processes and forces thanks to which we are conscious of ourselves and of the world or — to put it in the terms of The Philosophy of Freedom — of that thinking by means of which we think ourselves and the world.
Thinking — Steiner writes precisely — "is the unobserved element of the ordinary life of our spirit," since its peculiar nature "consists in the fact that the thinker forgets thinking while carrying it out. It is not thinking that occupies the thinker, but the observed object about which he thinks" (The Philosophy of Freedom — Antroposofica, Milan 1966, p. 35).
And Scaligero reiterates: "Man knows and in some way dominates the world by means of thought. The contradiction is that he neither knows nor dominates thought. Thought remains a mystery to itself" (Techniques of Inner Concentration — Mediterranee, Rome 1985, p. 9).
It is not therefore a matter of thinking about thought, as philosophy is wont to do, nor of observing the brain, as science is wont to do, but rather of observing thought, using for this purpose (as the science of the spirit teaches) adequate inner practices.
But let us return to ourselves.


---


Steiner writes: "From our point of view, the necessity of scientific knowledge and the going beyond experience are explained without any contradiction. The world of the senses appears to us as the primary and immediate given; it presents itself to us as an immense enigma, because we can never find within it the element that moves and operates. Then reason approaches it and, in the ideal world, places before the world of the senses the essence, the principles that form the solution of the enigma. These principles are as objective as the world of the senses; as to their content, it is indifferent that they do not appear to the senses, but only to reason. It is indeed true that, if thinking beings did not exist, these principles would never come to manifestation; nevertheless they would be the essence of the world of appearance" (pp. 109-110).

Man therefore does not create reality, but rather the consciousness of reality. Since, however, even the consciousness of reality is part of reality, man can be considered as that part of reality in which reality attains consciousness of itself.

Steiner continues: "With this we have opposed a truly immanent conception to the transcendent one of Locke (1632-1704 – ed.), Kant, the late Schelling, Schopenhauer (1788-1860 – ed.), Volkelt (1848-1930 – ed.), the neo-Kantians, and modern scientists. They seek the ground of the world in a quid extraneous to consciousness, otherworldly; immanent philosophy, in that which manifests itself through reason. The transcendent conception considers conceptual
                                                  106
knowledge as a picture of the world, the immanent one as the supreme form of manifestation of the same" (p. 110).


---


For what reason, in fact, should we not consider concepts as manifestations of the world? Just as, by opening the eyes of the body, colors present themselves to us, so, by opening the eyes of the spirit, concepts or ideas present themselves: with the difference, however, that the former normally open by themselves, whereas the latter it is we who must open.

The fact is that the eyes of the spirit – as we recalled last time – were once open, and this is why the further we go back through the course of history, the more we find men who speak of spiritual beings: for them the existence of the Gods was indeed as self-evident as the existence of things is for us. (Herodotus reports, for example, that the priests of Memphis, Thebes, and Heliopolis told him, unanimously, "that the first king of Egypt who was a man (our italics) had been Mina…" – Histories – Mondadori, Milan 2000, vol. I, p. 263).

The men of that time, however, experienced spiritual reality (and precisely for this reason they did not feel the slightest need to "prove it"), but not the physical and spatial one, and therefore they could not enjoy, as we do, an individual consciousness of the I.

Then, such men were in God (in the Father), whereas today God (the Son) is in us. Every modern man who does not receive (as a free being) the Logos, who does not allow His holy power (which indwells the I) to actualize itself and live in the soul, so that God may be in him and he in God, remains therefore "outside the grace of God"; and instead of considering himself on that account dead, he may even set about discoursing, following Nietzsche (1844-1900), on the "death of God."

In any case, it is the ordinary difficulty of reconciling being with thinking that feeds the dispute between the transcendent and the immanent conceptions.

It is true, in fact, that the so-called "natural light," that is, representational thinking, is, inasmuch as a reflection, nothing but an "image," and therefore "non-being"; but representation – as we have already had occasion to emphasize – is an image of the concept, not of the object ("Representations in general – Hegel writes precisely – can be considered as metaphors of thoughts and concepts" – Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989, p. 6). It stands, then, to the concept as, say, our photograph stands to us: that is, as the dead image of a living being stands to the living being.

In other words, ordinary representational thinking separates itself from living thinking in the same way in which the corpse separates itself from the living being of man; and as wherever there is a corpse (a non-being) there must first have been a living man (a being), so wherever there is a representation there must first have been a living concept.

I hope this makes clear the reason why Scaligero insists so much on the necessity of practicing "ascending the movement of thought": only by "ascending it" (starting from its non-being) can one rediscover and experience the being of thinking and that of the concept.

But what else does this necessity of "ascending the movement of thought" remind us of? That of bringing to light – as we have said and repeated – what conceals itself "on this side of representation." Since, both beyond the perceptual image and on this side of representation, one encounters the threshold that divides the sphere of the conscious from that of the unconscious, whoever lacks the courage to cross it will then try to explain the former by means of the latter, or vice versa: for Kant, the perceptual image would be nothing but a "projection" of representation, while, for naïve realists, representation would be nothing but a "reproduction" of the perceptual image.

As you see, it is a matter, as always, not of conjecturing, hypothesizing, or theorizing, but rather of "rolling up one's sleeves," as the saying goes, to go and see how things really stand.

In any event, the ordinary transcendent conceptions are right in asserting that representation is not "being," but they are wrong in identifying representing with thinking and representation with the concept, whereas the ordinary immanent conceptions are right in asserting that thinking and the concept are "beings," but they are wrong in merely speculating about their being abstractly, thus continuing, in fact, to present them as "non-beings."
                                                   107


---


Steiner writes: "We must start from reality in its immediate form, completely devoid of determinations, from that which is given to the senses before we set our thinking in motion, from the merely seen, the merely heard, etc." (p. 110).

This statement of Steiner's gives us occasion to recall – as emphasized in a quite particular way by Scaligero (especially in Logic Against Man – Tilopa, Rome 1967) – that the act of perceiving is an etheric phenomenon actualized by means of physical sense organs: that one thing, in other words, is the activity of seeing, for example, and another the reality of the eyes. It is not the eyes that see, in fact, but we who "cast or fix – as the saying goes – our gaze" and see through them. But what is this gaze that we "cast" or "fix," in itself? Nothing other, precisely, than a movement of our etheric body.

For this to become truly clear, however, one would have to commit oneself to the exercise of "pure perceiving," developed precisely by Scaligero. "Pure perceiving" – he explains – "is the technique by which the disciple comes to experience the inner process of sensory perception. He starts from a particular perception and turns toward it an attention that gradually enables him to have before him the content independent of the subjective form in which it is normally clothed: in essence, he separates from this content the representation and the sensation" (Practical Manual of Meditation – Tilopa, Rome 1984, p. 45).

We could also say, however, that by virtue of this exercise one comes to experience the act of perceiving before it coagulates, in the direction of the outer world, in the form of a perceptual image, and animates itself, in that of the inner world, in the form of sensation.

So instantaneous, in fact, is the translating of perceptions into sensations that even in scientific terminology the former are often confused with the latter, without realizing that we owe perceptions (the percepts) to etheric-physical activity and sensations to the activity of the soul (that of the sentient soul).


---


Steiner continues, in any case: "What matters is that we become conscious of what the senses provide us and of what thinking provides us. The senses do not tell us that things stand in some relation to one another, for example, that this one is cause, that one effect. For the senses, all things are equally essential to the structure of the world (…) At this level of consideration, Napoleon is not historically more important than Caius or Sempronius in the little village lost among the mountains. Up to this point, the theory of knowledge of our day had already arrived; but that it has not thought these truths through exhaustively is shown by the fact that almost all theorists of knowledge commit the error of immediately attributing the predicate of representation to this provisional, indeterminate picture, devoid of determinations, which we encounter at the first level of our perceiving. This means proceeding in the most crude way against one's own newly won opinion. Just as, so long as we remain at immediate sensory perception, we do not know that the falling stone is the cause of the hollowing out of the ground it strikes, so, equally little do we know that it is a representation" (p. 111).

We have already seen, in this regard, that the senses give us an X here, another X there, or first one X and then another X: that is, they give us unknowns (percepts) between which we can at first establish only connections of a spatial and temporal character. It is only after resolving the X that stands here, and comes first, into concept A, and the one that stands there, and comes after, into concept B, that we find ourselves in a position to establish between the two givens a possible connection of cause and effect.
                                                  108
"For the senses – Steiner says – all things are equally essential to the structure of the world": and why are they? Because, for the senses, they are all devoid of qualities. Inasmuch as "merely seen," for example, inasmuch as devoid of quality or of concept, one of those little plaster gnomes with which certain people like to decorate their gardens is worth in each and every respect the same as Michelangelo's Pietà.

What matters most, in any case, is to realize that, by attributing – as Steiner always says – "the predicate of representation" to the percept, one passes off a judgment as a perception, and thus practices, willy-nilly, an "impure perceiving."

The fact is that just as it is necessary to place oneself before the "naked" concept, so it is necessary to place oneself before the "naked" percept. To place ourselves before them in that way, however, it is we who must strip ourselves: that is, we must "withdraw – as the psychoanalysts are wont to say – our projections."

In the case of the percept – we saw a moment ago – "withdrawing our projections" amounts to holding back, within ourselves, that which would otherwise transform it, on the one hand, into a perceptual image (or representation) and, on the other, into sensation.


---


Steiner writes, in fact: "The senses do not tell me at all whether what they transmit to me is real being or mere representation. The world of the senses presents itself to us unexpectedly, and if we wish to have it in its purity, we must refrain from attributing to it any predicate that characterizes it. We can only say: it stands before us, it is given to us: with this, nothing has yet been said about it" (p. 111).

We have already said that the soul ordinarily moves within the narrow space comprised between the perceptual image and representation. Well, this space constitutes, for the soul, a "straitjacket" that does not allow its breath to extend, exhaling, beyond the former, or, inhaling, on this side of the latter.

On the other hand, were it to try to extend it materialistically beyond the perceptual image alone, it would come to suffer the prevalence of exhalation over inhalation, while were it to try to extend it idealistically on this side of representation alone, it would come to suffer the opposite.

Only the science of the spirit, in fact, allows the soul to expand both in the direction of the spirit and in that of the body.

I recall, in this regard, having some time ago met a person who asserted that he did not wish to concern himself too much with the spirit because he cared about his body. What naïveté! Since the body is nothing but the spirit seen from without, and the spirit nothing but the body seen from within, it is in fact impossible to be truly interested in the one without being interested in the other, or truly to know the one without knowing the other.

Steiner writes again: "We take the given as it is: as a multiplicity of… something that will unveil itself of its own accord, if we let ourselves be carried far away from it. We thus have the prospect of arriving at an objective knowledge, because we let the object itself speak. We can hope that the complex which stands before us will unveil to us what we need, if we do not render impossible, through an obstructing prejudice, the free access of its manifestations to our judgment" (p. 112).

As you see, where we have placed an X, Steiner places a "something" preceded by ellipsis dots: in both cases, the intent is to underscore the indeterminate character of the immediate datum of perception.

In any event, the passage of the given from indeterminacy (perceptual) to determination (conceptual) constitutes a process of gradual and progressive unveiling. It is well to remember, however, that this unveiling is possible – as we said at the time – only if the (subjective) psyche does not hinder, with its own cravings or with its own sympathies and antipathies, the union, in the soul, of the force of the percept with the form of the concept.
                                                   109
In the final analysis, the objective re-union of the percept with its concept (with the soul as bridesmaid) is equivalent to a "love match," whereas the subjective union of the percept with another concept (with the psyche as bridesmaid) is equivalent to a "marriage of interest."

L.R.
Rome, 6 February 2001


---


110
The Scientific Works of Goethe (23)

Let us begin reading at once.

Steiner writes: "Thinking is called upon to solve the enigma that perception has posed to us. Light is shed on this relation only if we seek the reason why perceived reality leaves us unsatisfied, while thought reality, on the contrary, satisfies us. Perceptible reality presents itself before us as something finished. It is simply there; we have done nothing to make it so. Therefore we find ourselves confronted by a being that is alien, not produced by us, and at whose production we were not even present (…) It is not so with our thinking. A complex of thoughts does not appear to me without my collaborating in its coming-to-be; it enters the field of my perception only when I lift it out of the dark abyss of imperceptibility. Thinking does not arise in me, like sense perception, as a ready-made and finished picture, but when I grasp it in a completed form, I become aware that it was I myself who brought it to that form" (pp. 112-113).


---
