Well, we can find an analogous distinction between that which, inasmuch as done or accomplished, imposes itself upon man, and that which, by contrast, man does, accomplishes, and posits, in Giambattista Vico (1668-1744).

Listen, in fact, to what Paolo Rossi writes in his introduction to The New Science: "In the pages of the De antiquissima italorum sapientia (a brief text published in Naples in 1710) Vico had formulated his doctrine of the verum-factum. The criterion of truth is not (as the Cartesians wanted) in immediate evidence, nor in the clarity and distinctness of ideas, but, instead, in the conversion of the true with the made. In Latin, Vico had asserted, the words verum and factum stand in a relation of reciprocity; they are synonymous or equivalent terms. This linguistic usage is the residue or the trace of an ancient philosophical truth to which the most ancient Italics had attained: the criterion of the truth of a thing lies in making it. Consequently, full knowledge of reality, the intelligentia, belongs only to God, the creator or maker of the universe, who knows the world precisely because he himself built it. Man's knowledge, on the other hand, has the character of cogitatio; it is a finite, limited, and imperfect knowledge. The world appears to man as an already-built reality, a totality of facts; he does not know, nor can he know, the rules that presided over the construction and ordering of those facts: he therefore cannot penetrate beyond the surface and external appearance of things, and must limit himself to an 'anatomy' of nature. But in mathematics and in geometry, there where man artificially and conventionally constructs entities, and employs them on the basis of rules – themselves also artificially constructed and conventionally held valid – a certain, irrefutable, and rigorous knowledge is possible (…) Mathematics and geometry are not, as Galileo had understood, revealers of the divine language present in nature; they say nothing about the world: they are a product of that singular capacity man has had to draw useful fruits from the constitutive limit of his mind. Around 1720, on the basis of a deepening of this doctrine of his, Vico arrived at a discovery of decisive importance (…) Man, it has been seen, was barred from a full science of the natural and material world; his knowledge seemed confined to the world of mathematics and geometry, condemned to move among fictitious entities and among rules artificially and conventionally established. Vico now expands the criterion of the verum-factum to historical reality, broadens it to include that world which is the work and construction of man. A world composed not of material and external objects (like the natural world), nor of fictitious entities (like the world of mathematics), but of motives, of purposes, of actions, of terrors, of hopes, of languages, of myths, of laws, of civil institutions. In the face of this world man is not a passive spectator: he can know this world from within, given that he himself, the one who knows, is actor and protagonist in that world" (introduction to G. Vico: The New Science – Rizzoli, Milan 1994, pp. 22-23).


---


111
Certainly, Vico is unaware that man can have – as Galileo maintained – "a full science of the natural and material world," or of the reality "already built" and "made," only insofar as he makes use, for that purpose, of his own physical body (in particular, of his own neuro-sensory system): that is, of that which in him too is "natural and material," or reality "already built" and "made."

What matters to note, however, is that just as Vico asserts that man can know the world of history "from within," because he is not a "passive spectator" of it but rather its "actor and protagonist," so Steiner asserts that man can know, in the same way and for the same reasons, the world of thinking: "a complex of thoughts – he says, in fact – does not appear to me without my collaborating in its coming-to-be; it enters the field of my perception only when I lift it out of the dark abyss of imperceptibility. Thinking does not arise in me, like sense perception, as a ready-made and finished picture, but when I grasp it in a completed form, I become aware that it was I myself who brought it to that form."

And he continues: "And this is what I must demand of every thing that enters the field of my perception, if I wish to understand it: nothing of it must remain obscure to me; nothing must appear to me as something finished; I myself must be able to follow it up to the point at which it became such. Therefore the immediate form of reality, which we are accustomed to call experience, urges us toward a scientific elaboration. When we set our thinking in motion, we ascend to those conditions of the given that at first remained hidden from us; we lift ourselves actively from the product to the production, until sensory perception has become transparent to us in the same way as thinking" (p. 113).

We could also say that, by setting "our thinking in motion," we reason, and that it is the I itself that, in reasoning, moves. We feel, in fact, that reasoning, when it is not the I that moves it, translates outright into "unreasoning."

Remember, for example, the algebraic expressions we used to do in school? Was it not enough for a moment of distraction to get a calculation wrong? And what is distraction? Precisely a sign of the (obscuring) absence of the I, just as attention and concentration are, conversely, signs of its (illuminating) presence.

Steiner says: "When we set our thinking in motion, we ascend to those conditions of the given that at first remained hidden from us; we lift ourselves actively from the product to the production, until sensory perception has become transparent to us in the same way as thinking." Which means that "when we set our thinking in motion, we lift ourselves actively" from the percept (from the "product") to the concept (to the "production"), until the former has become transparent to us in the same way as the latter. Only the transparency of thinking can, in fact, render perception transparent or luminous.


---


Steiner continues: "A process of the world appears to us as totally penetrated only if it is our own activity. A thought appears as the conclusion of a process within which we ourselves stand. Now, thinking is the only process into which we can insert ourselves totally, in which we can immerse ourselves fully. Therefore, to scientific consideration, experienced reality must appear as springing from the development of thinking in the same way as a pure thought. To investigate the essence of a thing means to start from the center of the world of thinking and to work from there until a formation of thought arises before the soul that appears to us identical with the experienced object" (p. 113).


---


Boncinelli maintains that "the fundamental entities that characterize and regulate the phenomena of the physical universe, independently of whether these are animate or inanimate objects, are three: matter, energy, and information" (The Brain, the Mind, and the Soul – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 11).

Animate and inanimate objects are therefore made not only of matter and energy, but also of information. Agreed, but since an "information" is (in itself) nothing other than "thought," why not say, then, that animate and inanimate things are made also of thought, and that man, in order to know them, must therefore bring the thought that is in him into encounter with the thought that is in things? Steiner says precisely that "to investigate the essence of a thing means to start from the center of the world of thinking," that is to say, from the I, "and to work from there," that is, to think, "until a formation of thought arises before the soul," that is, a concept, "that appears to us identical with the experienced object," that is, with the percept. (Not to mention that in the first of his Maxims one reads: "Anthroposophy is a path of knowledge that would lead the spiritual in man to the spiritual in the universe" – Anthroposophical Maxims – Antroposofica, Milan 1969, p. 15).

As you see, through the concept of "information" there is a tendency to obscure and conceal that of the "concept." Hegel observes precisely: "Certainly, in modern times, no concept has fared so badly as the concept itself, the concept in and for itself" (Aesthetics – Einaudi, Turin 1997, vol. I, p. 107).

In "modern times," however, together with that of the concept, both the concept of thinking and that of the I have "fared badly": and it could not have been otherwise, since the concept arises through thinking, and thinking is an activity of the I (an "activity of our own").

Certainly, in order to carry this activity forward we must force our way through a dense host or legion of illusory and deceptive forces. "One can never guard oneself enough – Goethe warns, in fact – against drawing hasty conclusions from experiments; for it is precisely at the passage from experience to judgment, from knowledge to application, that, as at a defile, all the secret enemies of man lie in ambush; imagination, impatience, haste, arrogance, obstinacy, frame of mind, preconceptions, laziness, frivolity, fickleness, or whatever else these enemies with all their retinue may be called, lie in wait for us at the pass, and unexpectedly overwhelm both the active man of the world and the calm scholar apparently free of passions" (The Experiment as Mediator Between Object and Subject, in Works – Sansoni, Florence 1961, vol. V, p. 31).

Let us keep in mind, in any case, that from the spiritual point of view, the truthfulness of the effort made is worth more than any result obtained; not only that, but just as it is true that "all that glitters is not gold," so it is true that not everything that presents itself as "thought" is thought.

Whoever, by observing it, learns to know real thinking, also learns to distinguish it from apparent thinking: that is, from that thinking (called "dialectical" by Scaligero) which traffics not with thoughts (with concepts), but with words.

Steiner observes precisely: "The greater part of what in ordinary life is called 'thinking' takes place in words; one thinks 'in words.' Much more than is believed, one thinks in words. And many, when they ask for an explanation of this or that, are content to be given any word at all, of which they know the sound and which awakens in them some memory; they then take for explanation what they feel with such a word, and believe they possess the 'thought'" (Cosmic Thinking – Basaia, Rome 1985, p. 3).

This holds, first of all, for those who concern themselves with the science of the spirit. The depth and richness of this science (defined by Steiner as "a high school of thinking") demand, in fact, serious and demanding study. "Not only for the esotericist proper – Steiner emphasizes, in fact – but also for whoever wishes to receive anthroposophical thoughts into his soul forces, it will be important to come to know something of the changes that the entire human entity experiences, whether a man performs exercises like those indicated in my writing Initiation, or as they are briefly summarized in the second part of my Occult Science, or also whether he simply, but with heart and soul, takes in anthroposophical thoughts. Anthroposophy, cultivated esoterically or exoterically and with seriousness, brings about in reality certain changes in the total organization of man. Through anthroposophy (this can be asserted with courage) one becomes different; one's entire human structure is transformed" (The Occult Development of Man in His Four Constituent Parts – Antroposofica, Milan 1986, p. 7).
                                                  113
To take in anthroposophical thoughts "with heart and soul," and "with seriousness," means, however, to study, and not simply to read, to listen, to attend groups, or to give oneself over to one of the many activities, more or less artistic, that appeal to anthroposophy.

Study is "study": that is to say, toil, effort, constancy, tenacity, interest and love, and not a hobby or a pastime. Whoever studies never tires or grows bored, for example, of returning to reflect upon the same things. Steiner observes, in fact: "We must not find any book too difficult. That would simply mean being too lazy to think. The best books are those that one must take up and take up again many times, that are not understood at once, that must be studied phrase by phrase. In study, what matters is not so much what one studies as how one studies" (Initiation and Mysteries – Rocco, Naples 1953, pp. 120-121).

I do not know whether you know, in this regard, that Steiner, to a person who had asked him to recommend a good "occult" book, advised studying Fichte's Doctrine of Science (Laterza, Rome-Bari 1993). I, for my part, happen to recommend (in my own small way) Hegel's Science of Logic (Laterza, Rome-Bari 1974), but it is the same thing: these are two works that do not lend themselves to being "read," but upon which it is necessary, rather, to "break one's head."

Steiner, moreover, wrote in the preface to the third edition of his Theosophy: "The way one is accustomed to reading in our times will not do for this book. In a certain sense, every page, often even a few sentences, must be conquered with effort. This has been consciously aimed at. For only so can the book become for the reader what it must be for him. Whoever merely skims it will not have read it at all" (Theosophy – Antroposofica, Milan 1957, p. 1).

It is well to keep this in mind, since, generally, those who say they love the rigor of scientific thinking disdain occultism, while those who say they love occultism disdain the rigor of scientific thinking, preferring perhaps the reading of works of "popularization" or of those many texts intended to gratify curiosity or the desire for those suggestions that derive from poking one's nose into the world of the so-called "mysteries."

On this precise point (and again in the preface to the third edition of his Theosophy) Steiner writes: "My style is not fashioned in such a way that my subjective feelings show through in the sentences. While I write, I deaden what rises from inner warmth and from the depths of feeling into a dry, mathematical style. But only this style can be an awakener; for the reader must arouse in himself the warmth and the feeling; he cannot let them be simply poured into him by the author, in a state of dimmed consciousness" (ibid., p. 5).

An intent that, in one of the prefaces to Occult Science (that of 1925), he reaffirms thus: "Quite consciously I aimed not to make a 'popular' exposition, but rather an exposition that would make it necessary to penetrate into the content with a right effort of thought. I thus impressed upon my books a character such that the very reading of them is the beginning of a school of the spirit, because the calm and deliberate effort of thought made necessary by the reading invigorates the forces of the soul and thus renders them capable of approaching the spiritual world" (Occult Science in Its General Outline – Antroposofica, Milan 1969, p. 10).

And further on, in the text, we read: "Whoever aspires to a science of the spirit can do nothing but simply set forth what he believes he can say. Only those who, refraining from any arbitrary verdict, know how to listen to his communications concerning the 'manifest mysteries" (Goethe – ed.) of the world, will be able to judge whether this aspiration is justified. It will be his task, moreover, to show how the results of his investigations fit into the remaining acquisitions of knowledge and of life, what oppositions prove possible, and what confirmations the immediate outer reality of life offers to his observations. He, however, should never give his exposition a character such that rhetorical skill takes the place of the efficacy of the content itself" (ibid., pp. 33-34).


---


L.R.
Rome, 13 February 2001
                                                   114
The Scientific Works of Goethe (24)

As we saw last time, Steiner asserts that "thinking is called upon to solve the enigma that perception has posed to us"; perception, in fact, gives us a content (the percept) that imposes itself, as pre-existing, upon our experience, whereas thinking gives us a content (the concept) that it is we who must seek and posit.

If one wished, one could compare the percept to one of those aircraft that during the war flew over the cities. The anti-aircraft battery became aware of its presence from the drone of the engines, but, in order to locate it, it had to project a beam of light (thinking) toward the sky; only after illuminating it could it, in fact, identify it (conceptualize it), and thus recognize it as friend or as enemy, as harmless or as threatening.

The image is crude, but apt. Certainly, one thing is to bathe an airplane in light, another to bathe an idea in light: in this case, in fact, the nature of the means of seeking is not dissimilar from that of the object sought, since it is precisely the light of human thinking that intercepts the light of the cosmic idea.

When, presently, we hear Steiner say that the idea is "built upon itself" and "strongly founded in itself" (precisely like a star that shines with its own light), it will therefore be well to remember that light, while illuminating (conceiving) the illuminable (the conceivable), also illuminates (conceives) itself: that is, that the illuminating is always illuminated, whereas the illuminated is not always illuminating.

But let us take up again from where we had left off.

Steiner writes: "If we speak of the essence of an object, or in general of the essence of the world, it can therefore be a question of nothing other than grasping reality as thought, as idea" (pp. 113-114).

Here is realism! We have said it and repeated it: so-called "naïve realism" is at fault, not for being "realism," but for being precisely "naïve," "primitive," or "dogmatic": it is at fault, that is, for being an uncritical realism of things (of what we perceive by virtue of the physical senses), and not a critical realism of ideas (of what we perceive by virtue of the spiritual senses).

In order to conquer this latter, and thus to go beyond both realism (naïve) and anti-realism (in Searle's sense), it is, however, necessary to cease projecting the luminous reality of ideas onto matter (like the naïve realists), onto energy (like the metaphysical realists), or onto information (like the information-age realists).

It is a question, in other words, of overcoming "nominalism" (the basis of materialism), not by regressing to medieval realism, that is, to the realism of the rational or affective soul, but rather by advancing from the realism of things, which (as the pendant of nominalism) characterizes the first (natural-scientific) phase of development of the consciousness soul, to the realism of ideas, which characterizes its second (spiritual-scientific) phase.


---


Steiner continues: "In the idea we recognize that quid from which we must derive everything else: the principle of things. What the philosophers call the absolute, eternal being, the ground of the world, what the religions call God, we, on the basis of our theoretical-epistemological examination, call: the idea (…) No other form of existence can satisfy us, except the one that derives from the idea. Nothing can remain excluded; everything must become a part of the great whole that the idea embraces. The idea, for its part, does not at all ask to be transcended. It is the entity built upon itself, strongly founded in itself" (p. 114).


---


Think of the times when men, to have an answer to their most important questions, used to consult the oracles. Well, thinking too is a kind of oracle, inasmuch as every time we perceive something (an X) we end up (more or less advertently) questioning it ("What is this?"; "What is that?"; "What does this mean?"; "What does that mean?"; etc.), expecting that sooner or later it will answer us and "unveil the mystery" for us.

Already the simple assertion of, say, "I am hungry," or "I am thirsty," amounts to determining (conceptually) the indeterminate perception of a state of need or discomfort in us. For that state would indeed not be removed if, when we were hungry, someone gave us drink, or if, when we were thirsty, someone gave us food.

Moreover, when we go to the doctor complaining of some malaise, do we not expect a diagnosis? And what is a "diagnosis," if not the (conceptual) determination of what, for us, is only a perception or a painful sensation?

In order to understand what the body and the soul need, we must therefore question thinking (the spirit), whereas thinking, in order to answer us, need question nothing but itself.

Just today it happened to me, at the newsstand, to read on the cover of a weekly this headline: In the liver there is a brain. But it is not that in the liver "there is a brain": it is that the liver, the heart, the lungs, the kidneys, and all the other organs of the body are "brain," since they are intelligence or, more precisely, intelligent beings.

This will seem less strange to us if we keep in mind that the intelligibles (this is what Aristotle calls the "entities" in the De anima) are such only insofar as they are intelligent, and that behind every intelligence there is an intelligent subject: for example, that (collective) subject we are accustomed to call a "species."

We said, last time, that by means of the concept of "information" the aim is to obscure those of thinking, of the concept itself, and of the I. For Boncinelli, for example, living beings, feeding more on information than on energy, would be "veritable informivores" (The Brain, the Mind, and the Soul – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 53).

Yes, but in order to feed on information, and in order therefore to be an "informivore," it is necessary to inform oneself: that is, it is necessary to carry out an activity that stands to information as metabolic activity stands to food. But what other activity (of the subject) could metabolize extrasensible foods (the informations), if not that of thinking?

The fact is that certain truths press so hard to be received and recognized by modern consciousness that present-day materialistic science, no longer able to reject them, can do nothing but de-form them or disguise them, so as not to see itself compelled to change itself.

But let us return to our subject.
                                                115


---


Steiner writes: "This seems to contradict what was said above, namely that the idea appears to us in a form satisfactory to us because we cooperate actively in its coming-to-be. But this fact does not derive from the organization of our consciousness. If the idea were not an entity founded in itself, such a consciousness could not even be had. If a thing does not have in itself, but outside itself, the center from which it springs, then I cannot, when it presents itself to me, declare myself satisfied with it, but must go beyond the thing; I must, precisely, arrive at that center. Only if I come upon a quid that does not lead me beyond itself do I attain the consciousness of now standing in the center, and of being able to stop there. My awareness of standing within a thing is only the consequence of its objective quality of carrying its own principle with it. In taking possession of the idea, we arrive at the nucleus of the world. Here we grasp that from which everything springs. We become a unity with this principle; therefore the idea, which is the most objective quid, appears to us at the same time as the most subjective" (p. 114).

Nevertheless, to consider it contradictory to assert that the idea is a being in itself, but that it presents itself to consciousness (as idea) only where the subject thinks it, is a bit like considering it contradictory to assert, say, that the actor is a being in himself, but that he appears on the stage (as actor) only where the audience desires him.


---


116
Try, in any case, to reflect on the relation prevailing among the so-called "elements." Is it not "air" that transforms itself into "water"? And is it not "water" that transforms itself into "earth"? Well, if one keeps in mind that the idea is related to the element "air," and that thinking is related to the element "water," what is one to conclude? It is simple: that just as water is a descending metamorphosis of air, and air an ascending metamorphosis of water, so thinking is a descending metamorphosis of the idea, and the idea an ascending metamorphosis of thinking.

Thinking and the idea are therefore two homogeneous realities or, better said, two different states, grades, levels, or moments of the manifesting or the becoming of the I.

One can, in fact, have the (imaginative) self-experience of the I in the force of thinking, just as one can have, at a higher level, the (inspirative) self-experience of the I in the form of the idea.

"Ideas – Steiner says – are for anthroposophy the vessels of love" (The Formation of Community – Antroposofica, Milan 1992, p. 12). Inasmuch as they are precisely "vessels" (vases or cups), they fill themselves with the force of love of the I, conferring upon it now the form A, now form B, now form C, and so on. In filling them, the I fecundates them, thus allowing them to generate. It is precisely for this reason that Goethe, in Faust, speaks of the realm of ideas as the "realm of the Mothers."

The world of forms is the world of essences or – as Florensky likes to say – the "noumenal world"; and what matters "for knowledge – Steiner observes precisely – is that one grasp the form, because it is the form, not matter, that gives things their essential being" (Philosophy and Anthroposophy – Antroposofica, Milan 1980, p. 22).

In order to fill, saturate, and fecundate the forms, the I must naturally go out of itself (out of its own being-potency) and actualize itself: and it is able to do so because it must be conceived both as "being," that is, as a subject at rest, and as "becoming," that is, as a subject in movement.

In the idea the light of love manifests itself. "The eye – Goethe observes, however – owes its existence to light. Out of indifferent subsidiary animal organs, light calls into being an organ that becomes akin to it; the eye is formed by the light, for the light, so that the inner light may move to meet the outer" (Theory of Colors, in Works – Sansoni, Florence 1961, vol. V, p. 298).

Thus, without the eye we would not have light, but without light we would not have the eye. This holds, however (and all the more so), also for the relation of thinking to the idea, and of the idea to the I; paraphrasing, we could therefore say: "Thinking is formed by the idea, for the idea, just as the idea is formed by the I, for the I."

Steiner writes: "Sensory reality appears so enigmatic to us precisely because we do not find in it its center; and it ceases to be enigmatic when we recognize that it has a center which is the same center of the world of thinking that manifests itself in us" (pp. 114-115).


---


Observe, for example, my hands, placed one facing the other. If we attributed to the right one the role of the subject (of man), and to the left one that of the object (of the world), it would not be difficult to imagine that the former, in perceiving the latter, would judge it as other than itself, perhaps proceeding to ask itself what the ground of such a "not-I" might be.

Well, the right hand, if it truly wished to answer such a question, would have to stop at appearances no longer, but penetrate into the left hand and, ascending along the arm, discover that ground in the trunk (in the heart). But what would it discover if it then asked itself what its own ground is, and, to answer itself, ascended through the arm up to the trunk (to the heart)? It would discover the same thing: it would discover, that is, that the ground of the subject (of man) coincides with that of the object (of the world).

When we join our hands in prayer, it is therefore as if we were (symbolically) welding shut that ring of (natural) being which was broken in order that consciousness might be born. For this to arise, the being (of man) had in fact to separate itself from the being (of the world): that is, such a fracture or opposition had to be created as would allow one part of being (that
                                                  117
of man) to encounter the other (that of the world) outside itself, so as to be unable to recognize it immediately (in perceiving it) as part of itself.

In the same way in which, in Parsifal, the wound of Amfortas can be healed only by the spear that made it, so the wound of consciousness can be healed only by the consciousness that made it. Spiritual consciousness is, in fact, called upon to heal material consciousness, welding shut the ring of being, and thus restoring the world to man and man to the world.

Steiner continues: "The idea is one and the same in all places of the world, in all consciousnesses (…) The ideal content of the world is founded in itself and perfect in itself. We do not generate it; we only seek to grasp it. Thinking does not produce it; it perceives it. For our thinking is not a producer, but an organ of perception. Just as different eyes see one and the same object, so different consciousnesses think one and the same content of thought. The many consciousnesses think the same thing, but they approach it from different sides; hence it appears to them variously modified (…) The diversity of human opinions is explicable in the same way as the diversity that a landscape presents to two observers situated in different places" (p. 115).


---


Just as a tree is a tree, and remains such even when observed from different points of view, so an idea is an idea, and remains such even when different opinions are held of it. We believe (naïvely) that we have different opinions about things, but we have instead different opinions about ideas.

That the idea is one thing, and consciousness of the idea another, must not, however, be believed, but experienced or realized: that is, we must become aware of it, become conscious of it.

With reality, on the other hand, this is the only thing one can do. Bertrand Russell (1872-1970) used to say (if I remember rightly) that no one could, by logic, convince others of the existence of whales. It is true: the only thing to do, in fact, would be to take them to see them.

But how can one go and see the whales if no one speaks of them, or indicates the road to follow to arrive where they live? It is clear, in fact, that by going, say, to the Sahara, it will be improbable to see any whales. Nevertheless, those who wish to convince others that whales do not exist give precisely this advice: namely, to go to the Sahara to ascertain that not even one whale is to be found there.

In speaking of whales, we are obviously speaking of ideas. We cannot, by logic, convince anyone of their existence, but we can help anyone who so wishes – through the study and practice of the science of the spirit – to seek them out and discover them.

But whoever discovers the reality of the idea also discovers that a single (objective) idea can give rise to an infinite series of (subjective) representations.

If, for example, the idea of "justice" did not exist, no one could, by grasping some particular aspect of it, form an opinion of it. One thing, however, is to be conscious of this; another is to believe that only opinions about "justice" exist, and not the idea of "justice."

Steiner writes: "So long as one is capable of penetrating to the world of ideas, one can be sure that, in the end, one will have a world of ideas common to all men" (p. 115).


---


Take care: having "a world of ideas in common" does not mean all thinking alike: were that the case, there would be not unity, brotherhood, or morality, but uniformity, conformity, or conformism.

As we have just said, it is rather a good that each person receive his own "part of the idea," as part of his own mission or destiny. If one wished, one could compare this "part of the idea" to one of the instruments that make up an orchestra. The instruments are many and diverse, but it is precisely by each one performing its own part that they concur, under the guidance of a conductor (in this case, the idea), in realizing a harmony. It is therefore a good that, of one and the same idea, subject
                                                 118
A should have part a, subject B part b, subject C part c, and so on, because harmony (the fraternitas) is born, yes, from the concurrence of diverse elements, but of elements that are at the same time recognized as parts of a whole that unifies and transcends them. Where, however, that whole (the idea) is removed or denied, it is then projected onto one's own part (onto one's own opinion), thereby placing it in conflict with the others. Part a, believing itself to be a "whole," will begin, for example, to deny part b its right to exist, or vice versa.

I recall, in this regard, an acquaintance of mine, a Marxist, who one evening said to me: "You know, I'm beginning to think that everything depends on sexuality, not on economics." Which goes to show that the whole he previously projected onto economics he was now beginning to project onto sexuality: he was not, in short, withdrawing that projection, but merely changing its object. It is not, in fact, that sexuality or economics is the whole; it is that within the whole there are both sexuality and economics, and that one would therefore have to discover what part they play there (as, moreover, all other human activities do).

It is in this way, in any case, that what Steiner calls "the war of all against all" begins: that is to say, the war of egos against egos.

For who, failing to discover, beyond opinion, the idea, could discover, beyond the ego, the I, and, within the I, the Logos (that is, the being or the "representative" of all humanity)?

Steiner says: "So long as one is capable of penetrating to the world of ideas." Now, in the epoch of the rational or affective soul, "philosophy" was considered the scientia scientiarum, whereas in the epoch of the consciousness soul, "epistemology" must be considered such. Provided, however, that this be not only theoretical but also experimental. As we have already said, however, we cannot consider "experimental" an epistemology which, instead of investigating the life of thinking, investigates – as today's neurosciences do – that of the brain.

What, in fact, should a scientia scientiarum give us? It is soon said: the possibility of arriving at a point of view that harmonizes, inasmuch as it is superior, what is found at a lower level, thus allowing us to appreciate its multiplicity and variety.


---


Steiner writes precisely: "What task, then, has the theory of knowledge accomplished in comparison with the other sciences? It has shed light for us on the aim and task of all science; it has shown us the significance of the content of the individual sciences. Our theory of knowledge is the science of the determination of all the other sciences. It has revealed how what has been conquered in the individual sciences is the objective ground of universal existence (…) Thus the theory of knowledge is, at the same time, the doctrine of the significance and mission of man (…) Our spirit has the task of educating itself in such a way as to put itself in a position to draw from all the reality given to it that aspect of it which proves to spring from the idea" (pp. 116 and 117).

A few lines after these passages, Steiner recalls that Goethe spoke of his "inner working" as a "living heuristic."

But a "living heuristic" is in the end nothing other than a "living explanation." For a "living explanation" to be possible, one must therefore live. But just as – according to the adage – "he who has bread has no teeth, and he who has teeth has no bread," so – according to what experience dictates – he who lives does not reflect, and he who reflects does not live. We live, in fact, dreaming (in feeling) and sleeping (in willing), and we die instead reflecting (a posteriori, in reflected or representational thinking).

If yesterday, say, I had fallen prey to anger, today, reflecting upon it, I would surely be repentant. And it is right that it be so. But would it not have been even better if I had been able to reflect at the very moment the anger was on the point of invading my soul? What happened instead? That yesterday, when the anger was in me, I was outside myself, whereas today, when I am in myself, the anger is outside me. How, then, is one to know it and transform it, if when it is present I am absent, and if when I am
                                                  119
present it is absent? (See, in this regard, The Mission of Anger, in R. Steiner: Metamorphoses of the Life of the Soul – Tilopa, Rome 1984).

This means, from a general point of view, that one can dispose of a "living heuristic" only if one disposes of a living thinking: that is, a thinking that is equal to life, and not, like ordinary thinking, equal only to death.

L.R.
Rome, 20 February 2001

120
The Scientific Works of Goethe (25-26)

Note: on 27 February 2001, in the course of the 25th meeting, the article by Francesco Giorgi, Mad Cow Disease and GMOs: A Look at the Daily Press, with which we had inaugurated, on 20 February 2001, the activity of our "Scientific-Spiritual Observatory," was read, commented upon, and discussed. We therefore resume the study of the Scientific Works of Goethe with the 26th meeting, of 13 March 2001.


---


Tonight we shall begin the ninth chapter, entitled: Knowing and Acting in the Light of Goethean Thinking, which is divided into five sections: Methodology; Dogmatic Method and Immanent Method; System of Science; On the Limits of Knowledge and the Formation of Hypotheses; Ethical and Historical Sciences. Let us therefore start with the first (Methodology).

Steiner writes: "We have determined the relation between the ideal world acquired through scientific thinking and immediately given experience. We have learned to know the beginning and the end of a process: experience devoid of ideas, and understanding of reality permeated by ideas. Between the two stands human activity. Man must, through his own activity, make the end spring forth from the beginning. The way in which he does this constitutes the method" (p. 119).


---


Let close attention be paid here, because the "beginning" of the process of knowledge is constituted by perception (by the percept), while the "end" is constituted (ordinarily) by representation, not by the concept.

In our examples, we have called the percept X, the concept A, and expressed the so-called "perceptual judgment" with the formula "X is A." For a (conscious) representation to be given at the "end" of the process of knowledge, however, a judgment (a relation between concepts) does not suffice: a syllogism (a relation between judgments) is required.

The syllogism – Hegel observes precisely – "is the essential reason of all truth; and the definition of the absolute is now this: that the absolute is the syllogism, or, expressing this definition in a proposition: everything is a syllogism" (Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences – Laterza, Rome-Bari, 1989, p. 174).

Upon a more careful and subtle analysis, the perceptual judgment "X is A" turns out, in fact, to be the "major premise" of a syllogism (if "X is A," and if "A is X," then "A is A") of which only the "conclusion," inasmuch as it is precisely a "representation" ("A is A"), reaches ordinary consciousness: the premise, that is, of an unconscious reasoning that arrives at a conscious conclusion. (Consult, on this precise point, the lecture of 30 August 1919, in R. Steiner: The Art of Education, vol. I, Anthropology – Antroposofica, Milan 1993.)

Steiner says that "man must, through his own activity, make the end spring forth from the beginning": that is to say, the representation from the percept. Man is situated, in fact, between the percept and the concept, which are – as we have said many times – "world," and he is called, after having divided them, to reunite them. The distinction between percept and concept does not, therefore, concern things, but rather the way in which man is organized to know them.

The art of man is therefore that of transforming, on the noetic plane, things into ideas and, on the ethical plane, ideas into things. But art is method and method is art: the ideas into which he must transform things must, in fact, be the ideas of the things (their objective essences), and not his own (subjective) ideas. Schelling rightly observes: "It is not a question of what opinion must be assumed about the phenomenon, so that it, made to conform to whatever philosophy, may be easily explained, but vice versa, of what philosophy is required, so that, grown together with the object, it may be equal to it. Not how the phenomenon must be
                                                  121
twisted about, made one-sided, reduced, so that it may in any case be justifiable from the principles we have once and for all resolved not to transgress, but rather: to what extent our thoughts must be broadened, so as to be in relation with the phenomenon" (Philosophy of Mythology – Mursia, Milan 1993, p. 8).


---


Steiner writes: "The method must therefore consist in answering, before every object, the question: 'What part has it in the unitary world of ideas; what place does it take in the ideal picture I form for myself of the world?' When I have recognized this, when I have seen how a thing links itself to my ideas, then my need for knowledge is satisfied. For this need of mine only one thing is unsatisfying: that an object should come toward me that in no way and in no place wishes to connect itself to my view of the world. What must therefore be overcome is the ideal discomfort arising from the fact that there exists something of which I should have to say to myself: 'I see it; it is here; when I approach it, it stares at me like a question mark; but nowhere in the harmony of my thoughts do I find the point where I could insert it; the questions I must put to it remain unanswered, however I may turn and turn my system of ideas.' From this we see what we require with regard to every object. When it presents itself to me, it stares at me as a single thing. In me, the world of thinking presses toward that point where the concept of that object lies" (pp. 119-120).

When the object "stares at us as a single thing," we have perception. Remember: "I know that something exists here and now, but I do not know what the thing that exists is." Well, this is the moment in which "the world of thinking presses – as Steiner says – toward that point where the concept of that object lies": where, that is, its essence or quality lies. Which is to say that "the world of thinking presses toward that point where" the form of that force lies.

Since at the origin (in the entelechy) force is form and form is force, and since it is man who has separated them, force "presses" toward its form and form "presses" toward its force. They cannot, however, reunite (in the human soul) by themselves: they must await the one who divided them to do so.

The fact is that the world is One or a Whole, and that the singular character of things derives solely from the act of perceiving. The so-called "atomists," for example, believe that "in the beginning were the elements" only insofar as they are unaware that these are born from (sensory) perception, and hence from an activity that precisely analyzes or anatomizes the Whole.

On the other hand, if we did not enjoy, in perceiving, the faculty of isolating the elements, the Whole would remain unconscious for us, just as it is for animals, plants, and minerals. In order that the natural Whole may be changed into a spiritual Whole, it is therefore necessary – to put it in alchemical terms – first to solve the natural unity (the a priori unity) in order then to coagulate the spiritual unity (the a posteriori unity).


---


Steiner writes: "All the progress of science lies in discerning the point where any phenomenon whatsoever can insert itself into the harmony of the world of thinking. This must not be misunderstood. It cannot be taken to mean that every phenomenon must be explicable by means of the concepts already in use, that our world of thought is already closed and all new experience must coincide with some concept we already possess. That pressing of the world of thoughts can also direct itself toward a point not yet ever thought by a human being. And the ideal progress of the history of science rests precisely on the fact that thinking casts up to the surface new configurations of thought" (p. 120).

In what, in fact, does the scientific spirit consist, if not in the will to direct the world of thoughts "toward a point not yet ever thought by a human being"? And in what, on the other hand, does the dogmatic spirit consist, if not in the will to explain every phenomenon or "all new experience" by means of the concepts already possessed or "already in use"?
                                                  122
Think, for example, of homeopathic remedies. Whoever uses them knows well how effective they are. Yet no one has so far succeeded in explaining why they are, even though, to do so, it would suffice to direct thinking "toward a point" (the etheric world) that is not, indeed, one "not yet ever thought by a human being," but one thought by Goethe and then taken up, reworked, and deepened by Steiner. But this the homeopaths do not do, for they attempt to explain the phenomenon with the concepts "already in use"; nor do the allopaths or, more precisely, the members of the so-called "scientific community," who, availing themselves precisely of the fact that the concepts "already in use" do not explain the phenomenon, attempt to deny it, asserting that such remedies do not work, and cannot work, because they are "plain water."

And to think (if I remember rightly) that the celebrated mathematician Francesco Severi (1879-1961) declared that he had seen a thousand theories fall before a fact, but had never seen a fact fall before a thousand theories.


---


Steiner continues: "The scientific method consists in indicating the connection between the concept of a single phenomenon and the remaining world of ideas. We call this process: deduction of the concept (demonstration). And all scientific thinking consists only in our discovering the transitions existing from concept to concept, and in making the one spring forth from the other. Scientific method is the going and coming from one concept to another of thinking in movement. One will say that this is the old story of the correspondence between the world of concepts and the world of experience, that one would have to presuppose, outside us, the world (the trans-subjective) corresponding to our world of concepts, if we were to believe that the going and coming from one concept to another led to a picture of reality. But this is nothing but a misunderstanding of the relation between the single phenomenon and the concept. When I place myself before a phenomenon of the world of experience, I do not yet know at all what it is. Only after I have overcome it, after its concept has arisen luminously in me, do I know what I have before me. But this does not mean that that single phenomenon and the concept are two different things. No, they are the same; and what comes toward me in the particular is nothing other than the concept" (pp. 120-121).

Whoever thought, at this point, that "this is the old story of the correspondence between the world of concepts and the world of experience," that is, the story of the "pre-established harmony" of Leibnizian memory (Gottfried Wilhelm von Leibniz, 1646-1716), would have understood nothing at all. Above all, he would not have understood – as Steiner says – that "what comes toward me in the particular is nothing other than the concept," or – as we have said and repeated – that the percept and the concept are not "two different things," but one and the same thing (the entelechy) apprehended by two different paths: as percept, by means of perceiving (willing); as concept, by means of thinking. He would not have understood, in short, that the percept is the perceived concept, while the concept is the thought percept.

There does not, therefore, exist a "pre-established harmony" between the objective percept and the subjective concept; rather, there exists, so to speak, an objective "pre-established unity" (pre-existing, in fact, before knowing) of percept and concept: a unity which the human cognitive organization first transforms into duality, in order then to reconstitute it as a unity, at a new and higher level (an "octave higher," a musician might say).

Steiner writes: "Every single phenomenon of reality represents, within the system of thinking, a determinate content. It is founded in the totality of the ideal world and can be understood only in connection with it. Thus every object must necessarily summon us to a double work of thinking. First of all, the thought that corresponds to it must be fixed in sharp outlines, after which all the threads that lead from it to the overall world of thinking must be followed. Clarification in the particular and deepening in the whole are the two most important demands of reality. The first belongs to the intellect, the second to reason" (p. 121).


---


123
We emphasized, at the time, that the object divides itself into a number of stimuli equal to that of the senses engaged by the subject in perceiving it. Object A, for example, transforms itself into stimulus a′ for sight, stimulus a″ for hearing, that one a‴ for touch, and so on.

The unity of the single object thus comes to be articulated into a plurality of stimuli which the intellect, in order to give us consciousness of the original reality of A, is called upon to reunify.

We have, therefore, an intellect (Verstand) that transforms into unity the plurality of sensory stimuli (that synthesizes what has been analyzed by perception) and, at a higher level, a reason (Vernunft) that transforms into unity the plurality of concepts (that synthesizes what has been analyzed by the intellect).

Steiner says that "first of all, the thought that corresponds" to the (perceived) object "must be fixed in sharp outlines." For who could claim to have the whole "clear and distinct" without first having the elements "clear and distinct"?

"It is painful to observe – Hegel remarks in this regard – how tasteless, unprincipled insipience and quackery, incapable of fixing thought on abstract propositions taken singly, still less on their interconnection, now posture as freedom and tolerance of thinking, now as genius" (Phenomenology of Spirit – La Nuova Italia, Scandicci (Fi) 1996, p. 41).

Steiner writes: "The intellect creates figures of thought for the single objects of reality, and answers to its task all the better the more precisely it delimits them, the more sharply it marks their boundaries" (p. 121).

It may perhaps be worth recalling that it is by virtue of the "principle of non-contradiction" (A is not not-A), of the "principle of identity" (A is A), and of the "principle of the excluded middle" (tertium non datur: between A and B no "third" or "middle" is given) that the intellect succeeds in delimiting those (qualitative) spaces that prevent the concepts from becoming confused with one another.

Steiner continues: "Reason, then, has to insert such figures into the overall harmony of the world of ideas. This presupposes, naturally, the following: in the content of the figures of thought that the intellect creates, that unity is already present; the unitary life common to all of them is already alive in them; only, the intellect holds them artificially separate; reason, on the other hand, without canceling clarity, abolishes the separation. The intellect takes us away from reality; reason leads us back to it" (p. 121).


---


That in the content of the figures of thought created by the intellect (in the content of the concepts) there already lives "the unitary and common life" (of the ideas) is shown also by the fact – as I took care to emphasize a moment ago – that the intellect and reason have in common that synthetic vis applied, by the former, to the plurality of (sensory) stimuli and, by the latter, to the plurality of concepts.

The concept is, in effect, a kind of "two-faced Janus": thanks to the face it turns toward the world of perception, we succeed in synthesizing the (sensory) stimuli and fixing them in individual concepts; thanks to the one it turns toward the world of reason, we succeed, instead, in setting the individual concepts in movement and synthesizing them in the idea.

Hegel writes in this regard: "Thinking that produces finite determinations and that moves within them is called intellect (in the most proper sense of the word)" (Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences, p. 39); and he adds: "The activity of separating is the force and the work of the intellect, of the most wondrous and greatest power, or rather of the absolute power. The circle that rests closed within itself (what we called, last time, 'the ring of natural being' – ed.) and that holds, as substance, its moments, is the immediate relation, which therefore arouses no wonder at all. But that the accidental as such, separated from its own sphere, that what is bound and real only in its connection with something else, should gain a determinate existence of its own and a distinct freedom of its own – all this is the immense
                                                   124
power of the negative; it is the energy of thinking, of the pure I" (Phenomenology of Spirit, p. 19).

"The energy of thinking," or of the "pure I," is therefore "the immense power" which, in its (dialectically) negative moment (that of the intellect), "takes us away from reality," but which, in its (dialectically) positive moment (that of reason), "leads us back to it."


---


Steiner writes precisely: "All the concepts that the intellect creates: cause and effect, substance and attribute, body and soul, idea and reality, God and world, etc., exist only to hold the unitary reality artificially divided; and reason must, without confusing the contents thus created, without mystically obscuring the clarity of the intellect, seek in the plurality the inner unity. Thus it returns to that from which the intellect had departed, that is, to unitary reality. If one wishes to have an exact nomenclature, one can call the configurations of the intellect concepts, and the creations of reason ideas. It will thus be seen that the way of science lies in raising oneself through the concept to the idea (…) Of the total reality there comes toward each one, so to speak, a cutting-out. This is worked over by his intellect, thus serving him as a means of setting out toward the idea. Although, then, we all perceive the same idea, we perceive it always in different fields. Therefore only the final result to which we arrive can be the same; while the paths can be different. In general, it does not matter at all that the individual concepts and judgments of which our knowing is composed should be in agreement; what matters is that in the end they lead us to sail in the wake of the idea. There, at last, all men must meet one another, if energetic thinking conducts them beyond the particular point of view" (pp. 122 and 123).

That is, beyond that opining of which Hegel further says: "Following one's own conviction is certainly worth more than surrendering to authority; but by converting belief founded on authority into belief founded on one's own conviction, the content is not necessarily changed, nor does truth succeed to error. To cling to the system of opinion and of prejudice out of another's authority or out of one's own conviction differs only by the vanity that lurks in the second manner" (Phenomenology of Spirit, p. 51).

L.R.
Rome, 13 March 2001

125
The Scientific Works of Goethe (27)

Tonight we shall concern ourselves with the second section of the ninth chapter: Dogmatic Method and Immanent Method.

We note, to begin with, that a "dogmatic" method that stands in contrast to an "immanent" one must of necessity be "transcendent."


---


For it – Steiner writes, in fact – "the objective being that determines my assertions lies in a world unknown to me; in my world there stands only my assertion, with its formal reasons (absence of contradiction, support from various axioms, etc.). A science founded upon this opinion is dogmatic. Such is the theologizing philosophy that rests upon faith in revelation, as well as modern experimental science; for there exists not only a dogma of revelation, but also a dogma of experience (…) Once, science was governed by the dogma of revelation; today it is governed by that of experience. At one time it appeared presumption to scrutinize the reasons of revealed truths; today it is held to be an impossibility to know anything other than what the facts enunciate. 'Why they speak thus and not otherwise' is held to be not experiencable and therefore unattainable" (pp. 124-125).

The ground of being is posited, by the "dogma of revelation," in the subject-in-itself (in God), by the "dogma of experience," in the object-in-itself (in the "thing-in-itself"): that is, in a subject and an object unattainable by the ordinary non-being of human thinking.

For both "dogmas," the idea, not having a content of its own (inasmuch as it is non-being), would therefore rest not upon itself, but upon something other than itself: namely, upon an (extra-ideal) content of which it can only be an "image."

Steiner observes, however: "So long as we have the capacity, we see that the idea has within itself everything that constitutes it, and that in it one has everything one can ask for. The whole ground of being has poured itself into the idea, entirely, without reservations, so that outside of it we cannot seek it anywhere. In the idea, therefore, we have not an image of what we seek in things, but the very thing sought" (p. 125).

And what is "the very thing sought"? It is the essence, "being – as Hegel would say – as a shining within itself" (Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989, p. 123): that is, reality or substance, and not image or appearance.

Steiner writes again: "To wish to explain the world by means of a real that is not idea is such a contradiction that one cannot even understand how it was ever possible to maintain it" (p. 126).

This confirms – as I sometimes happen to say – that "dark-seeing" is far more mysterious than "clear-seeing." What, in fact, is mysterious about our seeing "clearly"? On the physical plane, is it not precisely the sick eye, and not the healthy one, that constitutes a problem?

But wherein does the "dark-seeing" consist, in our case? In not noticing that thinking does not think things, but thoughts. "Things," in fact, are not "thought"; they are perceived.

We therefore feel things to be real because, in perceiving them, we encounter them in a living and direct way, whereas we feel ideas to be unreal (abstract) because, in thinking them (with ordinary consciousness), we encounter them reflected from the cerebral mirror.


---


126
And is not, in effect, the observation of a living cow quite different from that of a mirrored, painted, or photographed cow?

Our feeling ideas to be real or unreal depends, therefore, only on the way in which we think them, or on the level of consciousness with which we go to meet them.

It is contradictory – Steiner says – "to wish to explain the world by means of a real that is not idea." It is, however, understandable that whoever, going to meet the idea with ordinary consciousness, experiences it as unreal (as a non-being), should then wish "to explain the world by means of a real that is not idea": that is, should end up projecting elsewhere that reality, that being of the idea, of which he is not conscious.

Steiner observes precisely: "Only because man is often not in a position to comprehend that the being of the idea is immensely higher and fuller than that of perceived reality, he goes in search of a reality still more elevated. He regards the being of the idea as something chimerical, lacking real saturation, and is not satisfied with it; he is not capable of grasping the idea in its positivity; he possesses it only as an abstraction, without glimpsing its inner perfection and solidity" (pp. 127-128).


---


What, then, is the misunderstanding? That the only "reality still more elevated" than that of the abstract idea is that of the concrete idea. It is right, therefore, to go in search of something that is not "chimerical" or "lacking real saturation," but it is wrong to seek it outside thinking or the idea.

Moreover, it is one or the other: either one is not conscious of the limits of the intellect, and one then arrives at intellectualistic titanism (esoteric or exoteric); or one is conscious of them, and one then arrives at the conviction that beyond those limits there is something other than thinking, and not other thinking. "Other thinking" which is then true thinking, of which ordinary thinking – according to what we have said and repeated – is only the reflection, if not indeed the cast-off shell.

This holds, naturally, also for ideas. What, in fact, is an idea in its "positivity," in its "inner perfection" and its "solidity"? Nothing less than a spiritual entity; to express ourselves in a truly fitting way, we ought, in fact, to ask not: "What is an idea?" but rather: "Who is an idea?"

We have, then, the ideas, but not yet a consciousness equal to their (spiritual) reality: it is this (the Sophia) that we are therefore called upon to develop. You will agree, in fact, that one thing is to carry our life forward, believing that the concept (or the idea) is a name (flatus vocis), another to carry it forward, knowing that it is a spiritual entity.

In order to pass lucidly and critically from the one conviction to the other, avoiding possible drifts of a fideistic or mystagogic order, it is, however, necessary – as the science of the spirit indicates – to pass through intermediate experiences.

Steiner explains, in fact: "Man truly has, as an earthly being, something of what is lowest, and on the other hand has a reflected image of what is highest, which is attainable only in intuition. What he completely lacks, as an earthly being, are precisely the intermediate fields. He must conquer for himself imagination and inspiration" (Initiatic Knowledge – Istituto Tipografico Editoriale, Milan 1938, p. 67).

Let us keep in mind, in any case, that one cannot have experience of the reality of the concept or of the idea if one has not first had that of the (etheric) reality of thinking. Between the "thought" (the representation) and the concept or idea, there is, in fact, "thinking": that is, that living force which we can experience in a direct way thanks to the exercise of concentration.

Steiner writes: "Of culture it must be required that it work until it raises itself to that higher point of view where a being is also considered real that one does not see with the eyes and does not

127
touch with the hands, but grasps with reason. We have thus truly founded an idealism that is at the same time realism" (p. 128).

This "idealism that is at the same time realism" is precisely what we have called the "realism of ideas," opposing it both to "naïve realism" (of things) and to the various forms of what John Searle calls "anti-realism."


---


Steiner writes again: "We differentiate ourselves from realism through our full awareness of having only in the idea the means of explaining the world. Realism too has only this means, but it does not know it. It deduces the world of ideas, but believes it deduces it from some other reality. The Leibnizian world of monads is nothing but a world of ideas; but Leibniz believes he possesses in it a reality superior to the ideal one. All the realists commit the same error: they devise beings, and do not notice that they are not getting outside the idea" (p. 128).

(Naïve) realism thinks, but does not know that it thinks or – as Steiner says – it has "only in the idea the means of explaining the world," "but does not know it": it is not, that is to say, conscious of it. And what happens then? That the unconscious reality of the idea is projected onto some other content, more or less "devised."

It will be well to recall, in this regard, that the process of projection is spoken of not only by psychoanalysis, but also by Steiner: "A human faculty of which man knew nothing – he says precisely – would not be recognized by him as his own, but attributed to an entity alien to him" (Fundamental Lines of an Epistemology of the Goethean Conception of the World, in Philosophical Essays – Antroposofica, Milan 1974, pp. 103-104).

The reality of ideas is, in fact, attributed by Leibniz to hypothetical "monads," just as it is attributed (to come to our own day) by C. G. Jung to hypothetical "archetypes in themselves," by J. Eccles to hypothetical "psychons," and by E. Boncinelli to hypothetical "psychostates." (For the "archetypes in themselves," see J. Jacobi: Complex, Archetype, Symbol – Boringhieri, Turin 1971; for the "psychons," J. Eccles: How the I Controls Its Brain – Rizzoli, Milan 1994; for the "psychostates," E. Boncinelli: The Brain, the Mind, and the Soul – Mondadori, Milan 2000).

We are thus given to observe, once again, that the science of the spirit formulates no new "theory," but rather proposes a method capable of gradually bringing to consciousness what normally lives and works in unconsciousness.

"We have rejected (naïve) realism – Steiner writes – because it deceives itself regarding the ideal essence of its ground of the world; but we have also rejected that false idealism which holds that, since we cannot transcend the idea, we cannot transcend our consciousness either, and that therefore all the representations given to us, and the whole world, are nothing but subjective appearance, a dream that our consciousness dreams (Fichte). These idealists, for their part, do not comprehend that, although we cannot go beyond the idea, yet in the idea we have that objective element which is founded in itself and not in the subject. They do not consider that, although one cannot step outside the unity of thinking, yet with rational thinking we penetrate precisely into full objectivity. The realists do not comprehend that the objective is the idea, and the idealists do not comprehend that the idea is objective" (p. 128).


---


The fact is that it is not a question of "transcending" or "going beyond" the idea, but of transcending or going beyond the representational consciousness of the idea.

That the realists do not comprehend that the objective is the idea, and that the idealists do not comprehend that the idea is objective, is, moreover, a consequence of the action of the "obstructing" forces. The conception of the

128
(naïve) realists is, in fact, mortgaged by the Ahrimanic forces, that of the idealists by the Luciferic forces.

This means, however, that the former as much as the latter are partly right. It would therefore be well to practice observing what the part of right (and of wrong) of each of them is, because, in so doing, one would in fact arrive at assuming that central perspective of the I (and of the Logos) which, as the third, comprehends and surpasses them ("It is as certain as it is strange – Goethe observes in this regard – that truth and error spring from the same source; therefore one must often not damage the error, because at the same time one damages the truth" – Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 59).

Let us keep in mind, in any case, that one thing is (spiritual) individuality, another (psychic) subjectivity. Lucifer aims, in fact, at psychologizing the ideas (subjectivizing or relativizing them), while Ahriman aims at reifying or embalming them (transforming them materialistically into "things" or doctrinally into "dogmas").

Steiner continues: "We must still concern ourselves with the pure empiricists who regard every explanation of the real by means of the idea as an illicit philosophical deduction, and demand that we keep to what is sensibly perceptible. Against this point of view we can say simply that its demand can only be a formal one, of method. To say that we must stop at the given means only that we must appropriate what comes toward us. On the essence of the given, this point of view can decide less than any other; for that essence must, precisely, come toward it from the given itself. It is altogether incomprehensible to us how one can associate with the demand for pure experience that of not transcending the sensory world, given that the idea can, with equal title, satisfy the demand of being given. The positivistic principle of experience must leave entirely open the question of what the given is; it therefore accords very well with an idealistic result of the investigation. But then that demand also coincides with ours. And in our conception all points of view are united insofar as they are justified. Our point of view is idealism, because it sees in the idea the ground of the world; it is realism, because it qualifies the idea as the real; and it is positivism or empiricism, because it does not wish to arrive at the content of the idea by means of an a priori construction, but as at a given" (pp. 128-129).


---


In effect, the science of the spirit keeps not only to the sensory given, but also to the spiritual one. Together with the percept it considers, in fact, the concept, recognizing that man, in order to avail himself of his cognitive faculty, must first divide them (in perceiving) and then reunite them (in judging).

Empiricism is therefore right in claiming the rights of the given, but it is wrong in claiming them only for the sensory given.

It is well to assert – Hegel writes in this regard – "that what is true must be in actuality and be there for perception," but one must also observe "that in what is called experience, and is to be distinguished from the mere single perception of single facts, two elements are found: the one, which is the material, isolated in itself and infinitely various; the other, the form, the characters of universality and necessity" (Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989, pp. 48 and 49).

Steiner says that it is "incomprehensible how one can associate with the demand for pure experience that of not transcending the sensory world." How could one prove him wrong? Whoever truly wished not to transcend it would, in fact, have to guard against conceiving even a single thought or uttering even a single word with respect to it: that is, he would have to guard against doing the very thing that the various theorists of empiricism or positivism instead do (and certainly not sparingly).

Would one not then feel like saying, with Christ: "Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do"? (Lk 23:34).

Answer to a question


---


129
Between the sensory given (the percept) and the spiritual given (the concept) there is, in effect, an important difference: the former reaches us even if we are (psychically) "unworthy" of it; the latter reaches us only if we are (psychically) "worthy" of it or, better said, if we have made ourselves worthy of it ("Blessed are the pure in heart – the Gospel says, in fact – for they shall see God" – Mt 5:8).

But what does it mean to make oneself "worthy" of the concept (or the idea)? It means knowing it, loving it, and disposing oneself to receive it, firmly silencing "the man of the underground" (Dostoevsky): that is, that low and clamoring legion of cravings and prejudices that habitually obscure it or repel it.

Whoever knows psychoanalysis is well aware, for example, that patients have, with regard to therapy, an "ambivalent" attitude: on the one hand, they declare themselves disposed (in good faith) to do everything necessary to obtain healing; on the other, they oppose to it an unconscious "resistance." Well, this holds, to a large extent, also for those who seek the truth: on the one hand they declare themselves disposed (in good faith) to do everything necessary to find it; on the other, they oppose to it an unconscious "resistance." They oppose it to it, however, insofar as they sense, more or less obscurely, that to find the truth of the world one must find the truth of oneself, and that to find the truth of oneself one must transform oneself or – as Goethe would say – "perish and become."

"What is called fear of error – Hegel observes precisely – is rather to be recognized as fear of the truth" (Phenomenology of Spirit – La Nuova Italia, Scandicci (Fi) 1996, p. 49).

L.R.
27 March 2001

130
The Scientific Works of Goethe (28)

Last week we completed the examination of the second section of the ninth chapter; tonight we shall therefore concern ourselves with the third, entitled: System of Science.

Steiner writes: "What form has completed science, in the light of the Goethean way of thinking? First of all we must recall that the overall content of science is a given; given, in part, as sensory world from without, in part, as world of ideas, from within. All our scientific activity will therefore consist in overcoming the form in which this overall content of the given comes toward us, in order to attain another suited to satisfy us. This is necessary, given that the inner unity of the given remains hidden from us in the first form in which it presents itself to us, when only its outer surface appears to us. Now this methodical activity, which establishes the indicated connection, unfolds differently, according to the field of phenomena we take to study" (p. 130).


---


What does it mean that "the overall content of science is a given"? It means that science concerns itself with what exists, not thereby creating the existent, but rather the consciousness of the existent.

Since, however, the existent is given at various levels (for example, mineral, vegetable, animal, and human), its "methodical activity" must necessarily assume different forms.

But here a problem arises: if the "scientific method" is one (as is mostly said), how can it be adequate to the different levels of existence? And if it is not one, how can the scientific character of the different methods be guaranteed?

One could answer that the scientific method is a kind of "mother-method" able to give birth to as many "child-methods" as there are levels of reality. But if that were so, what form would this "mother-method" then have? And would it have one at all? Since the forms of the "child-methods" reflect the different qualities of the levels of reality to which they apply, should not a method that gives birth to the others, but does not apply itself, of itself, to any reality, be formless?

And it is precisely so, for the "mother-method," inasmuch as it is universal, stands to the "child-methods," inasmuch as they are particular, just as the (universal) concept stands to its (particular) representations. Just as from a single concept, which is form but has no form, there can derive several representations that have form, so from a single universal method there can derive several particular methods.

A "universal" method, however, is less a "method" than a concept, an essence, and therefore a spirit.

This demonstrates, then, that it is the scientific "spirit," and not the "method," that is one, and that it is precisely this spirit that makes itself the guarantor of the scientific character of the methods in which it is realized in order to adapt itself to the various demands of reality.

But what happens when science is not recognized as "spirit"? It is soon said: the universal (of which one is unconscious) is projected onto the particular (of which one is conscious). By universalizing what is particular, or absolutizing what is relative, one betrays, however, the scientific spirit, and arrives (willy-nilly) at the dogmatic one.

The fact is that just as "the sabbath – according to what Christ asserts – was made for man, and not man for the sabbath" (Mk 2:27), so the method was made for the spirit, and not the spirit for the method; which is to say, in other terms, that it is the scientific spirit that must guarantee the method, and not vice versa.

Present-day science (which declares "scientific" only the method suited to investigating the inorganic world) does, on the noetic plane, what the Old Testament does on the ethical plane. For just as, according to the latter, morality would be guaranteed by observance of the "Law," so, according to the former, scientific character would be guaranteed by observance of the "Method" (or of the various "protocols").

The "Law" of the New Testament is, however, the Spirit of freedom and of love ("Do not think – one reads, in fact, in Matthew (5:17) – that I have come to abolish the Law or the prophets; I have not come
                                                  131
to abolish, but to fulfill"): that is, the Spirit of that freedom of thought and that love for reality which alone can guarantee to science a constant, humble, and devoted adherence to the phenomena.

Think, for example, of the dream. Is it not a phenomenon? Well, try approaching it with the same method or the same logic with which mechanical phenomena are approached, and you will soon find that it becomes incomprehensible to you.

On the other hand, just as to master the mechanical world one must dispose, in consciousness, of a mechanical logic, so to master the world of dreams one must dispose, in consciousness, of an "oneiric" logic: that is, an imaginative and inspirative logic.

But is it perhaps important to "master" the world of dreams? Of course it is! It is very unlikely, in fact, that one who does not know how to master (at least in part) that world truly knows how to master himself, and is therefore (and in equal measure) free. "The dream – Steiner asserts precisely – is a manifestation, unfolding in a succession of sensory images, of what man truly is (…) If it is rightly referred not to the lower (material – ed.) world, but to the higher (moral – ed.) one," it can therefore "absolutely serve as a direction for human life" (Initiatic Knowledge – Istituto Tipografico Editoriale, Milan 1938, vol. I, pp. 189 and 191).

But let us return to our subject.

Having said that the methodical activity of science "unfolds differently, according to the field of phenomena we take to study," Steiner (having chiefly in view the difference between the field of inorganic phenomena and that of organic phenomena) proceeds as follows:


---


"The first case is the following. We have before us a multiplicity of elements given to the senses, which stand in a reciprocity of action. This reciprocity becomes clear to us when we penetrate ideally into the thing. In that case one of those elements appears to us as more or less determined in one way or another by the others. We deduce one phenomenon from another: we deduce the phenomenon of the heated stone, as effect, from the sunbeam as cause.
