In this case we explain what we perceive in one object by deducing it from another equally perceivable object" (p. 130).

Calling A the sensory phenomenon of the sunbeam and B the sensory phenomenon of the heated stone, we clarify (ideally) their "reciprocity of action" at the very moment we deduce B, as effect, from A, as cause.

It would seem easy, but it is not. The celebrated anthropologist Bronislaw Malinowski (1884-1942) reports, for example, that some natives, observing that women during pregnancy do not menstruate, were convinced that amenorrhea was the cause of pregnancy, and not – as everyone now knows – its effect.

Steiner continues: "We see in what way the ideal law presents itself in this field; it embraces the objects of the sensory world, stands above them; it determines the way of acting of one object according to given laws, recognizing it as conditioned by another object" (p. 130).

We can say "above" them, but also "outside" them. We have already compared the mineral object to a corpse: but when is it that a body transforms into a corpse? When the I, the astral body, and the etheric body come precisely out of it.

You will remember that, at the time, we also compared the law governing the inorganic phenomenon to a puppeteer who governs the puppet from without, in contrast to an actor who governs himself from within.

132
Steiner proceeds: "Here we have the task of arranging the series of phenomena in such a way that the one proceeds from the other by necessity, and that together they form a whole permeated by laws. The order of phenomena that is explicable in this way is inorganic nature" (p. 130).

Given phenomenon A and phenomenon B, it is therefore a question of ordering them, arranging them "in such a way that the one proceeds from the other by necessity": that is, in such a way that phenomenon B necessarily follows, in time, phenomenon A; it is a question, in other words, of ordering them according to a cause-effect relation. Care must be taken, however, for while the cause-effect relation is always a relation between what comes before and what comes after in time, the relation between what comes before and what comes after in time is not, however, always a cause-effect relation (that is, the post hoc does not always hold as propter hoc). This depends precisely on the fact that the cause-effect relation – as we now know – is a "qualitative" (and therefore "necessary") relation, and not a merely chronological one.


---


Steiner writes, in fact: "Now, in experience, the single phenomena do not at all present themselves to us in such a way that those nearest in space and time are nearest also with regard to their inner essence. One must first pass from spatial and temporal nearness to conceptual nearness. Before a phenomenon we must seek those that connect themselves to it immediately with regard to their essence. We must aim to bring together a series of facts that complete one another, support one another, carry one another. From this we obtain a group of sensory elements of reality that act upon one another; and the phenomenon that unfolds before us follows immediately from the relative factors, in a clear and transparent way. Such a phenomenon we call, with Goethe, the archetypal phenomenon or fundamental fact. This archetypal phenomenon is identical with the objective law of nature" (pp. 130-131).

Let us imagine, just to give a most banal example, that a bridge painted red collapses; is it possible that the fact that it is of that color has anything to do with the collapse of the bridge? It is decidedly improbable.

It is, in fact, one of the many accidental elements that science is called upon to eliminate in order to be able to bring to light the essential elements (the law) of the phenomenon.

What, then, is the "archetypal phenomenon"? It is the pure phenomenon, and for that very reason devoid of everything that has a fortuitous or contingent character.

Goethe writes precisely: "As I often have occasion to note, especially in the field in which I work, there are many empirical fragments that must be discarded before one obtains a pure and constant phenomenon (…) The observer never sees the pure phenomenon with his eyes." He therefore distinguishes it both from the "empirical" phenomenon and from the "scientific" (experimental) one, inasmuch as it is "the ultimate result of all experiences and all experiments"; it "can never be isolated, but shows itself in a constant series of phenomena"; and "to represent it, the human spirit determines what oscillates empirically, excludes the accidental, isolates the impure, develops the uncertain, and uncovers the unknown" (Experience and Science, in Works – Sansoni, Florence 1961, vol. V, pp. 42 and 43).

Steiner says that "we must aim to bring together a series of facts that complete one another, support one another, carry one another." Velocity V, time T, and space S can furnish us an example of this. One can, in fact, derive V by dividing S by T, just as one can derive T by dividing S by V, or S by multiplying V by T.

As one sees, these are three phenomena that precisely complete, support, and carry one another.


---


Steiner proceeds: "The above-mentioned bringing-together of facts can take place simply in thinking (…) Or I can also bring the single factors into real contact and then await the phenomenon that follows from their reciprocal action. This is the case in the experiment. While a phenomenon of the outer world is obscure to us because we know only the conditioned (the phenomenon), the phenomenon furnished by the experiment is, on the contrary, clear to us, because we ourselves have put together the conditioning factors. This is the way of the investigation of nature: that it start from experience, to see what is real; then proceed to observation, to see why that is real; and then ascend to the experiment, to see what can be real" (p. 131).

I recommend, to make this passage clearer, correcting (in the sixth line) the term "condizionato" ("conditioned") to "condizionanti" ("conditioning") (bedingenden Faktoren).

Let us return, in any case, to the example of the sunbeam and the heated stone. I sit down on the stone and feel that it is warm. This first experience is that of the conditioned. If then, filled with wonder ("The first impulse toward science – Goethe writes – arises from astonishment" – Thirty-Six Years After the Essay on the "Intermaxillary Bone," in Works, p. 222), I were to question myself about the reason for that temperature, I would have to, starting from the experience of the conditioned (the heated stone), go in search of the conditioning (the sunbeam).

To perform an experiment, on the other hand, I would have to start from what I think may be the conditioning (the cause), in order to verify whether it is in fact able to determine that conditioned (that effect).

What does it mean, then, that the first step on the way of the investigation of nature consists in starting "from experience, to see what is real"? That one must first take note of what actually exists (the fact). And what does it mean that the second step consists in "seeing why that is real"? That one must then find the law that explains the existence of the fact. And what does it mean, further, that the third step consists in "ascending" to the experiment "to see what can be real"? That one must lastly discover, starting from the law that explains that particular existence, how many others could be given.

One must first ascend, therefore, from the phenomenal plane of the conditioned to the noumenal plane of the conditioning, and then descend again, from the latter, to the phenomenal plane of the conditionable.


---


Steiner continues: "Goethe distinguishes three methods of natural-scientific investigation, resting on three different interpretations of the phenomena. The first method is common empiricism, which does not go beyond the empirical phenomenon, the immediate matter of fact (…) The level immediately above common empiricism is rationalism. This aims at the scientific phenomenon; it no longer stops at the mere description of phenomena, but seeks to explain them by uncovering their causes, erecting hypotheses, etc. It is the level at which the intellect deduces from the phenomena their causes and the relative connections. Both the first method and the second are declared by Goethe to be one-sided. Common empiricism is the crude non-science, inasmuch as it never raises itself from the mere apprehension of chance. Rationalism, on the other hand, interprets the world of phenomena by affixing to it causes and connections that are not contained in it. The first does not know how to raise itself from the crowd of phenomena to free thinking; the second loses sight of that crowd, and with it loses the firm ground under its feet, falling into the arbitrariness of fantasy and of subjective opinions (…) 'Theories [he says, for example, in the Prose Sayings] are generally the rashness of an impatient intellect, which would like to liberate itself from the phenomena, and therefore puts in their place images, concepts, and often merely words'" (pp. 132-133).

Here we are, then, once again, in the presence of two methods of investigation rendered "one-sided" by the mortgage of the obstructing forces. Common empiricism, in fact, "does not know how to raise itself from the crowd of phenomena to free thinking" because of the Ahrimanic "gravity," whereas rationalism distances itself from it so far as to "lose sight of it" and to fall "into the arbitrariness of fantasy and of subjective opinions" because of the Luciferic "unbearable lightness" (Kundera).

Do you remember what we said last time? That making oneself "worthy" of the concept (or the idea) means "knowing it, loving it, and disposing oneself to receive it, firmly silencing 'the man of the underground' (Dostoevsky): that is, that low and clamoring legion of cravings and prejudices that habitually obscure it or repel it."
                                                  134
Well, what holds for the concept holds also for the phenomena. Every theory that is the fruit – as Goethe says – of "the rashness of an impatient intellect, which would like to liberate itself from the phenomena, and therefore puts in their place images, concepts, and often merely words," is therefore nothing but an offspring of the "man of the underground."

Steiner writes again: "Both these ways, common empiricism and rationalism, are for Goethe way-stations toward the highest scientific method, but precisely only stages that must be gone beyond. To this surpassing, rational empiricism leads, which concerns itself with the pure phenomenon, which is identical with the objective law of nature" (p. 133).

Of rational empiricism, as the third and highest scientific method, however, we shall speak next time.

L.R.
Rome, 4 April 2001

135
The Scientific Works of Goethe (29)


---


We concluded our last meeting by recalling that Goethe distinguishes, within the field of natural-scientific investigation, the method of common empiricism, the method of rationalism, and the method of rational empiricism, placing them respectively in relation to the "empirical" phenomenon, the "scientific" phenomenon, and the "pure" phenomenon.

We then spoke of the first two, promising to take up the third tonight. Let us therefore resume our reading a couple of lines before the point at which we had broken it off.

To the surpassing of common empiricism and rationalism, Steiner writes, "leads rational empiricism, which concerns itself with the pure phenomenon, which is identical with the objective law of nature. Common empiricism, immediate experience, offers us only isolated objects, an aggregate of single, disconnected phenomena, and does not show them to us as a final conclusion of scientific study, but as first experience. Our scientific need, on the other hand, seeks only interconnected facts, comprehends the single fact only as a link of a connected whole. Thus the facts of nature and the need for comprehension diverge apparently. In the spirit there is nothing but connection; in nature there is nothing but separation; the spirit tends toward the species, nature creates only individuals" (p. 133).


---


As you see, the spirit tends to connect what is separated not only in nature, but also in the soul. What, in fact, is Goethe's rational empiricism, if not precisely a synthesis of the opposite characters of common empiricism and rationalism?

Let us keep in mind, however, that a true synthesis (a coniunctio oppositorum) is obtained not by virtue of a "compromise" between opposites, but by virtue of a third and higher reality able to harmonize and satisfy their contrasting demands.

Steiner says that "the spirit tends toward the species," while "nature creates only individuals." With regard to what nature creates, however, it will be well to speak of "singularity," and not of "individuality." One thing, in fact, is spiritual individuality; another, the soulic or psychic subjectivity; and yet another, the physical or bodily singularity.

Steiner himself asserts, moreover, that what "constitutes the singularity of an object cannot be comprehended, but only perceived" (p. 108).

Steiner says again that "in the spirit there is nothing but connection": that is, that the spirit is unity, the whole. What, then, is the problem? That man, in order to be able to know the unity of the spirit or of the world, must first shatter it. Think, for example, of a child who takes apart a toy and puts it back together. The toy, once reassembled, is the same; the child is not: before, in fact, he was ignorant of how the toy was made; now, instead, he knows. His consciousness has therefore been modified and enriched.

Well, we do something analogous with the world: first, perceiving (sensibly), we "take it apart," and then, thinking, we "put it back together," so as to restore it, in the end, to its original unity. The re-created unity is, however, different from the created one, inasmuch as it is a conscious or spiritual unity, and no longer an unconscious or natural one. Hegel observes precisely: "The true is the becoming of itself, the circle that presupposes and has at its beginning its own end as its own end, and that only through the actualization and its end is actual" (Phenomenology of Spirit – La Nuova Italia, Scandicci (Fi) 1996, p. 11).

The fact is that minerals, plants, and animals are all in the whole, whereas man is in part outside the whole (with the head) and in part in the whole (with the remaining organism): and it is for this reason that he can, unlike all the other beings of nature, observe and know the whole. But a whole of which he can begin to know only the part, inasmuch as he precisely observes and knows it with a part of himself, and not with his whole self.
                                                  136
Steiner continues: "The solution of this contradiction results from the consideration that, on the one hand, the connecting force of the spirit is devoid of content (inasmuch as an 'empty form' – ed.), and therefore incapable, on its own account, of knowing anything positive, whereas, on the other hand, the separation of the objects of nature is not founded in their very essence, but in their spatial appearance, so that, precisely by penetrating into the essence of the single particular phenomenon (into the 'blind force' – ed.), that phenomenon itself leads us to the species" (p. 133).


---


Of what "contradiction" is one speaking? Of that between the spirit, which tends toward universality (the species), and nature, which tends toward singularity.

Do you know the story – reported by Hegel – of the fellow who wanted to eat only "fruit," and who died of hunger because, instead of "fruit" being served him, he was always served apples, pears, bananas, peaches, apricots, etc.?

Why, then, did this fellow die of hunger? Because one can eat the singular, but not the universal or, better said, one can eat the universal only through the singular. In fact, "the separation of the objects of nature is not founded – as Steiner says – in their very essence, but in their spatial appearance, so that, precisely by penetrating into the essence of the single particular phenomenon (of the single apple, pear, banana, etc. – ed.), that phenomenon itself leads us to the species (to the fruit – ed.)."

Remember, in this regard, what Steiner says again in The Philosophy of Freedom? That it is quite curious that the naïve realist holds the single tulips to be real, but not the species "tulip," given that what he holds to be real proves perishable, whereas what he holds to be unreal proves enduring. But for what reason does he think this way? Because he has no consciousness of the fact that without the reality of the concept, that is to say of the universal, he could not even determine that of the single tulip: that is, judge – as we have seen – that A is A.

The fact is that what holds for the connection among the single phenomena holds also, at a prior level, for the connection among the single sensory stimuli or the single nerve impulses into which the single phenomenon (the percept) articulates and divides itself in us.

Steiner proceeds: "Another field of investigation is that in which the particular does not appear to us as the consequence of another particular standing beside it (…) Here a series of sensory elements appears to us as the immediate expression of a unitary principle to which we must arrive if we wish to comprehend the single phenomenon. In this field we cannot explain the phenomenon on the basis of effects coming from without, but must derive it from within. What before was determining is now only occasional (…) When I know the outer influence, I do not yet have the explanation of why the phenomenon unfolds precisely in this way rather than in another. I must derive the phenomenon from the central principle of that object upon which the outer influence has acted. I cannot say: This outer influence has this effect; but only: To this determinate outer influence, the principle of inner action responds in this determinate manner. What takes place is the consequence of a complex of inner laws" (p. 134).


---


Given the phenomena A, B, and C, we could therefore discover, in the inorganic field, that A is the cause of B and that B is the effect of A, but also the cause of C: we could discover, that is, a series or a chain of causes and effects.

In the organic field, on the other hand, we could discover that A, B, and C are all effects, or the "immediate expression," of the "unitary," "central," and "inner" principle D; not only that, but we could also discover that, given the action of the (outer) stimulus Z upon the "principles" or "complexes of inner laws" D, E, and F, the nature of the reactions aroused will depend more on the nature of D, E, and F than on that of Z.

137
Steiner writes precisely: "I must therefore know this inner complex of laws; I must investigate what configures itself from within outward. This self-configuring principle, which in this field lies at the basis of every phenomenon and which I must seek in every thing, is the type. We are here in the field of organic nature. What in inorganic nature is the primordial phenomenon (the pure phenomenon – ed.), in organic nature is the type" (p. 134).

In the inorganic field, science is therefore called upon to distinguish, so to speak, "the wheat from the tares": that is to say, what is essential and necessary from what is marginal or accidental; in the organic field, it is called upon instead to shift its gaze from the outside to the inside. In the former, it can, in fact, discover the essential (the pure phenomenon) by observing the phenomenon from without, whereas in the latter it can discover the essential (the type) only by observing the phenomenon from within.

Steiner adds: "The type is a general image of the organism, the idea of it; the animality in the animal" (p. 134).

What, in fact, is it that makes an organism an "organism" (vegetable or animal)? Precisely – as we now know – the type: that is, the idea, the essence, the entelechy, or the whole.

At this point, however, we shall take a step forward, because Steiner, after speaking to us of the science of inorganic nature and of that of organic nature, alludes, in concluding this section, to the ethical and historical sciences, which concern man directly (and which will be the theme of the following section).

He writes, in fact: "We have had to present again here, by necessity of connection, the main points of what we had already expounded in an earlier section concerning the 'type.' In the ethical and historical sciences, then, we have to do with the idea in the strictest sense. Ethics and history are sciences of the ideal: their realities are ideas" (pp. 134-135).


---


As you see, the further one ascends the kingdoms of nature, the more the idea tends to present itself for what it is: in the mineral kingdom (in the crystals) it manifests itself as an enduring imprint or as an external structuring force; in the plants, as a mutable form or as an inner shaping force; in the animals, as a model of behavior; and in human beings, finally, as idea: as that idea of whose world – according to what one reads in the last lines of The Philosophy of Freedom – man should "take possession," in order to "employ it for his own human ends, which go beyond the merely scientific ones."

Steiner asserts, in fact: "We must be able to place ourselves before the idea in a living way; otherwise one becomes its slave" (The Philosophy of Freedom – Antroposofica, Milan 1966, p. 230).

Plants and animals cannot, in fact, place themselves "before the living idea," for they are "living ideas"; man, on the other hand, can, inasmuch as he is an "I," which hitherto has, however, placed itself (as ego) before the reflected idea, and not the living one.

It ought not, therefore, to be the idea that dominates man, but man (as I) who dominates the idea, and thereby makes himself free.

Scaligero explains in this regard: "In the constitution of man the following are practically recognizable: the mineral kingdom, which forms his visible 'physical' body, called in Hinduism sthula sharira; the structuring force of the plant kingdom, which forms his vital, or 'etheric,' body, linga sharira; the soulic life of the animal kingdom, which works in him as sentient body, or 'astral body,' kama rupa, by means of which man has a sensory and sensual relation with physical life, not dissimilar from the animal one. To this threefold constitution, whose structural identity with the threefold domain of nature is recognizable, there is added in man a Principle that is not encountered in the other kingdoms of nature, because it dominates them from a more elevated degree: the Principle of individual consciousness, atma, or
                                                   138
the 'I,' essentially supersensible, which imprints the correlation of the three systems, down to its bodily appearance: precisely differentiating the human kingdom from the other kingdoms" (Practical Manual of Meditation – Tilopa, Rome 1984, p. 13).

It is, moreover, a duty to emphasize that comprehension of the relation between the I and the idea is – for whoever seriously studies the science of the spirit – of the utmost importance. The idea that rebels against the will of the I, because it would usurp its role and dominate it, characterizes, in fact, the Luciferic soul, whereas the idea that lovingly does the will of the I, because it knows and feels itself to be its "ancilla," characterizes the Sophianic soul.

But let us return to our subject.


---


Steiner writes: "It falls to the individual sciences to work over the given up to the point of reaching the primordial phenomenon, the type, or the guiding ideas in history. 'If the physicist (Goethe observes precisely – ed.) can arrive at the knowledge of what we have called a primordial phenomenon, he is safe, and with him the philosopher is safe. The former is safe because he is convinced of having reached the limit of his science, the empirical summit whence he is able to embrace, looking back, experience in all its levels, and, before him, to cast his gaze, if he does not enter it, into the realm of theory. And the philosopher is safe too, inasmuch as he receives from the hands of the physicist an ultimate quid that becomes for him a first.' (Cf. the Theory of Colors.) For here the work of the philosopher begins. He takes the primordial phenomena and places them in the satisfying ideal connection. Here we see with what, according to the Goethean conception, metaphysics can be replaced: with an observation, combination, and derivation of the archetypal phenomena in conformity with the idea" (p. 135).

In short, science ascends from things to ideas, whereas "philosophy" (so Goethe would have it) should ascend from ideas to the idea, and from the idea to the I.

Since it is very brief, and we still have a little time, I would say let us examine also the fourth section, entitled: On the Limits of Knowledge and the Formation of Hypotheses.

Steiner writes: "Much is said, nowadays, of the limits of our knowing. Our faculty of explaining the existent would reach only up to a certain point, and at that point we should have to stop"; if, however, "we consider that the object with regard to which a need for explanation arises in us must be given, it is obvious that the given itself cannot impose a limit upon us (…) What does not appear within the horizon of the given has no need of explanation. The limit can therefore arise only from the fact that, before a given real object, we lack the means to explain it. Now our need for explanation comes precisely from the fact that what we wish to predicate of the given, by means of which we wish to explain it, enters into the horizon of our thinking. Far from being unknown to us, it is precisely the explicative essence of a thing which, arising in the spirit, renders the explanation necessary. Both what must be explained and what is to give the explanation are present. It is only a question of joining the two. Explaining is not the search for an unknown quid, but an elucidation of the reciprocal relation of two known elements" (p. 136).


---


To comprehend this passage, it will be well to return to the syllogism we illustrated at the time (in the course of the 26th meeting – ed.). Remember? If "X is A," and if "A is X," then "A is A."

"Our need for explanation – Steiner says – comes precisely from the fact that what we wish to predicate of the given, by means of which we wish to explain it, enters into the horizon of our thinking": observing the first judgment of our syllogism, we notice, in fact, that "what we wish to predicate of the given" (of the percept X), and by means of which we wish to explain it, "enters into the horizon of our thinking" as A (as concept).

139
A, however, inasmuch as it has arisen from the spirit as the living concept (as the "explicative essence") of X, is indeed "present," but not fully "elucidated" in its relation to X. As we saw then, in order to "elucidate" that "reciprocal relation" it is, in fact, necessary, by passing through the middle term ("A is X"), to arrive at the conclusion "A is A": that is, it is necessary to arrive at the conscious reflected concept or, more precisely, at the representation.

Explaining does not, therefore, consist in "the search for an unknown quid," but in becoming conscious of what is already present (A) and asks to come to light, passing from unconsciousness to (representational) consciousness.

It would suffice, moreover, to observe children when they reach the famous age of questions, in which they begin to ask: "What is this?"; "What is that?"

Why – let us ask ourselves – do they now want an explanation of the same things that before they were content to put in their mouths, throw on the ground, or fling in the air? It is simple: because the concepts, which live unconsciously in them, now begin to press in order to be able to receive at least a name.

Steiner writes: "We should never wish to explain a given by means of something of which we have no notion. In principle, therefore, one cannot in any way speak of limits to the possibilities of explanation (of things)" (p. 136).

The fact is that things are already the explanation of things. Goethe says precisely: "The ideal would be to understand that every real element is already theory (…) Above all, one should seek nothing behind the phenomena: they themselves are the theory" (Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 137).

It is not, therefore, a question of inventing an explanation, but of discovering the explanation. And it is not that discovering it is easier than inventing it: on the contrary, it is much more difficult. Knowledge has, in fact, no absolute limits, but it can have relative ones (biographical, historical, cultural), and it can easily be hindered by a whole series of difficulties of an inner or outer order. It is therefore legitimate to speak of de facto limits, but not of limits of right or "of principle."


---


Steiner writes: "Connected with this is the theory of the hypothesis. A hypothesis is a supposition that we make, and of whose truth we cannot persuade ourselves directly, but only through its effects"; it, however, "may indeed presuppose something not perceived, but it must always presuppose something perceivable. Thus every hypothesis finds itself in the position that its content can be directly confirmed by a future experience. Only those hypotheses are justified that can cease to be such; those concerning central principles of science are not valid" (p. 137).

Let us consider, for example, that energy which Freud (in a "psychosexual" key) and Jung (in a "psychic" key) call "libido." One thing is to hypothesize the existence of an energy not-perceived but perceivable; another is to hypothesize – as Freud and Jung do – an energy not-perceived and not-perceivable.

That it should involve something not-perceived belongs, so to speak, to the game. What sense, in fact, would an inquiry have if there were nothing to be found? But what sense is there, likewise, in establishing, from the very beginning, that what is sought is unfindable, and that one must therefore limit oneself to registering and evaluating only its effects?

The fact is that every true inquiry ought to go from the "not-perceived-perceivable" to the "perceivable-perceived."

You see, the science of the spirit too, in speaking of imaginative thinking, of inspirative thinking, of intuitive thinking, or of the spiritual I, speaks to us of realities normally not-perceived: not-perceived, yes, but perceivable, so true is this that it presents and proposes itself as a (theoretical-practical) science intended to realize precisely such an objective.

140
If it spoke to us of the same realities, declaring them not-perceived and unperceivable, it would, in fact, no longer be a "science," but a "metaphysics" elaborated, yes, by thinking, but sustained by faith and feeling.

L.R.
Rome, 10 April 2001

141
The Scientific Works of Goethe (30)

Tonight we shall begin the last section of the ninth chapter, entitled: Ethical and Historical Sciences. It is a rather long section in which Steiner takes up those very themes that he will later develop in the second part of The Philosophy of Freedom.


---


Steiner writes: "The answer to the question: 'What is knowing?' has shed light for us on the position of man in the universe. Now the views to which we have arrived with regard to this question cannot fail to throw light also upon the value and importance of human action (…) The first task to which we shall now have to apply ourselves will be that of investigating the character of human activity. In what relation does that which we must regard as the effect of human action stand to other activities within the universal process? Let us consider two things: a natural product and a creation of human activity, the form of a crystal and, say, the wheel of a cart. In both cases the object in question appears to us as the result of laws expressible in concepts. The difference lies only in this, that the crystal must be seen as the immediate product of the laws that determine it, whereas for the cartwheel, between the concept and the object, man stands in between" (pp. 137-138).

What is knowing? It is the ascent from existence to essence. And what is creating? The descent from essence to existence.

The descent from essence to existence can, however, be immediate, and one then has the divine (natural) creation, or mediated, and one then has the human (cultural) creation. A crystal is an example of the former, a wheel of the latter.

To orient ourselves better, it will, moreover, be well to refer back to the celebrated distinction, made by Scholasticism, between the concepts or ideas (the "universals") "ante rem," "in re," and "post rem."

In knowing, man transforms the idea in re (the perceived idea) into the idea post rem (the known idea); in creating, he transforms, instead, the idea ante rem (the thought idea) into the idea in re (the perceived idea).

Steiner writes precisely: "In knowing, we learn what the ideal conditions of sensory experience are; we bring to manifestation the world of ideas that already lies within actuality; we therefore conclude the universal process in that we bring to manifestation the producer that eternally generates the products, which, however, without our thinking, would remain eternally hidden within them. But in action we complete that process, transmuting into actuality, inasmuch as it is not yet such, the world of ideas" (p. 138).


---


Do you remember that "living pendular movement" of which Steiner speaks in the addition to the tenth chapter of The Philosophy of Freedom?

It is a question of that rhythm with which the I oscillates, in the manner of inhalation and exhalation or of diastole and systole, between the pole of the (physical) body and that of the spirit. In knowing, the I sets out, in fact, from the body (from sensory perception) in order to arrive at the spirit (at the idea); in creating, it sets out, conversely, from the spirit (from the idea) in order to arrive at the body (at sensory perception).

It is important to keep this in mind, since it is impossible to come to grips with a whole series of problems if one does not conceive the activity of knowing dynamically. What, in fact, is the principal limit of the intellect? Precisely that of being static, and of therefore not succeeding in conceiving, except abstractly, the living reality of movement.

Good sense would, therefore, have us integrate what intellectual consciousness gives with what imaginative consciousness, inspirative consciousness, and intuitive consciousness give. It is precisely imaginative and inspirative consciousness that allow, in fact, the experience of the "living pendular movement," as self-experience of the I, both on the dynamic plane of movement and on the qualitative plane of enantiodromia (of the reversal into the opposite).
142
In acting, Steiner says, we transmute "into actuality, inasmuch as it is not yet such, the world of ideas." For not only nature exists, but also what has been created by us.

We are therefore responsible for the ideas we think (and in particular for the way in which we think them), just as for those we "bring into the world," transmuting them into actuality. It suffices to think, to be convinced of this, of those "ideocracies" (this is what François Furet calls communism, fascism, and Nazism) which afflicted the twentieth century in the most tragic manner: that is, what Robert Conquest not by chance defined as The Century of Murderous Ideas (Mondadori, Milan 2001).

Steiner writes: "Our knowing leads us to find, from the indications contained in the nature surrounding us, the tendency of the universal process, the intent of creation. When we have attained it, then our action has the task of collaborating autonomously in the realization of those intents. And thus our acting appears to us as a direct continuation of that species of activity which pervades nature as well" (p. 138).


---


Ideas work, then, in the real. In this, however, we must distinguish substances, forces, and laws, observing that substances are governed by forces, and forces by laws: that is to say, by thinking.

Imagine, just to give an example, that a roof tile falls on my head. In the very instant it strikes me, I experience (in spite of myself) a substance and a force. If the tile, instead of falling on my head from above, had been in my hands, I would, in fact, have experienced a substance, but only to a limited extent a force (its mere weight). Well, let us imagine that, once recovered from the trauma, I wish to discover with what velocity it struck me. What should I do? I should have to calculate it, on the basis of the so-called "law of falling bodies" (Galileo). That is, I should have to think it, because thinking (the law that governs the phenomenon) shows itself only to thinking.

Not only, however, do substances show themselves subordinate to forces, and forces to laws, but laws also show themselves subordinate, in their turn, to a unitary principle (a "whole") that harmonizes them, regulating their reciprocal relations. For ecology, for example, the environment is precisely nothing but the totality of the (physical, chemical, biological) conditions in which the life of organisms unfolds. The whole, if it is truly "attained," therefore reveals to us that "tendency of the universal process" to which we are called to collaborate through our actions.

You see, when we recite the Pater noster, we say: "Thy will be done": yet it is we who will that it be done; if it were done by itself (as in nature), we would, in fact, have no need to invoke it. We invoke it, rather, because it is done "in heaven" and not "on earth," since it can be done "on earth" only through men.

Steiner continues: "The natural product does not at all have within itself the ideal law by which it appears to be dominated. Something superior must come toward it, precisely human thinking; then there appears to thinking that by which the natural product is dominated. For human action the case is different. Here, the idea is immediately inherent in the active object; and if a superior being were to come toward it, it could find in the activity of that object nothing other than what it itself has placed there. For a perfect human action is the result of our intentions and of that alone" (p. 138).

Animals, for example, are dominated by that "ideal law" (species or idea) that determines, among other things, their behavior. They live this condition, but they cannot become conscious of it, inasmuch as they do not enjoy the faculty of thinking thinking. The "ideal law" to which they are subject can


---


143
be known, however, by "human thinking"; and it can be because it is men, as I's, who have the ideas, whereas it is the ideas that have the animals.

To human thinking, which observes an animal, there is therefore revealed the idea that transcends it and determines its behavior; but to a being endowed with a superior thinking, which observed a man, the same thing would not be revealed, since, in that case, it would be the individuality (the I) that transcends the idea and determines, by means of it, the action.

It is by means of the idea, in fact, that the I gives form to its own will; and an idea that informs the will of the I is nothing other than an "intent," an "intention," or a "purpose."

"A perfect human action – Steiner says precisely – is the result of our intentions and of that alone." It is not at all easy, however, to evaluate intentions, since they are found and act in the depths, and therefore at a level absolutely different from that of the facts. Not without reason do we say: "God judges the intentions, and not the facts."

And it is so because the evaluation of facts pertains to juridical activity, whereas the evaluation of intentions pertains to moral activity. It is therefore fated that the intellect, by its nature morally neutral (like mathematics, which best expresses its characteristics), can only base itself, for its moral evaluations, on the facts, thus ending up confusing the moral sphere with the juridical one, and thereby entrusting itself (in Old Testament style) to the "law."

The intention – we have said – is an idea that informs the will; but the idea – as we know – is an essence, an entity, or a spirit. How, then, to discern intentions, if one does not know the spirit? And how to found a morality higher than the heteronomous one, if one does not dispose, together with the sciences of nature, of a science of the spirit?

"If we observe a natural product acting upon another – Steiner writes – 'we see an effect which is determined by laws graspable in concepts. But if we wish to comprehend the effect, it is not enough that we put it in relation with some law; rather, we must have a second perceivable object, naturally also dissolvable into concepts. When we see an imprint on the ground, we seek the object that produced it. This leads us to the concept of such an effect, where the cause of a phenomenon appears in its turn in the form of an outer perception, that is to say, to the concept of force'" (p. 139).


---


By thinking the imprint I observe as an "effect," I set in motion the search for the "cause," since it is the very concept of effect that calls up that of cause. It does not suffice, however, after having conceptually determined the perceived imprint as effect, to determine the cause as a "concept"; the cause must also be perceived, and therefore hunted out as an "object."

As we already know, on this (inorganic) plane it is precisely necessary that both the cause and the effect be perceivable.

Imagining that it was a horse that left the imprint on the ground, it is easy to understand that the idea of the hoof could produce an "ideal" imprint, but not a "real" (sensibly perceivable) one. For the latter to be produced, a real hoof must, in fact, exercise its force, and the pressure it exercises must be greater than the hardness of the ground. As one sees, it is nothing but a play of forces (regulated, in any case, by laws).

Steiner writes: "Force can come toward us only there where the idea appears at first in a perceivable object, and only in this form acts upon another object. The contraposition to this is found there where the mediation is eliminated and the idea approaches the sensory world in an immediate way. Then the idea itself appears as causal function. And it is here that one speaks of will. Will is therefore the idea itself conceived as force. It is absolutely illicit to speak of a will standing by itself" (p. 139).

144
In the inorganic world, idea A, active as force A, can exercise an action upon object B solely by means of object A. Where this "mediation is eliminated," idea A, active as force A, "approaches" object B, instead, "in an immediate way": that is, without any longer acting by means of object A.

What therefore appears as "cause" is here no longer object A, but idea A, active as force A or, in this case, as will A.

Will, Steiner says, is "the idea itself conceived as force." Did we not say, at the time, that the idea is to be conceived on the astral plane as "essence," on the etheric plane as "force," and on the physical plane as "object" or "thing"?

Of this too, however, one is not as a rule conscious, and one then ends up considering will as a force "standing by itself." Typical is the case of Schopenhauer, who sees the Will in nature (Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989) as a force disjoined from that of thinking or of the ideas, and therefore acting as a blind impulse or a blind craving for life.


---


Steiner proceeds: "When man performs an action, one cannot say that to the representation the willing is also added. To express oneself thus means not to have grasped the concepts clearly. For what is the human personality, if one prescinds from the world of ideas that fills it? Evidently an active existence (…) But this active existence is nothing real; it is an abstraction without content, which cannot be grasped. If one wishes to grasp it, if one wishes for a content, one finds precisely the world of ideas in the act of acting. E. v. Hartmann makes of this abstraction a second constitutive principle of the world, alongside the idea. But it is nothing other than the idea itself, only in a given form of its manifestation. Will without idea would be nothing. The same cannot be said of the idea, since activity is an element of it, whereas it is the essence that bears itself" (p. 139).

We know well by now that ordinary consciousness furnishes us a dead experience of ideas. Nothing more logical, then, than that it should consider will as a something that has nothing to do with the idea, and that can therefore at most stand "alongside" it.

Only at a level of consciousness superior to the ordinary (representative) one can one therefore realize that will is the force of the idea and that the idea is the form of will. Steiner says that "will without idea would be nothing," but that "the same cannot be said of the idea, since activity is an element of it, whereas it is the essence that bears itself."

Why? Because, will being the life of the idea ("an element of it"), and the idea the "essence that bears" that life, a will without idea could be precisely nothing but a nothing.

Steiner writes again: "To explain a process of nature is an ascent to the conditions that determine it; a search for the producing (determining – ed.) element in the face of the given (determined – ed.) product (…) For human action the case is different. Here it is the very law that determines a phenomenon that enters into action; what constitutes (produces – ed.) a product presents itself upon the scene of action (…) Natural processes are therefore distinguished from human actions by the fact that in the former the law is to be regarded as the determining background of manifest existence, whereas in the latter existence itself is law and does not appear determined by anything other than itself. Therefore every natural process divides into a determining and a determined, of which the second follows from the first by necessity, whereas human action determines itself solely from itself. And in this consists acting in freedom (…) While all natural processes are only a manifestation of the idea, human action is the idea itself acting" (pp. 139-140).


---


The difference between a process of nature and a human action lies, therefore, in the fact that the relation to the idea has, in the former, a transcendent character, whereas in the latter it has an immanent character.
                                                 145
Remember? We have no need to seek what illuminates the Sun, for the Sun, while it illuminates everything, illuminates itself as well. Inasmuch as it is the source of light, it is, in fact, able to suffice for itself, or to rest upon itself.

Well, a process of nature needs to be illuminated (like a planet) from without (by man), whereas human action illuminates itself (like a star) from within.

In man, the I is equivalent, in fact, to a Sun, whose warmth and whose light take form, and for that very reason diversify themselves, pouring themselves into the ideas. Let us not forget that Steiner asserts that ideas are precisely "vessels of love."

Inasmuch as it is "being," the I is the ground of all "essences," and therefore the subject (the spirit) of all ideas. If one wished, one could compare it to a fire from which the sparks of ideas fly forth unceasingly.

When we observe, say, a bird building its nest, or a spider spinning its web, we must therefore ascend, to give an account of these behaviors, from the single specimen to the species, whereas, when we wish to explain the behavior of a man to ourselves, we must not go outside him, but rather ascend, within him, from the plane on which the action presents itself to that on which the idea or intention that generates it is found.

In other words, the intelligence and aptitudes that animals show do not belong to the individual, but to the species; the intelligence and aptitudes that the human being shows belong, instead, to the individual, since "every man – Steiner explains – is a species unto itself" (Theosophy – Antroposofica, Milan 1957, p. 50).

One could therefore say, somewhat bluntly, that it is the ideas that "use" the animals, whereas it is man who "uses" the ideas.

In any case, just as our need to explain animal behavior, having arrived at the species, feels itself satisfied, so, having arrived at the ideas and at the I, the need to explain human behavior (our own or another's) feels itself satisfied.

This implies, however, that the single animal is not responsible for the actions that descend (as "ought-being") from the ideas of the species (which we are accustomed to call "instincts"), whereas every man is responsible for the actions that descend (as "will-to-be") from his ideas and, all the more, from his ideals.

You see: one who has no ideals can become a "depressive," but one who has them can become a "maniac." And why? Because he believes he has an ideal, and does not notice that it is in reality the ideal that has him: that is to say, that it dominates or possesses him.

In order truly to "have" a human ideal, it is, in fact, indispensable to "be," not an ego, but an I (a homo): the only subject, that is, capable of dominating the ideas in a creative way, and of not being dominated by them in a destructive way.

It is needless to add that it is the Ahrimanic forces that take our ideals from us, rendering us depressive; and that it is the Luciferic forces, giving us to them as prey, that render us manic.


---


L.R.
Rome, 17 April 2001

146
The Scientific Works of Goethe (31)

Let us resume reading at once from the point at which we had left off last time.

Steiner writes: "Inasmuch as our theory of knowledge has arrived at the conclusion that the content of our consciousness is not only a means of forming for ourselves an image of the ground of the world, but that this ground itself, in its most proper form, manifests itself in our thinking, we cannot help recognizing immediately, in human action as well, the unconditioned acting of that very ground" (p. 140).

By virtue of the path of knowledge that ascends from the thought (from the representation) to thinking, from thinking to the concept (or the idea), and from the concept to the I, one discovers that the I is the ground not only of the world, but also of man, and that, as such, it is the ground of thinking as much as of willing, and therefore both a thinking subject and an acting subject.

Every action presupposes an acting. Acting is, however, an indeterminate force which, in order to be able to concretize itself now in action A, now in action B, now in action C, and so on, needs to determine itself, and therefore to take a form. But that is not all: just as action presupposes an acting, and as acting presupposes a form (a concept or an idea), so too the form presupposes an agent.

We must therefore take into consideration the action, the acting, and the agent, and, between the acting and the agent (the I), that "world of ideas" (Plato) or that "realm of the Mothers" (Goethe) which we could also call the "world of purposes" or of "motives."

In order to act, one must, in fact, be "motivated": that is, the I must give a form to the indeterminate force of acting.

The freedom or non-freedom of acting depends, therefore – as I hope I have succeeded in clarifying when we concerned ourselves with the second part of The Philosophy of Freedom – on the type of relation subsisting between the motive and the agent: if the motive (as inner cause of the action) is posited by the I, the acting will be free; if it is imposed upon the I, it will be unfree. Which is equivalent to saying that when the I is the cause of the motive of the action, there is freedom, whereas when there is given a motive of the action of which the I is not the cause, there is no freedom.

There is, therefore, no freedom whenever the motive of the action is imposed, more or less consciously, by nature (by constitution, temperament, character), or by culture (by what C. G. Jung calls the "collective consciousness" or by what sociologists call "patterns of behaviour").

Steiner continues: "We know of no guidance of the world that, outside ourselves, sets to our actions a purpose and a direction. The guidance of the world has renounced its power; it has put everything into the hands of man, annihilating its own separate existence, and has imparted to man the task of continuing the work" (p. 140).


---


We have just distinguished natural conditionings from cultural ones. A "guidance of the world" that stands "outside ourselves" obviously belongs to the latter, since, standing precisely "outside us," it cannot manifest its will except indirectly: that is, by means of rules, precepts, norms, commandments, or "categorical imperatives" (Kant).

Steiner writes again: "With this, the philosophy here expounded is the true philosophy of freedom. It does not make human actions depend either upon natural necessity or upon the influence of a creator or guide situated outside the world"; naturally, he adds, "the existence of innumerable human actions that fall only under natural necessity is not at all excluded. Man, inasmuch as he is a natural being,
                                                  147
is also to be understood according to the laws that hold for natural action. But purely natural laws will never suffice to explain him as a knowing and truly moral being. Here he steps out of the sphere of natural realities; and for this supreme potency of his existence, more ideal than real, what we have here established holds. The life of man consists precisely in evolving from the state of a purely natural being to that which we have here indicated: he must free himself from all natural laws and arrive as far as giving himself his own laws by himself. But we must reject also the influence on the part of a guide of human destinies situated outside the world. In this case too, one could not speak of true freedom, since that guide would determine the direction of human action, and man would have nothing to do but carry out its commands. He would not feel the impulse to action as an ideal that he sets for himself, but as the command of the guide. Therefore here too his acting would be conditioned, not unconditioned. He would not feel free in his inmost being, but dependent; a mere means of execution for the intents of a superior power" (p. 141).

Man is a free being, but does not yet exist as such, since he is called precisely to make himself free, or to become what he is.

We therefore begin to realize our freedom starting from a state of non-freedom. Nevertheless, the recognizing of what, deriving from nature or from culture, is necessity is already a manifestation of freedom. The I that permits us, on the noetic plane, to recognize necessity is, in fact, the same I that permits us, on the ethical plane, to realize freedom.

Man, Steiner says, must arrive "at giving himself his own laws by himself." I hope it is clear that this is not an objective that can be attained in a day, in a month, in a year, nor even in a lifetime, but a goal at which we should aim unceasingly, since what is at stake is our becoming, or not becoming, what we are: that is, free spirits. It is superfluous to add that the realization (however distant) of this ideal should constitute the most living and profound of our aspirations. What higher ideal, in fact, could a man have, if not that of becoming precisely that?


---


Steiner writes: "Whoever imagines a ground of the world outside our world of ideas thinks that the ideal reason why a thing is recognized by us as true is other than the one that makes it objectively true. Truth is thus understood as dogma. And what in science is dogma, in the field of ethics is the commandment. When man seeks in the commandment the stimuli to his acting, he works according to laws whose ground does not depend on him; he thinks a rule prescribed to his action from without: he acts out of duty. To speak of duty has a sense only for this conception. To act out of duty means to feel the spur from without, and to recognize the necessity of following it" (pp. 141-142).


---


What is a dogma? It is a thought that man must believe, because he cannot freely think it. And what is a commandment? It is a will that man must observe, because he cannot freely will it.

Thus, in the one case, we should have faith in another's thinking, whereas, in the other, we should follow another's will. What else, in fact, is duty, if not precisely the willing of a not-I?

In both cases, we should therefore in fact follow the example of the animals. Are not the intelligence and the will of the species imposed ("from without," and as a collective I) upon the single specimen?

But for what reason must what the animal does spontaneously instead be "imposed" upon man? Precisely because man is free – one will answer – whereas the animal is not.

Agreed, but if he is free, for what reason, then, could he not arrive at thinking truth by himself and at willing good by himself? For what reason, that is, should he choose (like the animals) to ought-be, and not to will-to-be? (Goethe says: "What is the best government? That which teaches us to govern ourselves" – Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 92).
                                                  148
I know well that it is customary to speak of nature as the kingdom of being and of morality as the kingdom of ought-being. But the fact, for example, that a diamond can exist only as a diamond, that a carnation can exist only as a carnation, or that a spider can exist only as a spider, whereas man can exist as man or as non-man (as beast, or else, today, as robot), does it not precisely indicate that the existing of the former cannot contradict their being, inasmuch as it is an ought-being, whereas the existing of man can contradict his being, inasmuch as it is a will-to-be, and for that very reason a willing to become, or not to become, what he is?

The fact is that the Creating Entities gave birth to, nourished, and educated man up to the point of putting him in a position to emancipate himself, to walk with his own legs (those of the consciousness soul), and thereby to take upon himself the responsibility of his own subsequent evolution: up to the point, in short, of being able to be "causa sui."

But wherein should this subsequent evolution of his consist? In ascending from representational consciousness (through imaginative and inspirative consciousness) to intuitive consciousness or, which is the same, from ego to I (that is, from the ego, through the "Spirit-self" and the "Life-spirit," to the "Man-spirit").

Today one is pleased to speak of "post-modernity." Since "modernity" is the ego, however, one cannot take this expression seriously unless one gives it the meaning we have just indicated.

A healthy "post-modernity" cannot, in fact, be realized except in a healthy "post-egoity," and thus in a surpassing of the egoism of thinking (of opinions), which opens the way both to the surpassing of the egoism of feeling (of narcissism) and to that of the egoism of willing (of utilitarianism).

What, moreover, is the ego? It is the I in the fetal state, and therefore an I that would above all need to be lovingly helped to grow, develop, and mature, in order to be able to arrive at having morality in itself in a way analogous to how the animal has instinct in itself. (A verse of the Pater Noster, in the version given by Steiner, reads precisely: "Thy will be put into effect by us as Thou hast placed it in our inmost essence.")

Between the ego and the I, just as precisely between the fetus and the grown man, there is therefore given a continuity of force (of essence) and a discontinuity of form (of existence).

But what happens when one knows nothing of all this? That one unconsciously attributes the continuity of the force to the form as well, thereby fixing the I in its fetal state, or else the discontinuity of the form to the force as well, thereby projecting the I into a distant and imaginary beyond.

In this second case, the willing of the I (that is, the willing of the ground of the ego) is transformed into a duty, since it presents itself precisely as the willing of another I, transcendent and unknown (like the willing – as Freud would say – of a "Super-ego").


---


Steiner writes: "Man performs an action only by virtue of the fact that the reality of it is for him a need. He acts because an inner (his own) impulse, and not an external power, drives him. The object of his working, as soon as he forms an idea of it, fills him so as to drive him to seek to realize it. In the need to realize an idea, in the urge to carry out an intent, must, in fact, reside the sole stimulus to our working. In the idea must be expressed everything that drives us to act. Then we work not out of duty, nor moved by an impulse, but we work out of love for the object to which our action is to apply itself" (p. 142).

"The sole stimulus to our working," Steiner says, must reside "in the need to realize an idea, in the urge to carry out an intent"; which is equivalent to asserting that this stimulus must reside in a pure willing guided by a pure thinking and animated by a pure feeling.

But when is it that thinking, feeling, and willing are "pure"? When they are, so to speak, "objective," and therefore free from every mortgage of a personal character.

149
Only love for the idea should, therefore, drive us to realize it. But do today's "intellectuals" perhaps love ideas? For the most part, no, inasmuch as they love themselves, and make use of ideas to assert themselves or to satisfy those infinite cravings born of the "human, all too human" (Nietzsche).

We must work, Steiner says again, "out of love for the object to which our action is to apply itself." But since the object is – as we have said and repeated – nothing but a concept, the love "for the object" should then transform itself into love "for the concept."

Who, on the other hand, is not ready to declare (in good or bad faith) that he acts "out of love for the object"? It is possible, however, to discover whether he truly loves it, only by verifying whether the object he declares he loves is, for him, a "concept" or a "thing."

"Love – Scaligero writes – is the spirit that wills the spirit in the other" (On Immortal Love – Tilopa, Rome 1982, p. 23): "in the other," which can therefore be a person, but also an animal, a plant, or a mineral, since there is nothing in the world in which the presence of the spirit does not manifest itself, even if at different levels (the Prologue of John reads, in fact: "All things were made through him, and without him not one of the things created was made" – Jn 1:3).

In the light of everything we have seen so far, we could therefore say, paraphrasing: on the human plane, love is the I that wills the "I" in the other; on the animal and the vegetable (organic) plane, it is the I that wills the "type" in the other; on the mineral (inorganic) plane, it is the I that wills the "pure phenomenon" in the other.

Steiner continues: "While we represent the object to ourselves, the object arouses in us the longing for an action suited to it; and only such an action is free" (p. 142).


---


Free, therefore, is only the action "suited" to the object.

Well, let us think of the many who proclaim that they wish to change the world, in order to create a "better" one. But is it perhaps legitimate to advance such a pretension if one is not at all able to "suit" the action to the reality of the world, because one is ignorant of how it is made? One thing, in fact, is to change the world by knowing it and acting upon it by means of what Steiner calls (in The Philosophy of Freedom) "moral imagination" and "moral technique"; another is to change it by violating it, to force it to go where one has taken it into one's head that it must go. (Nietzsche used to say that one cannot cure a defect with a vice.)

But what is it that one has taken into one's head? It is – like it or not – ideology: that is, the infernal counter-image of the idea.

The idea is, in fact, born of love for the object, whereas ideology is born of that dissatisfaction or unhappiness which our lower nature is a master at transforming into resentment, malice, and hatred.

Steiner writes: "An action that is not performed for the sake of love of it is not free. Egoism acts unfreely. And, in general, whoever performs an action for an incentive that does not derive from the objective content of it acts unfreely. To execute an action out of love of the action itself is to act out of love. Only whoever in acting is moved by love, by devotion to objectivity, acts truly freely. Whoever is not capable of this devotion free of egoism will never be able to regard his own activity as free" (p. 143).

As one sees, all these assertions imply the exercise of thinking. Who else, moreover, could open the way to "objectivity," if not precisely thinking? And who else, if not free thinking, could generate free acting?

Goethe observes, in this regard: "Lessing, who reluctantly tolerated limitations of various kinds, has one of his characters say: 'No one ought to ought.' A man full of spirit and joy of living said: 'Whoever wills, must.' A third, undoubtedly a cultivated man, added: 'Whoever comprehends, also wills.' And thus one believed one had closed the entire circle of knowing, willing, and owing. Yet,
                                                  150
as a rule, man's knowledge, of whatever type it may be, determines his conduct; so that nothing is more terrible than to see ignorance at work" (Maxims and Reflections, pp. 131-132).

Steiner writes: "If man's acting is to be nothing other than the realization of his own content of ideas, it is natural that such a content must reside in him. His spirit must work productively. For what should arouse in him the impulse to do something, if not an idea that is opening its way from the depths of his spirit?" (p. 143).


---


But why must the idea open a "way" for itself? Because along the route it must travel to go from the spirit (from which it is born) to the real (in which it would realize itself), it fatally comes to collide – as Scaligero would say – with "the immense power of the conventional" (Grail – Tilopa, Rome 1982, p. 60).

Goethe rightly observes: "In all ages it is only individuals who have given their contribution to science, not their epoch. It was the epoch that put Socrates to death with poison, the epoch that burned Hus: the epochs all resemble one another" (Maxims and Reflections, pp. 86-87).

For the idea (and for the I that bears it), the path toward the world is, in effect, long and tormented, since it is forced precisely to force its way through an endless series of obstacles, first of all inner ones. The idea is, in fact, born from the I, but, in order to arrive at the body, it must first pass through the soul; and this, if it is less a (solar) soul than a (lunar) psyche, ends up for the most part by trapping it, altering it, or distorting it, in order to put it at the service of itself (of the ego) and of its cravings.

In order to prevent the true from being transformed into the false, and the good into evil, we should therefore make ourselves so pure as to succeed in allowing the idea, just as it was born from the I, to arrive, through the soul, at the body and, through the body, at the world and at others.

Let it not be forgotten, in this regard, that the first stage of the modern path of initiation is represented precisely by the "catharsis" or "purification" of the astral body, attained by means of concentration and meditation. Steiner writes, in fact: "This catharsis or purification has the aim of eliminating from the astral body everything that prevents it from organizing itself in a harmonious and regular way, so as to be able to develop the higher organs, since the astral body is predisposed to develop those organs, and it suffices to lay bare, so to speak, the latent forces in it"; explaining, then, that one could progress far on this path by arriving, for example, "at intimately permeating oneself with and living the whole content of my book The Philosophy of Freedom, up to feeling that one has become capable of reproducing for oneself and faithfully the thoughts expounded in it" (The Gospel of John – Antroposofica, Milan 1995, p. 178).

L.R.
Rome, 24 April 2001

151
The Scientific Works of Goethe (32)

Tonight we shall continue to concern ourselves with the fifth section (of the ninth chapter), resuming the reading a few lines before the point at which we had left off last time.


---


Steiner writes: "If man's acting is to be nothing other than the realization of his own content of ideas, it is natural that such a content must reside in him. His spirit must work productively. For what should arouse in him the impulse to do something, if not an idea that is opening its way from the depths of his spirit? Such an idea will prove all the more fruitful the more it arises in his spirit in well-determined outlines and with a definitive content. For only something that in its content is fully determined can drive us with full force to its realization. An indistinct ideal, only obscurely represented, is not fit to drive us to action. How could it ever kindle us, if its content is not clear and manifest in the light of the sun?" (p. 143).


---


These last two assertions deserve particular attention.

If only we would renounce acting when we do not have clear ideas! Instead we act all the same, without realizing in the least that another spirit (since an "ideal" is – according to what we have seen – a "spirit" or a living idea), taking advantage of the fact that our "indistinct ideal, only obscurely represented, is not fit to drive us to action," has poured its force into that vague form, thereby leading us to act apparently in the name of our ideal, but in reality in view of its in-human or dis-human interests.

Let us think, for example, of the tragic (and for many still inexplicable) difference that history has unfortunately given us occasion to observe between "ideal" socialism and "real" socialism. Is it not in some way analogous to the difference between "ideal" Christianity and "real" Christianity or, to say it all, to the one that can not infrequently be ascertained (albeit at a quite different level) between "ideal" anthroposophy and "real" anthroposophy?

It will, for example, have happened to you, in this regard, to come upon someone who, after reading some books by Steiner (relating above all to the various fields of application of anthroposophy), feels it his duty to say: "These things must be put into practice; they must not remain theoretical." Quite right! But who is it that should put them into practice? The ego, perhaps? No, for the ego, mistaking "knowing" for "knowing-through" ("What is well known, – Hegel says – precisely because it is well known, is not known" – Phenomenology of Spirit – La Nuova Italia, Scandicci (Fi) 1996, p. 18), could do nothing, in putting them into practice, but imitate or ape the I.

"Evil – Scaligero observes precisely – is the idea that feigns itself actualized"; it is "activism that substitutes itself for the activity of thinking" (Why a Spiritual Association Lives, in On Immortal Love – Tilopa, Rome 1982, p. 316).

The first thing to be "put into practice" is, in fact, the I. "Rather seek the kingdom of God – one reads in Luke – and these things will be given you in addition" (Lk 12:31).

The contrast between theory and practice, just as that between thinking and willing, or between subject and object, is, moreover, nothing but the fruit of egoic or representational consciousness; and it is precisely for this reason that the science of the spirit proposes itself, so to speak, as a "practical theory" or a "theoretical practice." What, for example, is "living thinking," if not precisely a "willing thinking" or a "thinking will"?

But it is from the ego – one will say – that we must in any case start. Certainly, but one thing is to start from the ego in order to transform oneself, up to arriving at the I; another is to start from the ego in order to attempt to transform others, things, or the world directly, thereby remaining the same as before. (Who knows whether it was in thinking of this eventuality that a slogan of '68 read: "Don't liberate me, I can do it by myself!").
                                                  152
We must therefore transform ourselves, beginning by changing the quality of our ordinary thinking (which serves as a crutch to the ego); but in transforming ourselves, we begin to change the world, for we are part of the world.


---


Answer to a question

You see, the soul can be "kindled" by the warmth of an ideal, but also by that of the passions or the cravings, and therefore both from above and from below. You will recall that Scaligero opposes precisely the (gentle) "flamma non urens" of the spirit to the (burning) "flamma urens" of the cupiditas that characterizes our lower nature (the "infernals").

One must therefore, as always, be awake, so as to be able to distinguish the one "flame" from the other. To this end, it will be well to keep in mind that the ideal, like the Sun, illuminates and warms. Wherever there is warmth but no light (precisely an "indistinct ideal" or one "only obscurely represented"), one can therefore be sure that there is nature, and not spirit.

Steiner continues: "The impulses to our action must always manifest themselves in the form of individual intents. Everything fruitful that man accomplishes owes its origin to such individual impulses. General moral laws, ethical norms and the like, which should hold for all men, prove themselves totally devoid of value (…) Not all actions are equally worthy of being performed by anyone; rather, this one is suited to one man, that one to another, according to the vocation that each feels in himself" (p. 143).


---


Here one understands well why I proposed to you, some time ago, that we distinguish spiritual individuality both from soulic (or psychic) subjectivity and from bodily singularity. We would, in fact, misunderstand what Steiner says here were we to mistake the "individual intents," which descend from the I or from the spirit, for those which instead rise up from the psyche or from the body.

To distinguish what, in us, is an expression of the I from what is not, honesty and courage are, however, required. One thing, in fact, is to have thoughts that – as the saying goes – "whirl about in our heads" (and with which we are accustomed to identify ourselves); another is to strive to think the thoughts that "whirl about in our heads" (disidentifying ourselves from them), or to give ourselves thoughts knowingly (as one does with concentration and meditation).

The fact is that just as "all that glitters is not gold," so not everything that is thought is "idea." In the form of a real idea there lives, in fact, (as essence) the force (of the being) of the I, whereas in the form or empty shell of an apparent idea there lives the (karmically determined) force of our personal nature (easy prey of the "obstructors").

It will be well to remember that this nature, if sthenic (or hysteric), tends to render us "disobedients" or "rebels"; if asthenic (or neurasthenic), "obedients" or "followers" (Fromm). In the first case, one will therefore be impatient of norms and rules, and thus attracted by freedom, whereas in the second one will derive security from observing them, and will therefore fear freedom (Erich Fromm's book is not by chance entitled: Escape from Freedom – Mondadori, Milan 1994).

In this case too, it will therefore be well to learn to distinguish the freedom that is born of love for the I (for man) from that pseudo-freedom or, better said, from that arbitrariness that is born of impatience with, if not indeed hatred of, norms and rules.

The first is, in fact, a spiritual reality, whereas the second is a mere psychic fact. Goethe says: "Duty: when one loves what one commands to oneself" (Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 179). Well, the sthenic types or characters "love" (without realizing it), not what they command to themselves, but what viscerally commands them, and of which they are therefore slaves (Goethe says again: "No one is more a slave than he who holds himself free without being so" – Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 38). The "disobedient" or "rebel" types are, in short, slaves of

153
nature (Freud would say, of the "Es"), whereas the "obedient" or "follower" types are slaves of culture (Freud would say again, of the "Super-ego").


---


Steiner writes: "These considerations throw light upon the questions that a general ethics has to resolve. This is often regarded as a sum of rules that should direct human action. From this point of view ethics is opposed to natural science and in general to the science of the existent. While the latter has to transmit to us the laws of what subsists, what is, ethics would be called upon to teach us those of what ought to be. It would be a code of all the ideals of man, an exhaustive answer to the question: What is good? But such a science is impossible; there cannot be a general answer to this question. For moral acting is a product of what works in the individual; it is always given in single cases, never in general. There exist no general laws about what one must or must not do" (p. 144).

Here we are, then – as we said last time – in the presence of the contraposition between being and ought-being: that is, between science (thinking) and ethics (willing).

It will, in any case, be well to recall that heteronomous morality has helped (on the plane of collective evolution), and still helps (on that of individual evolution), the intellect and the ego to form or structure themselves (it is significant, for example, that among the "Ten Commandments," the seventh: "Do not steal," the ninth: "Do not covet another's wife," and the tenth: "Do not covet another's goods," are founded precisely on the distinction between the ego and the "thou," and therefore between "mine" and "thine").

This morality does not, however, help the ego to go beyond itself and to become an I (a "Spirit-self"): it has helped it, and helps it, therefore, to free itself of nature, but not to free itself of itself, in order to find itself again in the spirit.

The fact is that the ego, in order to be able to free itself of itself, must free itself of death. How has it, in fact, succeeded in freeing itself of nature? By making nature precisely die in that part of the body (the head) of which it avails itself in order to have (reflected) consciousness of the world and of itself: that is, in that part of the body which contraposes itself, in the quality of "dead nature," to the remaining organism, in the quality of "living nature."
