But just as death has freed us of nature, so the spirit (the Logos) must free us of death, thereby permitting us, like Lazarus, to rise again. Is it not significant that Golgotha means precisely "place of the skull"?

I hope it is clear, in any case, that taking oneself beyond the ego does not mean eliminating the ego, just as taking oneself, say, beyond the feet would not mean eliminating the feet, but rather developing also the legs, the torso, the arms, and the head.

There will, moreover, come a day (if we will it) when intellectual consciousness, which today represents the highest degree of consciousness (higher than those of dream-consciousness and sleep-consciousness), will instead represent the lowest degree and, so to speak, the "instrumental" one.


---


All the questions raised by ethics, Steiner writes, "can be comprehended in one: To what extent is man a moral being? But this has no other aim than to know the moral nature of man; it does not ask what man must do or not do, but what that which he does is, in his inmost essence. With this, the wall that divides all science into two spheres collapses: the doctrine of what is, and the doctrine of what ought to be. Ethics is, like all the other sciences, a doctrine of what is" (p. 145).

The problem, then, is not being or not being "moral," but rather being or not being "men," since morality is the very being of man (a verse of the Pater Noster given by Steiner reads, in fact: "Thy will be enacted, as Thou hast placed it in our inmost essence").

By eliminating man from the world, one would therefore eliminate morality.

154
But if ethical reality coincides with ontological reality, it then follows that man's being immoral coincides with his not being man. What, then, would normative morality demand? That man, while not being man, should behave as a man, thereby filling the void of his being with ought-being.

Which, as we have already observed, would also be legitimate, were his ought-being to serve, in the course of the "first half of life" (Jung), to prepare him to seek and realize, in the course of the "second," his own being (and therefore an "autonomous" morality). But it is not so, inasmuch as the validity of heteronomous morality is posited as absolute, and not as relative to a particular evolutionary phase of human consciousness.

It is superfluous to add that the difficulties one encounters in ascending from codified, stone-carved morality to living morality are the same ones one encounters in ascending from reflected thinking to living thinking (Goethe notes: "Error stands to virtue as sleep stands to waking. I have observed that in coming out of error one returns to truth as if tempered" – Maxims and Reflections, p. 88).

Steiner continues: "Now man does not belong only to himself, but is a member of two higher wholes. First of all he is a member of his people, to which usages and customs, culture, language, and common ways of seeing unite him. Moreover he is also a citizen of history, a single member in the great historical process of human evolution. From this twofold belonging to a whole, his free acting appears to be invalidated" (p. 145).


---


Man's free acting would therefore appear "invalidated" both by his belonging to a determinate space and by his belonging to a determinate time.

It would appear so, but it is not so. The I belongs, in fact, neither to space nor to time, and can therefore put both at its own service.

Think, for example, of a poker player. One thing is the cards he is dealt; another the way in which he will play them. It is known, in fact, that a bad player, even with good cards, may win little, if not indeed lose, whereas a good player, even with bad cards, may lose little, if not indeed win.

In order to "play" space and time well, however, we must know and objectify them, and not identify ourselves with them. The so-called "parochialists" or "chauvinists," for example, identify themselves with their space, whereas those who boast of being à la page or trendy identify themselves with their time.

In order to become the I that one is, one must therefore laboriously open a way for oneself through the jungle (physical, etheric, and astral) of false and illusory identities (the most widespread of which today is that of the "bodily I").

In any case, the I carries its own space with it as physical body and its own time as etheric body. But what is this time? It is that which elapses between the birth and the death of an earthly life.

Consider, just to mention one, that I which received the name of Giuseppe Garibaldi (1807-1882). Does it seem to you that his "free acting" was "invalidated" by his belonging to a determinate space and a determinate time? Or does it not seem to you, rather, that it was his "free acting" that stamped its mark upon both the one and the other? Do you remember what Manzoni says of Napoleon?

"…Let us bow our heads to the utmost / Creator, who willed that in him / the spirit of his own creator / should stamp a vaster imprint."

Steiner observes, in fact: "History assigns me a place in which to carry out my action. I am dependent upon the epoch of culture in which I was born; I am a son of my time. But if one considers man as a knowing being, at the same time as an acting one, this contradiction resolves itself. Thanks to his cognitive faculties man penetrates the character of his ethnic individuality; he realizes where his fellow countrymen are headed. What appears to determine him, he
                                                 155
surmounts, and receives it into himself as a fully recognized representation; it therefore becomes individual in him, and totally assumes the personal character that is proper to free action. The same happens with regard to the historical evolution within which man lives. He raises himself to knowledge of the guiding ideas, of the ethical forces that reign there; then they no longer act as coercive impulses, but become individual forces in him"; in short, he must not "be led, but lead himself": he must not, that is, "let himself be dragged along by the progress of civilization, but must make the ideas of his time his own. For this it is above all necessary that man understand his time" (pp. 145-146).


---


Paraphrasing the ancient saying: "Ducunt volentem fata, nolentem trahunt" ("Fate leads the willing, drags the unwilling"), we could therefore assert that one who understands his space and his time leads them, whereas one who does not understand them is dragged by them; and he is dragged by them because, inasmuch as he is "unwilling," he unconsciously assigns to them the role that would be proper to the I.

The fact is that our space and our time are part of our karma; and this – as you know – should not lazily drag us, but rather stimulate us to increase our knowledge, so as to be able to motivate our actions in an ever more conscious and responsible way.

Karma is, in the end, a bit like the "prima materia" of the alchemists: that is to say, that "lead" of necessity which we should learn to transmute, thanks to the "Ars magna" (Raimondo Lull), into the "gold" of freedom.

L.R.
Rome, 8 May 2001

156
The Scientific Works of Goethe (33)

Note: on the evening of 15 May 2001, before resuming the reading of the Scientific Works of Goethe, there was read, commented upon, and discussed the article by Francesco Giorgi, "Scienziaggini," published two days earlier (13 May) by our "Observatory."

Let us now return to our work.

Steiner writes: "In the sciences of the spirit man has to do with his own creations, with the works of culture, of literature, of art, etc. The spiritual is grasped by the spirit. And the sole aim of the sciences of the spirit must be that of making man recognize where destiny has placed him; he has to understand what has already been accomplished and what falls to him to accomplish. By means of the sciences of the spirit he must find the right point at which to participate with his own personality in the work of the world. Man must know the spiritual world and, according to such knowledge, determine his participation in it" (p. 146).


---


It is, then, "by means of the sciences of the spirit" that man can "find the right point at which to participate with his own personality in the work of the world," freely to carry out his mission or to actualize what, in contrast with ought-being, we have called his "will-to-be."

According to Martin Heidegger (1889-1976), however, man's is neither an ought-being nor a will-to-be, but a "being-able-to-be."

What does this mean? That we should not – according to him – orient and determine our existence on the basis of knowledge of the spiritual world, but limit ourselves, as beings placed or "thrown" into the world, to "projecting" it.

Yes, but what does "projecting" mean, if not willing to realize an idea? And is it not perhaps the case that the difference – which lies so close to his heart – between an "authentic" and an "inauthentic" existence is given precisely by the "authenticity" or "inauthenticity" of the idea one wishes to realize?

We could, in fact, define as "authentic" every idea that is a vehicle of the I, and as "inauthentic" every idea that is not. Do you remember what we said in this regard last week? That just as "all that glitters is not gold," so not everything that is thought is "idea," for if in the form of a real idea there lives the (free) force of the I, in that of an apparent idea there lives instead the (karmically determined) force of our personal nature.

For the ego, therefore, one thing is "being-able-to-be," on the basis of subjective opinion, this or that; another is "willing-to-be," on the basis of objective knowing, the I: that is, what really is.

Man, Steiner says, "has to understand what has already been accomplished and what falls to him to accomplish." There is, however, a problem: since "what falls to him to accomplish" is known and willed by the I, but is not known or willed by the ego, it is not sufficient to move about on the surface (on the plane of ordinary consciousness); rather, it is necessary to descend into the depths.

The language of the ego is, in fact, that of representations, whereas the language of the I is that of imaginations, inspirations, and intuitions. It is not possible, therefore, to understand what the I knows and wills, without developing, going beyond the representative, an imaginative, an inspirative, and an intuitive consciousness.

Not being with one's consciousness at the height of imaginations, inspirations, and intuitions can, moreover, prove risky for mental equilibrium.

Think, just to give an example, of the celebrated Austrian psychiatrist and psychoanalyst Wilhelm Reich (1897-1957), who ended his days tragically in the federal penitentiary of Lewisburg (Tennessee). This is a man who had a living presentiment of the etheric reality (called by him "orgonic
                                                   157
energy"), of the activity of the Luciferic and Ahrimanic obstructing forces (called by him "mystico-mechanistic"), and even of the entity of the Christ (seen by him as an ideal "orgonomic" man), but who then constrained and reduced all this within the narrow ambit of naturalistic consciousness (to the point of demanding, unlike Freud and Jung, that psychotherapists hold degrees in the Natural Sciences).

Steiner says again that man, "to participate with his own personality in the work of the world," must "know the spiritual world": which means, more precisely, that he must learn to listen, in the soul, to what the (guardian) Angel says for his good, and what the Archangel says instead for the good of his people (cf. R. Steiner: The Mission of Individual Folk Souls – Antroposofica, Milan 1983).

But how can we learn to listen to them? By learning to master ourselves, so as to reduce to silence all that, rising up from our lower nature, habitually makes noise in the soul. These Beings do, in fact, speak to us, but we do not succeed in listening to them, because the mute sound of their voices (of their imaginations and inspirations) is unfortunately covered over by that of our own chatter (of our own opinions). The more energetic we become in keeping our lower nature in check, the more receptive we therefore render ourselves toward them.

They live and work, in substance, in the unconscious of each of us; and it is for this reason that the science of the spirit would like to help us to raise little by little to consciousness what lives and works in the depths. Freud used to say: "Where the Es was, there the ego shall reign." "Agreed – one could have answered him – but it is certainly not with the cognitive instruments at your disposal that you will succeed in reaching the goal you set for yourself."

The fact is that in order to raise the unconscious contents to consciousness, one must modify (ordinary) consciousness. This, in fact, became master of sensory reality inasmuch as it repressed or thrust back into the unconscious the spiritual reality. How, then, can one dry oneself with a wet towel? How, that is, can one address the contents of the unconscious, know them, and receive them, without modifying that consciousness which became what it is precisely by turning its back upon them?

You see, we normally enjoy one level of consciousness, that of waking, and three levels of unconsciousness: those of dream, of sleep, and of death. Whoever truly wishes to make the I reign in place of the Es is therefore called to develop imaginative consciousness, to illuminate the dream level, inspirative consciousness to illuminate the sleep level, and intuitive consciousness to illuminate that of death. As every speleologist knows, in fact, the deeper one goes, the more light is required.

But be careful: even where the Es reigns in place of the I, consciousness transforms itself, but it does so in a destructive way (generating perversions, neuroses, and psychoses), and not in a creative way (promoting a moral development).

In this light, the ego, with its "aseptic" intellectual consciousness (rationalistic, illuministic, and today "computational"), truly represents – as Nietzsche would say – "a rope stretched between the beast and the Superman"; with the decisive difference, however, that what for Nietzsche was the "Superman" (Übermensch), for us is instead the Logos that lives in the I (in the "Spirit-self").

But let us return to ourselves.


---


After citing, with regard to the "life of the people," a passage from the German politician and man of letters Gustav Freytag (1816-1895), Steiner writes: "If Freytag had investigated this life of the people, he would certainly have discovered that it dissolves into the action of a sum of single individuals who overcome that obscure compulsion, raising the unconscious to their consciousness, and he would have seen how that which he qualified as the obscure compulsion (of a "primordial force" – ed.) proceeds from the impulses of the individual will, from the free action of man, when he spoke of a soul of the people" (pp. 146-147).
                                                  158
You see: "raising the unconscious to their consciousness." But we have just said it: in order truly to raise the unconscious, one must raise oneself from ordinary consciousness; otherwise one ends up, not with raising the unconscious, but with lowering that consciousness, throwing it into chaos and disintegration.

Steiner continues: "But another thing is to be considered with regard to man's action within his people. Every personality represents a spiritual power, a sum of forces that seek the possibility of working. Therefore everyone must find the place where his action can insert itself into his own ethnic organism in the most fruitful way. That he find such a place must not be left to chance. The constitution of the State has no other purpose than to provide that everyone may find an environment suited to his own action. The State is the form in which the organism of a people expresses itself" (p. 147).


---


In effect, the human I is an inexhaustible "energy resource": that is to say, an inexhaustible source of ideas and of actions. The art of social organization should therefore be that of permitting its single members to develop their aptitudes and their talents at their best.

There is, however, only one habitat in which this can happen: and it is the habitat of freedom. That true freedom – needless to say – which is inseparable – as we know by now – both from awareness and from responsibility. It is superfluous to say that this art is completely unknown to all those theocratic, nomocratic, and plutocratic systems which render their own members slaves, respectively, of the spirit, of politics, and of the economy.

And yet, what has been done thus far, if not to substitute economic slavery with political slavery and political slavery with economic slavery, thereby running the risk that, to escape the one as much as the other, one may sooner or later end up falling back into spiritual slavery?

You see, in order for man to be able to develop his own aptitudes and his own talents for the benefit of the community, there would be needed a self-governing spiritual life independent of the political life and of the economic one, just as there would be needed a self-governing political life independent of the spiritual and the economic life, and a self-governing economic life independent of the spiritual and the political one: there would be needed, in short, a threefolding of the social organism (cf. R. Steiner: The Essential Points of the Social Question – Antroposofica, Milan 1980).

It is clear, in any case, that it should be the people that makes the State, and not the State that makes the people, just as it should be the individuals that make the people, and not the people that makes the individuals.

Steiner proceeds: "Ethnography and political science must study the best way in which the single personality can suitably assert itself. The constitution must spring from the inmost essence of a people. The character of the people, expressed in single articles, is the best of state constitutions" (p. 147).

I hope the timeliness of these considerations does not escape you. Many today fear, in fact, that the so-called economic "globalization" may end up annulling the political and cultural identities of the various national States. This well-founded fear leads them, however, for the most part to reject not only the political and cultural globalization, but the economic one as well, and thus to oppose to the one-sidedness of the "pro-global" that of the "no-global."

One does not thereby realize that the economic life could universalize itself without putting at risk the particularity of the political life and the individuality of the spiritual one, only if these three human activities were given the possibility of organizing and developing themselves autonomously, according to their different ideal and material needs.


---


159
In the threefold social organism – proposed by Steiner – the economic life should, in fact, take its inspiration from the "socialist" ideal (from the fraternité), political life from the "democratic" ideal (from the égalité), and spiritual life from the "liberal" ideal (from the liberté).

Goethe observes precisely: "It is in ways of thinking that liberalism must be sought, and these are the living soul" (Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 69).

L.R.
Rome, 15 May 2001

160
The Scientific Works of Goethe (34)

If all goes well, tonight we shall finish the ninth chapter, and begin the tenth, entitled: Relation between Goethean Thinking and Other Conceptions.

Let us therefore resume reading at once.

Steiner writes: "To this we must add yet a few words on the method of history. History must always keep in mind that the causes of historical events are to be sought in the individual intents, designs, etc., of men. To try to derive historical facts from 'designs' residing at the basis of history is an error" (p. 147).

It is, then, an error to attribute the causes of historical events both – as Hegel does – to the "cunning of reason," over which the "World Spirit" would preside, and – as Marx and Engels do – to the "cunning" of matter, over which its "movement" would preside. (Cf. G. W. F. Hegel: Lectures on the Philosophy of History – La Nuova Italia, Florence 1966; K. Marx – F. Engels: The Materialistic Conception of History – Editori Riuniti, Rome 1959.)

In the one case as in the other, the will of individuals would, in fact, be conditioned or instrumentalized ("cunningly") by "designs residing at the basis of history."

To these transcendent and theologizing conceptions, the one idealistic and the other materialistic, we must therefore oppose the immanent and, so to speak, "anthropologizing" conception of the science of the spirit.

Steiner adds, in fact: "It is always a question of the purposes that this or that personality has set for itself, of the paths trodden by it, etc. History must be founded absolutely upon human nature; the wills, the tendencies of human nature are to be studied" (p. 147).

Which means that the impulses, motives, intents, or ideals of individuals "are to be studied," since what presents itself on the historical plane results from the concurrence of multiple and diverse wills.

Steiner writes: "We therefore seek the kernel of existence in man himself. No one reveals to him a dogmatic truth, no one drives him into action. He suffices for himself. All that he is, he must be by his own virtue, not by virtue of another being; he must draw everything from his own inmost depths; therefore also the source of his own happiness (…) Pleasure and displeasure exist for man only insofar as he himself confers upon objects the capacity to arouse in him such feelings. A creator who determined from without what must give us pleasure or displeasure would lead us by the halter like children" (p. 148).


---


But how do we manage to draw our happiness from ourselves? We said it when we concerned ourselves with The Philosophy of Freedom: by transforming, through thinking, the craved object and, through the craved object, the craving.

What is the craved object? It is the object of the craving. Inasmuch as it is an "object," it can therefore be considered either a physical reality or a "thing" (as naive realism does), or a spiritual reality or a "concept" (as the realism of ideas does). And what is the craving, instead? The necessary complement, as warmth without light, of the reflected thinking that characterizes, as light without warmth, ordinary consciousness (and therefore naive realism).

The difference between craving and loving is, then, nothing but the (volitional) counterpart of the difference between reflected thinking and living thinking; so much so that one could almost say: to love things is to crave, whereas to crave concepts (or ideas) is to love.
                                                  161
In order to seek, as Steiner says, "the kernel of existence in man himself," one must therefore seek the kernel of thinking: that is, seek, starting from its ordinary not-being, its being. It is natural, in fact, that one who experiences thinking (and therefore the I) as a not-being then sees himself constrained, being unable to "suffice for himself," to seek an "other" that may support him, as being. He will seek it, of course, outside of thinking and of the conscious, and for that very reason in a sphere (material, sub-material, or spiritual) that transcends them.

Steiner concludes: "With this, all optimism and pessimism are refuted. Optimism supposes the world to be perfect and a source of maximal satisfaction for man (…) Pessimism believes the world to be constituted in such a way as to render man eternally dissatisfied and unhappy. But what a wretched creature man would be if nature had to offer him gratification from without! Every complaint about the existence that renders us unhappy, about the world so harsh with us, must disappear before the thought that no force of the world could gratify us if we ourselves did not first confer upon it the magic power by means of which it uplifts and cheers us. Gratification must come to us from our own creations, from what we ourselves make of things. This alone is worthy of free beings" (pp. 148-149).


---


One thing, in effect, is optimism and pessimism as psychic facts or facts of nature; another is positivity as a spiritual conquest.

You know, in fact, that the sthenic character (especially if underlain by the sanguine temperament) is tendentially optimistic, whereas the asthenic one (especially if underlain by the melancholic temperament) is tendentially pessimistic.

In any case, the problem of the cravings arises again here. For today's "collective consciousness," for example, the idea of the spirit is, yes, noble and beautiful, but also (from the point of view of the cravings) unbearably "Lenten," since it is associated with that of "renunciation," of "penance," or of the "mortification of the flesh."

It is rare, in fact, that the idea of the spirit is associated with the idea of joy: that is, that beatitude which is equivalent, for Scaligero, to the "joy of being"; to a "joy of being" – note well – which certainly does not fail to cast its warmth and its light back upon existing.

One could even say that "eternally dissatisfied and unhappy" is precisely he who seeks the joy of existing directly: without deriving it, that is, from the joy of being; he who asks, in short, of Caesar that he give him not only the joy that Caesar can give, but also the one that only God can give.

We have thus arrived at the end of the ninth chapter. Let us therefore begin the tenth, entitled, as we have said: Relation between Goethean Thinking and Other Conceptions.

Steiner writes: "When one speaks of the influence exercised by earlier or contemporary thinkers upon the evolution of Goethe's spirit, one must not understand it in the sense that he had formed his own opinions by founding them upon their theories. The way in which he was disposed to think and to see the world was inherent in his whole nature, and precisely from earliest youth, and in this respect he then remained equal to himself his whole life long" (p. 151).

This is the problem (to which we once alluded) of the so-called "sources." What did Goethe draw – the historian and the philologist, in fact, ask – from this one, from that one, or from that other one still? Which – allow me the comparison – would be a bit like asking oneself what Titius and Gaius have nourished themselves on from their birth to today. The true problem, in fact, is not that of discovering what they ate, but that of discovering how it comes about that the one used that food to become Titius, and the other to become Gaius.

162
To discover it, however, one would have to bring their individualities (their I's) into the foreground, and not obscure them behind a long, dense, and learned curtain of "sources."

Let us be clear: it is not that it is unimportant to discover, for example, with which authors and with which works Goethe grappled and found himself more or less in tune; but it is important to discover it precisely in order to understand his individuality better, and not to blur or dissolve it.


---


Steiner continues: "Two important characteristics must here be considered in a quite special way. The first is the tendency to arrive at the springs, at the depths of being; in the last analysis, faith in the idea. Goethe is always pervaded by the intuition of something higher and better; and this is a profoundly religious trait of his spirit. He does not know that need, proper to so many people, of lowering things down to himself, stripping them of all holiness. On the contrary, he has the need to intuit higher spheres and to make every effort to raise himself up to them. In everything he seeks to find a side that renders it sacred. K. J. Schröer (1825-1900 – ed.) has shown this in a very spiritual way with regard to Goethe and love. Everything frivolous, light, is eliminated from it, and love becomes, for Goethe, religiosity" (p. 151).

Marcus Aurelius (121-180) used to say: "If man always looked at the sky, he would end by having wings." Well, if we always looked at the sky, if we were, that is, pervaded "by the intuition of something higher and better," we would end, not by having wings, but by becoming undoubtedly more human. How, moreover, could we better ourselves, if not by beginning to love that "better" being which at the moment we are not (except potentially)?

"To lower things down to oneself, stripping them of all holiness," that is to say, to banalize them, desecrate them, or render them precisely mere "things," means therefore to fix oneself in the state (the evolutionary phase) in which one is, thereby preparing the ground for a regression: since life goes forward in any case, if one does not elevate oneself, one will in fact end by declining.

Do you remember what the subtitle of Scaligero's book Grail is? Essay on the Mystery of Sacred Love. Here, then, is "sacredness": that sacredness which, when the soul regresses to psyche (to neurophysiological life), declines precisely to charm, attraction, or craving.

We could also assert, from this point of view, that Goethe's was a "healthy" nature: healthy because realist, certainly not in the sense of naive realism, but in that of critical realism or of the realism of ideas. What does it mean, in fact, to seek to find in everything "a side that renders it sacred," if not to seek to find the real in the appearing, the essence in the manifestation, the noumenon in the phenomenon, or the idea in the perception?

For what reason, for example, does materialism enjoy such success? Because it is true "at first sight," and therefore easy. In order to surpass it, one must, in fact, commit oneself to developing, so to speak, a "second," a "third," and a "fourth" sight (that is, the grades of higher knowledge).

I have said that Goethe's was a "healthy" nature, but I could also have said "holy." Between these two words there is, on close inspection, no great difference. As I have had occasion at other times to recall, the Latin term "salus" means, in fact, both "health" and "salvation," and stands, therefore, to indicate both a state of "sanity" and of "holiness" (as "sanity" of the soul).


---


Steiner writes: "Goethe never seeks to draw near to that higher element in an immediate way; but always through nature (…) Alongside faith in the idea Goethe also has another faith: that we reach the idea through the contemplation of reality; it never occurs to him to seek the Divinity anywhere other than in the works of Nature, but in these he always seeks to discover the divine side" (p. 152).

163
What does it mean not to draw near "to that higher element in an immediate way; but always through nature"? It means drawing near to it, not mystically, but cognitively: in such a way, that is, that that element does not remove us from the world, but rather puts us in communion with it.

Steiner continues: "There is therefore innate in Goethe that way of observing things which we have justified with our theory of knowledge. He draws near to reality with the conviction that everything is only a manifestation of the idea: we grasp the latter only by raising ourselves from the experience of the senses to spiritual contemplation" (p. 152).

This "healthy" conviction of his – we have said – stands in contrast with that of Kant: for Goethe, in fact, the phenomenon does not conceal the noumenon, but reveals it. He writes precisely: "The universal and the particular coincide: the particular is the universal manifesting itself under different conditions" (Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 136).

Certainly, the phenomenon reveals the noumenon only to one who, raising himself "from the experience of the senses to spiritual contemplation," is ready to receive in the soul, together with that of the senses, the revelation of the spirit.


---


Steiner continues: in order to bring "to clear consciousness before himself what he was, in order to arrive at knowing what in him was living action, he questioned the philosophers. He sought in them an explanation and a justification of his own being (…) In his earliest youthful years it seemed to the poet that his own being was expressed above all in Spinoza and in Giordano Bruno (…) Bruno considers universal reason as the generator and director of the universe. He calls it the inner artist, the one that shapes matter and form from within. It is the cause of everything that exists, and there is no being in whose life it does not participate with love (…) Such, too, was Goethe's opinion, namely that we can judge of a thing only when we see how, from universal reason, it has been placed in its position, and in what way it has become precisely such as it presents itself to us (…) Not otherwise Spinoza. It is the foundation of his doctrine that the Divinity has poured itself out into the world; therefore human knowledge can have no other aim than to immerse itself in the world in order to know God (…) God has renounced every existence of His own; He is nowhere other than in the world (…) Therefore every true knowing must be such as to transmit to us, in every fragment of knowledge of the world, a fragment of knowledge of God. Knowing, at its highest grade, is therefore a uniting with the Divinity (…) The laws that our spirit discovers in nature are therefore God in His essence; they are not only created by Him" (pp. 152-154).

To say, as Steiner does here, that "the laws that our spirit discovers in nature are therefore God in His essence, not only created by Him" is equivalent to saying that the thinking we discover in nature is God "in His essence," and not merely a thing created by Him: that it is, that is, subject, and not object.

What, for example, is Theology? It is the science of the nature of God. But can God – let us ask ourselves – be the object of a science, without being at the same time its subject? Can he be, that is, the known, without being at the same time the knowing and the knower?

He can (and in fact he is); as such, however, he is not the God-subject, living in knowing, but that God-object which the intellect has buried in the known, and of which Nietzsche celebrated precisely the death, but not (unfortunately) the resurrection.

Steiner rightly says: "Knowing, at its highest grade, is therefore a uniting with the Divinity." In knowing, in fact, the being of man encounters the being of the world, and from this their re-union the spirit is born: that is, self-conscious being.


---


164
With regard, still, to the laws that our spirit discovers in nature, we must recall that for the atheists or the materialists they are facts "of nature," with which God has nothing to do, whereas for the believers or the spiritualists they are creations, from which God remains outside.

But why do they think He remains outside? Because they do not know how to see – as Steiner would say – how, "from universal reason," things have been placed in their position, and in what way they have become such as they present themselves to us; because they do not know how to distinguish, in short, the various grades of the divine presence in the world.

I think you know, in this regard, that Steiner distinguishes four of them instead: the grade of the "divine-spiritual Entity" (the "I am who I am!" – Ex 3:14); the grade of "manifestation" (astral); the grade of the "working effect" (etheric); and that of the "completed work" (physical).

And which of these is the grade of the laws? That of "manifestation" (of the entelechies, the essences, or the ideas), in which – Steiner specifies – the divine-spiritual Entity "lives" and "plots" (Anthroposophical Maxims – Antroposofica, Milan 1969, pp. 85-89). This grade, in the terms of Hegelian logic, we could consider equivalent to that of "Quality" or of "determinate being," in which – Hegel explains – "determinateness has not yet dissolved itself from being" (Science of Logic – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1974, vol. I, p. 105).

Steiner writes: "Kant is generally regarded as the founder of today's philosophy. In his time he aroused so grandiose a movement that every cultivated person felt the need to take up a position with respect to him. This became a necessity for Goethe as well, for whom, however, it could not be fruitful, on account of the profound dissension between the teachings of Kantian philosophy and the Goethean way of thinking as we know it. One can even say that the whole of German thought unfolds in two parallel directions, of which the one is steeped, so to speak, in Kantian thinking, and the other is close to the thinking of Goethe" (p. 155).

And here Steiner presents us a brief and lucid synthesis of Kant's gnoseology, which will be worth our while to read in its entirety.


---


Steiner writes precisely: "For Kant the point of departure of human thinking is experience, that is to say, the world that offers itself to the senses (among which is included the inner sense that transmits to us psychic, historical facts, etc.). This given world is a plurality of objects in space and of processes in time. It does not matter which object presents itself to my eyes or which process I experience; it could also be otherwise; I can even think the whole multiplicity of objects and processes as non-existent. But what I cannot eliminate by thinking are space and time. For me there can be nothing that is not spatial or temporal. Even if an object without space or time existed, I could know nothing of it, for without space and time I can represent nothing to myself. Whether time and space are to be attributed to things or not, I do not know; but I do know that for me things must appear in these forms. Thus space and time are the premises of my sensory perception. I know nothing of the thing in itself; what I know is how it must appear to me if it is to exist for me. With these sentences Kant inaugurates a new problem; indeed, a new posing of problems. Instead of asking, like the earlier philosophers: 'How are things constituted?', he asks: 'How must things appear to us in order that they may become the object of our knowing?' For Kant philosophy is the science of the conditions under which the world is possible as a human phenomenon. Of the thing in itself we know nothing. We have not yet fulfilled our task when we have arrived as far as the sensory perception of a plurality in time and space; we aspire to gather that plurality into a unity. And this is the task of the intellect. The intellect is to be understood as a sum of activities having the aim of summarizing the sensory world according to certain forms pre-traced in the intellect itself. It unites two sensory perceptions, designating, for example, the one as cause, the other as effect, or else the one as substance, the other as quality, etc. Here too it is the task of philosophical science
                                                  165
to show under what conditions the intellect can form for itself a system of the world. According to Kant, therefore, the world is a subjective phenomenon that presents itself in the forms of the sensory world and of the intellect. One fact alone is certain: that a thing in itself exists; how it appears to us depends upon our organization. It is, therefore, naturally absurd to attribute to that world which the intellect forms in union with the senses a value beyond that which it has for our faculty of knowing. This becomes clearer than ever where Kant speaks of the significance of the world of ideas. For him ideas are nothing but higher points of view of reason, under which the inferior unities created by the intellect are comprehended. For example: the intellect connects with one another the phenomena of the soul; our reason, as the faculty of conceiving ideas, understands that connection as if everything emanated from a single soul. But this has no importance for the thing itself; it is only a means of orientation for our cognitive faculty. Such is the content of Kant's theoretical philosophy insofar as it can interest us here" (pp. 155-156).


---


For Kant, therefore, knowledge has a purely formal value: forms ("sensory intuitions") are, in fact, space and time; forms ("a priori") are the concepts of the intellect; and forms (a priori) again are the ideas (the "categories") of reason.

From this it follows that knowledge, with regard to the reality of the world (of the "thing in itself"), is illusory, whereas, with regard to man, it is useful.

You will recall, in any case, that when we treated The Philosophy of Freedom, I said that the "key" to the relation between naive realism, Kant's criticism, and empirical idealism or (as I prefer to say) Steiner's "logodynamics" resides principally in the way in which the representation is understood. (For the concept of "logodynamics," consult the study of The Philosophy of Freedom published by our website – note.)

For naive realism, the representation is, in fact, a passive re-production (a photograph) of the object; for Kant, it is instead an active production of the subject; whereas, for Steiner, it is, yes, a production of the subject, but as the result of the encounter, in his soul, of the outer object (the percept) with the inner object (the concept).

What it is most important to note, however, is that these differences derive from three different anthropological conceptions.

For naive realism, man is, in fact, only body; for Kant, he is instead body and soul (since the spirit is transcendent); whereas, for Steiner, he is body, soul, and spirit (I).

The first, therefore, does not permit us to become conscious of the reality of the soul and of the spirit; the second does not permit us to become conscious of the reality of the (immanent) spirit; whereas the third, permitting us to become conscious of body, soul, and spirit (of the I), allows us at last to become conscious both of the reality of the world and of that of ourselves.

I notice that we have stayed later than usual. We shall therefore continue next time.

L.R.
Rome, 22 May 2001

166
The Scientific Works of Goethe (35)

Tonight we shall continue the reading of the tenth chapter, resuming our talk of Kant.

Steiner writes: "Kant eludes the true gnoseological problem. At the beginning of his critique of reason he takes two steps that he does not justify, and this error contaminates the whole philosophical edifice of his theory. He enunciates at once the distinction between object and subject, without asking himself what significance there is, in general, in the fact that the intellect undertakes the separation of two fields of reality (knowing subject and object to be known). Then he seeks to establish conceptually the reciprocal relation of these two fields, here too without asking himself what sense such a specification has" (p. 157).


---


The first false step taken by Kant was, therefore, not that of having taken his start from the subject-object dualism, but that of having considered it originary, and therefore absolute and not derived, and for that very reason relative (to a particular phase of development of human consciousness).

It would, in fact, suffice to observe children in order to realize that, in their first period of life, they do not at all contrapose themselves, as "subjects," to things, as "objects," but that they need time in order to arrive lucidly at distinguishing themselves, as I's, from the not-I.

This dualistic consciousness, absent at the beginning both of ontogenetic evolution and of phylogenetic evolution, is therefore formed in time. But how?

To understand it, we can imagine that from the I, situated at the center (at the level of the heart), there depart, so to speak, two "currents" of force: one directed toward the head (toward the neuro-sensory system), the other toward the abdomen (toward the metabolic and limb system).

Inasmuch as they are emanated from the I, these are currents of warmth and of light. The one, however, meets, in the head, an organization that receives the light and repels the warmth, whereas the other meets, in the abdomen, an organization that receives the warmth and repels the light.

Where the light is received and the warmth repelled, the consciousness of the I or of the subject (of the ego) is born, whereas where the warmth is received and the light repelled there is born, so to speak, the consciousness of the not-I or of the object: that is, the non-consciousness of the I or of the subject.

It follows that the I, where it is conscious of itself, is not alive, since it is conscious only of its form (without force) reflected in the cerebral mirror, whereas where it is alive it is not conscious of itself, to the point of arriving at believing that the subject of this force (without form) is a transcendent not-I of a spiritual or material nature.

Hegel notes precisely: "Since in non-philosophical consciousness the I does not have the consciousness of its own activity in representing the object, it represents its own activity as the alien activity of the not-I" (Lectures on the History of Philosophy – La Nuova Italia, Florence 1981, vol. 3, II, p. 356).

Is it not significant, for example, that the celebrated Austrian neurologist and psychiatrist Viktor Frankl (1905-1997) entitled one of his books: God in the Unconscious (Morcelliana, Brescia 1975)? That he, that is, confined God in that very unconscious into which Kant confined the "thing in itself" and the "Transcendental Subject," Schopenhauer the "Will," and Eduard von Hartmann matter and spirit?

Here, then, is how there takes form that dualism between subject and object which Descartes, inaugurating modernity, had already posed in the terms of the "res cogitans" and the "res extensa."


---


Steiner proceeds, in fact: if Kant "had not avoided the principal problem of the theory of knowledge, he would have observed that the separation of subject and object is nothing but a point of transition of our knowing; that both have at their basis a more profound unity, graspable with our reason, and that what is recognized as the quality of a thing, inasmuch as it is thought in relation to a knowing subject, has by no means a subjective value. The
                                                  167
thing is a unity for reason, and the scission into a 'thing in itself' and a 'thing for us' is a product of the intellect" (p. 157).

But if "the separation of subject and object is nothing but a point of transition of our knowing," then today's "cerebral" (intellectual) thinking is nothing but a "point of transition" between the past "pre-cerebral" (mythic) thinking and the future "post-cerebral" (imaginative, inspirative, and intuitive) thinking.

Not having realized that the separation of subject and object is only a product of cerebral thinking, what, then, did Kant do? He sought "to establish conceptually the reciprocal relation of these two fields": he sought, that is, to form for himself, of that relation, a (subjective) opinion or an abstract idea.

And this was his second false step.

Steiner observes, in fact: "One error that permeates the whole edifice of the Kantian doctrine is this: that he considers the sensory multiplicity as something stable, and believes that science consists in reducing that whole multiplicity to a system. He does not so much as suspect that the plurality is not an ultimate quid; that it is, on the contrary, to be surpassed in order to be able to comprehend it; therefore theory is for him simply an addition that the intellect and reason bring to experience (…) According to the Kantian views we would be mistaken in wishing to consider things as derived from the idea; we can do no more than order our experiences as if they originated from a unity. Of the ground of things, of the 'in itself,' we have, according to Kant, not so much as an inkling. Our knowledge of things exists solely in relation to us; it is valid only for our individuality" (pp. 157-158).


---


To form for oneself a (subjective) opinion or an abstract idea of the relation between subject and object, elaborating on this basis a theory that "adds itself" to experience, means, however, prejudging the solution of a problem upon which depends, essentially, the future of human consciousness and the destiny of man.

Not without reason did Scaligero entitle one of his books: Healing with Thinking (Mediterranee, Rome 1993). But healing from what? Certainly not from a "slight indisposition," but rather from that "unhappy consciousness divided within itself" (Hegel) that characterizes, as the expression of the "old Adam" (Paul), either Nietzsche's "human, all too human," or Malraux's (1901-1976) "human condition."

"Old Adam," then, is both he who, to find the world, loses himself (as happens to the naive realist), and he who, to find himself, loses the world (as happens to Kant).

Knowledge – Scaligero writes – is "the passage to love within the limit of nature"; and he adds: "The power of the I is to be from the ground; but it is so when it immerses itself in the world, loses itself in the other, the I being that which the other seeks as ground. Therefore, in giving itself, the I actualizes its infinity: it fills the space that separates it from the other and by which the other is other" (On Immortal Love – Rome 1982, pp. 15 and 16).

The difference between Goethe and Kant is, in the end, all here: the knowledge of the former is, in fact, the passage to love of the world or of the other, whereas that of the latter is the passage to love of oneself.

Steiner observes precisely: "While in Kant every judgment about things (…) permits us to know only how the subject regards the object, in Goethe the subject immerses itself without regard to itself in the object, and draws the data of the judgment from the circle of the objects themselves" (p. 158).

But let us now leave Kant, and come to Schiller.

168
Steiner writes: "Up to the celebrated first conversation with Schiller (consult the 6th meeting – note), Goethe had been accustomed to contemplate the world in a certain way. He had observed the plants, had laid at the basis of his study the type-plant, and from it the single vegetable forms derived. This type-plant (and also a corresponding type-animal) had taken form in his spirit and served him to explain the relative phenomena. But he had never reflected upon what that type-plant was according to its nature. Schiller opened his eyes to him, saying: It is an idea. Only from that moment is Goethe conscious of his own idealism. And if, up to the moment of that conversation, he had called the type-plant an experience, because he believed he saw it with his eyes, later, in the introduction to the essay on the metamorphosis of plants, he says: 'Thus I now sought the type-animal, that is to say, in the last analysis, the concept, the idea of the animal'" (pp. 158-159).


---


Goethe was, then, ready, receiving Schiller's suggestion, to open his eyes. What do you say? Would Jung, Eccles, and Boncinelli have been equally ready to do so, had someone suggested to them that those hypothetical realities they have called, respectively, "archetypes in themselves," "psychons," and "psychostates" are, in truth, nothing but ideas?

But let us pass on to Fichte.

Steiner writes: "Goethe derived little benefit from Fichte; for the latter moved in a sphere too alien to Goethe's thinking to be of use to him"; his theory presents itself, in fact, "as a one-sided idealism that takes its entire content from consciousness. Goethe, who always aimed at the objective, could find very little attraction in the Fichtean philosophy of consciousness. He lacked the comprehension for the field where it has value, while the extension of universal science that Fichte gave it could not appear to the poet as anything but an error" (p. 159).

The fact is that Fichte succeeds in bringing the reality of the I into focus, but not in giving an account of the way in which the I enters into relation, by means of sensory perception, with the reality of the world. It almost seems that Fichte remained dazzled by the (solar) reality of the I, and that, for this reason, he did not succeed in seeing anything else. It is understandable, therefore, that Goethe, by reason of his profound need to enter into communion with empirical reality, did not succeed in feeling him spiritually near.

Different is the case of Schelling.


---


Steiner writes: "Many more points of contact did Goethe have with the Schelling of the first manner (of the years from 1792 to 1799 – ed.). This one was a disciple of Fichte, but not only did he carry the analysis of the activity of the I further, he also pursued within consciousness the activity by which consciousness grasps nature (arriving at the concept of the Absolute as the "identity of I and nature" – ed.). What unfolds in the I when it knows nature was, for Schelling, at the same time the objective element of nature, its true and proper principle (the "slumbering spirit" – ed.). Real nature was for him only a consolidated form of our concepts about nature. What lives in us as a conception of nature reappears to us outside ourselves, only spread out in space and time. That which comes toward us from without as nature is a finished product, is only the conditioned, the rigidified form of a living principle. This principle we cannot obtain from without through experience; we must first create it in our inner being (…) From this doctrine, which Goethe learned in part from Schelling's writings, in part from his personal relations with the philosopher, the poet was once again carried a step higher. There now developed in him the opinion that his tendency was to progress from the already accomplished, from the product, to the producing, to that which is in the course of becoming. And with a decided appeal to Schelling he asserts, in his essay on Intuitive Judgment, that he strives with every effort 'to render himself worthy, through contemplation, of a nature ever creating, to participate spiritually in its creations' (pp. 159-160).

169
What, in effect, is that which appears outside ourselves "spread out in space and time" as nature? It is – as the science of the spirit teaches – our own past. Our past is, in fact, the animal kingdom; our past is the vegetable kingdom; and our past is the mineral one. The spirit that is and that becomes in us, in knowing nature, therefore re-cognizes its own history, thereby arriving at consciousness of itself; and it will be by starting from consciousness of itself that it will one day be able to re-create nature.

Just as what today is nature was once spirit, so what is now spirit will one day be nature.

But what will be, essentially, that which will be born in this way from man? It is soon said: the son of man.

After Kant, Schiller, Fichte, and Schelling, let us now come to Hegel.


---


Steiner writes: "And, finally, the last advance on the part of philosophy came to Goethe from Hegel. By means of Hegel, in fact, he understood how that which he called the primordial phenomenon inserted itself into philosophy – Hegel having understood in the most profound way its importance (…) Thus, thanks to Hegel, there becomes clear to Goethe the idea that the empirical investigator must arrive as far as the primordial phenomena, and that from there the ways of the philosopher proceed. But from this it also becomes evident that the fundamental thought of Hegelian philosophy is a consequence of Goethe's way of thinking. The surpassing of the human, the deepening into it in order to ascend from the created to the creating, from the conditioned to the conditioning, lies at the basis of Goethean thinking, but also of Hegel's. In the end, Hegel, in his philosophy, wishes to offer nothing other than the eternal process from which proceeds all that is finite. He wishes to recognize the given as a consequence of that which he can admit as absolute" (pp. 160-161).


---


It is true: "the fundamental thought of Hegelian philosophy is a consequence of Goethe's way of thinking"; nevertheless, the "scientific" spirit that animated Goethe is other than the "philosophical" one that animated Hegel.

Not without reason did Steiner, while recognizing the immense stature of Hegel (to the point of arriving at defining him "the greatest philosopher in the world" – Philosophy and Anthroposophy – Antroposofica, Milan 1980, p. 27), nevertheless take his start from Goethe, declaring: "I must attribute particular value to the fact that attention be paid here, at this point, that I have taken thinking as my point of departure, and not the concepts and ideas, which can be conquered only by means of thinking, and therefore already presuppose thinking. Therefore one cannot apply without further ado to concepts what I have said with regard to the nature of thinking, which rests upon nothing but itself, is determined by nothing. (I make this observation expressly because in this consists my difference from Hegel: he posits, in fact, the concept as the first and originary element)" (The Philosophy of Freedom – Antroposofica, Milan 1966, pp. 48-49).

But that is not all. In another place, he has, in fact, also asserted: "Whoever today were a Hegelian and wished to carry Hegel's thinking into humanity in one form or another, would succeed in drying up the progress of our civilization. Whoever instead, in the inmost depths of his soul, makes Hegel's subtle way of forming thoughts his own, and on that basis takes the step that Hegel could not take, that of penetrating into the spirit, does what is right, does what is in the sense of the progress of humanity" (Answers of the Science of the Spirit to Social and Pedagogical Problems – Antroposofica, Milan 1974, p. 171).

It is precisely in order to exercise oneself in making "Hegel's subtle way of forming thoughts one's own" that I as a rule recommend the study of the Science of Logic or of the part of the Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences dedicated to logic.

Just think: Scaligero told me one day that in certain Zen monasteries Hegel's Science of Logic is even used for the purposes of meditation.

170
We could therefore say, if we wished, that Hegel is more a "mystic" or a "theosophist" of thinking, whereas Goethe is more a "realist" or an "anthroposophist" (ante litteram) of thinking.

The fact is that it is not, after all, so arduous to give a speculative account of matter starting from the spirit or of the spirit starting from matter; arduous, rather, is to succeed in really experiencing matter in the spirit and the spirit in matter (the thing in the idea and the idea in the thing).

L.R.
Rome, 30 May 2001

171
The Scientific Works of Goethe (36)

Last week we concerned ourselves with Kant, Schiller, Fichte, Schelling, and Hegel; tonight, continuing the tenth chapter, we shall concern ourselves briefly with Schopenhauer and, more at length, with Eduard von Hartmann.


---


Steiner writes: "Schopenhauer leans upon the poet, whom he supremely esteemed, at many points. We shall speak in a later chapter of his apologia for the Theory of Colors. What matters here is the general relation of Schopenhauer's theory to Goethe. At one point the philosopher of Frankfurt draws near to Goethe, namely where Schopenhauer rejects every derivation from outer causes of the phenomena given to us and admits only inner laws and the derivation of one phenomenon from another. This apparently is equivalent to the Goethean principle of taking from the things themselves the data for explaining them; but precisely only apparently. For Schopenhauer wishes to remain within the phenomenal, because, according to him, we cannot reach in knowing the 'in itself' that lies outside of it, since all the phenomena given to us are nothing but our representations, and our faculty of representation never leads us beyond our consciousness; Goethe, instead, wishes to remain in the field of the phenomena, because it is precisely there that he seeks in them the data for explaining them" (pp. 161-162).


---


"Schopenhauer – Steiner says – wishes to remain within the phenomenal, because, according to him, we cannot reach in knowing the 'in itself' that lies outside of it, since all the phenomena given to us are nothing but our representations": agreed, but is it not perhaps the case that by reaching the "in itself" of the representations one can also reach the "in itself" of the phenomena?

Do you remember what we said a couple of times ago? That the "key" to the relation between naive realism, Kant's criticism, and empirical idealism or (as I prefer to say) Steiner's "logodynamics" resides precisely in the way in which the representation is understood.

Well, Schopenhauer understands it in the same manner as Kant, even if, unlike the latter, he understands the "in itself" as "will," and not as "thing."

Schopenhauer, too, therefore, did not realize that the "in itself" of the representation is the concept: that very concept which constitutes – as we have seen – the essence or the "in itself" of the phenomenon.

Not only did he not realize it, he even gave way to inveighing, losing his composure, against one who – like Hegel – saw precisely in the concept the essence of the real.

As a philosopher of the will, he had, in fact, an aversion to Hegel, as a philosopher of that thinking which he, in contrast to the force of willing, experienced only as empty form, and therefore as not-being.

The fact is that, in observing nature, we should – we have said it – win for ourselves the capacity to discern thinking in willing, since it is precisely thinking in willing that constitutes the "law" of the phenomenon: that is to say, the form or the way in which it unfolds. The heart and the liver, for example, are, yes, inasmuch as they are alive, "volitional" organs, but the laws that regulate the functions of the former are not the same as those that regulate the functions of the latter.

Well, what are these laws (in themselves), if not living thoughts: that is, thinking wills? And what, then, is (organic) nature, if not precisely a great, living, and unconscious organism of thinking?

A great, living, and unconscious organism of thinking which, however, is not able, except in the case of man, to think itself, and which precisely for this reason exists as the kingdom of necessity, and not of freedom. The existence of every natural being is, in fact, determined directly by the idea, whereas that of the human being is determined indirectly by his degree of consciousness of the idea.

In any case, just as it was necessary, in order to penetrate the (inorganic) kingdom of unconscious dead thinking, to develop conscious dead thinking (to the point of becoming, in this very sphere
                                                  172
of death, technically creative), so it is necessary, in order to penetrate the (organic) kingdom of unconscious living thinking, to develop conscious living thinking.

Goethe notes in this regard: "The field of the mathematician is the quantitative; it embraces, that is, everything that can be determined with number and measure, and therefore in a certain sense the externally knowable universe. But if, insofar as our capacity allows us, we observe this with all our intellect and our forces, we recognize that quantity and quality must be considered as the two poles of the apparent world; consequently the mathematician gives to the language of his formulas such an amplitude as to embrace, as far as is possible for him, within the measurable world the incommensurable world. Everything therefore appears to him accessible, tangible, mechanical, and he renders himself suspect of a veiled atheism in believing himself able to comprehend at the same time also the incommensurable, which we call God, and thus seeming to deny the existence of that particular and exalted Being" (Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 241).

But let us pass on to von Hartmann.


---


Steiner writes: "Hartmann's philosophy is idealism. It is true that he does not wish to be purely an idealist. Nevertheless, when he needs something positive to explain the world, he still has recourse to the idea, and, what matters most, thinks the idea as that which lies at the basis of everything. For his assuming an unconscious wants only to assert that what exists in our consciousness as idea is not necessarily bound to this form of manifestation within consciousness. The idea is not existent (effective) only there where it is conscious, but also in another form. It is more than a mere subjective phenomenon; it has an importance founded in itself. It is not only present in the subject, but is an objective principle of the world" (p. 163).


---


Let us keep in mind that Eduard von Hartmann (by reason of his Philosophy of the Unconscious) is generally regarded, together with Schelling, Schopenhauer, and Carl Gustav Carus (1789-1869), as a precursor of psychoanalysis.

I doubt, however, that he would have welcomed such a recognition, because in the unconscious in which von Hartmann saw the "ideas," Freud believed he saw instead the "instincts," and Jung the "archetypes in themselves": that is to say, hypothetical entities of a mysterious and inscrutable nature.

Neither the founder of psychoanalysis nor that of analytical psychology made their own, therefore, the most precious content of von Hartmann's thinking: that is, the conviction that ideas, as realities in themselves, are present and active in us even when we are not conscious of them. (It is to say the least disconcerting, moreover, that Jung, despite the lesson of Leibniz [1646-1716] concerning "empirical apperception," and that of Kant concerning "transcendental apperception" [or the "I think"], did not have clearly in mind the difference between consciousness and self-consciousness. He writes, in fact: "The very thought that there exists an enormous difference between the consciousness of the existence of an object and the 'consciousness of consciousness' of an object borders on a subtlety that will very hardly be able to find any correspondence" – introduction to D. T. Suzuki: Introduction to Zen Buddhism – Ubaldini, Rome 1970, p. 21.)

The fact is that one thing is the idea, another the consciousness of the idea.

And what consciousness do we normally have of the being of the idea? Paradoxically, that of its not-being: that is, that of its mere image given back to us by the cerebral mirror. Inasmuch as we take note of it only at the moment in which its extinguished reflected image (its representation) appears in the physical body (in the brain), we are ignorant, in fact, of the existence of the idea for the whole time in which it is present in a living way in the astral body and in the etheric body.

Let us think, for example, of the idea of freedom: so long as it does not activate itself, we do not at all feel the need for freedom; when it "grows virulent" (as Jung would say), when, that is, it begins to press to be realized, we instead feel the urgent need – as the saying goes – to "give it body."
                                                  173
But what does it mean to "realize" an idea? It means bringing toward the force of the idea, rising up from the (unconscious) sphere of the will, the form of the idea, descending from the (conscious) sphere of thinking.

Woe, therefore, to bring toward the force of freedom a form different from the one that originally and objectively belongs to it (the living idea being a unity of form and of force), since it is precisely in this way that arbitrariness and license are born: that is to say, further and more deceitful forms of slavery. "No one is more a slave – Goethe says precisely – than he who considers himself free without being so" (Maxims and Reflections, p. 38).

Which holds – naturally – for everything that presents itself in us as an impulse. What, in fact, is an "impulse"? It is a dark idea (an idea of which one feels the force, but of which one does not know the form). And what, instead, is an idea? A luminous impulse (an idea of which one feels the force and of which one knows the form).

It follows that a "weak" thinking, or one devoid of force (like ordinary thinking), will never be able to unite itself creatively with a willing devoid of form.


---


Steiner continues: "Although, alongside the idea, Hartmann also admits the will, among the constitutive principles of the world, it is still incomprehensible that there should still be philosophers who regard him as a Schopenhauerian (…) The single being, the particular phenomenon, cannot interest Schopenhauer, since of them he can say nothing essential other than that they are a configuration of the will. Hartmann, instead, grasps every particular existence and shows how everywhere the idea is to be perceived. The fundamental trait of Schopenhauer's conception is uniformity; of Hartmann's conception, unity. Schopenhauer posits at the basis of the world a uniform impulse, empty of content; Hartmann the whole rich content of the idea" (p. 163).

To be noted, at the beginning of this passage, is that "alongside." For what reason does Steiner emphasize it? Because Schopenhauer sees the will and not the idea, whereas von Hartmann sees the one and the other, but sees the will precisely alongside the idea, and not in the idea.

Steiner continues: "With his objective idealism Ed. v. Hartmann stands totally upon the ground of the Goethean conception of the world. When Goethe says: 'All the things we perceive and of which we speak are only manifestations of the idea' (Prose Sayings), and when he demands that man educate in himself a faculty of knowledge such that the idea becomes evident to him, as outer perception does to the senses, he stands upon that ground where the idea is not only a phenomenon of consciousness, but an objective principle of the world; thinking is the coming-to-light within consciousness of that which objectively constitutes the world. The essential in the idea is, therefore, not what it is for us, for our consciousness, but what it is in itself. For by its own proper nature it stands as a principle at the basis of the world. Therefore thinking is the perception of what is in itself and for itself. Although, then, the idea would not at all manifest itself if consciousness did not exist, it must nevertheless be conceived in such a way that its characteristic does not lie in being conscious, but in what it is and has in itself, independently of its becoming conscious. Therefore, according to Ed. v. Hartmann, we must posit at the basis of the world the idea, as unconscious and operative, prescinding from its becoming conscious. The essential, in Hartmann, is that one seek the idea in everything that is devoid of consciousness" (p. 164).


---


As we shall never tire of repeating, one thing, then, is the idea, another the consciousness of the idea (just as one thing is consciousness, another self-consciousness).

What, in fact, are ideas for ordinary consciousness? They are representations, and therefore mere images of "things" (for naive realism) or of "things in themselves" (for criticism).

And what are they, instead, for imaginative consciousness? They are the real contents of the imaginations or of the symbols; Goethe says precisely: "True symbolism is that in which the particular element
                                                  174
represents the more general, not as dream and shadow, but as a living and instantaneous revelation of the inscrutable" (Maxims and Reflections, p. 87).

And for inspirative consciousness? They are qualities. And for intuitive consciousness? They are "I's": that is, beings or spiritual entities.

How much road there is to travel, then, before arriving at a full consciousness of the reality of the ideas! In order to undertake this path, the first thing to do is, in any case, to cross the threshold that divides the (physical) sphere of the (conscious) representations from the (etheric) sphere of the (unconscious or pre-conscious) imaginations.

To seek "the idea in everything that is devoid of consciousness" means, in fact, nothing other than to seek it beyond both the perceptive images and the representations (beyond, that is, the "blinkers" that limit the horizon of ordinary consciousness).

Steiner observes precisely: "In distinguishing between conscious and unconscious one has not yet done much; this distinction, in fact, holds only for our consciousness. One must confront the idea in its objectivity, in the whole fullness of its content; it is not enough to recognize that the idea acts unconsciously, one must seek what this quid that acts is"; von Hartmann recognizes, in fact, that "the idea is not to be understood only as unconscious, but that in that which one must nevertheless call unconscious one must immerse oneself and, transcending this quality, reach its concrete content in order to derive from it the world of the particular phenomena. Thus from the abstract monist that he still is in his Philosophy of the Unconscious, Hartmann has evolved as far as the concrete monist. And it is the concrete idea that Goethe cites under three forms: primordial phenomenon, type, and 'idea in the strictest sense'" (pp. 164-165).


---


What, then, is the unconscious? The thinking we do not yet know how to think, the consciousness of which we are not yet conscious, or the I of which we do not yet know, and which we therefore believe to be a not-I. And what is the unconscious idea (that which "acts unconsciously")? The idea of which we are not yet able to form an idea for ourselves.

Scaligero rightly writes: "One can speak of the unconscious only on condition that it be the act of consciousness, as a guarantee of the conscious Principle in experience, not of its elimination. The possibility of the experience of the 'unconscious,' as a moment of inquiry of self-consciousness, must be guaranteed by a superior experience of consciousness, or by the Science of the Spirit" (Psychotherapy – Perseo, Rome 1974, p. 17).

Which means that "the possibility of the experience of the 'unconscious,' as a moment of inquiry of self-consciousness, must be guaranteed" by a thinking capable of penetrating, and thereby raising to consciousness, what we experience naturally (and for that very reason unconsciously) in the state of dream (REM sleep), of sleep (non-REM), and of death.

But this is precisely what one can do by developing, respectively, imaginative thinking, inspirative thinking, and intuitive thinking.

You will recall that, in concerning ourselves with The Philosophy of Freedom, we compared the levels of consciousness to the various floors of a house, and thinking to the stairs that permit us to ascend (noetically) and to descend (ethically) from the one to the other.

In knowing, we ascend, in fact, from the thing to the idea (from the singular to the universal); in acting, we descend in the opposite direction, from the idea to the thing (from the universal to the singular).

Answer to a question

It is true – as Steiner specifies (see 12th meeting – note) – that the idea is a "configuration" created by reason, while the concept is "the single thought as it is fixed by the intellect"; no less true, however, is it that the idea, inasmuch as it is precisely a "configuration" or "whole" of concepts, is not

175
qualitatively different from the latter. If one were to compare the concept to a "star," the idea would, in fact, be a "constellation."

Steiner asserts precisely: "Everything we discern and of which we can speak is only a manifestation of the idea; we express concepts, and in this sense the idea itself is a concept" (Fundamental Lines of a Gnoseology of the Goethean Conception of the World, in Philosophical Essays – Antroposofica, Milan 1974, p. 69).

L.R.
Rome, 5 June 2001

176
The Scientific Works of Goethe (37)


---


We left off, last week, speaking of Eduard von Hartmann and of the relations between the idea, the conscious, and the unconscious.

Given the importance of the theme, before resuming our reading I should like to make a few observations.

We know that Steiner places the beginning of the evolutionary phase of the consciousness soul in 1413, and that of the scientific mentality between 1440, the year in which the De docta ignorantia of Nicholas of Cusa (1400/1401-1464) sees the light, and 1543, the year in which the De revolutionibus orbium coelestium of Nicolaus Copernicus (1473-1543) sees the light.

He asserts, in fact: "Whoever wishes to understand what led to the birth of natural science must study in an intelligent way the century that stands between the De docta ignorantia and the De revolutionibus orbium coelestium" (Birth and Historical Development of Science – Antroposofica, Milan 1982, p. 21).

From the philosophical point of view, the so-called "modernity" (as expression of the consciousness soul) is, instead, inaugurated by Descartes (1596-1650).

Well, what is the first characteristic of his conception? That of being founded upon the "cogito": that is to say, upon the activity of thinking. In saying: "Cogito, ergo sum," it is, in fact, from the activity of thinking that the consciousness of the I is derived.

But upon what type of thinking does Descartes found himself? Upon the mathematical and geometric thinking that is, on the one hand, "clear" and "distinct," but, on the other, merely "formal."

And what is formal thinking? It is a thinking from whose form the content (the substance) has been extracted, a bit like a citrus fruit from which the juice has been extracted, and of which only the rind remains.

This is what happens when one has consciousness, not of real thinking, but only of its image reflected in the cerebral organ (in the cortex).

Let us try to think, for example, of the image of ourselves that the mirror gives back to us. Inasmuch as it is devoid of content, of substance, of thickness, or of body, is it not mere form or appearance, and therefore a not-being?

And what consciousness of the I can one derive from a merely formal thinking, if not a consciousness that gives us, of the I, precisely the form, but not the force (the being)?

Do you have in mind Steiner's critique of the "cogito"? If it were true – he says – that I am inasmuch as I think, then during sleep I ought not to be, inasmuch as I do not think.

The fact is that one thing is reflected thinking, of whose activity we are conscious in the waking state; another is living or real thinking, of whose activity we are semi-conscious (or sub-conscious) in the dream state and altogether unconscious in that of sleep.

What, then, is Cartesian consciousness? The tip of that iceberg which Freud used to compare, for its emerged part, precisely to the conscious, and, for the submerged one, to the unconscious.

For a long time, this "emerged part" represented (for better and for worse) a sort of "limbo" or "neutral field" in which one could freely give oneself over to "calculation" (to measuring, weighing, or reckoning), taking advantage of the fact that "Santa Pupa" (the "protectress of the unconscious") assured one of not being too much disturbed, in this exercise of the esprit de géométrie, by what obscurely pulsed and seethed in the "submerged part": that is to say, in the man of the "underworld."
