At a certain moment, however, this protection came to an end, and the man of the "underworld," or of the "volo, ergo sum," began to rise up and to penetrate into the field of the man of the "surface," or of the "cogito, ergo sum."

This moment can be roughly made to correspond, historically, to that in which India, politically colonized by England (in 1876, the Disraeli bill proclaims Queen Victoria sovereign of the Indian empire), begins to export its own culture into Europe and the West (the Theosophical Society, for example, was founded in New York in 1875).
                                                  177
It is thus that the force of the unconscious and of the will (in the words of the theosophists, of the life of the being "unique, emerging from the abysses of the dark infinite") begins to burst into the "clear" and "distinct" universe of Cartesian forms.

In this new and disquieting situation, the ego (the I of the cogito) comes to find itself before three possibilities:

1) that of pretending nothing is amiss, sticking its head under the sand or committing itself to repressing what would wish to rise to consciousness (as, in a broad sense, the "rationalists" or the "illuminists" do);

2) that of depotentiating such contents (naturalizing them, as Freud does, or psychologizing them, as Jung does), thereby realizing a sort of compromise between its fear of changing and what would instead wish to renew it;

3) that of growing and changing itself (as Steiner indicates), dilating and deepening the dimension of the cogito (of the conscious) so much as to be able to receive within itself that of the flight (of the unconscious).

It is clear, however, that the first two of these possibilities do not shelter the ego from eventual "obsessions" or "possessions" on the part of what is repressed or depotentiated: here, in fact, the ancient (and already recalled) saying holds more than ever: "Ducunt volentem fata, nolentem trahunt" (see 32nd meeting – note).

Certainly, for the "fata to lead," one must be master of oneself; but one does not become master of oneself – as many seem to believe – by repressing oneself and becoming rigid (that is, by becoming "hard").

In his Tale of the Green Snake and the Beautiful Lily, Goethe has the Old Man say, in fact: "Love does not dominate, but forms, and this is more" (Favola – Adelphi, Milan 1995, pp. 60-61). But does "forming" – according to what we have seen – not mean precisely reuniting the forms of thinking with the forces of the will, so that the former may find again their life and the latter their light?

But let us resume our reading.


---


Steiner writes, still referring to von Hartmann: "Since he had recognized that in the fact of being conscious the essence of the idea does not lie, he had to recognize the latter also as an objective thing, existing in itself and for itself. However, the fact of receiving besides, among the active constitutive principles of the world, also the will, distinguishes him from Goethe (…) He admits it because he regards the idea as something static which, in order to become effective, needs the push of the will" (p. 165).

What, in fact, did we say the other time? That von Hartmann sees, yes, the will and the idea, but he sees the one "alongside" the other, and not the one in the other.

This depends upon the fact that he continues, despite everything, to consider the idea (like Kant) as a merely formal reality which, "in order to become effective," needs "the push of the will." Which corresponds, on close inspection, to the Freudian idea of the so-called "libidinal investment." When does one, in fact, have such an "investment"? When the libido "charges," with its "psychosexual" energy, a representation (or a group of representations), thereby for that very reason vivifying it and rendering it precisely "effective."

But one thing – as we know by now – is that the inert and static form of thinking or of the idea be vivified and rendered effective by the spiritual force of thinking itself or of the idea; another is that it be excited or inflamed by the natural force of the instincts, the cravings, or the passions.

Despite his notable merits, his monism, and his intention of conjoining the thinking of Hegel with that of Schelling and above all of Schopenhauer, von Hartmann ends, therefore, by reproposing the Cartesian dualism, no longer in the terms of the res cogitans and the res extensa, but in those of the idea and the will.

Steiner writes, in fact: "According to him the will alone can never arrive at the creation of the world, since it is the empty and blind urging toward existence. If the will is to generate
                                                      178
something, the idea must be added to it, since only this gives to the will the content of its action. But what must we do with that will? It escapes us when we wish to grasp it, since one cannot conceive that empty urging devoid of content. From this it follows that all that we truly grasp of the principle of the world is idea, since the graspable must precisely possess a content (…) If, therefore, we must grasp the concept of will, it too must appear to us in the content of the idea; it can appear only in the idea and with the idea, as the form of its manifesting itself, never independently (…) Therefore Goethe represents the idea as active, effective, no longer in need of any push (…) The idea is to be understood as entelechy, that is to say, already as an active existence; and from this its form of active existence one should first make an abstraction, if one then wishes to find it again under the name of will" (pp. 165-166).


---


Do not let yourselves be confused by the fact that it is said that the idea gives a "content," and not – as we have said thus far – a "form," to the empty and blind force of the will, since that content is precisely the form.

In any case, the necessity of distinguishing the idea from the consciousness of the idea presents itself again here.

The idea, inasmuch as it is "entelechy" (that is, a unity of form and of force), is, in fact, active and effective, whereas the ordinary reflected consciousness of the idea is only an inert form which, having "chased the force out the door," then sees it "re-enter through the window," as "will."

Static, and for that very reason in need of a "push" (of the good will to develop its latent higher grades), is not, therefore, the idea, but the ordinary reflected consciousness of the idea.

Answer to a question

Consider, for example, fanaticism. The fanatic seems to have strong convictions, but in reality it is not he who has them; rather, it is the strong convictions that have him. And they have, or possess, him inasmuch as the force with which he would wish to impose those ideas does not belong to the ideas (and therefore to the I), but to his lower nature, unconsciously manipulated by the obstructing entities. (Consult, in this regard, the article by Francesco Giorgi, "In the Face of Islam," of 12 May 2002. There one finds discussed this equivocal assertion of Gianni Vattimo: "If Hitler had had weak thinking, he could at most have killed the Jews who were his neighbors. To carry out a genocide he needed a very strong metaphysics that guaranteed it" – note.)

Let us remember, therefore, once again, that ideas – as The Philosophy of Freedom teaches – should be at the service of man (of the I), and not man at the service of ideas.

Answer to a question

The fanatic is almost always an individual endowed, on the one hand, with weak thinking (and therefore with a weak consciousness of the I) and, on the other, with a strong instinctuality that inflames his feeling from below.

Keep in mind, in this regard, that the life of feeling has a symptomatic valence, and not an etiological one: in it there do, in fact, nothing but manifest themselves the equilibria or the disequilibria between thinking and willing. In the sthenic or hysteric types, for example, the prevalence of willing over thinking overheats the feeling, whereas, in the asthenic or neurasthenic types, the prevalence of thinking over willing cools it.

Goethe observes precisely: "Content without method leads to fanaticism, method without content to empty quibbling" (Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 45).

It seems to me I have already recalled, moreover, that the inner reality of feelings corresponds to the outer one of colors, in which – according to Goethe – there precisely comes to manifest itself the interaction of "light" (thinking) with "darkness" (willing). ("Colors – he says – are actions of light, actions and passions..." – The Theory of Colors, in Works – Sansoni, Florence 1961, vol. V, p. 289).

But let us return to ourselves.


---


179
Steiner writes: "If we have recognized in Hartmann's views on nature an affinity with the Goethean conception, we find it in a still more significant way in the ethics of this philosopher. Ed. v. Hartmann thinks that the whole race for happiness and the toiling of egoism is ethically devoid of value, since by this path we can never arrive at a gratification. Acting out of egoism with the aim of gratifying it is, for Hartmann, something illusory. We must understand the task that is given to us in the world and act purely to fulfill it, renouncing ourselves. We must find our goal in devotion to the object, without the pretension of deriving anything from it for our subject" (p. 166).

This conclusion, Steiner asserts, coincides with that of Goethean ethics; with this decisive difference, however: that the renouncing of ourselves in order to "find our goal in devotion to the object" derives, in Hartmann, from duty (and is therefore accompanied by pessimism); in Goethe, from love ("Duty: when one loves what one commands to oneself" – Maxims and Reflections, p. 179).

Steiner observes, in fact: "When we raise no personal pretension, when we work only because driven by what is objective, finding in the action itself the motives of the action, then we work morally. But in that case we work precisely out of love. Every own will, every personal motive must disappear" (pp. 166-167).


---


One must be careful, however, not to take these expressions in a "moralistic" sense. It is not, in fact, a question of denying sic et simpliciter the ego (the "personal pretension," the "own will," the "personal motive"), but rather of asserting the I, denying the ego to the same measure in which it is a negation of the I; it is not a question, that is, of denying the ego in the name of whatsoever "Super-ego," but of denying it in the name of the I: in the name, that is, of its very being or ground.

The renunciation of the ego is, therefore, nothing but the negation of a negation: that is, the renunciation of the not-being or of the not-I. "All our ability – Goethe says, in fact – consists in renouncing our existence in order to exist" (Maxims and Reflections, p. 85).

All this is, however, impossible if it is not knowledge that opens the way to morality. But what knowing can do this? Only that knowing which is born, in its turn, from morality. Just as it is necessary to know in order to love, so it is necessary to love in order to know, since truth is the good, as it presents itself to thinking, and the good is truth, as it presents itself to the will.

Not without reason is The Philosophy of Freedom divided into two parts: the first (The Science of Freedom), which could also be entitled: The Good as Truth; the second (The Reality of Freedom), which could also be entitled: Truth as the Good.

If the science of the spirit takes its start (noetically) from the "good as truth," and not (ethically) from "truth as the good," it is owed, then, to the fact that, precisely in the name of freedom, it can only start from our ordinary consciousness and from the thinking we carry out (autonomously) in the waking state (whereas feeling and willing unfold, respectively, in the dream state and the sleep state). "The ascent toward the supersensible state of consciousness – one reads, in fact, in Occult Science in its General Lines (Antroposofica, Milan 1969, p. 250) – can set out only from the normal waking consciousness."

Steiner writes again: "The superior man cannot desire anything other than to have to conquer his own happiness for himself (…) Precisely because the world leaves us dissatisfied, we create for ourselves the most beautiful happinesses in our own working" (p. 167).

"I pity men – Goethe observes to the same effect – who lament so much the transitoriness of things and lose themselves in the contemplation of earthly vanity. We exist precisely
                                                  180
in order to render eternal what is fleeting; and this can happen only if one appreciates both the transitory and the eternal" (Maxims and Reflections, p. 60).


---


Steiner concludes, therefore: "We have expounded all this here because we were concerned to show the intimate and profound solidity of the Goethean conception of the world. It is so profoundly rooted in the being of the world that we must encounter its fundamental traits wherever an energetic thinking penetrates as far as the springs of knowing. Every characteristic of Goethe is so original, so far from any secondary fashionable opinion, that even a reluctant one is obliged to think in his sense. In single individuals there precisely expresses itself the eternal enigma of the world; in modern times most significantly in Goethe; therefore one can assert that today the height of a man's conception can be measured according to the relation in which it stands before the Goethean conception of the world" (pp. 167-168).

L.R.
Rome, 12 June 2001

181
The Scientific Works of Goethe (38)

Tonight we shall take up the eleventh chapter, entitled: Goethe and Mathematics, but I believe we shall begin the twelfth as well, since these are two very short chapters.

Steiner writes: "Goethe expressed his admiration for mathematics in so resolute a way that it is absolutely not legitimate to assert that he held this science in disdain. On the contrary, he would wish that the whole of natural science were permeated by the rigor that is proper to mathematics. 'The prudence that lies in deducing from every single fact only that which immediately follows from it, we must learn from the mathematicians; and even there where we do not make use of calculation, we must always proceed as if we were obliged to render account of it to the strictest of geometers.' 'I have heard myself accused of being an adversary, an enemy of mathematics, whereas no one could hold it in greater esteem than I'" (p. 169).


---


But why does Goethe hold mathematics in such great consideration? Because "it is not – as the saying goes – an opinion": because it is, that is, impersonal, objective, and rigorous.

Before the fact that two plus two makes four, all the sympathies and antipathies fall silent, as do all the personal, philosophical, or ideological points of view.

It is, therefore, a precious "life preserver" to which above all the scientists cling in order to remain on the (spiritual) plane of objectivity and not be swallowed up by the magnum mare (psychic) of subjectivity.

In this respect, mathematics is, in effect, a great educator of the spirit.

We shall become even more aware of this if we remember that the natural sciences represent for man a means, and not an end: a means by virtue of which he learns to overcome his own lower nature (his own subjectivity), in order thus to be able to confront the knowledge of himself ("Nosce te ipsum"), which is not only material, but also, and in the first place, spiritual.

All the effort nobly expended in natural-scientific research represents, therefore, a sort of "training" that man carries out, for the most part unconsciously, with a view to the spiritual-scientific inquiry.

Not without reason, in the first of the two passages cited by Steiner, does Goethe's accent fall upon "prudence": that is, upon a quality of the soul that implies judgment, measure, reflection, and deliberateness.

Once these qualities have been learned from mathematics, however, one can also prescind from mathematics itself or, more precisely, from calculation.

It is, in fact, impossible to do science if one prescinds from the spirit of mathematics, whereas it is possible to do it without calculating or computing.

Goethe observes in this regard: "Mathematicians are strange people; by virtue of the great things accomplished by them, they have erected themselves into a universal corporation, and no longer admit anything in the world except what lies in their field and can be treated with their method. An eminent mathematician had occasion to say, with regard to a physical theory that was being recommended to his attention: 'But can one really reduce nothing at all to calculation?'" (Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 239).

But for what reason was calculation so close to the heart of this "eminent mathematician"? For the simple reason that he reified the spirit of mathematics, projecting it (unconsciously) onto calculation, in the same way as those – as we have already seen – who reify the spirit of science, projecting it onto the method (if not indeed onto the techniques or the protocols).

Goethe says: "That one can reduce and discard a phenomenon by calculation, or by words, is a mistaken idea." As you well know, it is precisely, however, "by calculation (the famous Avogadro number – ed.), or by words" that many members of today's "scientific community" would wish, for example, to reduce and discard the "homeopathic" phenomenon.
                                                 182
Do you remember what Steiner said? That "Goethe is the Copernicus and the Kepler of the organic world" (p. 70). He worked, in fact, within the ambit of organic reality, with the same rigor with which a mathematician works within the ambit of the inorganic one.


---


Steiner writes: "Goethe knew that the ways to truth are infinite, and that each one can travel the one that is conformable to his faculties, and arrive at the goal (…) 'The most mediocre man can be complete when he moves within the limits of his qualities and faculties; whereas excellent endowments can be obscured, neutralized, and destroyed when that equilibrium required by circumstances is lacking'" (p. 170).

In the specific case, we could therefore say, by way of a joke, that "excellent endowments can be obscured, neutralized, and destroyed when" the measure of what is measurable and of what is not is lacking. Goethe says: "As if nothing existed except what can be demonstrated mathematically! It is as if one could be so mad as not to believe in the love of his beloved, merely because she is not able to demonstrate it to him mathematically! Mathematically she will be able to demonstrate her dowry to him, not her love!" (J. P. Eckermann: Conversations with Goethe – Laterza, Bari 1912, vol. I, p. 193).

Steiner writes: "The object of mathematics is magnitude; it is that which admits of a 'more' or a 'less.' But magnitude is not something that exists per se. In the vast circle of human experience there is no object that is pure magnitude. Alongside other characteristics, every object has certain ones that are determinable by means of numbers. Since mathematics concerns itself with magnitudes, it has for its object no thing of experience that is complete in itself, but of every thing only that which can be measured or counted; and it separates off from the things everything that can be subjected to these operations. Thus it obtains a world of abstractions within which it then works (…) It is, therefore, decidedly an error to believe that one can grasp, with mathematical judgments, the whole of nature in its totality. Nature is not only quantity, it is also quality; and mathematics has to do only with the how-much. Mathematical treatment, and the other that looks purely to the qualitative, must give each other the hand, and collaborate; then they will meet in the object, of which each comprehends one side" (p. 171).


---


The fact is that one thing is to say: "Compŭto, ergo sum," another to say: "Sum, ergo compŭto." In the first case, in order to be able to feel oneself an I with the necessary continuity, one cannot, in fact, stop computing; in the second, one can instead allow oneself the freedom to do it, where it is essential or useful (in the sphere of quantity), and not to do it, where it is inessential or useless (in the sphere of quality).

As one sees, one who says (unconsciously): "Compŭto, ergo sum" concerns himself not so much with the world as with himself; whether he finds himself grappling with a mineral, or with a plant, or with an animal, or with a human being, he can therefore do nothing but compute, in order to be able to feel himself an I (and indeed a "scientist").

From the soulic point of view, this is equivalent, however, to a "paresis": that is, to a grave limitation of the free motility assured by the "Sum, ergo compŭto."

Think, for example, of the so-called "genetic engineering." That with an engineering mentality one should give oneself to the construction of bridges or tunnels is more than understandable; but that with the same mentality one should instead give oneself to constructing organisms (genetically modified) is much less so. And why, then, does one do it? It is simple: because one does not know how to do anything else or, better said, because one only knows how to think in that way, and from that way one derives the sensation or the feeling of the I.

We must therefore be grateful to those who, with an engineering mentality, realize the so-called "great works," but not to those who, with the same mentality, confront, on the theoretical and practical plane, the problems of life, of the soul, and of the spirit.

Steiner says: "The object of mathematics is magnitude." But what is "magnitude"? It is – as Hegel explains – a "quality without qualities."
                                                 183
Do you have in mind that riddle which asks whether a kilo of straw or a kilo of iron weighs more?

Well, does it not play precisely upon the fact that the conspicuous qualitative difference between iron and straw can induce someone to blot out the equality of their weight? And does it not demonstrate (in spite of itself) that quantitative equality is given precisely because the qualitative difference is taken into no account at all?

When one arrives at quantity – Hegel asserts, in fact – the essence (the quality) becomes insignificant. ("Quantity is the pure being in which the determination is posited no longer as one with being itself, but as surpassed or indifferent" – Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989, p. 115).

A flower, for example, is "as surpassed or indifferent" when, cut, it is "no longer as one" with the being of the plant, just as the (physical) body of the animal or of man is "as surpassed or indifferent" when, a corpse, it is "no longer as one" with its being: when, that is, it finds itself separated or estranged from its reason for being.

Which therefore stands to signify that the sphere of quantity is that very sphere of death. ("In its true significance – Steiner observes, in fact – death is nothing other than the character impressed upon certain substantialities which now do not find themselves in their proper seat, which are now excluded from their true seat. Even when death supervenes in man, it is always a question of the same process. For if we look at the corpse, if we look at what of the dead man persists in the world of maya, we find contained in it only a substantiality which, at the moment of death, is excluded from the existence of the I, of the astral body, and of the etheric body, is estranged from the seat which alone would confer upon it its true sense. For the physical body of man has no sense whatsoever without the etheric body, without the astral body and the I" (R. Steiner: The Spiritual Reality of the Earth, in Anthroposophy – Monthly Review of the Science of the Spirit, year XXXVIII, N° 1-3, January-March 1983, p. 9).

It is singular, therefore, that today one should take such pains to "certify" or "brand" quality: it is singular, that is, to see the "man without qualities" (Musil) busily and clumsily grappling with a reality of which he has not the least scientific consciousness. In order to have it, he would, in fact, have to develop inspirative consciousness, which lies beyond not only ordinary representational consciousness, but imaginative consciousness as well.

Which implies, first of all, the surpassing of that prejudice which, on the trail of Locke's (1632-1704) celebrated and arbitrary distinction between "primary" and "secondary" qualities, leads us to believe that quantity is objective and quality subjective.

Let us not delude ourselves: quality is in the first place soul, and no one will therefore be able to restore quality to the life of the world and of himself so long as he does not restore the soul to both.

Let us keep in mind, moreover, that quality comprehends quantity (and can therefore put it at its own service), whereas quantity does not comprehend quality (and tends therefore to lower it or reduce it to its own level).


---


Here we are, then, arrived at the twelfth chapter, entitled: Goethe's Fundamental Geological Principle.

Steiner writes: "Often Goethe is sought where he is absolutely not to be found. This has happened, among other things, in the judgments upon the poet's geological investigations (…) Here he should above all be judged according to his own maxim: 'In the works of man, as in those of nature, the intents are especially worthy of attention,' and 'the spirit according to which we work is the supreme thing.' For us it is exemplary, not that which he attained, but the way he worked to attain it. It is not a question of an opinion to be taught, but of a method to be communicated. The former depends upon the scientific means of the time, and can be surpassed; the latter proceeded from the great spiritual dispositions of Goethe and holds fast even when the scientific instruments are perfected and experience is broadened" (p. 173).
                                                  184
As one sees, this is yet one more demonstration that the "how" is more important than the "what," since it is the "how" that reveals the "who": that is, "the spirit according to which we work."

"It is not a question – Steiner says – of an opinion to be taught, but of a method to be communicated." Opinion is, in fact, a "thought," whereas method is a "thinking," a process or a dynamic and qualitative act: knowing accumulates or capitalizes the former, whereas knowing-through nourishes itself on the latter. (Intellectual Capital: this is the emblematic title of a recent book by T. A. Stewart – Ponte alle Grazie, Milan 1999).

It is needless to recall, once again, that this holds also, and all the more, for anthroposophy. This is, in fact, not a knowing or a doctrine to be learned, but a method or – as Steiner says – a "way" of knowledge in which it is precisely the "how," availing more than the "what," that guides us to the "who": that is, to the spirit that animates it.

You see, we could learn all of Steiner's lectures by heart and not take a single step that brings us closer to the spirit that speaks through his work; but if we do not draw near to that spirit, that spirit does not draw near to us.

You know, when someone tells me that Titius or Gaius "knows anthroposophy," I always answer: "It remains to be seen, however, whether anthroposophy knows him." For only he whom It knows truly knows It. (Consult S. O. Prokofieff: The Being Anthroposophy – Arcobaleno, Oriago di Mira (Ve) 1996 – note.)

But what is the spirit that animates the science of the spirit? That very spirit which stands at the center of the well-known wooden group carved by Steiner, and by him significantly indicated as the "Representative of Humanity" (like the "Ecce homo").

Just think: Steiner went so far as to say that anthroposophy, were it to be uprooted from its spirit, could become dangerous. And why? But it is clear: because it would no longer be anthroposophy, since in the body of its teaching another spirit would precisely have been implanted.

But let us return to ourselves.


---


Steiner writes: "Goethe was led to geology by the duties of his office relating to the mines of Ilmenau (…) Amid the relative practical work there was born in him the scientific need to discover the laws of those phenomena which he was in a position to observe at close hand (…) Here there emerges at once a peculiarity deeply inherent in Goethe's nature. He has a need essentially different from that of many scientists. While for the latter the important thing lies above all in the knowledge of the particular, and an ideal construction, a system, generally interests them only insofar as they receive help from it in the observation of particulars, for Goethe the single phenomenon is only a point of passage toward a vast general comprehension of existence. In the essay on Nature one reads: 'She lives in countless creatures, and where is the mother?'" (pp. 173-174).

Where, that is, is that Nature, that One, which presents itself to us now in mineral garb, now in vegetable, now in animal?

Steiner says that already then many scientists did not pose a question of this kind, being interested above all in the knowledge of the particular; imagine, then, whether they could pose it now that they have passed, not only from the particular to the microscopic or the infinitely small, but also to managing this "minimalistic" vocation with computers, and therefore in a purely "computational" key.

It is by now difficult for us to imagine, in effect, with what "aura" the work of the rare scientists of former times was surrounded, since, with the advent (after the Second World War) of the so-called "Big Science," while the number of researchers or scientists has gone on growing, the "aura" pervading their activity has gone on shrinking and turning gray
                                                  185
(presumably feeling the effect of the "gray matter" of which these researchers and scientists, as good "cephalocentrists," exclusively avail themselves).

Steiner continues, in any case: "The same longing to recognize, not only what exists immediately, but the most profound grounds of the existent, we find also in Faust: 'Behold every sap at work, every seed.' Thus what he observes above and below the surface of the earth becomes for him a means of penetrating into the enigma of the formation of the world. What he writes on 28 December 1789 to Duchess Louise: 'The works of nature are always like a word spoken by God'" (p. 174).


---


To understand such a "word," however, it is not sufficient to spell it out. One thing, in fact, is the "spelling," by which one isolates the single consonants and vowels, say, of the word "TORTA" (T-O-R-T-A); another is the word "TORTA" as a whole. It is not, in fact, the letters of which it is composed that characterize it, but rather the order in which they are arranged in it.

It would, for example, suffice to invert the O and the R in order to realize that one is dealing no longer with a "TORTA" (cake), but with a "TROTA" (trout).

I realize that a consideration of this kind can seem at first sight banal. Many, however, wonder today at the fact that toads (tricks of number!) have a quantity of genes far superior to that of human beings.

Which, to return to our example, would be like wondering that the word "Io" (I), of two letters, and the word "Dio" (God), of three letters, have a significance far superior to that of the word "precipitevolissimevolmente," which counts twenty-six of them instead.

L.R.
Rome, 19 June 2001

186
The Scientific Works of Goethe (39)

Note: On the evening of 26 June 2001, before resuming the reading of the Scientific Works of Goethe, there was read, commented upon, and discussed the article by Francesco Giorgi: "Globalization and Threefolding," published the day before by our "Observatory."

Let us now resume the reading of the twelfth chapter, dedicated to "Goethe's fundamental geological principle."

After citing the following passage from Wilhelm Meister: "Now, if I were to treat these gashes and crevices as letters that I had to decipher and form into words, and finally read through completely, would you have anything against it?", Steiner writes: "Thus, from 1780 onward, we see the poet unceasingly occupied with deciphering that script. His effort aimed at reaching a vantage point that would show him, in their inner, necessary connections, what he saw in isolation. His method was the one 'that develops and unfolds, not the one that classifies and orders.' It did not suffice him to see here the granite, there the porphyry, etc., and simply to place them one alongside the other according to exterior characteristics; he sought a law that lay at the basis of all the stony formations and that, kept present to the spirit, would make plain how precisely here granite, there porphyry, must arise from it. He started from what distinguishes, in order to ascend to the common element" (p. 175).


---


As we know, it is Linnaeus, in the botanical field, who classifies and orders: that is, who does what is in the first instance necessary when one finds oneself in the presence of the immediate multiplicity. Goethe makes a treasure of it, but he does not stop here, inasmuch as he feels powerfully the need of a synthesis or of a unitary vision.

Very differently – we have said – do the mystics and the mechanists behave: the latter stop on the analytical plane of the orderings and the classifications; the former take flight at once toward the synthesis, thereby avoiding passing under the (intellectual) "sacrificial pincers" of analysis.

I recall, for example, that when it happened to me, in speaking with some (Jungian) psychotherapist, to allude to the sentient soul, the rational soul, and the consciousness soul, I almost always heard it said that such distinctions have no sense, inasmuch as "the soul is one." To which I often answered: "The body, too, is one, but for all that one who suffers from a headache does not spread a callus-remover on his head."

But for what reason did Goethe feel so strongly the need of a unitary vision? It is soon said: because this need is all the stronger the stronger is the need to reach a full consciousness of the I. The ego, without realizing it, seeks, in fact, the I: that is, its true reality or its true being.

The mystics themselves confirm it. Their ego, too, seeks the I, but, precisely by virtue of rushing prematurely toward the synthesis, instead of finding the I in God and God in the I (the beginning of the Pater Noster given by Steiner reads, in fact: "Father who wast, who art, and who wilt be in our inmost essence..."), they end by finding a God who is not in the I, and thereby (luciferically) dissolving the I in that God.

The need to reach a unitary vision of the world is, therefore, nothing but the counterpart of the need to reach a unitary vision of oneself; man, as I, is, in fact – Steiner explains – a living synthesis "of various constituent parts. Of corporeal character are the physical body, the etheric body, and the astral body. Soulic are: the sentient soul, the rational soul, and the consciousness soul. In the soul the I spreads its light. And spiritual are: the spirit-self, the life-spirit, and the man-spirit" (Occult Science in its General Lines – Antroposofica, Milan 1969, p. 63).


---


187
Steiner writes: Goethe "seeks the common principle which, according to the different circumstances in which it expresses itself, generates now this, now that quality of stones. In experience there is for him nothing fixed at which one could halt; fixed is only the principle that lies at the basis of everything (…) It is an error to believe one has refuted this Goethean method by saying that today's geology knows of no such transition from one mineral to another. Goethe never asserted that granite actually transforms itself into another stone. Once it has become granite, it is a finished and concluded product, and no longer has the inner force of impulse to become, by its own force, anything else. But what Goethe sought is precisely what is lacking in today's geology: the idea, the principle that constitutes granite before it has become such; and this idea is the same one that lies at the basis of the other formations as well. If, therefore, Goethe speaks of the transition of one stone into another, he does not intend an actual transformation, but a development of the objective idea which, in developing itself, produces the single configurations, now fixing this form and becoming granite, now unfolding from itself another possibility and becoming slate, etc." (pp. 175-176).


---


In order to begin to form for ourselves an idea (however approximate) of this "fixed" principle that "generates now this, now that quality of stones," we could think, for example, of the so-called "mechanical properties" of metals (the ego – it is known – prefers to speak of "properties," rather than of "qualities"). We know that the metals are characterized and differentiated also by such properties, but it is not always remembered that, with their passing (by fusion) from the solid state (supported by the ether of life) to the liquid state (supported by the chemical ether), these tend to vanish. If, therefore, we were to imagine making them then pass from the liquid state to the gaseous one (supported by the ether of light), and from the gaseous one to the caloric one (supported by the ether of warmth), we could picture to ourselves a final unification of them: that is, the return of each one of them to their "common principle."

Gunter Wachsmuth writes in this regard: "What we perceive in nature with the organs of the senses are not, in truth – as is well known to every student of philosophy and of nature – substances and forces, but states and their transitions from the one to the other (…) If, therefore (…) we start not, like Newton, from the point of view of the matter of the bodies, but from that of the primal forces," we can say: "The etheric primal forces (the formative forces), inasmuch as they are bound in the bodies of the world of phenomena, conserve the state they have produced so long as other free etheric forces, or stronger ones of the same species, do not work a change of the present state" (The Etheric Formative Forces – Atanòr, Rome 1980, p. 47).

Steiner asserts that "it is an error to believe one has refuted this Goethean method by saying that today's geology knows of no such transition from one mineral to another." Given A, say, granite ("the spinal column of the earth") and B porphyry, it is, therefore, mistaken to think that, for Goethe, A passes over into B or that B passes over into A, since he is instead convinced that it is C (the idea) that passes over into A and into B or, better said, that manifests itself here as A and there as B.

We have said "here" and "there" deliberately, because the passing of C into A or into B depends above all upon the place (the space) in which its manifestation occurs.

Wilhelm Pelikan observes, in fact: "The metal is present everywhere; and as the human organism shows us the presence of blood in every part of it, but in greater quantity in the vessels and in the heart, so the Earth certainly contains everywhere gold, silver, lead, copper, iron, and so on, but only a few places contain a significant quantity of them. With this there comes to light that each metal presents its specific type of distribution over the whole Earth" (Seven Metals – Arcobaleno, Oriago di Mira (Ve) 1992, p. 6).


---


Steiner writes precisely: "For Goethe, the principal thing becomes the history of the formation of the earth, and every particular must insert itself into it. What matters to him is the place that a mineral takes in the terrestrial complex; the particular no longer interests him except as a part of the whole. In the last analysis there appears right to him that mineralogical-geological system which re-creates the processes of the Earth, showing
                                                    188
why in such and such a place precisely this mineral had to be born, in such and such another that other one. The geographical distribution of the deposits becomes for him decisive. Therefore in the theory of Werner, whom he otherwise holds in high regard, Goethe finds fault with the fact that Werner does not order the minerals according to the distribution that enlightens us about their formation, but according to accidental exterior characteristics. The perfect system is not made by the scientist; nature itself has made it" (p. 176).

It will be as well to recall that Abraham Gottlob Werner (1750-1817) is the German mineralogist and geologist to whom is owed the theory of "Neptunism" (according to which the rocks would derive from processes of marine sedimentation), whereas James Hutton (1726-1797) is the English geologist (cited in the passage that follows) to whom is owed the theory of "Plutonism" (according to which the rocks would instead derive from processes of volcanic eruption).

Goethe, Steiner continues, could not, however, "admit that in the geological phenomena, which are simply inorganic entities, other motor impulses should hold than in the rest of inorganic nature. The extension of the laws of the inorganic to geology is Goethe's first geological action (…) The geological theories of a Hutton, of an Élie de Beaumont (French geologist, 1798-1874 – ed.), were inwardly repugnant to him. What value could he attribute to such explanations that contradict the whole order of nature? It is a commonplace, the phrase one often hears repeated, that the theory of the upheavals and subsidences was contrary to Goethe's calm nature. No, it was contrary to his sense of a unitary conception of nature; he could not insert it into that. And he owes to this sense his having arrived in time (already in 1782) at that view to which official geology arrived only after decades: that the petrified remains of animals and plants stand in a necessary connection with the minerals within which they are discovered" (pp. 176-177).


---


"The perfect system – Steiner says – is not made by the scientist; nature itself has made it." Which stands to signify that in nature there works an intelligence which man can discover inasmuch as it is, for him, an intelligible.

As a new Prometheus, modern man has, in effect, succeeded in taking possession, wresting it from nature, of the intelligence that governs the inorganic world (the "completed work" of the divine-spiritual Entity). If he were to decide to grow, and thereby to develop the imaginative grade of consciousness, he could then take possession also of the intelligence that governs the organic world (the "working effect" of the divine-spiritual Entity).

The fact is that "cosmic" thinking creates and sustains man and nature, whereas "human" thinking knows man and nature; in knowing them, therefore, it does not create them, but re-creates them. We have already said, one evening, that the world of technology, for example, is precisely an inorganic world re-created by man, with a view – naturally – to being able one day to re-create himself.

But to fill the void that divides the thinking that thought us and thinks us from that with which we currently think, there is much road to travel. What, in fact, is the thinking with which we currently think? We have just said it: it is that dead (completed) part of cosmic thinking of which we have already succeeded in taking possession.

It is precisely here, however, that the game (the destiny of the world and of man) is played. For of the two, one: either we give ourselves humbly and lovingly to discovering and making our own its other parts; or we give ourselves egoistically (or narcissistically) to inventing something (as unfortunately one tends to do today), thereby inflicting upon nature and upon ourselves damages far graver than those proverbially inflicted by elephants upon knick-knacks.

Steiner writes: Goethe "went in search of a simple, natural explanation of the presence over vast expanses of granite masses, very distant from one another. The explanation that they had been
                                                   189
hurled there during the tumultuous upheaval of mountains situated much farther back in the region, he had to reject, because it derived a natural fact not from the natural laws still existing and operative, but from an exception to them, indeed from an abandonment of them" (p. 177).


---


And here, then, is the Deus ex machina: that is, the "relished" idea that a phenomenon, of which one is not capable of discovering the law, is due to chance, to an accident, or to an incident. ("The genetic code – Edoardo Boncinelli writes, for example – is arbitrary, or if we prefer gratuitous, with regard to the universal laws of the physical world. That it is what it is, and that it is universal, that is, valid for all living beings, is due to chance, to a particular combination of events that took place more than three billion years ago and that even today make their effects felt. The genetic code established itself by chance (…) It was a true and proper frozen incident..." – The Brain, the Mind, and the Soul – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 29).

Do you have in mind, just to give another example, how many and what fantastical hypotheses have been formulated (and are still formulated) to explain the so-called "disappearance" of the dinosaurs?

Well, would it not have been (and would it not be) simpler, more natural, and more realistic to think that those beings did not "disappear," but rather metamorphosed?

Ermanno Bencivenga writes that "there is by now common agreement on the thesis that the birds derived from the dinosaurs," so much so that one has begun to say poetically that "the dinosaurs did not become extinct: they flew away" (The False Steps of Science – Garzanti, Milan 2001, p. 155).

But what should we say if we were to discover that from the dinosaurs there derived not only the birds, but also – as Hermann Poppelbaum maintains – the serpents, the ruminants, and the carnivores? (cf. A New Zoology – Philosophic-Anthroposophic Press, Dornach/Switzerland 1961).

Dear friends, the hour has come to say farewell. We shall take up the five remaining chapters in September, when, the vacations ended, we shall resume, God willing, our meetings and our study.

L.R.
Rome, 26 June 2001

190
The Scientific Works of Goethe (40)

Dear friends, I see with pleasure that we have "survived" the holidays, and that we are all here again to resume our work.

Having finished, in June, the twelfth chapter, tonight we shall take up the thirteenth, Goethe's Meteorological Conceptions, and the fourteenth, Goethe and Scientific Illusionism.

These are, in fact, two short chapters: the first of them, very short, will permit us to cast a rapid glance back at what we said at the beginning of our study (which perhaps does no harm, since we are precisely setting out to re-begin it), whereas the second, though short, will give us some trouble.

Steiner writes: "Just as in geology, one is also mistaken in meteorology if one looks at the actual discoveries made by Goethe and seeks in them the essential. His meteorological experiments are, in fact, not completed; everywhere one must look only at the intents. His thought was always directed to finding the essential point from which a series of phenomena is regulated from within" (p. 179).


---


Do you remember what I said on the first day of the course? That the subjects would be various, but one only, on the contrary, would be Goethe's way of approaching them; and that the "how," that is, the way, would therefore be more important than the "what."

Well, it will certainly have happened to you, in this regard, to hear someone say of another: "I don't like (or I like) how he thinks"; or else: "I don't like (or I like) his way of thinking." In the first assertion the "how" is present, in the second the "way." Both, on close inspection, refer, however, to the "what," and not to the "how" or to the "way."

If, for example, one of the left were speaking of a politician of the right, it would be evident that what displeases him is precisely the "content" (the "what") of the other's thinking: that is, his thought, and not his thinking.

But for what reason does one then express oneself in that way? Because one does not so much as suspect, as a rule, that one can think otherwise than "how" one normally thinks, or that there can be a "way" of thinking different from the ordinary one.

One is for the most part convinced, in fact, that content A can find itself in agreement or disagreement with content B, but not that, beyond representational thinking, there could perhaps be given an "imaginative" one.

Nevertheless, in the passage we have read, the "actual discoveries" represent precisely the "what" (the content) of Goethe's thinking, whereas the "intents" (directed "to finding the essential point") represent, instead, the "how" or the "way" (its modality or quality).

It is needless to say that this different type of thinking does not substitute itself for the ordinary one, but adds itself to it, just as, say, the sight surgically recovered by one born blind does not substitute itself for the other senses, but adds itself to them, thereby enriching the (perceptive) experience.


---


Steiner concludes, in any case: "He wished to trace back the phenomena of the atmosphere to their causes lying in the nature of the earth itself. It was a question, first of all, of finding the point at which the fundamental law that conditions all the rest expresses itself immediately. Such a phenomenon was given by the barometric state; and this, in fact, Goethe too regarded as the type-phenomenon, seeking to reconnect everything else to it (…) One must, however, be on guard against interpreting this Goethean explanation in too extensive a way. Goethe is opposed to the making of hypotheses. He did not wish to furnish anything other than an expression of an observable phenomenon, not a true actual cause, in the sense of today's natural science (…) More than anything else there interested him the formation of the clouds, for which he had found in the doctrine of Howard (the Englishman Luke Howard, author of On the Modification of Clouds –
                                                  191
1772-1864 – ed.) a means of fixing those ever-changing formations in determinate fundamental conditions, in order 'to concretize in enduring form of thought what fluctuates in unstable semblance' (…) The thread to which he solders the single forms (stratus, cumulus, cirrus, nimbus – ed.) is offered to him (…) by a diversity of characteristics of the atmosphere at the different levels of height (…) One must keep in mind that Goethe could never so much as dream of considering that thread as something real. He was exactly conscious that only the single formation is to be regarded as real for the senses in space, whereas all the other higher explicative principles exist solely for the eyes of the spirit" (p. 180).

This said, we can pass to the examination of the fourteenth chapter, the only one that Steiner did not write to introduce a scientific work of Goethe.

He explains, in fact: "This exposition was not written because a Goethean edition had to include the Theory of Colors furnished with an introduction, but sprang from a profound spiritual need of the editor himself. He, having taken his start from the study of mathematics and physics, was led, by inner necessity, through the many contradictions pervading the system of our modern conception of nature, to the critical investigation of its methodological basis. His initial studies led him to the principle of rigorous experimental knowing; the vision of those contradictions, to a severely scientific theory of knowledge. His positivistic point of departure preserved him from falling into purely Hegelian conceptual constructions and, finally, with the help of his studies in the theory of knowledge, he discovered that many errors of modern natural science depended upon the altogether false position assigned by it to simple sensation" (p. 183).


---


Very well, let us then open this Dictionary of Psychology (Arthur S. Reber – Lucarini, Rome 1990) to see what it says under the entry "sensation": "Sensation is customarily distinguished from perception, the latter being constituted by the interpretation and the elaboration of sensation."

One distinguishes, therefore, rightly, sensation from perception, but one maintains, erroneously, that the former precedes the latter.

Steiner explains, in fact: "What has unfolded as sensory perception is to be distinguished from what you continue to carry further into the soul, from what you detach from the outer world. What you experience of things we shall call perception, and what you carry further into the soul we shall call sensation" (Anthroposophy, Psychosophy, Pneumatosophy – Religio, Rome 1939, p. 81).

The fact is that when one speaks of "sensation" one speaks of "consciousness." We are accustomed, in fact, to say: "sentient soul," "rational soul," and "consciousness soul," but we could just as well say: "sentient consciousness," "rational consciousness," and "self-consciousness."

We owe sensation (Empfindung or Sinnesempfindung), therefore, to the soul or to the "sentient" consciousness, whereas we owe perception (Wahrnehmung) to the physical body, to the etheric body, and to the sentient body.

To understand why Steiner says that "many errors of modern natural science" depend "upon the altogether false position assigned by it to simple sensation," it will, in any case, be better to read what this Medical Dictionary says in this regard (The Dictionary of Medicine – Fabbri, Milan 1981, vol. V, p. 1963): "To illustrate the concept of perception the following example is classically cited: seeing a sensible quality (the whiteness of the paper) is a sensation; seeing, instead, the object (the white paper) is a perception."

As one sees, what escapes the author of this "classic" example (and all those who cite it) entirely is the fact that the object (the white paper) is a "sensible quality" (papery) absolutely equal to that of the "whiteness," and that one should therefore speak of two perceptions or of two sensations, and not of one sensation and one perception.
                                                  192
But why, then, does one do it? Because one divides the field of experience into two parts, declaring (one knows not why) the one objective (the quantitative one) and the other subjective (the qualitative one), then making use of the former to explain the latter.


---


Our science – Steiner observes precisely – "places all the sensible qualities (sound, color, warmth, etc.) in the subject, and holds that 'outside' of it there correspond to these qualities only processes of movement of matter. These processes of movement, which should be the only thing existing 'in the kingdom of nature,' can, naturally, no longer be perceived, but are deduced on the basis of the subjective qualities. But such a deduction must appear deficient before a coherent thinking. Movement is, first of all, nothing but a concept borrowed by us from the world of the senses; it therefore presents itself to us only in objects endowed with sensible qualities. We know no movement apart from that which we discern in sensible objects. Now, if one applies this predicate to non-sensible beings, such as the elements of discontinuous matter (atoms) should be, one must be well conscious that with such an application one comes to ascribe to an attribute perceived sensibly (to 'movement' – ed.) a form of existence ('atomic' – ed.) conceived in a way anything but sensible" (pp. 183-184).

It is known, for example, that "dialectical" materialism (Marxism) is distinguished from "mechanistic" (or "dogmatic") materialism precisely because it bases itself not upon simple "matter," but upon "matter in movement."

But whoever brings movement into the affair brings in a force, an energy, or a becoming: that is, a reality that has to do directly with time, and only indirectly with space.

Our senses, in fact, always perceive the "moved," and never the "movement": they are, that is, always grappling with the effects, in space ("in objects endowed with sensible qualities"), of a force that works in time or that, better said, is time.

There appears, therefore, "deficient" ("before a coherent thinking") a "logic" that extracts or abstracts, from "objects endowed with sensible qualities," a force (or a quality) imperceptible to the senses, in order then to attribute it to "non-sensible beings," and therefore equally imperceptible to the senses.

Steiner observes: "Someone attributes to the atom the qualities of impenetrability, of the irradiation of force; another extension and the like; but they are always qualities borrowed from the sensible world. Otherwise one remains completely in the void." And he adds: "In this lies the deficiency. One draws a line in the very midst of the sensibly perceivable world and declares one part of it objective, the other subjective. But one thing alone is coherent; namely, that, if the atoms exist, they are simply a part of matter, endowed with the qualities of matter and imperceptible to our senses only on account of their smallness (…) For me it was, therefore, clear that the movement of the ether, the position of the atoms, etc., are to be placed in the same category as the sensations themselves (die Sinnesempfindungen selbst – ed.)" (p. 184).


---


What, in fact, did we say just now with regard to the example of the paper? That the object (the white paper) is a "sensible quality" (papery) absolutely equal to that of the "whiteness," and that both the one and the other should therefore be placed, as Steiner would say, "in the same category as the sensations themselves" (and not – as is done instead – the one in the "category" of perceptions and the other in that of sensations).

How, therefore, can one not judge "deficient" the "logic" of one who considered, for example, the whiskers, the paws, and the tail as parts of the cat, and the face as the cat, instead of considering whiskers, paws, tail, and face as parts of the cat, and therefore placing them all on the same plane?
                                                  193
But from what do such deficiencies descend? From the simple fact that the supersensible reality of quality, of which one is not conscious, is fatally projected upon a sensible or sub-sensible content.

This is precisely what happens, in fact, when one sanctions that quantitative sensations (Locke's "primary qualities") are objective, and therefore essential, and that qualitative sensations (Locke's "secondary qualities") are subjective, and therefore inessential.

Steiner writes: "Such subjectivistic opinion then finds a second support in physiological considerations. Physiology demonstrates that sensation arises only as the final result of a mechanical process which sets out, first of all, from that part of the corporeal world that lies outside our corporeal substance, communicates itself to the terminal organs of our nervous system, within the sense organs, and is then transmitted from there as far as the superior center, where it precisely arouses the sensation. The contradictions of this physiological theory are set forth further on, in the chapter Goethe against Atomism. But only the form of the movement of the cerebral substance can be indicated here as subjective" (p. 185).


---


According to physiology, sensation is, then, aroused in the brain (in the "superior center") when the latter is reached (by the afferent path) by that physical or environmental stimulus (from "that part of the corporeal world that lies outside our corporeal substance") which the receptors (the nervous terminations of the sense organs) have in the meantime taken care to transform into a nervous impulse.

Agreed, but in what way is it aroused?

Edoardo Boncinelli explains it to us: "The excitation of each receptor gives rise to a nervous signal which ascends toward a small center of the brain stem, called the solitary nucleus. Here it passes to another neuron which reaches a specific center of the thalamus, which is found in the diencephalon and from which a third neuron sets out, sending its axon directly toward a pair of regions of the cerebral cortex. It is these that integrate the various signals and give us the flavor of the peach, of the apricot, or of anything else we may be eating or tasting. Here one passes from a series of nervous excitations, which we can collectively call a neurostate, to a sensation of central origin, which we can call a psychostate. To be more precise, we have a first neurostate at the level of the solitary nucleus, a second neurostate at the level of the thalamic nucleus, and a third neurostate at the level of the cortex. In this latter seat, magically, a psychostate is associated to the cortical neurostate. We are not able, for the moment, to comprehend what happens in the last passage, which moreover cannot be of a purely biological nature, but we can turn our attention to the relations among the three neurostates that precede it and that have caused it" (The Brain, the Mind, and the Soul – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 112).

In the brain, therefore, sensation is not directly aroused; rather, a "neurostate" is activated, to which there comes to be associated, "magically" (sic!), a "psychostate," that is, precisely a "sensation" ("the flavor of the peach, of the apricot").

In what else, therefore, can that "last passage" from the neurostate to the psychostate consist ("magic" apart) (which, Boncinelli moreover specifies, "cannot be of a purely biological nature"), if not precisely in the passage – as we have said – from the body (physical, etheric, sentient) to the (sentient) soul?

If one were not burdened with materialistic prejudices, it would not, therefore, be difficult to recognize that the brain is the "seat" of the neurostates, whereas the soul is the "seat" of the psychostates: that is, the "seat" – as we have also hinted – in which the implicit objective content of perception (of the physical or environmental stimulus), after having assumed the subjective forms of the nervous impulse and of the neurostate, finally begins to make itself explicit: that is, to reveal itself for what it is (which will, however, happen fully only when, beyond the sentient soul, it is given in the form of the concept: that is, in a "form" without form).
                                                 194
I hope it is clear, thus, why Steiner says that "only the form of the movement of the cerebral substance can be indicated here as subjective."


---


Steiner proceeds, in fact: "What is, first of all, to be considered subjective? The constitution of the whole organism; therefore, also of the sense organs and of the brain, which will probably appear in every man in a somewhat different modification. But what can be demonstrated by this path is only a determinate configuration of the ordering and of the function of the substances by means of which the sensation is transmitted. Subjective, therefore, is truly only the path that the sensation has to travel before it can be called my sensation. Our organization transmits the sensation, and these paths of transmission are subjective; but the sensation is not" (pp. 185-186).

Objective, therefore, is the content of the sensation, whereas subjective (inasmuch as they belong to the subject) are the paths along which it is transmitted and the various forms it assumes in crossing them.

Let us imagine, just to give a banal example, throwing a stone, and that this, along its trajectory, first breaks a windowpane and then falls back into the water.

Well, is the stone not different from the air, from the glass, and from the water? And is its nature (its quality) not also different from the nature (the quality or the form) of the effects that its passage produces in those media?

Steiner concludes: "There would remain now the path of inner experience. What do I experience in my inwardness when I indicate that a sensation is mine? I experience making, in my thinking, the reference to my individuality, extending the sphere of my knowing to this sensation; but I am not conscious of generating myself the content of the sensations. I determine only the reference to myself, but the quality of the sensation is a fact founded in itself. From whatever side one begins, from within or from without, one does not arrive at the point at which one could say: Here is given the subjective character of the sensation. To the content of the sensation the concept of 'subjective' is not applicable" (p. 186).

The concept of "subjective," as a concept of what is present within the subject, and to which the subject therefore confers its form, is, in effect, not applicable to the content of the sensation, just as it is not applicable to the content of the perceptive images (to the percept), to that of the mnemic images (to the memory in itself, to the engram or the mnestic trace), to that of the dream images (to the Jungian archetype in itself), and to that of the representations (to the concept). And why? Because all these contents have the same nature or quality as the concept: and this – as we recalled a short while ago – is form (potential), but does not have form (actual).

L.R.
Rome, 4 September 2001

195
The Scientific Works of Goethe (41)

Tonight we shall begin the fifteenth chapter: Goethe as Thinker and Scientist. It is a rather long chapter, divided into six sections, the first of which is entitled: Goethe and Modern Natural Science.


---


Steiner writes: "If it were not a duty to say the truth without reserve when one believes one has discovered it, the following considerations would certainly never have been written; for, as far as I am concerned, there can be no doubt about the judgment that the specialists in the field pass upon them, given the tendency dominant today in science. In them one will discern an amateurish attempt on the part of a man who wishes to have his say regarding a subject long since passed into settled judgment by all the 'experts.' If I picture to myself the disdain of all those who today believe themselves the only ones called upon to speak of scientific questions, I must confess that this attempt is really not very seductive in the ordinary sense of the word. And yet I could not let myself be dismayed by such probable objections, since I can make them all for myself, and therefore know how little solid they are. To think 'scientifically' in the sense of the modern scientific theories is really not difficult" (p. 187).


---


Allow me to read to you, in this regard, some considerations of Francesco Giorgi relating to an article by Diego Marconi, entitled: The Critical Spirit and the Telling of Tales, published by Il Sole 24 Ore, and dedicated to the book of Ermanno Bencivenga: The False Steps of Science.

"Marconi writes: 'According to Bencivenga one must respect the professionalism of the scientists, but "without forgetting the critical spirit." Now, how can one not pay homage to the critical spirit? And yet, in the case of the scientific debate, the only sensible criticisms are the competent ones: one who opposes to a scientific opinion an uninformed contestation gives proof not of critical spirit, but merely of arrogance.' As one sees, Marconi, after rendering a dutiful though summary homage to the 'critical spirit,' proposes to distinguish the 'sensible' criticisms of the competent from the senseless (or arrogant) ones of the incompetent. He does not consider at all, therefore, that the 'critical spirit' also requires a specific competence, and that this concerns the activity of thinking and not its objects (the thoughts). It is not difficult, in fact, to find scientists who gather 'competently' the data of their own researches (and of which they are therefore 'informed'), but who then relate them to one another incompetently, deriving from them thereby senseless theories (or conclusions). While being competent, just to give an example, in the way a radio is made, one could be convinced, at the same time, that it creates or produces voices and sounds, and not that it simply transmits them. It suffices, in fact, to remove some component of it to reduce it to silence. Well, does one proceed any differently when one declares oneself convinced that it is the brain that thinks, feels, and wills? And do those who, knowing how the brain is made, make these assertions, perhaps know, having observed them with the same rigor with which they observe the physical phenomena, how thinking, feeling, and willing are made? Or do they not seek, rather, to explain, by means of that in which they are competent, that in which they are on the contrary incompetent? The fact is that Marconi believes that the 'critical spirit,' or – as Paolo Sarpi preferred to say – 'the art of thinking well' (cf. Unpublished Philosophical Writings – Carabba – Lanciano 1911 – ed.), is a natural capacity, and that it therefore needs no specific, severe, and laborious education. It is certainly true that the philosophers, cultivating one-sidedly the development of this 'spirit,' almost always end up losing sight of the facts; but no less true is it that the scientists, cultivating it insufficiently or not cultivating it at all, almost always end up losing themselves, equally one-sidedly, among the facts" (cf. on our site: The Critical Spirit and the Telling of Tales, 27 May 2001 – ed.).

196
I should only like to add, to these words, that this happens because one goes on ever more confusing, unfortunately, the scientific spirit (ever rarer) with the technical spirit (ever more widespread); so much so that one is by now accustomed to speak of "techno-scientific" culture or of "technoscience."


---


Steiner proceeds: "At present, one considers an amateur anyone who takes seriously, in general, a philosophical reflection upon the essence of things. For our contemporaries of 'mechanical' and even 'positivistic' cast of thought, having a conception of the world is held to be an idealistic whim. Such an opinion becomes, certainly, comprehensible when one sees in what a pitiable cognitive incapacity these positivistic thinkers find themselves whenever they pronounce upon the 'essence of matter,' the 'limits of knowledge,' the 'nature of the atoms,' and other similar subjects. In the face of such examples, one can make true studies of what amateurism is in questions of the highest scientific importance. One must have the courage to admit all this before contemporary natural science, notwithstanding the mighty admirable conquests it has to its credit in the field of technology. For such conquests have nothing to do with a true need for the knowledge of nature (…) One thing is to observe the processes of nature in order to place their forces at the service of technology; another is to seek, with the help of those processes, to look more deeply into the essence of natural science. True science is only where the spirit seeks the satisfaction of its own needs, without exterior aims" (pp. 188-189).

The scientific spirit is, therefore, a cognitive spirit, whereas the technical spirit is a utilitarian spirit. It is not – note well – that practical benefits do not derive from what the cognitive spirit conquers; but it is that this spirit should have at heart, first of all, the real, and not the use that can be made of it (Christ says precisely: "Seek first the kingdom of God and his righteousness, and all these things will be given you in addition" – Mt 6:33).

To whom has it not happened to hear it lamented that "today there is no more religion"? Unfortunately, however, not only "is there no more religion," but art and science as well are on the way to extinction (without there being an association analogous to the WWF to protect them). Both are, in fact, no longer in the hands of the spirit, but in those of politics and, above all, of the economy.

A research that is not spiritually oriented cannot, however, be "scientific," just as a research that is not scientifically oriented cannot be spiritual.

Steiner writes: "True science, in the higher sense of the word, has to do solely with ideal objects; it can be nothing other than idealism; for it has its ultimate reason for being in needs that originate from the spirit (…) Scientific questions are, therefore, essentially an affair that the spirit must settle with itself. They do not lead it outside of its element. Now the field in which the spirit lives and works, as that which is primordially its own, is the idea, the world of thinking (…) Take scientific observation: it must lead us to the knowledge of a law of nature. The law itself is purely ideal. Already the need to discover valid laws behind the phenomena is born from the spirit. A being devoid of spirit would not have this need" (p. 189).


---


That "true science" can "be nothing other than idealism" is a fact of which one should merely take consciousness.

Normally, we believe, in fact, that we think "things," and we do not notice that inasmuch as we succeed not only in thinking them, but also in discovering their interrelations, we continually resolve them into concepts or ideas.

But what is yet more interesting is that thinking, though it can traffic only in thoughts, succeeds in grasping the real and in modifying it. And this can only mean – we have said it – that thinking is in the real, and that thinking is the real.

197
It is, in any case, evident that "true science," being deputed to satisfy the "needs that originate from the spirit," is not much "desired" by all those who feel far more the physical and psychical needs than the spiritual ones. Which seems to me to explain sufficiently the present course of things.

Let us always remember that thinking, feeling, and willing are all three faculties of the soul, but that willing and thinking are respectively mortgaged, below, to the body and, above, to the spirit (to the I), whereas feeling lives at the center of the soul (or of the psyche).

For this reason Steiner says that "the field in which the spirit lives and works, as that which is primordially its own, is the idea, the world of thinking."

He writes again: "Now we set about observation. What do we truly wish to reach by means of it? To the question engendered in our spirit, do we perhaps expect that something that can give us answer be furnished from without, from sensible observation? Never! For why should we feel more satisfied by a second observation than by the first? If in general the spirit could satisfy itself with the observed object, it should receive satisfaction already from the first. But the true request does not go toward a new observation, but toward the ideal ground of the observations (…) Therefore the results of science can proceed only from the spirit; and can therefore only be ideas. Nothing can be raised against this necessary consideration; but with it the idealistic character of all science is asserted" (p. 190).

What, in sum, is the problem? That science is not self-conscious: that it is conscious, that is, of the things with which it concerns itself, but not of itself.

Steiner observes, in fact: "Modern natural science, in all that constitutes its essence, is not capable of believing in the ideality of knowledge. For it the idea is not the first, originary, creative element, but is the last product of the natural processes" (p. 190).


---


But it is "not capable of believing in the ideality of knowledge" inasmuch as it has no consciousness of what it does.

It believes, for example, that it is possible to know the "entities" (the singulars), but not the "essences" (the universals), only because it is ignorant of the fact that to know the "entity" – as we demonstrated in due course (if X is A, and if A is X, then A is A) – the "essence" (the universal) is indispensable, or that the knowledge of the entity (of the percept) necessarily implies that of the essence (of the concept).

It is not a question, therefore, of believing or not believing this or that, but of being or not being conscious of what one does.

The fact is that science would not be such, nor would it succeed in doing what it does, if it did not work in the way we are illustrating. Not being aware of this, however, it believes that things stand otherwise: it believes, for example, that the idea is not "the first, originary, creative element," but rather "the last product of the natural processes."

But the cognitive process, Steiner writes, "presents itself in this way: we perceive with our senses facts that unfold entirely according to the laws of mechanics, then phenomena of warmth, of light, of magnetism, of electricity, and finally vital processes, etc. At the highest grade of life we establish that it raises itself as far as the formation of concepts, of ideas, whose bearer is precisely the human brain. Springing from this sphere of thinking we find our own 'I.' This seems to be the highest product of a complicated process transmitted through a long series of physical, chemical, and organic facts. But if we investigate the ideal world that constitutes the content of that 'I,' we find in it more than the simple terminal product of that process. We find that the single parts of it are connected among themselves in a way quite other than are the parts of that
                                                 198
merely observed process. Inasmuch as one thought arises in us and then arouses another, we discover that between these two objects there is an ideal connection of quite another species than if I observe the dyeing of a fabric as the consequence of a chemical agent. It is entirely obvious that the successive stages of the cerebral process have their origin in the organic metabolism, although the cerebral process itself is the bearer of those configurations of thinking. But why the second thought follows the first, I do not find in this metabolism, but rather in the logical connection of the thoughts. In the world of thoughts there reigns, therefore, besides the organic necessity, another higher ideal necessity" (pp. 190-191).


---


What, then, are we called upon to discover? That the bond holding between concepts (or ideas) is a bond qualitatively different from that holding between physical, chemical, or nervous facts.

Having spoken of it at length when we occupied ourselves with The Philosophy of Freedom, it will suffice here to recall that concept A binds itself to concept B for purely logical or ideal reasons.

Today's science knows the nature of atomic, electrical, chemical, or intermolecular bonds, but not yet that of logical bonds; it does not even suspect, therefore, that the bond that presents itself, on the atomic plane, in atomic form, or, on the chemical plane, in chemical form, is the same bond that presents itself, on the logical plane, in logical form: with the essential difference, however, that here it finally unveils its intimate nature of thinking.

The bond is, in fact, "relation"; relation is thinking, and relating is thinking.
