Steiner writes: "The world of thoughts arises in our inwardness, places itself before the sensibly observable objects, and asks: what relation has, with myself, the world that now confronts me? What is it with respect to me? I stand here, with all my ideal necessity hovering above all that is transient; I have in me the force of explaining myself. But how can I explain what arises before me?"; it is a question, therefore – he adds – of the question concerning "the nexus between spirit and nature. What, then, is the relation existing between these two entities that always appear to us as separated one from the other? If this question is rightly raised, the answering of it is not so difficult as it seems. What sense can this question have? It is not posed by a being that, situated, as a third, above nature and spirit, from that its point of view investigates the said relation; no, the question is posed by one of the two entities themselves, that is, by the spirit. This asks: what nexus exists between me and nature? But this in turn means: how can I myself enter into a relation with the nature that stands before me? How can I express this relation according to the needs that live in me? I live in ideas; what idea corresponds to nature, how can I express in idea what I contemplate as nature?" (pp. 191-192).


---


It is, in effect, arduous to discover what nexus there is between the spirit and nature, if one starts from the presupposition (the prejudice) that thinking stands only within man (in his head), and that nature, lying outside of man, has nothing to do with it.

The case would, however, be different if we were to realize that thinking stands as much in us as in nature. We would already have realized it, in truth, if, instead of speaking – as is done today – of "information," one were to speak precisely of "thinking."

Do you remember, in fact, what Boncinelli says? That "the fundamental entities that characterize and regulate the phenomena of the physical universe, independently of whether it is a question of animate or inanimate objects, are three: matter, energy, and information" (The Brain, the Mind, and the Soul – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 11).

If we had, therefore, the open-mindedness to lift the fig leaf of "information," we would not only discover that thinking stands as much in us as in nature, but we would also realize that it is thanks to the former (to the conscious spirit) that it is given to us to discover the latter (the unconscious spirit).

199
Schelling says that the spirit is "invisible nature," whereas nature is "visible spirit"; since it is the spirit that speaks, it is, however, as if it said: "In contemplating nature, I contemplate myself as nature"; and it then asked itself: "But what does it mean, for me, to be nature?"

"It means – it could only answer itself – not yet to have arrived at the consciousness of myself, or at self-consciousness."

Which stands to signify that the problem of the nexus between the spirit and nature is resolved, in the last analysis, in that of the nexus between the conscious (thinking) and the unconscious (feeling and willing).

Steiner writes, in fact: "The spirit seeks everywhere to go beyond the series of facts that mere observation furnishes to it, and to penetrate as far as the ideas of things. Science begins precisely where thinking begins" (p. 192).

Where thinking begins, however, opinion ends: that is, that light garment of thinking with which we clothe the body of the sympathies, the antipathies, the desires, or the cravings that originate from our personal (psycho-physical) nature. One thing is, in fact, the ideas of things; another, the ideas about things: that is, those of the so-called "opinionists" or opinion makers.

What, on the other hand, decrees the success of today's culture? We have already said it: that it asks us to inform ourselves, and not to form ourselves; that it does not ask us, that is, to change. The ace up the sleeve of today's maîtres à penser consists precisely in giving to understand that anyone can become "somebody" while remaining a perfect "idiot" (in the original sense – it is understood – of idios: "own," "private," "personal").

And yet, Goethe has said, "one must be something, in order to be able to do something" (J. P. Eckermann: Conversations with Goethe – Laterza, Bari 1912, vol. I, p. 294). And what must one do, in order to be able "to be something"? One must precisely free the I from the idiotism of the ego.


---


Steiner writes: "We can take our start from wherever we wish; if we have sufficient spiritual force, we meet at the end the idea. Inasmuch as modern physics completely disowns this, it is led into a whole series of errors. I wish to allude here to one of them. Take the force of inertia, generally cited among the 'qualities of bodies.' One habitually defines it thus: 'no body can, without exterior cause, alter the state of motion in which it finds itself' (it is a question of the first principle of dynamics, which reads precisely thus: 'a body persists in its state of rest or of uniform rectilinear motion so long as external forces sufficient to modify that state do not intervene' – ed.). This definition awakens the representation that the concept of the body inert in itself is abstracted from the world of phenomena"; but it is not so, inasmuch as "the concept of the inert body arises purely by a conceptual construction. Calling 'body' that which is extended in space, I can imagine for myself bodies in which the changes proceed from exterior influences, and others in which the changes take place by their own force. If, then, in the outer world, I find something that corresponds to my pre-formed concept of 'body incapable of changing itself without an external push,' I call it inert, that is, subject to the law of inertia. My concepts are not abstracted from the world of the senses, but freely constructed from the idea, and only with their help do I begin to orient myself in the world of the senses. The above-mentioned definition could be enunciated only thus: 'a body that is not capable of altering its state of motion by its own force is called inert'; and once I have recognized it as such, I can apply everything that is connected with an inert body to the body in question" (pp. 192-193).

As you can see, the first statement (Newton's) gives no account of organic reality (which does not need the intervention of "external forces" in order to modify "its state of rest or of motion"), whereas the second (Steiner's) gives account both of organic reality and of inorganic reality.

Paraphrasing Karl Popper (1902-1994), who spoke – as is known – of "open society" and "closed society" (cf. The Open Society and its Enemies – Armando, Rome 1996), we could, therefore,
                                                    200
speak, and perhaps with all the more reason, of "open science" and "closed science," without wondering that the many "friends" of the latter are "enemies" of the former.

L.R.
Rome, 11 September 2001

201
The Scientific Works of Goethe (42)

Last week we concluded the examination of the first section of the fifteenth chapter. Tonight, therefore, we shall begin to occupy ourselves with the second, entitled: The "Primordial Phenomenon."


---


Steiner writes: "If we could follow the whole series of the processes that are accomplished during any sensory perception, from the peripheral terminus of the nerve in the sense organ, as far as the brain, we would nevertheless never arrive at that point at which the mechanical, chemical, and organic processes, in a word the spatial-temporal processes, cease, and there arises what we call sensory perception, that is, the sensation of color, of light, of sound, etc. The point at which the causal motion passes over into its effect, the perception, is not to be traced. But seen and considered this fact, can we still say that the two things stand to one another in the relation of cause and effect?" (p. 193).

Following the entire process that goes from the (environmental) stimulus, through the (nervous) impulse, to the brain, one never arrives at what Steiner here calls "sensory perception" or "sensation."

John Eccles (1903-1997) asserts, in fact: "Thus far, it has been impossible to develop any neurophysiological theory that explains the way in which the synthesis of a diversity of cerebral events can be reached, so that there may be a unified conscious experience of a global or Gestalt character. The cerebral events remain disparate, since they are essentially the single effects of innumerable neurons that are organized in modules and thus enter into forming part of the spatio-temporal schemata of activity. The cerebral events furnish no explanation of our most common experience, namely the visual world observed as a global entity, moment by moment"; and again: "There exists no explanation for the tremendous enigma of the unification of our perceptive experiences" (How the I Controls its Brain – Rizzoli, Milan 1994, pp. 49-50 and 140).


---


Steiner continues: "Let us for once investigate the facts quite objectively," supposing that, in our consciousness, there appears the sensation of the color red. "Once I have the sensation of red, together with the indication of a determinate place, I am led, first of all, to some object of the outer world as the bearer of that sensation. I can now certainly ask myself what spatial-temporal processes unfold in that object while it appears to me endowed with the red color; and then, as an answer to my question, there will present themselves to me mechanical, chemical, and other processes. I shall therefore continue in the investigation, and seek what processes have unfolded along the path from that object as far as my sense organ, in order to transmit to me the sensation of red. Here, too, there cannot present themselves to me as intermediaries anything other than processes of motion, or electrical currents, or chemical changes. The same result I should have to obtain even if I could go further and investigate the further transmission from the sense organ as far as the cerebral center. The quid that is transmitted along this whole path is the perception of red in question. But how that perception presents itself in a determinate object lying along the path that goes from the stimulus as far as the perception depends solely upon the nature of that object. The sensation is present at every place, from the stimulus to the brain, but not as such, not explicit, but rather as corresponds to the nature of the object existing at that place" (pp. 193-194).


---


Since, however, we have learned to distinguish between the perceptive act, which (going to meet the stimulus) sets the process of perception in motion; the perceptive image, which concludes it; the percept, as content of the perception; and the sensation, as sentient-soulic experience of the percept (we spoke of it a couple of times ago), it will be well for us to re-read this passage in the following way: "Once I have the sensation of red, together with the indication of a determinate
                                                  202
place, I am led, first of all, to some object of the outer world as the bearer of the content of that sensation. I can now certainly ask myself what spatial-temporal processes unfold in that object while it appears to me endowed with the red color; and then, as an answer to my question, there will present themselves to me mechanical, chemical, and other processes. I shall therefore continue in the investigation, and seek what processes have unfolded along the path from that object as far as my sense organ, in order to transmit to me the content of the sensation of red. Here, too, there cannot present themselves to me as intermediaries anything other than processes of motion, or electrical currents, or chemical changes. The same result I should have to obtain even if I could go further and investigate the further transmission from the sense organ as far as the cerebral center. The percept that is transmitted along this whole path is the content of the perception and of the sensation of red in question. But how that content presents itself in a determinate medium lying along the path that goes from the stimulus as far as the perceptive image (as the outcome of the perceptive process) depends solely upon the nature of that medium. The content of the sensation is present at every place, from the stimulus to the brain, but not as sensation, not explicit, but rather as corresponds to the nature of the medium existing at that place."

These modifications aim, obviously, at making things clearer, and not at correcting Steiner. For the rest, what he says in the last sentence, "the sensation is present at every place, from the stimulus to the brain, but not as such, not explicit...," could already suffice to understand that what "is present at every place, from the stimulus to the brain" (the percept) is not yet the sensation, inasmuch as the latter will be given, "as such," only when the percept arrives at making itself explicit in the sentient soul.

In order to go from the body to the spirit or, which is the same thing, from sleep to waking (to the consciousness soul or to the representation), the percept must, therefore, accomplish a journey, whose stages do not modify its intrinsic nature (which always remains what it is), but only the form of its various manifestations (for us, unconscious or conscious).


---


Steiner observes, in fact: "From this there results, however, a truth that is suited to illuminate the whole theoretical foundation of physics and of physiology. What do I learn by investigating a thing (Ding – ed.) drawn from a process that presents itself in my consciousness as sensation? Nothing more than the way in which that thing (Ding – ed.) answers to the action that proceeds from the sensation; in other words: the way in which a sensation extrinsicates itself in whatsoever object (Gegenstand – ed.) of the spatial-temporal world. Far from being the cause that arouses in me the sensation, that spatial-temporal process is the effect of the sensation in a thing (Ding – ed.) extended in space and in time" (p. 194).

Very well, let us then take up again what Boncinelli says about sound (which "is constituted by a vibration of the molecules of the air that propagates itself with a certain velocity, like a pressure wave" – The Brain, the Mind, and the Soul – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 16), and let us try to re-read, in this key, this passage as well (all the more so since the translation is not of the best here): "What do we learn by investigating the vibration of the molecules of the air, drawn from a process that presents itself in my consciousness as sound? Nothing more than the way in which those molecules of the air answer to the action that proceeds from the sound; in other words: the way in which the sound extrinsicates itself in the air (in water, or in any other medium, it would extrinsicate itself in a different way). Far from being the cause that arouses in me the sound, that vibration of the molecules is the effect of the sound in the air."

Answer to a question

Sound is in itself a quality, and for that very reason an essence that appears in a different way according to the ambit in which it presents itself. Thanks to Steiner we know, for example, that one and the same "solar" quality presents itself, in the ambit of the musical notes, as the note A (la); in the ambit of the metals, as gold; and, in the anatomo-physiological one, as the heart. Tradition is accustomed to speak, in this regard, of "analogies"; but these are founded precisely upon the fact that the essence is one, whereas its manifestations
                                                   203
can be manifold. Remember, moreover, that the quality or the essence is given to us as concept (A), and that it is this – as we know – that is present, from the very beginning (of the perceptive process), in the garb of the percept (X). We have said, in fact, that the percept is the unknown concept, whereas the concept is the known percept.


---


"Let us now see – Steiner writes – of what kind those transmitting processes are. Do we investigate them otherwise than by means of our senses? Is it perhaps that I can examine my senses themselves with other means than precisely those same senses? Are the peripheral terminations of the nerves, the convolutions of the brain, not given precisely through sensory perception? (…) In following the perception from the point at which it is excited, as far as the organ of perception, we investigate nothing other than the continuous passing over from one perception into another. Before us stands the 'red,' as that for the sake of which we undertake, in general, our whole investigation. The red leads us back to its stimulus. In this we observe other sensations connected with the red. They are processes of motion. These same show themselves then, as further processes of motion, between the stimulus and the sense organ, and so on. But all these, in their turn, are likewise perceived sensations; and they represent nothing but a metamorphosis of processes which, inasmuch as they fall under the observation of the senses, dissolve totally into perceptions. The perceived world is, therefore, nothing other than a sum of metamorphosed perceptions" (p. 195).

In effect, we observe the environmental stimulus metamorphose into the nervous impulse; we observe this ascend, along the nervous paths, to the brain (metamorphosing, in the synaptic interval, into a bio-chemical impulse), and metamorphose in its turn, in the brain (in the neocortex), into a "neurostate" (Boncinelli) or into an "exocytosis from the presynaptic vesicular reticula of the pyramidal cells of the dendrons" (Eccles – op. cit. p. 140).

As one sees, it is always a question of sensible perceptions (today supported by ever more refined instruments). What, then, is the problem? It is that thinking should discover the nexus that binds all these perceptions to one another, instead of arbitrarily arrogating to itself the right of raising some of them to the objective rank of "causes" and lowering others to the subjective rank of "effects."

Steiner writes: "We have had, for convenience, to make use of an expression that cannot be brought entirely into agreement with the present result. We have said that every object inserted in the intermediate space between the stimulus and the organ of perception brings to expression a sensation in the way that is conformable to its nature. Taking the thing rigorously, the object itself is nothing other than the sum of those processes in which it manifests itself" (p. 195).

But it is precisely in order to take "the thing rigorously" that we first made some distinctions and re-read, modifying them, a couple of passages. What, in fact, does Steiner mean? That "every object inserted in the intermediate space between the stimulus and the organ of perception" (in the case of sound, the air, the ears, the nervous system, or the brain) "is nothing other" than a further aggregate of perceptions ("the sum of those processes in which it manifests itself").

But through all these changes, what is it, then, that "endures"?


---


Steiner writes in this regard: "One demands that over against the fleeting sensation there stand an enduring objective process which, as such, is in its turn objectively delimited in time, that is, has beginning, duration, and end. Such a process should unfold in a matter without beginning or end, that is to say, indestructible, eternal. This, it is said, is the enduring element in the changing of the processes. But must we not rigorously distinguish between the content of the sensation and the manifesting of the same? (…) For this content, taken purely as such, is it not perhaps a matter of complete indifference that precisely in this moment it enters into my consciousness, and after a certain number of
                                                  204
minutes or seconds goes out of it? What constitutes the content of the sensation, that is, that thing which alone enters objectively into consideration, is entirely independent of it. Now one cannot regard as an essential condition of the subsistence of a thing that which is a matter of complete indifference for its content" (p. 196).

What, in fact, is the percept if not precisely that content of the perception which begins to reveal itself (in its quality) in the sensation? As quality, essence, or concept, this content is independent of time and of space, but it comes to meet us in time and in space.

I think you will remember, in this regard, the schema that was of help to us in the study of The Philosophy of Freedom. I refer to that schema in which, alongside the concepts of "physical body," "etheric body," "astral body," and "I," we placed those, in order, of "space," "time," "quality," and "being," noting moreover that the "threshold" that divides the sphere of existing (of the physical body and the etheric body) from that of being (of the astral body and the I) divides also the sphere of space and time from that of quality and being.

Well, Steiner, in the passage just cited, stresses precisely the fact that time has to do not with the essence of the phenomenon, but solely with its manifestation. Time – Hegel explains, in fact – "is the pure being in itself inasmuch as it is simply a coming out of itself," whereas space is "the juxtaposition of the wholly ideal, because it is the being outside of itself..." (Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989, pp. 234 and 229).

Time is, therefore, the coming out of itself of quality (a process), whereas space is its being outside of itself (a state).


---


Steiner writes: "If on a given day there emerges a new quality that conserves itself during some time in different states of evolution and then disappears again, here, too, we must regard as essential the content of that quality; and this, as such, has absolutely nothing to do with the concepts of beginning, duration, and end. By 'essential' we understand here that by virtue of which a thing is precisely as it presents itself. What matters is not that something emerges at a determinate moment, but what emerges. The sum of all these determinations expressed by the 'what' forms the content of the world. Only, this 'what' extrinsicates itself in the most various determinations and in the most diverse forms; forms that stand in reciprocal relations and determine one another. With this they enter into the relation of derivation according to space and time" (pp. 196-197).

The content of the world is formed, therefore, by the "sum" (the aggregate) of the qualia, and not only of the quanta. To comprehend it truly, one would need, therefore, to dispose both of a science of quantities and of a science of qualities (of a science of the spirit). For if it is a limitation to know only quantity, it is, on the other hand, a true and proper evil to reduce – as is generally done – quality (which one does not know) to quantity (which one knows).

Steiner continues: "Only from an altogether erroneous interpretation of the concept of time was the concept of matter generated. One believes one would volatilize the world into a substance-less semblance if one did not think of something unchanging, which endures in time and remains the same while its determinations change, as underlying the whole changing sum of the events. But time is not a receptacle within which the changes unfold; it does not exist before and outside of things. Time is the sensible expression of the circumstance that the facts, according to their content, depend successively one upon another (…) Time begins to appear only where the essence of a thing manifests itself. Time belongs to the world of manifest phenomena. It has as yet nothing to do with the essence itself. This is graspable only ideally. Only one who is not capable of accomplishing in his thinking this re-ascent from the manifestation to the essence hypostatizes time
                                                  205
as something that precedes the facts. But then he requires an existence that survives the changes. As such, he assumes indestructible matter" (p. 197).


---


Hegel observes precisely: "Everything, one says, is born and dies in time; if one abstracts from everything, that is, from the filling of time and also from the filling of space, there remain empty time and empty space – that is, one then posits and represents those abstractions of exteriority as if they were per se. But it is not in time that everything is born and dies: time itself is this becoming, this birth and death" (op. cit. p. 234).

This stands, therefore, to signify that our (physical) body is our space, and that our etheric body is our time. But what is our time? It is the duration of our earthly life or, better said, of our earthly manifestation.

Time is, therefore, a fact, and not other than the facts; it is living itself as force, movement, or becoming, and not, as Steiner would say, a "receptacle" in which life unfolds.

Steiner says again that "only one who is not capable of accomplishing in his thinking this re-ascent from the manifestation to the essence hypostatizes time as something that precedes the facts." Why "re-ascent"? Because the (human) cognitive activity is called upon to traverse in reverse, and therefore precisely to "re-ascend," that path of the (cosmic) creative activity whose principal stages – as we have already had occasion to recall – are indicated by Steiner (in the Anthroposophical Maxims) as those of the "divine-spiritual Entity" (I), of its "manifestation" (astral world), of its "working effect" (etheric world), and of its "completed work" (physical world).

Answer to a question

We could also say, if we wished, that "only one who is not capable of accomplishing in his thinking this re-ascent" from space to time and, above all, from time to quality (not having realized how quality first flows as time and then precipitates as space) feels the need to seek something "enduring" that stands, not beyond, but rather on this side of time and space.

To accomplish this re-ascent, there is required, in fact, a thinking capable of moving among these levels, and of experiencing their reality by metamorphosing itself. What else, in fact, are imaginative thinking and inspirative thinking if not ascending metamorphoses of the (ordinary) representative one? We compared, in due course, those levels to the stories of a house, and thinking to the staircase that joins them. As, without the staircase, we should not be given to ascend and descend from one story to the other, so, without thinking, we should not be given to ascend (noetically) and descend (ethically) from one level of manifestation to the other. If one does not discover and experience the essential continuity of thinking, one cannot, therefore, discover the reality of the different levels of consciousness.

Inasmuch as it is an act of the I, or the I in act, thinking represents, in fact, the "quintessence": that is, the unique and enduring essence of the manifold and changing manifestations.

L.R.
Rome, 18 September 2001

206
The Scientific Works of Goethe (43)


---


Tonight, before continuing the reading of the second section of the fifteenth chapter, I should like to make some further consideration upon the problem of time.

In our schema, which represents a hierarchy – as René Guénon would say – of the "states of being" (R. Guénon: The Multiple States of Being – Edizioni Studi Tradizionali, Turin 1965), we have placed "time" between "space" and "quality," characterizing it as "a fact, and not other than the facts," as "living itself as force, movement, or becoming."

If Goethe holds it to be a "sensible-supersensible" reality, and Hegel a "sensible-insensible" reality, it is owed to the fact that time is deputed precisely to mediate between the "sensible" sphere of space and the "supersensible" or "insensible" sphere of quality.

Inasmuch as it is the mediator between what presents itself to the eyes of the physical body and what presents itself to those of the spirit, time has a fluid and elusive character: "Time – Hegel observes, in fact – is the being that, while it is, is not, and while it is not, is; becoming intuited" (Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989, p. 233). (Faust declares to Mephistopheles: "If ever I say to the fleeting moment: Stay! You are beautiful! Then you may put me in fetters..." – Faust – Einaudi, Turin 1967, p. 47.)

This dynamic character of its should help us to conceive our schema in the same way. If it is important, in fact, to succeed in grasping the spirit in the (hierarchical) being of its "states," it is equally important to succeed in grasping it in their becoming one from the other: in the becoming of the essence or of quality from the I AM (from the "divine-spiritual Entity"); in the becoming of time from the essence or from quality; in the becoming of space from time.

On the cognitive plane, however, one must proceed – we have said it – in reverse: one must, that is, start from the consciousness of space (representational) in order to make it become consciousness of time (imaginative); start from that of time in order to make it become consciousness of the essence or of quality (inspirative); start from the consciousness of the essence or of quality in order to make it become consciousness of the spirit or of the I AM (intuitive).

What happens, in fact, when one is not capable of doing it? It happens that one studies existing, without ever succeeding in ascending to being; or else one studies being, without ever succeeding in descending to existing.

Do you remember the dream of Jacob? "And behold a ladder set upon the earth, whose top reached to heaven; and behold the angels of God, who ascended and descended upon the ladder" (Gn 28:12). Well, should we not also learn, like the angels, to ascend and descend the ladder of the spirit?

But let us now take up the text again, beginning to read a little before the point at which we had broken off.


---


Steiner writes: "Only one who is not capable of accomplishing in his thinking this re-ascent from the manifestation to the essence hypostatizes time as something that precedes the facts. But then he requires an existence that survives the changes. As such, he assumes indestructible matter. With this he would have created something upon which time can do nothing, something that would endure in the midst of every change. In reality, however, he has done nothing other than show his own incapacity to penetrate from the temporal manifestation of the facts to their essence, which has nothing to do with time (…) The essence of a thing cannot suffer destruction, since it is outside of time and determines time itself" (pp. 197-198).

You see: "it determines time itself." As we have said, it is, in fact, the essence that, "coming out of itself," unfolding or manifesting itself, determines time (makes itself time).

207
Steiner continues: "With this we have cast light upon two concepts for which today there is still found very scant comprehension: the concepts of essence and of manifestation. One who understands the thing rightly, in the manner set forth above, cannot go in search of a demonstration of the indestructibility of the essence of a thing, because destruction implies within itself the concept of time, which has nothing to do with the essence. After these considerations we can say that the sensible image of the world is the sum of the contents of the perceptions in continuous metamorphosis, without a matter underlying them" (p. 198).

By saying "without a matter underlying them," one does not, obviously, deny matter as manifest reality, but rather that "indestructible matter" which one would wish to make valid as an essence: that is, as a quality, deprived, however, of qualities, and therefore – according to what we saw in due course – as a quantity (do you remember what Hegel says? "Quantity is the pure being in which the determination is posited no longer as one with being itself [as is the case with quality – ed.], but as surpassed or indifferent").

This means, in other words, that matter is true, whereas materialism is false. A conception that places at its own foundation a reality that is not spiritual because it is material, and is not material because it is imperceptible (to the senses), is, in fact, "metaphysical," and therefore anything but "scientific" (as it is instead accustomed to present itself).


---


Answer to a question

It is true – as you say – that when a person dear to us dies, we suffer precisely because their manifestation is missing from us. But all the more do we suffer in no longer perceiving their manifestation, the less we have perceived, during their life, their essence. The physical body dies (and this is what we see); the etheric body dies; the astral body dies; but the I does not die: that is, the manifestations of the I die, but not their essence.

Representation gives us, however, the semblance, not the essence. The grief we feel at the death of those we love is, therefore, a proof of our dependence upon that representational consciousness which is firmly bound to sensible perception, and which, when a dear person disappears, allows us to keep in memory only their physical semblance.

We said, one evening, that today's materialism is not so much a philosophical or ideological fact as rather an "existential state"; to the point that, paraphrasing the title of a famous article by Benedetto Croce (1866-1952), "Why We Cannot But Call Ourselves Christians" (La Critica, 20 November 1942), we could very well assert: Why We Cannot But Call Ourselves Materialists.

We cannot, in fact, but call ourselves such, because our ordinary thinking, inasmuch as it is reflected, abstract, "weak" (Vattimo) or – as Scaligero likes to say – "dialectical," is truly "earthbound," and our ordinary level of consciousness never raises itself, in consequence, above the sensible.

One may even be convinced of having surpassed materialism, and of speaking therefore in the name of one of the many other "isms" that "pass the muster" (modern and post-modern), but one will not, in fact, be anything but materialists.

Steiner observes precisely (and we are in 1918!): "One now often hears it said that the materialism of the nineteenth century has been scientifically surpassed. But in truth it is not at all. Only, today one very often does not notice that we have no ideas other than those with which one can approach only what is material. Thus materialism conceals itself at present, whereas in the second half of the nineteenth century it declared itself openly. But toward a conception that comprehends the world spiritually, the concealed materialism of today is no less intolerant than the confessed materialism of the last century. Only, it deceives many men, who believe they must reject a conception of the world that tends toward spirituality, for the fact that the conception of the natural sciences has 'long since abandoned materialism'" (The Philosophy of Freedom – Antroposofica, Milan 1966, p. 154).
                                                  208
Enough, then, of idle chatter: either one decides to develop a grade of consciousness superior to the ordinary one (to that of the ego), or one will find oneself ever less able to confront and overcome the trials that destiny holds in store for us on the individual and collective plane.

That pathological state of the intellect (and of the ego) that goes by the name of "intellectualism" believes, in its pride and presumption, that it can take seriously everything that passes through its head, and that it can therefore "fiddle its own tune" in defiance of reality. But the materialistic or scientistic metaphysics that it improvises in this way is not fed and sustained by thinking, but (more or less unconsciously) by fear.

Steiner maintains it, but John Eccles (Nobel laureate for neurophysiology, in 1963) also recognizes it, and he writes: "If one had to describe the deepest motivation of materialism, one could assert that it is simply a terror of consciousness (…) The deepest reason for the fear of consciousness lies in the fact that consciousness possesses the substantially terrifying character of subjectivity": that is – we might add – of the I or of the spirit (How the I Controls its Brain – Rizzoli, Milan 1994, p. 74).

In order to conquer materialistic or scientistic intellectualism, it is, therefore, necessary to conquer fear, and therefore the (preponderantly Ahrimanic) resistance to transforming, by means of thinking, one's own feeling and one's own willing (celebrated, in this regard, are the following verses of Goethe: "So long as you do not make your own / this 'die and become,' / you are but a somber stranger / upon the darkling earth" – Blessed Longing, in The West-Eastern Divan – Rizzoli, Milan 1997, p. 97 – trans. by Ludovica Koch).

But let us return to ourselves.


---


Steiner writes: "The exposition made does not strike, naturally, anything but that concept of matter which physics places at the basis of its considerations, identifying it with the old, equally erroneous, concept of substance of metaphysics. One thing is matter thought as the true reality placed at the basis of the phenomena; another is matter as phenomenon, as manifestation. Our considerations regard solely the first of these two concepts. The second is not touched by them" (p. 198).

This is what we were saying just now: one thing is matter as "phenomenon," another matter as "noumenon." The former is, in fact, a physical reality, whereas the latter is a metaphysical illusion.

Steiner continues: "The world of what presents itself to us as perception, that is: extension, motion, rest, force, light, warmth, sound, electricity, etc., is the object of all science. Now, (…) if everything that manifests itself were a perfect expression, in no way disturbed, of the interior essence of things, then science would be the most useless thing in the world. For the task of knowledge would be already fully and totally fulfilled in perception. Indeed, we could not even distinguish between essence and manifestation; both, as identical, would fully coincide. But it is not so. Let us suppose that the element A, contained in the world of facts, stands in a certain relation with the element B. Both, according to our exposition, are naturally nothing but phenomena. The connection between the two manifests itself in its turn as a phenomenon, and this phenomenon we shall call C. What we can now establish, within the world of facts, is the relation of A, B, and C. But alongside A, B, and C, there still exist in the sensible world infinitely many other similar elements. Let us take any one of them, D; as soon as it is added to them, everything will present itself modified. Instead of having C as the consequence of A plus B, with the intervention of D there will arise an essentially different phenomenon: E" (pp. 198-199).


---


What does this mean? It means that the phenomena A, B, C, and D are all participants in the complex phenomenon E. But which of them are essential, and which accidental? Which constitute, that is, a conditio sine qua non for the being-given of E, and which do not? This is yet to be discovered.
                                                   209
Steiner writes, in fact: "The cause why the phenomenon E comes to meet us are other phenomena, in relations now nearer, now more distant. Some are absolutely indispensable in order that such a phenomenon can come about; others, even if lacking, would not impede its existence, and yet determine that it happens precisely so. From this one sees that we must distinguish between the necessary conditions and the accidental conditions of a phenomenon. Now, the phenomena that arise under the sole action of indispensable conditions we can call original (originary); the others, derived. If we comprehend the original phenomena starting from their premises, we can, by adding new premises, comprehend the derived ones equally. Here the task of science becomes clear to us. It has to penetrate into the world of phenomena so deeply as to find there those that depend only upon necessary conditions. And the conceptual expression for such necessary connections is: laws of nature" (pp. 199-200).

In the "law of nature" there expresses itself, therefore, conceptually, Goethe's Urphänomen. The law, inasmuch as it is the necessary condition for the being-given of the phenomenon, is always equal to itself, whereas the "derived" phenomena can vary, and can therefore bring it about that the "original" phenomenon presents itself now in one way, now in another.

It will be well, in any case, to stress that the separating of the essential from the inessential, if it is already not easy in the ambit of the natural phenomena, is still less so in that of human affairs. It is above all in this field, in fact, that, as a function of the personal sympathies and antipathies, of the cravings or the prejudices (all the more conditioning the less conscious they are), one can be induced to pass off the inessential as essential, and vice versa.

Let us suppose, just to give a most banal example, that a foreigner steals my wallet; that I, becoming aware of it, accuse him of the theft; and that someone, hearing me accuse a foreigner, calls me a "xenophobe." I am, however, indicting him not because he is a foreigner, but because he is a thief. In relation to what has happened, it is, in fact, essential that he has stolen my wallet, and not that he is a foreigner. It is not, therefore, I who am afflicted with xenophobia, but rather one who judges me such who is, if anything, afflicted with xenophilia.


---


Answer to a question

From our point of view, we could consider "classical" physics as the physics of the ego, of representational consciousness, and of the "completed work."

Between the end of the nineteenth century and the beginning of the twentieth, when the problem began to be posed of surpassing (evolutively) this level of self-consciousness, of consciousness, and of reality, the physicists, however, began to orient themselves, not toward the supersensible, but toward the subsensible, thereby ending by taking away from the ego, little by little, a great part of its certainties, without furnishing it with others of a higher order. The theory of relativity, for example, united space and time in a hypothetical and abstract "four-dimensionality," and not – as would have been desirable – in quality or in essence; just as quantum physics has not given us, with the quanta (subsensible), anything but a counter-image of the qualia (supersensible).

If one considers, moreover, that the certainties taken from the ego by "modern" physics went to add themselves to those already subtracted from it by Marx, Nietzsche, and Freud (by the so-called "school of suspicion" – cf. P. Ricoeur: On Interpretation – An Essay on Freud – Il Saggiatore, Milan 1967), one will no longer wonder at witnessing today an disquieting involution and disintegration (in the first place moral) of the ego, rather than a positive evolution of it.

The fact is that those certainties (proper to the first evolutionary phase of the consciousness soul), precisely by reason of their sensible base, should have constituted a springboard for the development of the ego in the direction of the spiritual Self (of the social or moral I), and not change themselves into a

210
definitive and dogmatic acquisition, destined, as such, sooner or later to expose its flank to the assault of disintegrating or dissolving forces (still more formidable than the Luciferic and Ahrimanic ones).

One reads, in fact, in the Gospel: "Then the disciples came to him and said: 'Why do you speak to them in parables?' He answered: 'Because to you it is given to know the mysteries of the kingdom of heaven, but to them it is not given. For to him who has, it will be given, and he will be in abundance; and from him who has not, even what he has will be taken away'" (Mt 13:10-12).

L.R.
Rome, 25 September 2001

211
The Scientific Works of Goethe (44)

Tonight we shall finish the second section of the fifteenth chapter, and we shall occupy ourselves with the third, entitled: The System of Natural Science.

We saw, last time, that the "laws of nature" are "the conceptual expression" of the "connections" or of the "necessary" relations among the phenomena.

Since, however, a determinate connection or relation, that is, a thought (a concept), presupposes the indeterminate activity of connecting or of relating, that is, of thinking, we must see in it the result of a cosmic thinking which human thinking is called upon solely to discover.


---


Steiner writes, in fact: "The system of science is distinguished from that of nature by the fact that, in the former, the nexus between the phenomena is established by the intellect, and thereby made comprehensible. Science must absolutely never add anything to the world of the phenomena, but solely discover its hidden connections. The use of the intellect must limit itself uniquely to this work. By having recourse to something that does not appear, in order to explain what appears, the intellect and all scientific work exceed the limits of their own competence" (p. 200).


---


What, therefore, should the scientist do? Observe the world, both by means of the physical senses and by means of thinking. As it happens when one turns one's gaze about in the hope of seeing what one seeks, so one should "turn thinking about" in the hope of discovering what one seeks. But it is precisely when one (actively) turns thinking about that one truly thinks. It is not a question, therefore, of waiting for the manna to rain from heaven, but of creating the conditions suited to making it rain. If it is not prepared by a serious and deep commitment of thinking, what one believes descends from above (as happens, for the most part, with the so-called "intuitive" types) rises instead from below, giving voice not to the reality of the phenomena, but to that of our lower nature.

Steiner says that "by having recourse to something that does not appear, in order to explain what appears, the intellect and all scientific work exceed the limits of their own competence." Well, is it not precisely Kant who explains the "phenomenon" that appears by means of the "noumenon" that does not appear?

This is not, in any case, the point at which he "exceeds the limits of his own competence"; he exceeds them, in fact, when he does not content himself with observing that the noumenon "does not appear" (to the physical senses), but arrives at decreeing that it is absolutely impossible that it appear (and yet, Goethe says: "'Nature hides God!' But not from everyone!" – Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 177).

But what has Kant to do – one might object – with "scientific work"? The "molecular biologist" Boncinelli explains it to us once again: "I am personally very interested in the problem of knowledge. Kant already expressed it in a genial way: man knows the world by imprinting upon it a series of shelvings or of a priori forms. This active intervention superimposes itself upon the chemical and physical stimuli coming from without. When one ascends the hierarchical scale, one arrives at that preconstituted mental reality that is intelligence, categorization, or in other words the attribution of names to things (sic! – ed.). Future generations will witness the triumph of the Kantian intellectual enterprise, which will answer the most interesting questions about how we know, about why we behave in a determinate way, and, in the final analysis, about who we are" (And Now? – Einaudi, Turin 2000, p. 40).

In reality, everything appears, but, by reason of our cognitive organization, the phenomenon appears to the physical senses, whereas the noumenon appears to thinking. Whoever cannot see this should not, therefore, take it out on the noumenon, but rather on his thinking, and therefore on himself.


---


Steiner continues: "Only one who sees the absolute rightness of our deductions can comprehend the Goethean theory of colors (…) He knew the attributions of intellectual thinking; for him light was given as sensation, and when he wished to explain the nexus between light and color, he could not do it by means of a speculation, but only by means of a type-phenomenon, inasmuch as he sought the necessary condition that must be added to light in order that color can be generated. Newton, too, saw color arise in connection with light, but he limited himself to speculating: How is color born from light? This was inherent in his speculative way of thinking, whereas it was not inherent in the Goethean one, which was objective and capable of comprehending itself rightly. Therefore the Newtonian supposition: 'Light is composed of colored lights,' had to appear to Goethe the result of an erroneous speculation; he felt himself authorized to assert something about the nexus between light and color only when a given condition was added to it, but not to assert anything about light itself, with the aid of a speculative concept" (pp. 200-201).

Let us note well the difference: one thing is to rack one's brains to answer the question: "How is color born from light?"; another is to commit oneself to creating the conditions necessary for color and light themselves to answer the question.

Let it be observed, moreover, that Newton (1642-1727), like all the so-called "atomists," moreover, shows himself to have no inkling whatever of the essential or qualitative reality of a "whole." Light is not, for him, in fact, anything but a "corpuscular aggregate," and therefore a physical (quantitative) reality, resulting from the union of the elements (called today "photons") that compose it.

Steiner writes: "With this the deepest reason is laid bare why Goethe, when he looked through the prism, could not adhere to the theory of Newton. According to the latter, the prism was supposed to be the first condition of the birth of color. But another condition, namely the presence of a dark element, manifested itself to him as still more originary for the arising of color. The prism came only as the second condition" (p. 201).


---


It is by virtue of the glass prism (of its "angle of refraction") that one obtains, in fact, the so-called "spectrum" of light: that is, the supposed "decomposition" of a ray of (white) sunlight into what are believed to be its "single component colors."

For Newton, this phenomenon was "originary"; for Goethe, it was, on the contrary, "derived," since he considered originary the one determined by the encounter (or collision) of light with a "dark element": that is to say, with a turbid medium.

As you see, there here recurs the problem of distinguishing (we spoke of it last week) the essential from the inessential. For color to be generated, it is, in fact, essential that light encounter (or collide with) a turbid medium (with darkness or with gloom), whereas it is inessential that this turbid medium be constituted by the prism utilized by Newton, under altogether particular conditions.

Goethe notes, in fact: "Newton's experiment, upon which the traditional theory of colors rests, is of an extreme complexity: it combines, in fact, all these conditions. In order that the spectrum of light appear, there are necessary: 1) a glass prism; 2) that it have three faces; 3) and be small; 4) a shutter; 5) with an opening; 6) that it be very small; 7) an edge of the Sun passing through it; 8) at a certain distance, in a 9) certain direction upon the prism; 10) that it project upon a board, 11) placed behind the prism at a certain distance. Discard conditions 3, 6, and 11; make the opening large, place the board near to the prism, and the dear spectrum cannot appear, and will not appear" – Maxims and Reflections, p. 242).

Steiner concludes, in any case: "Naturally, I would not so much as dream of wishing to defend all the single particulars of the Goethean theory of colors. What I should like to see maintained of it is solely the principle. Here my task cannot even be that of deriving from its principle all the phenomena of the theory of colors still unknown in Goethe's time. If, one day, I should have the fortune to possess the time and the means to write a theory of colors in the Goethean sense,
                                                  213
fully equal to the modern conquests of science, I could fulfill that task only in such a work" (pp. 201-202).

We have thus finished the second section; let us pass, therefore, to the third: The System of Natural Science.

Steiner writes: "Let us consider two perceptions: A and B. These are given to us at first as entities devoid of concepts. No conceptual reflection can make me change the qualities given to my sensory perception into something else. Nor can I find any quality of thinking by means of which to construct what is given to me in sensible reality, if the perception is lacking. I can never procure for a color-blind man a representation of the quality 'red,' however much I may describe it to him conceptually by all possible means. The perception of the senses has, therefore, a quid that never penetrates into the concept, which must be perceived if, in general, it is to become the object of our knowledge" (p. 202).


---


A and B, inasmuch as they are "perceptions" and "entities devoid of concepts," are, therefore, two percepts: that is, two entities that present themselves (like the will) as "force," and not (like thinking) as "form." What, in fact, is the first characteristic of perceptive experience? That of being, inasmuch as it is the result of the encounter (or collision) of the being or essence of the subject with the being or essence of the object, the immediate experience of a quid that is and exists. The living perception or sensation of any reality whatsoever is, therefore, a thing quite different from the abstract idea we subsequently form of it. We are, in fact, avid, as a rule, of perceptions and sensations precisely because these, unlike ordinary thinking, make us feel in contact with reality (with that of ourselves and of the world).

Perception is, therefore, the point of departure of the journey of knowing: a journey that should, however, be one of "going and returning," and not of "one way only," as is that of intellectualism. The latter, in fact, does not start from the perception in order to arrive at the concept, so as then to be able to return (with the concept) to the perception, but starts from the perception in order to go gradually losing itself along the intricate and tortuous paths of abstraction, which lead it ever further from reality.

Steiner continues: "What function, therefore, has the concept that we conjoin with any perception of the senses? It must evidently be an altogether autonomous element, and bring something new that belongs to the sensible perception, but that does not appear in it. It is certain, however, that this new quid, which the concept comes to add to the sensible perception, is that which first expresses what comes to meet our need for explanation. Only when we have a concept of it are we able to comprehend any element of the world of the senses (…) By means of the concept we are able to say, about the world of the senses, something that cannot be perceived. From this there appears immediately that, if the essence of the sensible perception were exhausted in the sensible quality, nothing totally new could be added to it, in the form of the concept. The sensible perception is, therefore, not at all a totality, but is only one aspect of the totality, and precisely that part which can only be looked at. Only by means of the concept does it become clear to us what we are looking at" (pp. 202-203).


---


You see, the concept "must bring something new that belongs to the sensible perception, but that does not appear in it." The concept, in fact, "appears" within us, whereas the percept "appears" outside of us: thanks to the latter we are able to experience a force that could not be thought; thanks to the former we are able to think a form that could not be perceived (by the physical senses).

This depends, however, upon us, and not upon the reality of the object or of the phenomenon with which we are grappling. This is, in fact, one, but we are organized in such a way as to experience, at one (volitional) level, its force (as percept), and to apprehend, at another (thinking) level, its form (as
                                                  214
concept). Only by re-uniting the percept with the concept and the concept with the percept do we, therefore, obtain the entelechy: that is to say, the essence of the object or of the phenomenon.

It is fundamental, therefore, to distinguish the essence both from the percept, which represents solely the way in which the essence is given to perceiving, and from the concept, which represents solely the way in which the essence is given to thinking.

Steiner writes, in fact: "We can now express the substantial importance of what we developed as method in the preceding chapter (section – ed.). With the conceptual grasping of a given of the world of the senses, the quid contained in the datum of the perception manifests itself" (p. 203).

The concept illumines, therefore, the essence of the percept. Let us imagine, for example (repetita iuvant), moving about in a room with our eyes closed and our arms outstretched, and arriving thus at touching something. From this perception there will immediately spring the following judgment: "Here there is something!" Expressed in more learned terms, the same judgment would sound thus: "Now and here, something is" (or: "Now and here, an entity is"). Thanks to this, we know, therefore, that something is, but we do not yet know what the thing that is, is: we know the concept as percept, that is, but not yet the percept as concept. Only after having opened our eyes and recognized the percept in the concept and the concept in the percept, can we therefore say that we have known "what the thing that is, is," and that we have therefore truly had "experience" of it.

One thing is, in fact, experience, as synthesis of percept and concept; another, perception, as its initial and singular (sensible) moment.

Steiner continues: "Only in the concept, therefore, does the world receive its full content. We have seen, however, how the concept indicates to us, beyond the single phenomenon, the nexus between things. Therefore, that which in the world of the senses presents itself separate, isolated, appears for the concept a unitary whole. Thus, by means of our scientific method, there is born as final goal the monistic natural science; but not an abstract monism that presupposes the unity, and then forcibly inserts into it the single facts of concrete life, rather a concrete monism that shows, step by step, how the apparent multiplicity of sensible existence proves in the end an ideal unity" (p. 203).


---


We perceive, for example, here the phenomenon A and there the phenomenon B and, for the very fact of perceiving them, we experience them as separate and isolated. At the moment in which the percept A reunites itself with the concept A, and the percept B reunites itself with the concept B, we notice, however, that between concept A and concept B there subsists a relation, and that it is precisely this (conceptual) relation that liberates the two (sensible) phenomena from their apparent isolation. (Do you remember what Steiner said? "What constitutes the singularity of an object cannot be comprehended, but only perceived.")

It will be of use, therefore, to recall once again that if we were not given the possibility of experiencing, by means of sensible perception, separation and isolation, we should never be given that of experiencing ourselves as individuals; we should never, in fact, have arrived at experiencing ourselves as I's if we had not isolated ourselves, separating ourselves from everything else and experiencing it precisely as a not-I.

Steiner writes: "Now the way in which the concept (the idea) extrinsicates itself in the world of the senses constitutes the difference between the different kingdoms of nature. If the real essence of the sensible arrives only as far as an existence that places it completely outside of the concept, so that in its own changes it is dominated by it as by a law, we call that being inorganic (…) In this sphere we have to do with phenomena and laws which, when they are originary, can be called type-phenomena. Here, therefore, the perceptible conceptual element stands outside of the perceived multiplicity" (p. 204).

215
But what, properly speaking, is "this sphere"? That of space. Do you remember what Hegel asserts? "Space is the juxtaposition of the wholly ideal, because it is the being outside of itself." It is in space, in fact, that things exist "juxtaposed," and therefore separate ("to juxtapose" means "to place side by side without connecting") not only from one another, but also from their essence (inasmuch as – as Hegel again says – they are outside of themselves, or – as Steiner says – outside of the concept).

Steiner continues: "But the same unity perceivable by the senses can already indicate something that transcends it; it can compel us, when we wish to comprehend it, to proceed to other determinations beyond those perceivable by us. Then that which is graspable conceptually appears as sensible unity. The two things, concept and perception, are not identical, but the concept does not appear as a law outside of the sensible plurality, but rather within it as principle. It lies at its basis, as the element no longer sensibly perceivable that pervades it, and which we call type. With this the organic natural science has to do" (p. 204).


---


The essence manifests itself as "law" in the inorganic world and as "type" in the organic one; in neither the former (mineral) nor the latter (vegetal and animal) does it, therefore, present itself as concept (or idea). Man is, therefore, the only being capable of experiencing his own essence and that of others as concept: capable, that is, of having consciousness of his own essence and that of others, in the form of the concept (or of the idea).

I should like to recommend to you once again, in this regard, to reflect upon the tripartition of the Hegelian logic into logic of being, logic of essence, and logic of the concept. What, in fact, does the logic of the concept represent? Nothing other than the way (or the form) in which the logic of essence, operative in the world, manifests itself in thinking, operative in the human soul.

Steiner concludes: "But here, too (in the organic world – ed.) the concept does not yet appear in its own proper form of concept, but as type. Where, then, this no longer appears merely as such, that is, as pervading principle, but rather in its very form of concept, there it appears as consciousness; and here finally there manifests itself what on the lower rungs existed solely in its essence. The concept itself becomes perception; here one has to do with self-conscious man. Law of nature, type, concept are the three forms in which the ideal element extrinsicates itself. The law of nature is abstract and stands above the sensible multiplicity; it dominates inorganic science. Here idea and reality are completely sundered. The type already unites the two in a single being. The spiritual becomes operative essence; yet it does not yet exist and act as such, but must, if it wishes to be observed in its existence, be looked at sensibly. This happens in the kingdom of organic nature. The concept exists in a perceivable way. In human consciousness the concept itself is the perceivable element. Perception and idea coincide. What is contemplated is precisely the ideal element. Therefore, at this rung, the vital nuclei of the lower natural rungs can also manifest themselves. With the arising of human consciousness there is given the possibility that what on the lower rungs of life exists but does not appear, now becomes apparent reality as well" (pp. 204-205).

What does, in the final analysis, the aggregate of the human kingdom and of the three lower natural kingdoms represent? It represents a hierarchy of grades of consciousness (waking, dream, sleep, and death). When we speak of soul, we speak, in fact, of thinking, feeling, and willing; whereas when we speak of spirit, we speak precisely of grades or levels of consciousness (of spiritual Hierarchies).

We have thus finished the third section as well; next time we shall therefore occupy ourselves with the fourth.

L.R.
Rome, 2 October 2001

216
The Scientific Works of Goethe (45)


---


I hope you will not mind if tonight, taking advantage of the fact that we shall be taking up the fourth section, entitled: The System of the Theory of Colors, I propose to you, by way of introduction, some considerations of a general character.

We saw, last time, that one thing is the theory of colors of Goethe, another that of Newton; and we also heard this assertion of Steiner: "Naturally, I would not so much as dream of wishing to defend all the single particulars of the Goethean theory of colors. What I should like to see maintained of it is solely the principle."

It is not a question, therefore, of taking sides for the one or for the other, but of bringing to light what the "principle" is that distinguishes them: what, that is, the spirit is that animates the one, and what that which animates the other.

Newton – Hegel observes in this regard – "never knew that he was thinking and had to do with concepts; he believed he had to do only with physical things (…) Newton treated concepts as sensible things, and took them in the same way one is accustomed to grasp in the hand a stone or a piece of wood" (Lectures on the History of Philosophy – La Nuova Italia, Florence 1981, vol. 3, II, p. 180).

What, then, is the spirit that animates his theory? That of the realism of things. And what, on the other hand, is the spirit that animates the theory of Goethe? That of the realism of ideas.

It appears, therefore, legitimate to consider as inspirer of the former the ego (as subject of representational consciousness), and as inspirer of the latter the spiritual Self (as subject of imaginative consciousness).

If, therefore, the theory of Goethe had prevailed, and not that of Newton (or, in a broad sense, Goetheanism, and not the utilitarianism of Anglo-Saxon stamp), European culture would have made, spiritually, a leap in quality.

So, however, it was not, and this has brought it about that against modern individualism, anachronistically and egoistically fixed at the grade of (quantitative) representational consciousness, there have begun to raise themselves, in more or less violent form, various anti-modern forces (political and religious) of an anti-individualistic or collectivistic character.

What, in fact, is the ego? We have said it: it is the I in the embryonic or fetal state, and for that very reason an I that should be helped to grow, and not aborted or eliminated.

Why do I say these things? Because I should like it to be clear that the science of the spirit (anthroposophically oriented) is the only way capable of leading us back to spirituality or religiosity, without for this compelling us to renounce modernity (individuality).

Today, in fact, one who seeks the consciousness soul (modernity) finds materialism, and not spirituality or religiosity; and one who seeks spirituality or religiosity finds what survives of the rational or affective soul (if not of the sentient soul), and not the consciousness soul.

One thus ends by convincing oneself that there cannot exist a spirituality or a religiosity that is equal to modernity, inasmuch as it is the fruit of that freedom of thought and of conscience which modernity itself, albeit within determinate limits, had the merit of asserting.

In this light, the passage from the ego to the spiritual Self could also be seen as the passage from "juridical" individualism (the "liberal" one, based upon the various "Bills of Rights") to "ethical" individualism (that of The Philosophy of Freedom, based upon the spirit). (Which implies that in "economic" individualism – the "laissez-faire" one – one could discern a sort of counter-image of "ethical" individualism, the fruit of a displacement of "juridical" individualism of the soul in the material direction of the body, rather than in that of the spirit.)

But let us come to ourselves.


---


217
Steiner writes: "Goethe cannot be thought without Kant, Fichte, Schelling, and Hegel. If these spirits aimed above all to look into the depths and the highest heights, the contemplation of Goethe rested upon the objects of immediate reality. But in this contemplation there was something of that same depth. Goethe exercised this vision in the observation of nature. The spirit of that epoch is poured out like a fluid upon his natural observations; hence their potency, which, even in the observation of particulars, always conserves its grandiose trait. The science of Goethe always aims at the center" (p. 205).

But what is "the spirit of that epoch" if not that of "Goetheanism": that is, a spirit that, in Goethe, for the first time stripped off its philosophical garb, in order to don the scientific-spiritual one, or (ante litteram) the anthroposophic one?

Steiner says that "even in the observation of particulars," this spirit "always conserves its grandiose trait." A trait, therefore, that has, unfortunately, gone disappearing – we may say it – from contemporary science, little by little as the latter has made itself the slave of materialism. If the science of Goethe always aimed at the center, the present one, on the other hand, always moves in the periphery, pretending all the while that what is peripheral and infinitely small should count as central and grandiose.

To make oneself the slave of materialism means, however, to make oneself the slave of Ahriman: precisely of that spirit, that is, which the Russian writer Fyodor Sologub (1863-1927) not without reason called, in the title of his best-known novel (of 1905), The Petty Demon.

Steiner continues: "More than elsewhere we can discern this in his theory of colors, which, together with the essay on the metamorphosis of plants, is the only one that arrived at perfection. And how rigorously concluded according to the demands inherent in the thing itself is the system it presents to us! Let us for once consider this edifice in its inner structure. In order that something founded in the being of nature can arrive at manifestation, an occasional cause is required, an organ in which 'something' can present itself. The eternal brazen laws of nature would reign, it is true, even if they never presented themselves in a human spirit; but their manifestation as such would not be possible. They would exist only according to their essence, not according to their semblance" (pp. 205-206).

There would exist, that is, nature, but not the consciousness of nature; there would be the idea, but not the consciousness of the idea; or there would be the I, but not the consciousness of the I (self-consciousness). Man is, therefore, a creature of the world like the animals, the plants, and the minerals, but a creature that, unlike the others, can allow the world to become conscious of itself.


---


Steiner continues: "The same would happen with the world of light and color if an eye capable of perceiving it did not stand over against it. Color, in its essence, must not be derived from the eye, as Schopenhauer derives it; rather, in the eye there must be shown the possibility that color manifest itself. The eye is not the cause of color, but it is the cause of its manifesting" (p. 206).

Goethe writes, in fact: "The eye owes its existence to light. From indifferent subsidiary animal organs, light calls into life an organ that becomes akin to it; the eye is formed in the light and for the light, so that the inner light may move to meet the outer. And here there comes back to us the ancient Ionian school, which never tired of repeating with great wisdom that like is known only by like, the akin by the akin; and the words of the ancient mystic (Plotinus, 205-270 – ed.) which we should like to translate thus: Could we look upon the light, / If the eye were not of the sun? / Could we be intoxicated with God / If His force were not in us?" (The Theory of Colors, in Works – Sansoni, Florence 1961, vol. V, pp. 298-299).
                                                  218
Steiner proceeds: "From here the theory of colors must take its start. It must investigate the eye, lay bare its nature. Therefore Goethe begins with the physiological theory of color (…) What is produced when light and darkness act upon the eye? What happens when delimited images enter into relation with it? He does not ask, at first, what processes unfold in the eye when this or that perception is produced, but seeks to establish what can be produced by means of the eye in the living act of seeing (…) Goethe wishes to take the eye into consideration only inasmuch as it sees, and not to explain seeing starting from the observations that can be made upon the dead eye (…) From here he then passes to the objective processes that produce the phenomena of color. And here it is important to keep in mind that, when he speaks of these objective processes, Goethe does not at all mean the hypothetical material or movement processes that are no longer perceivable; he absolutely wishes to remain in the field of the perceivable world. His physical theory of colors, which forms the second part of the work, seeks the conditions that are independent of the eye but connected with the arising of colors (…) Only in another separate chapter, that of the chemical theory of colors, does he pass to those attached to, fixed in, the bodies. If in the physiological theory of colors he answers the question: How, in general, can colors appear?, if in the physical one he answers the question: How do colors arise under given external conditions?, here he answers the problem: How is it that the world of bodies appears endowed with color? (…) But here he does not halt; rather, at the end, he considers the higher relation that the colored world of bodies has with the soul, in the chapter: Sensuous-Moral Effects of Color" (pp. 206-207).


---


We have finished the fourth section. Before passing to the fifth, I should, however, like to say something about the relation that "the colored world of bodies has with the soul."

You see, psychology takes color away from the world and confines it, as subjective quality, within man; whereas physics takes color away from man and confines it, as objective quantity, within the world. "A beam of white light – Boncinelli writes precisely – contains within itself an infinity of luminous rays of different wavelength, as one can easily observe by making it pass, for example, through a glass prism. But it neither contains nor transports 'colors.' It is our eye, connected with our brain, that identifies, discerns, and distinguishes in it the various colors. The world of itself is populated neither with sensations nor with stimuli" (The Brain, the Mind, and the Soul – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 118).

In studying color, psychology believes, therefore, that it can know something of man, but not of the world; whereas physics believes it can know something of the world, but not of man.

Here is wherein Goethean science differs! In it, one knows man by studying the colors, and one knows the colors by studying man. Not – note well – because one sets out with the intention of wishing to know man through the colors and the colors through man, but because it is precisely by studying the colors as "colors" and man as "man" that one arrives at such a result. At the very moment in which one truly grasps the reality of the colors, one grasps, in fact, that of man; and at the very moment in which one truly grasps the reality of man, one grasps that of the colors.

And what is the reality of the colors? That of the feelings, seen from without. And what is the reality of the feelings? That of the colors, seen from within. And what is the reality of that interaction between light and darkness from which, according to Goethe, color is born? That of the interaction between thinking and willing from which, according to the science of the spirit, feeling is born.

This said, let us pass to the fifth section, entitled: Goethe's Concept of Space.


---


Steiner writes: "Since a full comprehension of Goethe's works on physics is possible only on the basis of a conception of space identical with his, let us seek to illustrate it. But first it is necessary that, from our preceding considerations, we have acquired the following convictions: 1st) the objects that in experience appear to us in isolation have between them a reciprocal interior relation; in reality
                                                   219
they are held together by a unitary cosmic bond; there lives in them one principle, common to all; 2nd) when our spirit draws near to the isolated objects, seeking to embrace them by means of a spiritual nexus, the conceptual unity it establishes is not extrinsic to the objects, but drawn from the intimate being of nature itself. Human knowledge is not a process that unfolds outside of the things, springing from a mere subjective arbitrariness; no, what presents itself to our spirit as law of nature, and extrinsicates itself in our soul, is the pulsation of the very heart of the universe" (pp. 207-208).

On the basis of these convictions, let us, therefore, confront the question of space, keeping well in mind, however, what we have already said in this regard.

Let us merely presuppose – Steiner writes – "two factors of which each is capable of producing an impression upon our senses (…) Moreover we wish to suppose that the existence of one of these factors does not exclude that of the other. A single organ of perception can perceive them both. If, instead, one were to assume that the existence of one of the two elements was in some way dependent upon that of the other, the problem would be quite different. If the existence of B were such as to exclude the existence of A, while being dependent upon it according to its own being, then A and B would stand to one another in a relation of time. For the dependence of B upon A requires, if at the same time one supposes that the existence of B excludes that of A, that the latter precede the former" (p. 208).

In order to posit, between A and B, a purely spatial relation, one must, therefore, suppose them simply coexistent, juxtaposed, and therefore reciprocally exterior: one must, that is, prescind toto coelo from their qualities.

"Space – Hegel says – is pure quantity" (Encyclopedia of the Philosophical Sciences – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989, p. 230). And what is quantity? We have said it: a quality deprived of qualities.

Steiner observes, in fact: "For no one can there subsist a doubt about the kind of relation that I establish between things when I do not enter into the merits of their particular constitution, of their essence.
