One who asks himself what transition can be found from one thing to the other, when the thing itself remains indifferent, must without doubt give himself for answer: space. Any other relation must found itself upon the qualitative constitution of what appears in the world in isolation. Space alone takes into consideration nothing other than the fact that the things are precisely separate from one another" (p. 209).


---


Scaligero says: "Love is the being of the spirit"; and he explains: "In giving itself, the I actualizes its infinity: it fills with its movement the space that separates it from the other, and by which the other is other" (Of Immortal Love – Tilopa, Rome 1982, pp. 11 and 16).

The spirit, or the I, in actualizing "its infinity," tends, therefore, to heal the pain of separation by placing and embracing every thing (every "other") in the unifying dimension of space. Thanks to this idea, it succeeds, in fact, in making the manifold one.

Steiner writes precisely: "What our spirit wants, when it draws near to experience, is to surpass the separation, to show that in the single thing there is to be seen the force of the whole. In spatial perception it wishes to surpass nothing other than separation as such; that is, to establish the most general relation of all. That A and B are not each a world unto itself, but belong to a common aggregate – this is what spatial observation says. This is the sense of being near in space. If every thing were a being unto itself, this would not be possible; one could not establish, in general, any reference of the beings to one another" (p. 209).

But why does the I feel separation painfully? It is soon said: because separation from the world separates it, in reality, from itself, and is, therefore, lived as a soulic laceration or an
                                                  220
"ontological fracture" (the expression is that of the well-known English psychiatrist and psychoanalyst Ronald Laing [1927-1989], author of The Divided Self – Einaudi, Turin 1969).

A and B are, therefore, not each a world unto itself, but belong to a common aggregate, inasmuch as the I finds the one and the other within itself, and can therefore reunite them in its interior "space."

Steiner continues: "I think A alongside B. The same I can do also with two other elements of the sensible world: C and D. Now I wish to prescind totally from the elements A, B, C, and D, and to relate to one another, in their turn, only the two concrete relations. It is clear that I can relate these two to one another as well, as separate entities, precisely as I did with A and B. Here I relate concrete relations which I can call a and b. If I now proceed a further step, I can, in its turn, relate a to b. But in that case I have already lost every separation (…) Further I cannot go. I have reached what I had previously sought: space itself stands before my soul" (pp. 209-210).


---


Let us imagine, therefore, that an apple tree (A) stands near a pear tree (B), and that a peach tree (C) stands far from a chestnut tree (D). What would it mean, in this case, "to prescind totally from the elements A, B, C, and D, and to relate to one another, in their turn, only the two concrete relations"? It would mean to prescind totally from the pear tree, the apple tree, the peach tree, and the chestnut tree, and to relate the concrete relation "near" (a) to the concrete relation "far" (b). But do these two relations perhaps have something in common? Certainly: they are, in fact, two determinate relations that have precisely in common that quid which, in the one case, determines itself as "near" and, in the other, as "far"; or, in other words, that quid which, in the one case, assumes the form a and, in the other, the form b.

Well, what else could that quid be, if not the pure ideal "substance" of space? Now, Steiner says rightly, "space itself stands before my soul."

But this is not all.

He writes, in fact: "In this lies the secret of the three dimensions (…) The three dimensions have the following part in it: The first dimension establishes a relation between two sensible perceptions; it is, therefore, a concrete representation. The second dimension relates to one another two concrete representations, and thereby passes into the field of abstraction. Finally the third dimension establishes nothing other than the ideal unity between the abstractions" (pp. 210 and 211).

One could also add, if one wished, that the first (three-dimensional) dimension is that of representational consciousness (bound to the physical body); that the second (two-dimensional) is that of imaginative consciousness (bound to the etheric body); and that the third (one-dimensional) is that of inspirative consciousness (bound to the astral body). The object of the first is, in fact, the relation between things; the object of the second is, on the other hand, the relation between relations; and the object of the third is, finally, the essence of relation (relation in itself) as concept: that is, precisely, as "space." (The I, as subject-object of intuitive consciousness, is "dimensionless.")


---


Steiner concludes: "Thus far we have spoken of space as a rapport, a relation. One can now ask: Does only this relation of nearness exist in general? Or does there exist for every object an absolute determination of place? The latter case is, naturally, not touched by our preceding explanations. But let us now seek whether such a relation of place exists or not, a well-determined 'here.' What do I indicate, in reality, when I speak of a 'here'? Nothing other than an object, in the immediate vicinity of which the object in question is situated. 'Here' means: in the vicinity of an object indicated by me. But with this the absolute indication of place is traced back to a relation of space, and the inquiry adumbrated falls of itself. Let us raise once more, in a well-determined way, the question: What is, according to the preceding investigations, space? We shall have to answer: Nothing other than a necessity, inherent in the things, of surpassing their separation in an altogether exterior manner, without entering into the merits of their essence, and of reuniting them in a unity, in the exterior manner already said. Space is, therefore, a way of grasping the world as a unity. Space is an idea" (pp. 211-212).

We have run a little late, but in compensation we have finished the fifth section as well; next time we shall occupy ourselves with the sixth.

L.R.
Rome, 9 October 2001

222
The Scientific Works of Goethe (46)

If all goes well, we shall finish the fifteenth chapter tonight; we shall, in fact, occupy ourselves with its last section: Goethe, Newton, and the Physicists, which will give us occasion to reflect once more upon some of the subjects treated up to now.


---


Steiner writes: "When Goethe drew near to the study of the essence of colors, he was moved, in substance, by an artistic interest (…) In the History of the Theory of Colors he himself gave an ample account of the whole historical side of his study. Here we wish to treat only of the psychological and practical side. Immediately after his return from Italy, Goethe began his studies, which became particularly intense in the years 1790 and '91, and then continued uninterrupted until the poet's death. Let us picture to ourselves the situation of the Goethean conception of the world at the moment in which Goethe began the studies upon colors. He had already conceived his grandiose idea of the metamorphosis of organic beings; and, thanks to his discovery of the intermaxillary bone, there had arisen in him the vision of the unity of the whole of natural existence. The single particular appeared to him as a special modification of the ideal principle that reigns in the totality of nature. He had already asserted, in his letters from Italy, that a plant is a plant only by the fact of carrying within itself the idea of the plant. This idea was for him something concrete, like a unity full of spiritual content present in every single plant. It was not perceivable with the eyes of the body, but with the eye of the spirit. One who is capable of discerning it sees it in every plant. With this, the whole kingdom of plants and, by extension of this view, the entire kingdom of nature, appears as a unity graspable with the spirit. But no one can, starting from the mere idea, construct the whole multiplicity that presents itself to the outer senses. The idea can be recognized by the intuitive spirit; the single configurations are accessible to it only when it turns the senses to the outer world, when it looks and observes. Why a modification of the idea appears as sensible reality precisely thus and not otherwise cannot be excogitated by the brain, but must be sought in the kingdom of reality" (pp. 212-213).


---


One thing, therefore, is to seek to induce the idea starting from the things; another, to seek to deduce the things starting from the idea.

We have, in fact, seen that the (cosmic) creative activity descends from the idea to the things, whereas the (human) cognitive activity re-ascends from the things to the idea. The seeking to deduce the things starting from the idea configures itself, therefore, as a (philosophical) hybris: that is to say, as a presumptuous (Luciferic) attempt to make human knowledge travel (instead of re-travel in reverse) the same road traveled by the divine creation.

Science would wish to oppose – it is true – this attempt, seeking precisely to induce the idea starting (as empiricism wills) from the things; but it arrives only at making itself an idea about the things, and not at grasping the idea in the things. And why? Because the weight of materialism (the tamas of the Orientals) crushes it down upon the things, granting it to take wing only in order to conjecture abstractly and sterilely upon them.

The Luciferic forces tend, therefore, to exalt one-sidedly the luminous power of the concept, whereas the Ahrimanic ones tend to exalt one-sidedly the dark power of the percept. One who is dazzled by the former cannot, therefore, succeed in seeing the idea in the things (since he knows how to see it, so to speak, only in the air); and one who is benighted by the latter cannot succeed in seeing the things in the idea (since he knows how to see them, so to speak, only on the ground).

I beg you, in any case, to note that, though we have not yet resumed treating of Goethe's theory of colors, we have already found ourselves speaking of light and of darkness.

223
Steiner writes: "Such is the peculiar Goethean way of seeing, which can most appropriately be called empirical idealism, and summarized in the words: at the basis of the objects of a sensible multiplicity, inasmuch as they are congeners, there stands a spiritual unity that produces that similarity and that appurtenance. Starting from this point, there arose for Goethe the question: What spiritual unity lies at the basis of the multiplicity of the perceptions of colors? What do I perceive in every modification of color? It soon became clear to him that the necessary basis of every color is light. There is no color without light. There then remained for him to seek that element of reality that modifies and specializes light; and he discovered that it was light-deprived matter, active darkness, in short, that which is opposite to light. Thus every color became for him light modified by darkness" (p. 213).

Here precisely, once again, are light and darkness. The fact is that from the encounter of the former with the latter there are born not only (we have said it) the colors and the feelings, but also the representations: according to what The Philosophy of Freedom teaches, these originate, in fact, from the encounter of the concept (of light) with the percept (with darkness). It will be well to keep this in mind, if one wishes to understand the way in which Goethe conceives light.


---


Steiner writes precisely: "It is altogether erroneous to believe that Goethe meant by light that concrete solar light which is generally called 'white light' (…) Light, as Goethe understands it, in opposing darkness to it as its opposite, is a purely spiritual entity; it is simply the element common to all sensations of color" (pp. 213-214).

Light is, therefore, in itself an extrasensible reality. With the eyes, we do not, in fact, see light; rather, we see (the things) thanks to light: that is, we never see the illuminating, but always and only the illuminated.

Steiner continues: "Although Goethe never expressed this clearly, yet his whole theory of colors is so constructed that nothing else is legitimate to derive from it (…) What we obtain for the theory of colors with the help of sunlight is only an approach to reality (an approximation – ed.). Goethe's theory is not to be understood as if in every color he saw light and darkness really contained. The reality that presents itself to our eyes is only a given nuance of color. Only the spirit is capable of resolving this sensible fact into two spiritual entities: light and not-light. The exterior facts by which this happens, the material processes in matter, are not in the least touched by it. This is quite another thing. One does not at all wish to deny that, while the 'red' appears before us, a wave process takes place in the ether. But what in reality produces a perception has nothing to do, as we have already shown, with the essence of the content" (p. 214).

Steiner says that "the reality that presents itself to our eyes is only a given nuance of color," and that "only the spirit is capable of resolving this sensible fact into two spiritual entities: light and not-light." The spirit does, therefore, with "a given nuance of color" what it does, for example, with water. If it had not resolved or analyzed it, would it ever have discovered that it is composed, in a determinate proportion, of nothing less than two gases (hydrogen and oxygen)?

"What in reality produces a perception – he says again – has nothing to do (…) with the essence of the content." As we saw in due course, one thing is, in fact, the essence of the percept (of the content of the perception); another, the nature of the media that convey it, or through which it arrives at consciousness.


---


Steiner exemplifies precisely: "If someone, from place A, sends me a telegram while I am residing in B, that which arrives in my hands is originated wholly in B. In B is the telegraphist,
                                                   224
who writes upon a sheet of paper and with an ink that have never been in A; he himself does not know A, etc.; in short, one can demonstrate that in the telegram that stands before me nothing derives from A. And yet all that derives from B is a matter of complete indifference for the content, for the essence of the telegram; what matters to me is only transmitted from B. If I wish to explain the essence of the content of the telegram, I must totally prescind from what derives from B" (pp. 214-215).

Let us speak plainly. Everyone would understand and accept these things, if for the encounter with the living reality of the spirit the intellect alone (or, as one says today, the mere "mind") sufficed.

Let us think, for example, of Paul. After the encounter with Christ (on the road to Damascus) he was no longer Saul, but became precisely Paul: that is, an "other." As we have already had occasion to stress (citing Eccles as well), the true obstacle to comprehension is constituted, therefore, by the (Ahrimanic) fear of dying to the ego (to the "old Adam"): to that sole subject, that is, or that sole form of manifestation of the I, that we are ordinarily given to know. ("Truth – Goethe observes in this regard – contradicts our nature, error does not, and this for a very simple reason: truth demands that we recognize our limitedness, error deludes us that we have capacities in one way or another unlimited" – Maxims and Reflections, TEA, Rome 1988, p. 86).

Boncinelli maintains, for example, that "in nature the scent of violets does not exist, just as there does not exist a chord in C or straw yellow," since "each of these is a segment of reality cut out by one of our senses and elevated by them to the rank of sensation" (The Brain, the Mind, and the Soul – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 118).

But if perchance their respective essences existed, could there then not exist that of him who smells, hears, and sees them? Could there exist, that is, the essences of the scents, the sounds, or the colors, without that of the subject who experiences and knows them? No, they could not exist; and it is precisely for this that one then decides, (unconsciously) putting one's hands forward ("prevention is better than cure!"), to declare them nonexistent.

But let us return to ourselves.


---


Steiner writes: "The spatial-temporal material processes can be of the greatest importance for the coming-into-being of the perceptions; but they have nothing to do with the essence of the latter (…) If one investigates what happens in the spatial extension while the entities in question (light, warmth, electricity, etc. – ed.) are transmitted, one must arrive at a unitary movement. For a medium in which only movement is possible must react to everything with movement; and it will accomplish by means of movements all the transmissions to which it is called. If I then investigate the forms of that movement, I do not apprehend what the transmitted thing is, but rather in what way it is transmitted to me. It is simply absurd to say that warmth or light are movement. Movement is only the reaction of the matter susceptible to movement" (p. 215).


---


It is, in truth, "absurd to say that warmth or light are movement." But why, then, does one say it? For the reason of always: because the transmitted content is not perceivable by the (physical) senses, whereas the movement of the medium that transmits it is. Of that content one could at least hypothesize the existence, but the materialistic prejudices keep watch to prevent even this.

"The processes of the occult world – Steiner notes in this regard – reveal themselves with their effects in the manifest one. When one recognizes that the results of supersensible investigation render the visible processes comprehensible, in this confirmation that life furnishes one has the demonstration that it is legitimate to require for such things. One who does not wish to make use of the means we shall indicate later on in order to arrive at supersensible observation can make the following experience. He can begin by accepting the data of supersensible knowledge and then applying them to the things manifest in the field of his experience. He will then find that life becomes, by this means, clear and comprehensible; and the more exactly and the more deeply he observes ordinary life, the more will he convince himself of it" (Occult Science in its General Lines – Antroposofica, Milan 1969, p. 69).
                                                 225
In order to make this experience, however, open-mindedness and love for reality would be required: there would be required, that is, a true, serious, and deep interest in loosening (scientifically) the enigmas of life. ("Only one who finds in anthroposophy what he must seek by reason of an inner exigency can recognize anthroposophy – Steiner writes, in fact. There can, therefore, be anthroposophists only those men who feel certain problems concerning the being of man and of the world as a vital necessity, as one feels hunger and thirst" – Anthroposophical Maxims – Antroposofica, Milan 1969, p. 15).

I cited just now the example of the telegram to illustrate the fact that these truths would be within the reach of everyone, if for the encounter with the living reality of the spirit the intellect alone sufficed.

Well, I should like to propose to you another, no less efficacious, just as Enzo Erra reports it: "A book is what it is independently of him who chooses it on a shelf and leafs through its pages; and if it is a treatise on geometry, no reader has the power to transform it into a collection of lyric poems. And yet, the content of a book, though inseparably connected with the printed characters that objectively enclose and configure it, does not dissolve, does not free itself, does not reveal itself in its physiognomy – the weave of a tale, a series of concepts, an interlacing of feelings – except in the inwardness of the reader. And though a book is indisputably an object, it does not become what it intrinsically is – novel, biography, historical essay, or philosophical system – until a hand does not open it, an eye does not run through it, a thought does not think it: until the intervention of a volitional, subjective activity does not call it to manifest itself, does not awaken it to life" (A Hundred Years After, in The Goethean Conception of the World – Tilopa, Rome 1991, p. 160).

What else is there to do, therefore, but recall – as the adage has it – that "there is no one deafer than he who will not hear"?


---


Steiner concludes: "It suffices to liberate ourselves from the idea that for Goethe light and darkness are real entities, and to regard them as simple principles, as spiritual entities; then one will acquire an opinion quite different from the usual one, upon his theory of colors. When, like Newton, one understands light as a simple mixture of all the colors, every concept of the concrete being 'light' vanishes; it totally volatilizes into an empty generic representation to which in reality nothing corresponds. Such abstractions were foreign to the Goethean conception of the world. For Goethe, every representation had to have a concrete content; only, for him the 'concrete' did not identify itself with the 'physical.' Modern physics truly has no concept of 'light'; it knows only specified lights, colors that, in determinate combinations, arouse the impression of 'white.' But this 'white,' too, must not be identified with 'light' in itself. White, too, is, at bottom, nothing other than a combined color. 'Light,' in the Goethean sense, is not known to modern physics; and neither is 'darkness.' So that Goethe's theory of colors moves in a sphere that does not so much as touch the conceptual determinations of the physicists. Physics simply ignores all the fundamental concepts of the Goethean theory of colors; so that, from its point of view, it cannot judge it at all. Goethe begins where physics ends" (pp. 215-216).

With regard to "principles," I should like to recall, before finishing, the Freudian ones of Eros and Thanatos. That the materialist neurologists and psychiatrists make fun of them does not surprise at all; but that they have not heretofore been taken entirely seriously by the Freudian psychoanalysts themselves can, on the other hand, disconcert. What would have happened, in fact, if they had taken them seriously? That they would sooner or later have discovered – as the science of the spirit teaches – that "light" (abstract thinking) reveals itself connected, in the garb of Thanatos, to the catabolic processes of the neurosensory system, and that "darkness" (instinctive willing) reveals itself, on the other hand, connected, in the garb of Eros, to the anabolic processes of the metabolic system and of the limbs.

In the lower pole – Victor Bott explains, in fact – "we find an intense vitality and, as a consequence, a corresponding activity of the etheric body. The constant regeneration of the cells of the intestine, the cellular multiplication in the organs of reproduction, are etheric processes par excellence, are
                                                  226
vital manifestations. On the contrary, at the neurosensory pole processes of death prevail. This state of things reaches its culminating point at the level of the nerve cell, which is incapable of regeneration. One has the impression that little suffices for it to die totally" (Anthroposophical Medicine – IPSA, Palermo 1991, vol. I, p. 20).

L.R.
Rome, 16 October 2001

227
The Scientific Works of Goethe (47)


---


Tonight we shall take up the sixteenth chapter, entitled: Goethe against Atomism. It is a rather long chapter, divided into nine sections.

First, I should, however, like to show you this book: it is a conversation between the well-known and heterodox Jungian psychoanalyst James Hillman and Silvia Ronchey. It has come out in these days, and I wished to purchase it – as is easy to imagine – above all by reason of its title: The Pleasure of Thinking (Rizzoli, Milan 2001).

Not that I entertained – let it be clear – any illusions; but I was, in any case, curious to know what the nature of the pleasure that accompanies – according to Hillman – thinking might be.

It took little to discover it. Listen, in fact, to how he answers a question of Ronchey's: "Yes, there is an entire theory of education in my words. The pleasures of thinking, the passion of ideas, the eroticism of the mind. Perhaps, in those days, Sartre and Simone de Beauvoir were, in our eyes, the images of it (…) It is the demon Eros who sets fire to the mind and draws two bodies toward each other like a magnet. Bodies that are at times ill-matched. Then when it is over, when the catastrophe comes, those two blame each other. 'Seduction.' Do you see? But the only one to blame is Eros, who takes great, great pleasure in thinking!" (pp. 32-33).

A little later, however, he thinks better of it and, to another question of his interlocutor, answers: "Perhaps I am giving you a one-sided vision of thinking, as if it were all passion and pleasure and something very physical. Thinking is also work: hard work. And also a sort of devotion or rigor – which is in itself also a pleasure, by the way" (p. 36).

Whereupon Ronchey asks him: "When does thinking become work? How do you identify pleasure and rigor?"; and Hillman answers: "The pleasure of rigor. Ah! I like that. A sort of anal pleasure, at once sadistic and masochistic. Seeking very, very hard to make one's way in thinking as far as clarity. Correcting oneself. Seeking a reference one cannot remember where one read. Checking, checking, checking. Reading truly terribly difficult books, like Kant's Pure Reason" (pp. 36-37).

Is there any need of comment? I think not; forgive me, rather, for having proposed to you an example of this kind; but it is, unfortunately, necessary, from time to time, to call attention to the "quality" of the so-called "culture" that forms the background of our work today.

But now let us begin the first section of the new chapter.


---


Steiner opens it by citing some passages from a lecture given in Lübeck, on 20 September 1895, by the German physicist, chemist, and philosopher (Nobel laureate in chemistry in 1909) Wilhelm Ostwald (1853-1932); and he then writes: "In my dissertations upon the Goethean theory of colors I fought the same fight against the fundamental scientific representations of our time as Prof. Ostwald in his lecture on the Overcoming of Scientific Materialism. But what I substitute for those representations does not at all accord with the ideas of Ostwald (…) Now I should like to discuss more deeply the modern conception of nature, seeking to recognize, from the aim it sets before itself, whether it is sound or not" (pp. 218-219).

"If we reflect – Ostwald says, for example – that all one knows of a given matter is the knowledge of its properties, we see how the assertion that there exists, it is true, still a determinate matter, which, however, no longer possesses any of its properties, is truly not far from being a veritable absurdity. Effectively, this merely formal assertion serves solely to conjoin the general facts of the chemical processes, and especially of the stoichiometric laws of measure, with the arbitrary concept of a matter unchangeable in itself."


---


228
We said, in fact, some time ago, that matter is true, but materialism is false; and that it is false precisely because "it is truly not far from being a veritable absurdity" (or an arbitrary metaphysical pretension) to assume an "immutable" matter as foundation. As Ostwald says, "all one knows of a given matter" is not only, in fact, "knowledge of its properties," and therefore of a matter determined qualitatively as substance, but is also knowledge of a matter determined morphologically as body.

To place at the basis of "scientific materialism" (so Ostwald calls it) a matter that has neither substance nor body is, therefore – as Steiner says – "unhealthy" (on the first page of the section, he writes, in fact: "As for the fundamental ideas with which modern natural observation seeks to comprehend the world of experience, I regard them as unhealthy and insufficient before an energetic thinking" – p. 217).

The fact is that we find ourselves once again grappling with a projection. Let us reflect: is it true, or is it not true, that everything we perceive with the senses has a foundation? It is true. And is it true, or is it not true, that the foundation of everything we perceive with the senses is not perceivable by the senses? It is true. What, then, is the problem? It is that this foundation is not recognized, as would be dutiful, as spiritual in nature, but is rather projected beyond the perceived world, into a phantasmal universe that should be, at once, material and immaterial. (Under the entry "matter," in the most recent Garzantine dedicated to the Sciences, one reads: if one considers its "elementary constituents (molecules, atoms, particles)," matter "then turns out to have a single origin, but the properties usually attributed to it (impenetrability, divisibility, compressibility, etc.) can no longer be considered valid, so that one limits oneself to studying only the experimental data without concerning oneself to give a universally valid definition of matter" – note.)

We have just seen that Steiner judges "unhealthy and insufficient" the "fundamental ideas with which modern natural observation seeks to comprehend the world of experience." Why "unhealthy"? Because there exist, it is true, diseases – as psychosomatic medicine teaches – whose causes lie in the psyche and whose effects manifest themselves in the body; but there exist also others – as, for example, those "asymptomatic" or "existential" neuroses, defined by Viktor Frankl "noogenic," could teach – whose causes lie in the spirit and whose effects manifest themselves in the soul.

In order to cure them, one would need, therefore, to take into consideration not only the body and the psyche, as nearly all contemporary psychology does, but also the spirit: not, however, in an abstract, sentimental, or fideistic way, but in a "concrete" way, and for that very reason scientific-spiritual.

One is accustomed to say (especially in times of "cultural relativism") that "one idea is as good as another"; but does it perhaps result to you that one food is as good as another? That a poisonous mushroom, say, is worth as much as an edible one? Certainly not! And for what reason, then, does what holds for foods not hold for ideas? It is soon said: because we take in the former in a concrete way, whereas we take in the latter in an abstract way: that is, because ideas penetrate only into the head, whereas foods penetrate into the whole organism.

So long as we have ideas only in the head, it is, in effect, not easy to distinguish the true ones from the false ones or, better said, the healthy ones from the unhealthy ones; by dint of staying in the head, however, they can arrive, little by little, at taking body in the feelings and in the will, thereby revealing their salutary or pathogenic nature. In fact, all those ideas are salutary that help man to become ever more man (to become what he is); whereas all those are pathogenic that lead him away or turn him aside from that goal (perhaps to divert him, more or less explicitly, toward that of animality or, in a broad sense, of robotics). Steiner observes in this regard: "The spiritual conception of the world, the sum of spiritual truths we have drawn from the heights of the cosmos, will flow into the human souls; thus, for the humanity of the future, it will itself become a curative remedy nourished from the innermost depth of man. In time to come, the science of the spirit will become ever more a medicament for the souls" (The Gospel of Luke – Antroposofica, Milan 1996, p. 143).

But let us return to ourselves.


---


229
After reporting a passage in which Descartes distinguishes what – according to him – can be known clearly and distinctly, such as magnitude or extension (in the sense of length, breadth, depth), from what can, on the other hand, be known only obscurely and confusedly, such as the sensible qualities (colors, odors, sounds, flavors, etc.), Steiner concludes thus: "Thinking in the sense of this Cartesian proposition has become a habit for the followers of the modern conception of nature, to such a degree that they deem no other manner of thinking worthy of consideration. They say: What is perceived as light is effected by a process of motion expressible with a mathematical formula. When in the perceivable world a color appears, they trace it back to a wave motion and calculate the number of its vibrations in a given period of time. They believe that the whole sensible world will remain explained as soon as one has succeeded in tracing all the perceptions back to relations expressible in such mathematical formulas (…) To reduce the world to an arithmetical operation is the ideal of the modern conception of nature (…) Since, without the existence of forces, the parts of the supposed matter could never be set in motion, the modern scientists also take in energy among the elements with which they explain the world (…) With the introduction of the concept of energy, mathematics passes over into mechanics (…) Du Bois-Reymond (Paul, 1831-1889 – ed.) writes: 'It is a tested psychological fact that where such a resolution (of the natural processes into atomic mechanics) succeeds, our need for causality feels itself provisionally satisfied.' For Du Bois-Reymond this may be a fact of experience. But one must also say that there exist other men who, from an explanation of the corporeal world such as Du Bois-Reymond holds out to them, feel themselves anything but satisfied. Among the number of these other men is Goethe; and those whose need for causality is paid when they succeed in tracing the natural processes back to a mechanics of atoms do not possess the organ for comprehending Goethe" (pp. 219-220-221).

Which stands to signify that these people do not possess the organ for comprehending Steiner and anthroposophy either.

Steiner himself asserts, in fact: "When one points out today (we are in 1918 – ed.) that the world has missed the opportunity to occupy itself with Goethe – and it has, in truth, missed it – when one notes that the world has not succeeded in reaching any relation with Goetheanism, one does not wish to criticize or blame the world, but one seeks to invite it to find the right relation with Goetheanism. To continue Goetheanism means, however, to enter into the science of the spirit, anthroposophically oriented; without it the world will not emerge from the catastrophic situation of today. In a certain sense, the best way to approach the science of the spirit is precisely that of beginning from Goethe" (The Study of Historical Symptoms – Antroposofica, Milan 1961, p. 162).

And let us now pass to the second section.


---


Steiner writes: "Magnitude, figure, position, motion, force, etc. are perceptions, precisely in the identical sense as light, colors, sounds, odors, flavors, the sensations of warm, of cold, etc. One who separates the magnitude of an object from its other qualities and considers it per se has no longer to do with a real object, but only with an abstraction of the intellect. The greatest conceivable nonsense is that of attributing to an abstraction isolated from sensible perception a degree of reality other than that of an object of sensible perception itself. The relations of space and of number have no other advantage over the other sensible perceptions than that of being simpler and more easily embraceable at a glance. Upon these two qualities rests the security of the mathematical sciences. When the modern conception of nature traces all the processes of the corporeal world back to elements expressible mathematically, this is owed to the fact that for our thinking mathematics and mechanics are easy and convenient to use. And human thinking loves convenience" (p. 221).

230
What, really, is an "object"? An aggregate of qualities. And how, then, to pretend to know it, by extracting and isolating from the aggregate some qualities and assigning to them a value different from that of the others that constitute it?

And yet one does it. And why? Well, let us imagine we have before us some object. It gives itself to us, offers itself or donates itself to us, without hiding anything from us. But what do we do? Since it gives itself to us as an "aggregate of qualities," we choose those most akin to the quality of our intellect and declare them the cause of the others. And which are the qualities most akin to the intellect? It is obvious: those that best lend themselves to calculation. "Not without reason – Hegel observes, in fact – was" intellectual thinking "equated to calculating, and conversely calculating to this thinking" (Science of Logic – Laterza, Rome-Bari, 1974, p. 34).

Is it not, therefore, more "easy and convenient" (as Steiner says) to have an adaequatio rei ad intellectum (an adequation of the thing to thinking or to knowing) than an adaequatio intellectus ad rem (an adequation of thinking or of knowing to the thing)? (See: The Scientific Works of Goethe (7) – ed.)

But it is more "easy and convenient" – Freud would probably say – inasmuch as it is an expression of the "narcissistic" libido, and not of the "objectual" one: inasmuch as it is, in other words, the fruit of egoism, and not of love.

Steiner writes: Ostwald, for example, "isolates energy from the field of perception, that is, abstracts from everything that is not energy. He traces everything perceivable back to a single quality of it, to the energetic manifestation, therefore to an abstract concept (…) In substance, that Du Bois-Reymond resolves the natural processes into a mechanics of atoms, or that Ostwald dissolves them into manifestations of energy, is the same. The two solutions both spring from the tendency of human thinking toward convenience" (p. 222).


---


There is, therefore, one who isolates matter (extension), one who isolates movement (energy), and one who (today) isolates information; and there is even one who isolates and sums up – as we saw Boncinelli do – all three factors. But where, and what, is the object? Where and what is, that is, that aggregate of which extension, movement, and information are but single aspects? (Goethe observes: when they discourse upon the products of nature, the French make use of the word composition. "Now, I can put together the single parts of a machine broken to pieces, and speak, in that case, of composition; but not when I intend the single parts of an organic whole; which form themselves by virtue of a vital process, and are pervaded by a common soul" – J. P. Eckermann: Conversations with Goethe – Laterza, Bari 1912, vol. II, p. 338).

Boncinelli arrives, for example, at asserting: "The hardness, the malleability, or the conductivity of a metal are properties of the metal itself, not of the atoms and the molecules that compose it" (The Brain, the Mind, and the Soul – Mondadori, Milan 2000, p. 21).

Very good; but who, then, is it that has such properties? That is, where, and what, is the "metal itself"? We know that the "metal itself," that is to say the essence of the metal, lies beyond the threshold that divides the existential reality of space and time from the essential reality of the qualities (and of the I). To re-ascend from the consciousness of space (representational) to that of time (imaginative), and then, crossing that threshold, to re-ascend from the consciousness of time to that of quality (inspirative) is, however, neither easy nor convenient; and precisely for this one prefers – according to what we have said and repeated – to reduce quality and time to space, that is, to quantity.

In this regard, Ostwald, in a passage reported by Steiner, asserts: "However useful and necessary for the comprehension of nature, is energy also sufficient for this purpose (that is, for the explanation of the corporeal world)? (…) To this question one must answer with a no. However superior the merits of the energetic conception of the world may be to those of the mechanical or materialistic conception, there can, so it seems to me, already now be indicated some points that are not covered
                                                  231
by the cornerstones of energetics already known; and that therefore allude to the existence of principles that transcend it. But energetics will continue to subsist alongside these new principles. Only, in time to come, it will no longer be, as it is today for us, the most all-embracing law of all for embracing and dominating the natural phenomena; it will appear, rather, probably as a special case of relations still more general, of whose form we most certainly have not, for the moment, the palest idea" (pp. 222-223).

And even today, in effect, science has not "the palest idea" of the idea.

L.R.
Rome, 23 October 2001

232
The Scientific Works of Goethe (48)

Last time we finished the second section of the sixteenth chapter as well; tonight we shall therefore begin the third.


---


Steiner writes: "Just as one cannot 'dissolve' the processes of the corporeal world into a mechanics of atoms, equally little can one dissolve them into relations of energy. In so proceeding one does nothing other than divert attention from the content of the real world of the senses in order to turn it toward an unreal abstraction, whose miserable fund of quality is, moreover, likewise drawn from the sensible world. One cannot explain one of the groups of qualities of the world of the senses – light, colors, sounds, odors, flavors, conditions of warmth, etc. – by 'dissolving' it in the other group of qualities of the same sensible world: magnitude, form, position, number, energy, etc. Not this 'dissolving' of one genus of qualities in the other can be the task of natural science, but rather the inquiry into relations and rapports among the various perceivable qualities of the sensible world" (p. 223).


---


We said, the other time, that, in order to re-ascend from the consciousness of space and from that of time to the consciousness of quality, one must cross the threshold that separates the sphere of existing from that of being. This must not, however, make us forget that the first and most important step consists in re-ascending from the (dead) consciousness of space to the (living) one of time.

How many would wish to carry themselves – like Ostwald – beyond matter in the name of energy have, as a rule, no consciousness of what it is: they have, that is, no consciousness of carrying themselves from the static sphere of space to the dynamic one of time, or from the region in which things lie to that in which, on the contrary, the forces act.

They are convinced of thus surpassing materialism, but in reality they do not surpass it; and they do not surpass it because they attribute the possession of energy (inasmuch as it is a "physical magnitude") to substance, which is subordinate to it (sub-stăntia), and not to quality (to the essence), which is superordinate to it.

Let us think, for example, of the brain. One thing is the brain as (physical or anatomical) object; another, the brain as (etheric or physiological) activity. Nowadays, not a few maintain, in fact, that it is impossible to attribute mental (extrasensible) activity to the cerebral (sensible) substance. Granted; but if this activity, though unfolding in the brain, is not of the brain, whose is it, then? What is, that is, its subject?

As one sees, one halts before the threshold: one halts, that is, before the reality of that essence which one is incapable of thinking in its extrasensible nature inasmuch as one has not previously been capable of thinking the reality of energy in its sensible-extrasensible nature (that is to say, in its mediating function).

The fact is that, in nature, the (physical) substances are subordinate to the (etheric) forces; the forces are subordinate to the laws (to the essences of the astral world); and the laws are, finally, subordinate to a single principle (the I), which sums up and harmonizes them all.

Steiner says that by "dissolving" the processes of the corporeal world into relations of energy, "one does nothing other than divert attention from the content of the real world of the senses in order to turn it toward an unreal abstraction": that is, toward a something which is "unreal" inasmuch as it has been precisely abstracted or extracted from the real aggregate of which it is but part or moment.

Since the object is – as we have said – an aggregate of qualities, it would, therefore, be dutiful to inquire what the essence is that comprehends and sums up all the qualities, instead of promoting some of them to the (primary) rank of "essence" (objective), degrading the others to the (secondary) one of "manifestation" (subjective).

It is, moreover, singular, in this regard, that one should esteem and admire Kant so much, while neglecting or ignoring, at the same time, his distinction between "superordinate" and "subordinate" concepts: that is to say, between (superior) concepts, which comprehend and subsume other concepts, and (inferior) concepts, which are, on the contrary, comprehended and subsumed by other concepts. ("The extension or the sphere of a concept – he writes, in fact – is the greater, the more things stand under that concept and can be thought with it" – Logic – Laterza, Rome-Bari, 1984, p. 88).
                                                   233
Steiner says again: "Not this 'dissolving' of one genus of qualities in the other can be the task of natural science, but rather the inquiry into relations and rapports among the various perceivable qualities of the sensible world." Every "aggregate" characterizes itself, in fact, by the qualities it comprehends, but, above all, by the reciprocal relation in which these find themselves. In this regard, we have already given, one evening, the example of the words. Let us take the word orma (footprint): does it perhaps differ from the word ramo (branch) by the number of letters, or by the consonants and vowels that compose it? No; it differs, rather, by the way in which these stand in relation to one another; this relation is not, however, a "thing": that is to say, a quid that one can see in the same way as the single consonants and vowels; just as they are not "things," all the more reason, both the concept expressed, by means of the first relation, by the word orma, and that expressed, by means of the second, by the word ramo.

As, therefore, the concept expresses itself, in the sphere of language, by disposing the consonants and the vowels in a particular relation, so the essence expresses itself, in the field of nature (let us remember the difference, in the Hegelian logic, between the logic of the concept and the logic of essence), by disposing the elements (whatsoever they may be) in a particular relation. In order to discover the essence, it is necessary, therefore, first to re-ascend from the (physical) elements to the (etheric) relation, and then from the relation to the (astral) world of the essences or of the ideas.


---


Steiner writes: "Thus we discover certain conditions, given which, one sensible perception necessarily draws another behind it; and we discover that between certain phenomena there exists a more intimate nexus than between others. Then we no longer link the phenomena together in the way they offer themselves to casual observation; for we recognize that certain connections of phenomena are necessary; over against these, others are accidental. Goethe calls the necessary connections between phenomena type-phenomena or primordial phenomena (Urphänomene). The expression of a type-phenomenon always consists in this: that of a given sensible perception it is said that it necessarily calls forth another. This expression is what is called a law of nature" (pp. 223-224).

As you see, we no longer have to do, here, with the "internal" relation among the qualities of a single phenomenon, but with the "external" relation between two or more phenomena. But what holds for the former holds also for the latter. What else manifests itself, in fact, by means of that "necessary connection" we call a "law of nature," if not an essence (broader or more extended) superordinate to those of the single phenomena?

Do you remember what Steiner said? That "Goethe thinks the world as a circle of circles" (The Scientific Works of Goethe (15) – ed.). Well, are we not precisely discovering that the world is an essence of essences, a concept of concepts, or an idea of ideas? Hegel rightly observes: "Truth is its own movement within itself"; "by means of such movement the pure thoughts become concepts, and only then are they what they truly are: self-movements, circles; they are what their substance is, spiritual essences" (Phenomenology of Spirit – La Nuova Italia, Florence 1996, pp. 28 and 20).


---


Steiner writes: "The type-phenomenon represents a necessary connection between elements of the world of perception"; and, after reporting the following assertion of H. Helmholtz (Hermann von Helmholtz, 1821-1894 – ed.): "It is deplorable that Goethe did not know at that time the wave theory of light, then already discovered by Huygens (Christiaan Huygens, 1629-1695 – ed.); this would have furnished him a much more just and evident type-phenomenon than that process so complicated and unsuited chosen by him for this purpose in the turbid media," he adds: "The 'dissolving' of the sensibly perceivable processes into imperceptible mechanical movements has become so much a habit for the modern physicists that they seem not to notice in the least that they are placing an abstraction in place of reality. Sentences like that of Helmholtz will be able to be pronounced only after all the Goethean sentences of the kind of this one have been eliminated from the world: 'The summit of science would be to comprehend that the fact is already theory. The blue of the sky reveals to us the fundamental law of chromatics. Let one beware of seeking anything behind the phenomena; they themselves are the theory.' Goethe remains within the world of the phenomena; instead, the modern physicists gather some shreds from the world of the phenomena and transfer them behind the phenomena, in order then to derive from those hypothetical realities the phenomena of the true perceivable experience" (pp. 224-225).

234
You know that the way indicated by Steiner is called the "way of thinking." Nevertheless, in the light of what we have just read, it could also be called the "way of perception." To assert – as Goethe does – that the phenomenon is already theory is equivalent, in fact, to asserting that the percept is already concept (which is what we have attempted up to now to demonstrate). In the same measure in which one removes oneself from sensible perception, one removes oneself, therefore, also from the concept or, which is the same thing, from spiritual perception. One who removes himself from both sensible perception and spiritual one removes himself, therefore, from reality, fatally ending by losing himself in those abstractions of which the obstructive forces are extremely fond.

We have finished the third section. Let us begin at once the fourth, which is, moreover, very short.


---


Steiner writes: "Some younger physicists assert that they attribute to the concept of matter in motion no sense that goes beyond experience." He then cites a passage in which one of these, Antonio Lampa ("who has the singular knack of being at the same time a follower of the mechanical theory of nature and of Indian mysticism"), polemicizes with Ostwald, maintaining (in his "Nights of a Seeker, Braunschweig, 1893") that this one's fight is quixotic because "the giant of scientific materialism" does not exist; and he continues thus: "Lampa must be designated as the type of the normal contemporary naturalist. This one applies the mechanical explanation of nature because it is convenient to handle. But he avoids reflecting upon its true character, because he fears becoming entangled in contradictions that his thinking does not feel ready to surpass. How can one who loves clear thinking conjoin a sense with the concept of matter, without going beyond the world of experience? In this there exist bodies of determinate magnitude and position; there are movements and forces, and moreover the phenomena of light, color, warmth, electricity, life, etc. Experience does not tell us that magnitude, color, warmth, etc. are attached to a matter. Nowhere can we find matter within the world of experience. One who wishes to think it must add it to experience with thought" (pp. 225-226).


---


I do not know this Antonio Lampa, but the fact that he is a physicist who "has the singular knack – as Steiner says – of being at the same time a follower of the mechanical theory of nature and of Indian mysticism" inevitably makes me think of the American physicist Fritjof Capra (b. 1939), who, in his well-known The Tao of Physics (Adelphi, Milan 1994), as one reads on the back cover: "Explains to the reader, on the one hand, the concepts, the paradoxes, and the enigmas of the theory of relativity, of quantum mechanics, and of the submicroscopic world; and, on the other, makes him savor the profound and disconcerting charm of the Eastern mystical philosophies."

One who "loves clear thinking," and strives, therefore, to proceed – as Scaligero would say – along The Way of the Solar Will (Tilopa, Rome 1986), does not, however, allow himself to be deceived or deluded by this "mystical materialism" or "materialistic mysticism," for he does not toil much to recognize it as the fruit of the sinister union between the (mechanistic) Ahrimanic forces and the (mystical) Luciferic ones. An undeniably intelligent fruit, indeed an extremely intelligent one; one must, however, beware of the seduction
                                                   235
of the "big brains," inasmuch as what is most cerebral is not necessarily more human. The human being is, in fact, in part "cortex," whereas the Ahrimanic beings are only "cortex."

Steiner says that "nowhere can we find matter within the world of experience"; nowhere in the world of experience can we, in effect, find matter "without beginning or end," "indestructible," "eternal," or "immutable." And why? Because such "matter" is an idea, and not (as is believed, and as one would have believed) a thing, endowed more or less with energy.

Steiner writes: "Such an adding, with thought, of matter to the phenomena of the world of experience is met with in the physical and physiological considerations that have become familiar to modern natural science under the influence of Kant and of Johannes Müller (…) If an eye capable of feeling did not exist, there would be no color either, but only ether in motion: so the modern scientist of nature believes (and so Boncinelli still believes, when he says – as we saw a couple of times ago – that, in nature, 'straw yellow' does not exist – ed.). The ether would be objective, the color only subjective, formed solely in the human body. Prof. Wundt of Leipzig (Wilhelm Wundt, 1832-1920 – ed.), whom one hears at times extolled as one of the greatest contemporary philosophers, says, therefore, of matter that it is a substratum 'which never itself becomes visible to us, but always only in its effects.' And, in his Logic, he adds that 'a contradiction-free explanation of the phenomena succeeds only when such a substratum is presupposed.' The Cartesian illusion of clear and confused representations has become the fundamental representation of physics" (pp. 226-227).


---


Answer to a question

Taking our cue from an assertion of Heisenberg's, we said, one evening (The Scientific Works of Goethe (12) – ed.), that what is true is always useful, whereas what is useful is not always true; specifying then that what is true is useful to the human being, whereas what is not true is useful to some other being; and a short time ago (The Scientific Works of Goethe (41) – ed.) we also read the following words of Steiner's: "One must have the courage to admit all this before contemporary natural science, notwithstanding the mighty admirable conquests that it has to record in the field of technology. For such conquests have nothing to do with a true need for knowledge of nature (…) One thing is to observe the processes of nature in order to place their forces at the service of technology; another is to seek, with the help of those processes, to look more deeply into the essence of natural science. True science is only there where the spirit seeks satisfaction of its own needs, without exterior aims."

What, then, is the problem? It is that of the satisfaction of the needs of the spirit, without exterior aims, there is almost no longer any trace. Modern science, which had, it is true, emancipated itself from medieval philosophy, inasmuch as it was the "handmaid of theology," has by now, in fact, become, especially after the Second World War, the "handmaid of economics," and for that very reason a maid ever more of the useful and ever less of the true; to the point that Federico di Trocchio (professor of the History of Science at the University of Lecce), in the preface to his The Lies of Science, came to write: "To swindle has always been an art. Of late it has also become a science. I should propose to call it swindlology, or better, as Tullio De Mauro suggests, swindlonics. It is a discipline of the avant-garde that does not constitute a subject of teaching, but has by now become an integral part of the cultural baggage of the professional scientists. It does not consist in rendering the incredible and the impossible credible to the common people, as astrologers, magicians, healers, and vulgar impostors do, but in doing the same thing with one's own colleagues. Which is at the same time easier and more difficult. Easier because the practitioners of the trade are often strangely more naïve than the ignorant (…) More difficult because one must know the subject matter and the details of the experimental techniques (…) Swindlonics is, therefore, the science that teaches scientists how to swindle the other scientists.

236
These in their turn convince the journalists, who finally seduce the masses" (Mondadori-DeAgostini, Milan 1994, pp. 3-4).

One must say, however, that swindlonics, before even being – as di Trocchio says – "the science that teaches scientists how to swindle the other scientists," is the science with which Ahriman teaches how to swindle, first of all (and unconsciously), oneself.

We have finished the fourth section as well. Next week we shall occupy ourselves with the fifth.

L.R.
Rome, 30 October 2001

237
The Scientific Works of Goethe (49)


---


We shall take up tonight the fifth section of the sixteenth chapter, and, God willing, we shall examine the sixth as well, since it is very short.

Steiner writes: "A thinking that has not been radically spoiled by Descartes, Locke, Kant, and modern physiology will never succeed in understanding how one can regard light, color, sound, warmth, etc., as purely subjective states of the human organism, and nevertheless assert the existence of an objective world of processes outside of the organism" (p. 227).

Two times ago, we saw that Steiner judges the ideas of physics "unhealthy and insufficient," and tonight we read of a thinking "radically spoiled by Descartes, Locke, Kant, and modern physiology."

These are "strong" expressions that might seem, at first sight, even excessive or out of place. But it is not so. Were we not accustomed, as we are, to live in abstraction, or to have with ideas an exclusively cerebral relation, we would soon realize, in fact, that what is false for thinking is ugly for feeling and evil for willing.

Living in abstraction, we recognize, instead, the evil of willing, but not the falseness of thinking; and we thus give ourselves to blaming the former moralistically and to praising the latter naïvely, behaving like that fellow who – according to Bertrand Russell (1872-1970) – was wont to praise keys and blame the holes of locks.

The fact is that, alongside the rehabilitation centers for motor activity, which provide for repairing the damages produced by physical traumas, it would be fitting to create rehabilitation centers for thinking-activity, to provide for repairing the damages produced by scholastic education and by the present culture.

Such institutes – let it be clear, indeed most clear – should not indoctrinate thinking, educating it to think this or that (for that is already done), but should detoxify it, re-educating it to freedom: that is, to being simply itself.

A free thinking could never, for example, accept that one divide the reality of the world with a line, declaring that what lies beyond it is objective, while what lies on this side is subjective, and therefore, in substance, illusory.

Steiner continues: "One who makes of the human organism the generator of the processes of sound, of warmth, of color, etc., must surely see in it the generator of extension, magnitude, position, movement, forces, etc. For these mathematical and mechanical qualities are, in reality, inseparably connected with the remaining content of the world of perception. The separation of the relations of space, number, and movement, as well as of the energetic manifestations, from all the other sensible qualities (sound, color, etc.) is but a function of the thinking that abstracts. The laws of mathematics and of mechanics refer to abstract objects and processes, severed from the world of experience, and for that reason can also find application only within the ambit of the world of experience" (p. 227).


---


The so-called (by Locke) "primary" qualities are, in reality, wholly equivalent to the "secondary" ones, since both the former and the latter are given by perception (and are therefore percepts). It is, therefore, thinking that divides them and assigns to the qualities of extension, of position, or of movement, a "primary character," and to the qualities of sound, of warmth, or of color, a "secondary character," thus superimposing, upon an aggregate of perceptions, two judgments.

238
Steiner says that the laws of mathematics and mechanics can "find application only within the ambit of the world of experience" inasmuch as "they refer to abstract objects and processes, severed from the world of experience." This explains sufficiently why, from such laws, it is possible to extract many things materially useful, but few spiritually useful. A thinking that traffics only with "abstract objects and processes, severed from the world of experience," can, in fact, render great services to technology, but not to the knowledge of the reality of the world and of man.

The intellect (the "computational mind") operates, in fact, like a sieve which, of the reality of the world, lets pass all that is quantitative, and for that very reason mathematically comprehensible, but nothing of what is living and qualitative, which is thus retained in the unconscious.

This mind, therefore, knows only inasmuch as it measures.

Let us try, however, to think of what would happen if, ardently desiring to know the beloved person, we gave ourselves with zeal and rigor to measuring her, as if she were a skeleton. But if we find it grotesque or repugnant to do such a thing with the beloved person, for what reason do we then do it with the world? Do we perhaps love it less, or do we not love it at all?

It will be well, therefore, to remember that Ahriman (that is, the being in whom "lives the icy hatred against everything that evolves in freedom") opposes the "incalculable" just as much as Lucifer opposes the "calculable." "The intent of Ahriman – Steiner explains, in fact – is to make a cosmic machine of what he emanates from the earth into the universal spaces. His ideal is solely: 'Measure, number, weight.'" An intent, therefore, altogether different from that of the Archangel Michael, who (in perfect harmony with the intent of Christ) aims, on the contrary, at establishing "the balance between the incalculable and the calculable" (Anthroposophical Maxims – Antroposofica, Milan 1969, p. 152).

Steiner writes: "So long as the modern scientists and their caudatories, the modern philosophers, will maintain that the sensible perceptions are but subjective states aroused by objective processes, a healthy thinking must always object that they are playing with empty concepts, or else attribute to objectivity a content taken on loan from the world of experience that they declare subjective" (pp. 227-228).


---


Well, let us then take this small encyclopedia and see what it says under the entry "quark": "Elementary particle hypothesized as the constituent of all the hadrons (protons, neutrons, etc.), that is, the subatomic particles that feel the strong interaction (which acts 'within the atomic nuclei, holding protons and neutrons together' – ed.) (…) The idea of the existence of quarks was born to explain the disproportion between the high number (some hundreds) of hadrons discovered in comparison with the leptons (the family of elementary particles that feel the weak interaction (manifesting itself, for example, in radioactive β decay – ed.), which are 6 in number (plus the respective antiparticles): this drove the search for a simplification for the hadrons. In the '60s the hypothesis was advanced by the American physicist M. Gell-Mann (b. 1929) that the true elementary particles are 6 quarks, each characterized by a particular flavor (it is an expression of pure fantasy): up, down, strange, charm, top, beauty; each type of quark can present itself in three colors (again with expressions of fantasy): red, blue, and green" (Encyclopedia of Science and Technology – DeAgostini – Novara 1994, p. 867).

As you see, not only does one attribute "fantastically" to the quarks (to the presumed "objectivity") contents (flavors and colors) taken on loan – as Steiner says – from that "world of experience that is declared subjective," but one even indulges in a fatuous and cloying anthropomorphism.

But this is not all. Does the search for "a simplification for the hadrons" not recall, in fact, what we saw asserted in due course by Barsanti concerning Linnaean philosophy? Namely, that "as it soon became clear that in spite of certain premises it led to an artificial system, so it was at the same time equally clear that this constituted a great advantage for botanical science, and perhaps the most important advantage. Gilibert realized it with great lucidity: notwithstanding that of Tournefort was more natural, the system of Linnaeus was to be unquestionably
                                                   239
preferred to it because it was 'more uniform in its articulation,' that is, it avoided furnishing too many criteria for classifying the bodies (which disorients the naturalist) and furnished, much more efficaciously, a single parameter, the key for disentangling oneself in the labyrinth of nature" (The Scientific Works of Goethe (4) – ed.).


---


After citing another passage of Antonio Lampa's, "Mathematical method and mathematics are not identical, for the mathematical method can be adopted without the application of mathematics. A classic example of this fact is offered to us, in the field of physics, by the experimental researches of Faraday (Michael Faraday, 1791-1867 – ed.), who scarcely understood the squaring of a binomial. Mathematics is but a means of abbreviating logical operations, and thereby of resolving them even in most complicated cases in which ordinary logical thinking would leave us in the lurch. At the same time, however, it accomplishes something else besides; inasmuch as every formula implicitly expresses the process by which something comes to be; it throws a living bridge as far as the elementary phenomena that have served as the point of departure of the research," Steiner writes: "Every mathematical formula does, it is true, construct a 'living bridge' toward the elementary phenomena that have served as the point of departure of the researches. But these elementary phenomena are of the same species as those that are not elementary, from which the bridge is constructed" (pp. 228-229).

What does it mean that "the elementary phenomena are of the same species as the non-elementary ones"? It is simple: that both have mathematical character.

Steiner explains precisely: "The mathematician relates the properties of complicated numerical and spatial figures, as well as their reciprocal relations, to properties and relations of simpler numerical and spatial figures"; and he proceeds: "The mechanic does the same in his own field. He relates complicated processes of movements and actions of forces to more simply, more easily embraceable movements and actions of forces, and for this purpose he makes use of the mathematical laws, as far as the movements and the actions of forces are expressible in spatial figures and numbers" (p. 229).

Let us note well the difference. Both the mathematician and the mechanic reduce non-elementary phenomena to elementary phenomena; but whereas the former, by reducing complex "numerical and spatial figures" to simple "numerical and spatial figures" "of the same species," does not go beyond the sphere of mathematics, the latter, by reducing complex "processes of movements and actions of forces" to simple "numerical and spatial figures" of a different species (with which he would wish to express simple "processes of movements and actions of forces"), goes beyond the sphere of mechanics.

Steiner observes, in fact: "In a mathematical formula that expresses a mechanical law, the single members no longer signify purely mathematical figures, but forces and movements. The relations in which these members stand to one another are not determined by purely mathematical laws, but by the properties of the forces and of the movements. As soon as one prescinds from this particular content of the mechanical formulas, one no longer has to do with mechanical laws, but only with mathematical ones" (p. 229).


---


Since we are speaking of "mechanics," it will perhaps be fitting to recall that this traditionally comprises statics, kinematics, and dynamics. Let us reopen, therefore, our small encyclopedia at the entry "mechanics," and let us see how it explains the difference among the three: statics studies "the conditions of equilibrium of bodies subjected to determinate forces"; kinematics describes "motion independently of the causes that produce it"; dynamics treats "of motion, taking into consideration the forces that determine it."

240
Let us keep in mind, therefore, that Steiner's observations refer in general to mechanics, but in particular to dynamics.

Steiner writes: "As mechanics stands to mathematics, so physics stands to mechanics. The physicist's task is to relate complicated processes in the field of color, of sound, of warmth, of electricity, of magnetism, etc., to simpler phenomena within the same sphere" (p. 229).

In other words, when mechanics and physics, in the intent of tracing complex phenomena back to simple ones, go beyond their respective spheres, the former reduces itself to mathematics, inasmuch as it reduces "forces" and "movements" to "purely mathematical figures," and the latter to mechanics, inasmuch as it reduces the qualities to "forces and movements."

Steiner writes precisely: "Not the reduction of the processes of color, sound, etc., to phenomena of movement and to relations of forces within a matter deprived of color and of sound corresponds, in the field of physics, to the mathematical method ('mathematical method and mathematics – Lampa said precisely – are not identical' – ed.), but the inquiry into the relations within the phenomena of color, sound, etc. Modern physics passes over the phenomena of sound, color, etc., as such, and considers only immutable forces and movements of attraction and repulsion in space. Under the influence of this way of representing, the physics of today has already become applied mathematics and mechanics, and the other fields of natural science are on the way to becoming the same. It is impossible to link with a living bridge (as Du Bois-Reymond too recognizes – Steiner recalls – in The Limits of Natural Knowledge – ed.) the two following facts: 1st) that at a given point of space a determinate process of movement of colorless matter holds sway; 2nd) that at that point man sees red" (pp. 229-230).

"Under the influence of this way" of thinking science (and no longer only, by now, "natural" science) has, therefore, become – as one says – "more royalist than the king" (than mathematics). According to what we have seen, one thing is, in fact, to reduce mathematically complex numerical and spatial figures to simple numerical and spatial figures; another, to reduce mathematically to simple numerical and spatial figures the phenomena of life (the forces), of the soul (the qualities), or of the spirit (the thoughts). (For example, the celebrated German mathematician Georg Cantor [1845-1918], who died in an insane asylum at Halle, was "one who sought to find God through mathematics" – J. Volpi: In Search of Klingsor – Mondadori, Milan 2000, pp. 40-41).


---


Steiner concludes: "The quality 'red' and a determinate process of movement are, in reality, an inseparable unity (…) To wish to derive the fact: 'I see red,' from a process of movement is as absurd as deriving the real properties of a piece of rock salt crystallized in cubic form from the mathematical cube" (p. 230).

We should, in fact, know nothing of the qualities of a crystal of rock salt if we knew only what it has in common with the mathematical cube: what does not yet, that is, make of it a crystal of rock salt.

We have finished the fifth section; let us occupy ourselves, therefore, with the sixth, which is – as you can see – very short.

Steiner writes: "The modern physicists, only because seduced by the preconception that a simple mathematical or mechanical fact is more comprehensible than an elementary phenomenon of sound or of color, eliminate from the phenomena the specific character of sound and of color and consider only the processes of movement corresponding to the perceptions of sense. And since they cannot think movements without something that moves, they consider as bearer of the movements the matter stripped of all its
                                                   241
properties. One who is not entangled in this preconception of the physicists must recognize that the processes of movement are conditions connected with the sensible qualities. The content of the wave movements corresponding to the sound phenomena is the qualities of sound. The same holds for the other qualities of sense" (p. 231).

The fact is that the intellect, in order to enjoy the fact that "the accounts – as one says – come out right," must reduce even the incalculable to the calculable, thereby expelling the former from reality. And yet – Goethe's Urphänomen demonstrates it – the incalculable could be even more "simple" than a "simple mathematical fact." In order to be able to discover it, however, one would have to observe the real phenomena (given by sensible perception) lovingly, and not spend the greater part of one's time at the blackboard or, as is done today, at the computer.

Jorge Volpi recalls, for example: "In order to be able to meditate scientifically upon hypotheses that, otherwise, he could not have verified, Einstein often put into practice a method of work that he called Gedankenexperiment, or 'thought experiment'"; and of the young physicist Francis P. Bacon, the protagonist of his book, he says: "Ever since his precocious passion for pure mathematics had manifested itself, he had done his best to keep at a distance from concrete questions, concentrating on formulas and equations that seemed ever more abstract, and to which, in many cases, it was scarcely possible to associate a real explanation" (Op. cit., pp. 63 and 64-65).

We can well understand Goethe, therefore, when he writes: "One who were capable of teaching a critique of the human intellect would be a benefactor of humanity. To circumscribe the human intellect within its own ambit" (Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 266).

L.R.
Rome, 7 November 2001

242
The Scientific Works of Goethe (50)


---


I think that tonight we shall finish the sixteenth chapter, since the last three sections (the seventh, the eighth, and the ninth) are very short.

Let us begin, therefore, with the seventh.

Steiner writes: "I know that these views of mine will seem absurd to the contemporary physicists. But I cannot share the point of view of Wundt, who, in his Logic, designates the thought-habits of the modern naturalists as rigorous logical norms. The thoughtlessness of which he renders himself guilty is clear especially where he treats of Ostwald's attempt to put, in place of matter in motion, energy in oscillatory movement" (p. 232).

What does Wundt object? That "the derivation of the luminous phenomena from a mechanical process is an indispensable exigency," inasmuch as a "real movement can only be defined as the change of place of a real substratum situated in space," and is therefore "unthinkable without a substratum that moves."

Steiner observes, however: "Ostwald's concept of energy stands much nearer to reality than does the so-called 'real' substratum of Wundt. The phenomena of the world of perception – light, warmth, electricity, magnetism, etc. – can be traced back to the general concept of the emission of force, that is, of energy. When light, warmth, etc. produce a change in a body, an energy has thereby precisely been exercised. And when one indicates light, warmth, etc. as energy, one leaves aside what is specifically proper to the single sensible qualities, in order to consider a general property that pertains to them in common. This property does not, it is true, exhaust all that is real in the things, but it is a real property of those very things" (pp. 232-233).


---


It will be fitting here to take up again that schema in which, in correspondence with the concepts of "physical body," "etheric body," "astral body," and "I," we placed, respectively, the concepts of "space," "time," "quality," and "being," stressing that the "threshold" that divides the sphere of existing (of the physical body and the etheric body) from that of being (of the astral body and the I) is the same that divides the sphere of space and time from that of quality and being (The Scientific Works of Goethe (42) – ed.).
