We find ourselves, in fact, in the presence of a Wundt, representative of what in The Philosophy of Freedom is called "naive realism," and that we have characterized as a realism of space (or of things), and of an Ostwald, representative of what, again in The Philosophy of Freedom, is called, on the other hand, "metaphysical realism," and that we have characterized as a realism of time (or of force).

Let us take up, therefore, our schema, writing, in place of "space," "matter," and, in place of "time," "energy." As one sees, the dispute between Wundt and Ostwald concerns solely what is found on this side of the threshold, for neither of the two takes into consideration the quality (the concept, the idea, the essence, or the entelechy), which stands beyond it.

Remaining on this side of the threshold, and holding (as both do) that energy does not explain itself, it is, however, not easy to give account of its source; Ostwald distinguishes it, in fact, from matter, subordinating it to unknown "principles that transcend it" (The Scientific Works of Goethe (47) – ed.), whereas Wundt, who likewise distinguishes it from (sensible) matter, subordinates it to a no less unknown extrasensible matter: that is, to "a real substratum situated in space."

One sees well, therefore, why Steiner says that "Ostwald's concept of energy stands much nearer to reality than does the so-called 'real' substratum of Wundt." Observing our schema, we see, in fact, that Ostwald's concept of energy "stands much nearer to reality" – that is, to the quality (to the "principles that transcend it") – than does that of Wundt, which places itself, inasmuch as it is associated with the concept of "substratum," even on this side of, or below, space. As one sees, "scientific materialism" excogitates (more or less knowingly) an energetic source (at once material and immaterial) intended to substitute for and surrogate the supersensible one of quality.

Wundt maintains that Ostwald's concept of energy, being "a double concept composed of a phenomenic part (manifest energy, or ex se – ed.) and of a purely conceptual part (unmanifest energy, or in se – ed.)," seems "to demonstrate decisively that the very concept of energy requires an analysis that leads back to the elements of observation." Oh, really! And does not his own concept of "substratum" require the same thing?


---


Steiner observes precisely: "It is incomprehensible how Wundt can assert that the concept of 'radiant energy' is impossible because it comprises an observable part and a conceptual part. Does the philosopher Wundt not, then, succeed in comprehending that every concept referring to objects of sensible reality must necessarily contain an element of observation and an element of the conceptual?" (p. 233).

The fact is that Wundt does not even succeed in comprehending that to declare – as he does – that the "real substratum can reveal itself only through actions of forces of which we consider it the bearer" is equivalent to explaining movement by means of the substratum, and the substratum by means of movement. In any case, what does it imply – let us ask ourselves – to place the source of energy or of movement in quality, and to place it instead in matter? That in the former case one brings into play a supersensible reality that one can come to perceive by educating and developing one's own soul (one's own cognitive forces), whereas in the latter one brings into play a subsensible reality that one strives in vain to come to perceive in its fundamental constituents (the so-called "elementary particles"), making use of instruments. The soul is thus solicited to busy itself materially, and not to evolve – as would be its duty – spiritually.

Steiner says that energy "does not, it is true, exhaust all that is real in the things, but is a real property of those very things": it is precisely by means of this "real property," in fact, that the extrasensible essence or quality takes to manifesting itself in the sensible world.

We have finished the seventh section; let us begin the eighth.

Steiner writes: "The development of natural science in the last centuries has led to the destruction of all the representations by which this science could be part of a conception of the world apt to satisfy the higher human needs. It has brought it about that the 'modern' scientific minds deem it absurd to assert that concepts and ideas belong to reality just as much as the forces working in space and the matter that fills space. Concepts and ideas are, for such spirits, a product of the human brain and nothing more" (p. 233).


---


The materialistic representations of present-day science, and the techniques that derive from them, are, in fact, "part of a conception of the world apt to satisfy the" inferior "human needs"; and it is significant that the common denominator of such representations is constituted by nominalism: that is, by the conviction that concepts or ideas are nothing other than names (do you remember, for example, what Boncinelli said? That intelligence is "the categorization, that is, the attribution of names to things"). (The Scientific Works of Goethe (44) – ed.).

Listen to what Friedrich Engels (1820-1895) says in this regard, citing Karl Marx (1818-1883): "The cradle of all modern materialism, from the seventeenth century on, was England and no other country. Materialism is the legitimate son of Great Britain. Already its great scholastic, Duns Scotus, had asked himself whether matter cannot think. To realize this miracle he had recourse to the omnipotence of God, and thus compelled theology itself to preach materialism. He was, moreover,
                                                  244
a nominalist. Nominalism represents the principal element of the English materialists, just as it is, in general, the first expression of materialism. The authentic father of English materialism is Bacon (Francis Bacon, 1561-1626 – ed.). Natural science is for him the only true science, and physics, founded upon the experience of the senses, is the essential part of natural science" (The Development of Socialism from Utopia to Science – Edizioni in Lingue Estere, Moscow 1947, p. 15).

Well, let us then hear how much Hegel says of Francis Bacon, defined by him as the "leader" of the philosophy of experience: "Bacon is even today extolled as the one who would have indicated to knowledge its true source, experience; he is, in fact, properly the precursor and the representative of what in England one is wont to call philosophy, and upon which the English have not yet succeeded in raising themselves. For they seem to constitute in Europe the people which, confined to the intellect of 'actuality,' is destined, like the shopkeepers and the artisans in the State, to live ever immersed in matter, and to have as object 'actuality,' but not reason."

And let us hear also how much he says of the philosophy of experience (of empiricism): "We have already recalled how much it matters to aim at the content as content of the reality of the present: the rational, in fact, must have objective reality. The reconciliation of the spirit with the world, the sublimation of nature and of every reality, must not be a beyond, a 'then,' but must be accomplished now and here. This moment of the now and the here is that which in substance comes, by such a way, to self-consciousness. The experiences, the experiments, the observations, however, do not know what in truth they do, namely, that the only reason why they interest themselves in the things is precisely the intimate unconscious certainty that reason has of finding itself again in reality; the observations and the experiments, when they are rightly instituted, arrive precisely at the result that only the concept is the objective. From the experiments there escapes, precisely, from between the hands the single sensible thing, and it becomes a universal (…) Man does not halt at the single thing, nor can he halt there. He seeks the universal, and it consists of thoughts, if not of concepts. The most conspicuous form of thought is that of force: one speaks thus of an electrical force, of a magnetic force, of a force of gravity. But force is a universal and not a perceivable; therefore, in abandoning themselves to these determinations, the empiricists operate in a wholly uncritical way and without consciousness" (Lectures on the History of Philosophy – La Nuova Italia, Florence 1981, vol. 3, II, pp. 17-18 and 27-28).


---


Just as Wundt operates, in fact, in his capacity as representative – we have said it – of naive realism. He, in fact, not only does not notice – as Hegel says – that force (energy) "is a universal and not a perceivable," but does not even realize how absurd it is to give to the idea of matter (as "substratum") a material, and not an ideal, valence.

Steiner continues: "The Scholastics still knew how things stood in this regard; but Scholasticism is disdained by modern science; it is disdained, but it is not known; and one ignores, above all, what in it is healthy and what is not healthy. Healthy is its feeling that concepts and ideas are not only chimeras invented by the human spirit in order to comprehend the real things, but that they truly have to do with the objects themselves, more still than matter and force. This healthy feeling of the Scholastics is an inheritance of the great philosophical perspectives of Plato and Aristotle" (pp. 233-234).

Not by chance does Steiner speak here of a healthy "feeling." Plato and Aristotle, no less than Scholasticism, bring us back, in fact, to the epoch of the rational and affective soul (of feeling in thinking), and therefore to an epoch that could not yet have, with concepts and ideas, that relation that we men of the consciousness soul (of willing in thinking) could have, did we not let our wings be clipped by materialism.

245
The principal determinations of Bacon – Hegel observes in this regard – "consist in presenting himself polemically against the scholastic method then in vogue, of conquering knowledge by the deductive way, against the syllogistic forms" (Op. cit., p. 25).

One thing, however, is to reject the deductive method or the syllogizing; another, to reject (with an "unhealthy" feeling) the reality of concepts and ideas. Do you remember, in fact, how much Steiner asserts? "The objective datum does not at all coincide with the sensible datum, as the mechanical conception of the world believes. The sensible is only one half of the datum. The other half is the ideas, which are also object of experience, though of a higher experience, having thought as its organ. The ideas, too, are accessible to an inductive method" (The Scientific Works of Goethe (16) – ed.).

Steiner says again that concepts and ideas have "to do with the objects themselves, more still than matter and force." In our schema we see them, in fact, beyond the threshold, and therefore superordinate both to time and to space, or both to energy and to matter.


---


Steiner takes up again, in any case: "Unhealthy, in Scholasticism, is the confusion between this feeling and the representations that introduced themselves into the medieval evolution of Christianity. This evolution finds the source of every spirituality, and therefore also of concepts and ideas, in the unknowable God, because extra-universal. It needs to believe in something that is not of this world. But a healthy thinking holds to this world; and occupies itself with no other world than this. At the same time, however, it spiritualizes this world. It sees in concepts and ideas realities of this world, just as it sees them in the things and events perceivable by the senses. Greek philosophy is an emanation of this healthy thinking" (p. 234).

There would be much to say, in effect, about a Christianity that, heedless of the incarnation of the Logos, intended to liquidate definitively the opposition between immanence and transcendence, obstinates itself, with an Old-Testamentary spirit, in placing its faith in an "unknowable God, because extra-universal." But let it be, for the matter would carry us too far; let us pass, rather, to the ninth and last section, which is truly very short.

Steiner writes: "In the course of the centuries the Christian representations became more efficacious than the obscure feelings inherited from Greek antiquity. The feeling of the reality of concepts and ideas was lost. But together with it, the faith in the spirit itself was also lost. The adoration of what is purely material began; the Newtonian era in natural science began. No longer did one speak of the unity that lies at the basis of the multiplicity of the world; every unity was denied; it was degraded to a 'human' representation. In nature one saw only the plurality, the multiplicity" (p. 234).

To adore "what is purely material" is, however, equivalent to adoring death or, better said, the spirit that lives of death and in death, and to which Steiner – as we know – gives the name of Ahriman.

We could also say, therefore, that, "in the course of the centuries," the Ahrimanic forces "became more efficacious than the obscure feelings inherited from Greek antiquity." The Ahrimanic forces militate, in fact, one-sidedly, for multiplicity (or for intellectual analysis), whereas the Luciferic ones militate, equally one-sidedly, for unity (or for sentimental synthesis).

Goethe writes: "From nature, from whatever side one looks at it, the infinite springs forth" (Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 260).

There will not, therefore, be able to understand Goethe, and all the more reason anthroposophy, either the one who, unconsciously mortgaged to the Ahrimanic forces, does not see the spirit (the infinite) spring forth from nature (from the finite), or the one who, mortgaged to the Luciferic forces, sees the spirit (the infinite) spring forth, but not from nature (from the finite).

246
L.R.
Rome, 13 November 2001

247
The Scientific Works of Goethe (51)

Tonight we shall begin the seventeenth and last chapter (divided into five sections), entitled: The Goethean Conception of the World in the Poet's "Prose Sayings."


---


Steiner writes: "Man does not satisfy himself with what nature donates spontaneously to his observing spirit; he feels that, in order to generate the multiplicity of its creations, it has need of impulsive forces (Triebkräfte – ed.) which at first it hides from him. Nature does not of itself pronounce its last word; what it knows how to create, our experience shows us, but this does not tell us how such creation comes about. In the human spirit itself lies the means of revealing the impulsive forces of nature; from it arise the ideas that throw light upon the way in which nature accomplishes its creations. What the phenomena of the outer world conceal makes itself manifest in the inwardness of the human being. The laws of nature that the human spirit discovers are not something that it adds of its own to nature, but are the very essence of nature, and the spirit is only the scene upon which nature makes visibly appear the secrets of its action. What we observe in the things is but a part of them. The other part arises in our spirit when it places itself over against the things. It is the very things that speak to us, now from without, now from within; and only in conjoining the language of the outer world with that of our inwardness do we have the total reality" (p. 237).


---


The "impulsive" ("motive") forces of nature are, at first sight, forces of "will"; and thus Schopenhauer saw them, in fact (cf. The Will in Nature – Laterza, Rome-Bari 1989). Within them, however, thinking is present: that thinking which informs them, and which we bring to light every time we discover the law that governs a phenomenon.

Nature presents itself, therefore, as immediacy and unconsciousness precisely because, in it, thinking works within willing.

It is impossible, therefore, to penetrate into the essence of nature if one does not bring to meet its thinking-in-willing a willing-in-thinking: that is to say, a thinking that lives in the consciousness of man with the same vigor with which it lives in the unconsciousness of nature. We are referring, of course, to the living, ensouled, and spiritual (human) nature, for to penetrate into dead nature ordinary representational thinking suffices.

Steiner says that "it is the very things that speak to us, now from without, now from within," and that "only in conjoining the language of the outer world with that of our inwardness do we have the total reality"; it is, in fact, by conjoining the language of the dead outer world (of inorganic nature) with that of the dead inner world (of the cerebral cortex) that "we have the total reality" of the inorganic world, or so-called "sensible cognition."

Galileo recognized an objective value in it, for he was convinced that, in discovering the laws that regulate the phenomena, one discovers the "footprints of the Creator." He had, therefore, faith that human thinking can meet the divine, at least there where the latter lies buried – as he said – in the "stones," or – as Steiner says – in the "completed work."

Steiner asserts again that "what we observe in the things is but a part of them," and that "the other part arises in our spirit when it places itself over against the things." Let us note well that he says "spirit," and not "body," "soul," or "psyche." This signifies that the objective "other part" of the things reveals itself only to one who is capable of surpassing himself in order to be, at once, himself and world.

Steiner continues: "What did the true philosophers of all times will? Nothing other than to reveal the essence of the things, which these very things pronounce when the spirit offers itself to them as organ of expression" (p. 237).


---


248
Today, however, the task of revealing the essence of the things has been inherited by the scientists. Do you remember, for example, what Rita Levi Montalcini declared? That, thanks to the progress of the neurological and cognitive sciences, one will not be long in precisely discovering the essence of the human species (The Scientific Works of Goethe (6) – ed.).

But this is but an illusion. The scientists have, in fact, the "bread" of perception, but not the "teeth" of thinking; whereas the philosophers (as is consonant with the rational and affective soul) have the "teeth" of thinking, but not the "bread" of perception.

I should like to recount to you, in this regard, a small experience of my own. Many years ago, I had to sustain (as a private candidate) the examination for the teaching diploma, and therefore to confront a philosophy examination as well. Well, the professor who examined me addressed to me, to begin with, this question: "What do you expect from science?"; whereupon I promptly answered: "The knowledge of truth." Would that I never had said it! Visibly losing his composure, he brusquely asked me a few other things, and then dismissed me, saying to me, with ill-concealed disdain: "You are a medieval!"

I have often thought back on this episode, arriving at the conclusion that we were both right: I was right in expecting that science would guide me toward truth (toward the essence of things); and he was right because present-day science – as he said explicitly – can serve at most to construct airplanes, automobiles, television sets, or refrigerators, but certainly not to discover truth.

But if philosophy and science have renounced the inquiry into truth, to whom, then, should one turn who still has it at heart? I am almost sure that if we put this question to a philosopher, to a scientist, or to one of those intellectuals who preach, one day yes and the next day again, from the pulpits of radio, television, or the press, we would be advised to turn to a psychiatrist or a psychoanalyst, for the wishing to inquire into truth can constitute, nowadays, only a symptom of regression (perhaps to the "Middle Ages"), and for that very reason of neurosis.

Thanking God, one who still has truth at heart can, however, turn to the science of the spirit, and thereby undertake a way which, teaching him to chew the good "bread" of perception with the robust "teeth" of thinking, will permit him to carry himself beyond the customary ones of philosophy and science.

Steiner writes: "If man lets his own inwardness speak about nature, he recognizes that it remains below what it could accomplish by virtue of its impulsive forces. The spirit sees in a more perfect form what experience contains, and discovers that nature, with its creations, does not reach its own intents" (pp. 237-238).


---


What does Steiner mean here? It is not difficult: that nature never presents (to the senses) a pure phenomenon (an Urphänomen) in pure form, but always and only in spurious form, inasmuch as it is mixed – as we saw in due course – with what is inessential or accidental.

For this reason nature remains "below" its potentialities, whereas to man it is given to reach what it cannot accomplish.

He continues, in fact: "The spirit (…) feels itself called to represent those intents in more perfect form: it creates figures in which it shows what nature willed, succeeding, however, in accomplishing it only up to a certain degree. These figures are the works of art. In them man produces in a perfect way what nature shows imperfectly. The philosopher and the artist have the same goal. They seek to configure the perfect that their spirit discerns, when they let nature act upon them. But, to reach this goal, different means stand at their disposal. In the philosopher, before a natural process, a thought, an idea, lights up, and this he expresses. In the artist there arises an image of that process, which shows it more perfectly than it can be observed in the outer world" (p. 238).

249
One who grasps the essential, carrying himself beyond what the phenomenon presents to the senses (beyond what, that is, is a hybrid of necessary and accidental elements), can, therefore, seek to confer upon it a form that does not at all feel the influence of the inessential with which it finds itself united in nature.

Expressed in image, the essential gives itself as symbol. Florensky writes in this regard: "In artistic creation the soul is lifted from the earthly world and enters the celestial world. There, without images, it nourishes itself on the contemplation of the existence of the celestial world, touches the eternal noumena of things, and, impregnated, laden with knowledge, returns to the earthly world. And returning down by the same way, it arrives at the frontier of terreneity, where its spiritual acquisition is invested in symbolic images – the very ones which, fixing themselves, form the work of art" (The Royal Doors – Adelphi, Milan 1977, p. 34).

The "eternal noumena of things" give themselves, therefore, in the philosopher or the scientist, in the form of thoughts or ideas, and, in the artist, in the form of images. The same thing, said in Jungian terms, would sound thus: the "eternal noumena of things" give themselves, in the philosopher or the scientist, in the garb of "archetypes in themselves," and, in the artist, in the garb of "archetypal images."

These must, therefore, be distinguished from the images that are habitual to us in the waking state. Florensky observes precisely: "Going from reality to the imaginary, naturalism offers a fantastic image of the real, a superfluous exemplar of everyday life; the opposite art, on the contrary, symbolism, embodies in real images a different experience, and in offering it to us creates a higher reality" (ibid., p. 35).

As the science of the spirit teaches, one thing is, in fact, "fantasy," as subjective imagination; another, "Imagination," as objective fantasy.


---


Steiner concludes: "Now, if in the spirit of a true artist there express themselves, besides the perfect images of the things, also the impulsive forces of nature in the form of thoughts, the common source of philosophy and of art makes itself manifest to our eyes with particular clarity. Goethe is such an artist. He reveals to us the same secrets, both in the form of his works of art, and in the form of thought. What he shapes in his poems, he expresses also in the form of thought in his scientific essays and in his Prose Sayings" (p. 238).

We have thus finished the first section; let us pass to the second.

Steiner writes: "The content of thought that springs from the human spirit when it places itself before the outer world, is truth (another "medieval"? – ed.). Man cannot require any other knowledge besides that which he himself produces. One who behind the things seeks still something other that signifies their true essence has not made himself aware of the fact that all problems concerning the essence of the things spring solely from the human need to penetrate with thinking also what is perceived. The things speak to us, and our inwardness speaks when we observe the things. These two languages proceed from the same essential origin, and man is called to establish between them a reciprocal understanding. In this consists what is called knowledge; this and nothing else does he seek who comprehends the needs of human nature" (p. 239).

That the men of culture of today have renounced, in principle or in fact, the inquiry into truth stands, therefore, to signify that the "human need" of spiritually nourishing the soul has gone ever more weakening itself, just as, in a sick body, the need for food goes ever more weakening. Superfluous to add that it is precisely this unconscious lack of spiritual nourishment that one for the most part seeks to surrogate, desperately, with psychic "binges" (individual or collective): by means, that is, of that whole legion of impressions, sensations, and emotions (natural or artificial) that end only by deteriorating the equilibrium and the health of the body.

250
Christ says, in fact: "Not by bread alone shall man live, but by every word that proceeds from the mouth of God" (Mt 4:4).

Steiner writes: "The objects are exterior so long as we merely observe them; when we begin to think upon them, they cease to be outside of us; we fuse ourselves with their intimate essence. The contrast between objective outer perception and subjective inner thinking subsists for man only so long as he does not recognize the reciprocal interpenetration of these two worlds. The inner world of man is the inwardness of nature" (p. 239).


---


What did we say, in fact, when speaking of The Philosophy of Freedom? That there is the outer world; that there is the inner world (of the soul); that there is the outer world of the inner (of the spirit); and that this world, "outer of the inner," is nothing other than the inner world of the outer: that is to say, the spiritual essence of the physical world. "The things speak to us" from this world, but the deafening noises of the outer one (mediatic) and of the inner one (psychic) normally prevent us from hearing them.

The outer world has, moreover, rendered itself ever more deafening since "information" has been in vogue. One thing, however, is to know; another, to understand; and the world of today is ever more populated by persons who – it pains to say it – know everything, but understand nothing.

"Truth – Scaligero stresses, in fact – is not a piece of news, but a conquest" (Isis-Sophia: the Unknown Goddess – Mediterranee, Rome 1980, p. 57).

Steiner continues: "These thoughts are not refuted by the fact that different persons form different representations of the things (…) For what matters is not whether men form, or do not form, an identical judgment about the same thing, but whether the language that man's inwardness speaks is precisely that which expresses the essence of the things. The single judgments are diverse, according to the organism of man and the point of view from which he contemplates the objects; but all the judgments spring from the same element and introduce us into the essence of the objects" (pp. 239-240).

One is wont to say: "The world is beautiful because it is varied." But it is above all we who are "varied." Were we, therefore, conscious that the world is one, and that it is we who, according to how we are made and where we find ourselves, grasp diverse aspects of it, we would scarcely come into conflict with one another. One does not, in fact, enter into collision with others because one grasps diverse aspects of the real, but because one believes that the one grasped by oneself is the real, and not only one aspect of it. Were "the language that man's inwardness speaks" that – as Steiner says – "which expresses the essence of the things" (or, by means of which, one and the same spirit expresses itself), two persons, who loved diverse or opposite things, would be led, not to oppose one another, but to help one another, knowing well that they can and must learn, the one from the other.

To get along, it is necessary, in short, to share the love for thinking, and not for a thought.

Steiner writes: "Man is the organ by means of which nature reveals its secrets. In the subjective personality there appears the most profound content of the world. 'When nature, healthy in man, works as a whole, when he feels himself in the world as in a whole great, beautiful, noble, and worthy, when harmonious joy gives him a pure and free rapture, then the universe, if it could have consciousness of itself, would cry out with exultation as for a goal reached, and would admire the summit of its own becoming and of its own being.' (Goethe, essay on Winckelmann)" (p. 240).


---


251
Today, when the scientists have set themselves to studying mice in order better to understand human beings (let one consult, in this regard, the note by Francesco Giorgi, Men and Mice, of 13 July 2001), who would any longer have the courage to pronounce such words?

And yet – Goethe always says – "in order that man may accomplish all that is demanded of him, he must believe himself to be worth more than he is worth" (Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 51).

At present, however, one believes that he "may accomplish all that is demanded of him" by teaching him that he is little more than a mouse, a gorilla, or a chimpanzee.

"If man were always to look into the sky – Marcus Aurelius (121-180) said – he would end by having wings." And what will he then end by having (or by losing) if he continues – as the scientists do today – always to look at the earth (if not underground)?

Do you know what the "wings" of which Marcus Aurelius speaks really are? They are those "astral" sense-organs ("lotus flowers," "wheels," or chakras) of which Steiner speaks in Initiation. "In the unevolved man – he explains – the 'lotus flowers' are of a dark color, and stand still, without movement. In the clairvoyant, on the contrary, they are in movement and have luminous shades of colors (…) When, therefore, a disciple of occultism begins his exercises, what happens first of all is that the lotus flowers brighten; later they begin to turn. When this latter fact occurs, the capacity of clairvoyance begins. These 'flowers' are, in fact, the sense-organs of the soul. Their rotation signifies that something is perceived in the supersensible. No one can see anything supersensible before his astral senses have thus developed themselves" (Initiation, Antroposofica, Milan 1971, p. 97).

To speak of man as of an "animal" (even if an "intelligent" one) signifies, therefore, mortifying man (let one note, in the verb, the presence of death), so that he, no longer being able to discover his true being, can be more easily dominated (if truth makes free, falsehood makes, in fact, slaves).

The adage rightly has it: "If I were king and did not know it, it would be as if I were not."

L.R.
Rome, 20 November 2001

252
The Scientific Works of Goethe (52)

Tonight we shall take up again the second section of the last chapter, and I believe, since only a few pages remain to the end of the book, that we shall finish our study.


---


Steiner writes: "The aim of the universe and the essence of life do not lie in what the outer world offers us, but in what lives in the human spirit and bursts forth from it. Therefore Goethe considers it an error of the naturalist to wish to penetrate into the inwardness of nature by means of instruments and objective experiments, for 'man himself, inasmuch as he makes use of his healthy senses, is the greatest and most exact physical apparatus that can exist, and the greatest misfortune of modern physics is precisely that of having isolated (so to speak) the experiments from man, and of wishing to know nature solely from what artificial instruments show, thereby limiting the demonstration of what it is capable of accomplishing' (…) Man must make the things speak from the inwardness of his spirit, if he wishes to know their essence. All that he has to say about that essence is taken on loan from the spiritual experiences of his inwardness. Only from his own inwardness can he judge the world. Man must think anthropomorphically (…) One humanizes nature when one explains it; one introduces into it the interior experiences of man. But such subjective experiences (of the subject – ed.) are the intimate essence of the things" (pp. 240-241).


---


Goethe says again: "In observing nature, as much in its great phenomena as in its small ones, I constantly put to myself this question: 'Is it the object that speaks, or is it you?' And it was also under this light that I observed my predecessors and collaborators" (Maxims and Reflections – TEA, Rome 1988, p. 140).

Let us reflect: how does one who puts such a question to himself show himself, at the same time, convinced that one "must think anthropomorphically"? Would it not seem, thinking in such a way, that it is the subject that speaks, and not the object?

It would seem so, but it is not so. In order to understand it, one must, however, keep well in mind the difference between the concept (the other face of the percept) and the representation. Both spring, in fact, from man, and are, therefore, from this point of view, "anthropomorphic"; through the concept, however, the object speaks, whereas through the representation the subject speaks; so true is this that the psychologists, when they administer a projective test (for example, that of Hermann Rorschach – 1884/1922), expect, precisely by virtue of the representation the subject makes for himself of the object (a blot), to know the former, and not the latter.

The psychologists aim, therefore, at knowing the subject by means of the object (utilizing, to that end, the representations), whereas the scientists aim, or ought to aim, at knowing the object by means of the subject (utilizing to that end the concepts or the "laws").

Steiner says that "one humanizes nature when one explains it," because "one introduces into it the interior experiences of man." Everything lies, therefore, in seeing whether those "interior experiences" are truly those of man, or of the ánthrōpos. One thing is, in fact, to introduce into nature, in a free and conscious way, the spiritual experiences of the I; another, to introduce into it, in a constrained and unconscious way, the mechanistic ones (of the physical-mineral body), the vitalistic ones (of the etheric-vegetal body), or the psychologistic ones (of the astral-animal body).

Everything lies, in short, in distinguishing real (spiritual) anthropomorphism from illusory (psychophysiological and projective) anthropomorphism. Only man – it is true – can explain nature by "humanizing" it: "men," however, one is not born; one becomes.

Thus prayed, for example, the great German writer Ernst Wiechert (1887-1950): "Lord, grant that, before dying, I may become a man" (cit. from memory – ed.).


---


253
In order not to "talk past ourselves," but to make the things speak and to lend them an ear, we must not, therefore, remain "as mother made us," but transform ourselves. Knowledge is, in fact, ascesis, and ascesis entails not the "mortification of the flesh," but the exaltation of our noetic and ethical commitment.

Listen to how much Giovanni Colazza says precisely: "Man, just as he is in everyday life, serves little or nothing for the work in the spiritual world. Many may have good qualities as well, but our personal qualities have no importance for the spiritual world until we begin to work upon them in order to carry them forward deliberately. What we carry forward with élan of the soul and conscious impulse of the will is what serves the work of the spiritual worlds upon the Earth, the work to which we are called to collaborate. And that which we do in ourselves is not for us, but for our work" (Of Initiation – Tilopa, Rome 1992, pp. 15-16).

But let us go forward, and let us see what the note says.

"The Goethean conceptions stand in the acutest contrast with the Kantian philosophy. This one takes its start from the opinion that the world of representations is dominated by the laws of the human spirit, and that, therefore, everything that comes to meet it from without cannot exist in it except as subjective reflection. Man would perceive, not the 'in itself' of the things, but the semblance arising from the fact that the objects produce an impression upon him, and that he connects such impressions according to the laws of his intellect and of his reason. Of the circumstance that through this reason the essence of the things speaks, Kant and the Kantians have not the palest idea. For this reason the Kantian philosophy could never signify anything for Goethe. When he appropriated certain single sentences of it, he gave them a sense totally different from that which they have in the doctrine of their author. From an annotation that came to light only after the opening of the Goethe Archive of Weimar, it results how the poet made himself clearly aware of the contrast between his own conception of the world and the Kantian one (…) Goethe holds that the Kantian definition does not correspond to the knowledge of man, but only to the relation in which he places himself with the things when he contemplates them with respect to his own pleasure and displeasure" (pp. 241-242).


---


Perhaps you will remember that, in treating of The Philosophy of Freedom, I said that what Kant maintains (that we are given to know the things as they are for us, but not as they are in themselves) can hold for feeling, but not for thinking or for willing. With feeling, in fact, we never (ordinarily) go out of ourselves; for this reason, I then defined it as "intransitive" or "narcissistic" (in the Freudian sense), in difference from thinking and willing, which I defined, on the contrary, as "transitive" or "objectual" (again in the Freudian sense). Thinking and willing are, in fact, at the service, respectively, of the concept of the object and of the object of the concept, whereas feeling is at the service of the reaction of the subject to the presence or the action of the object (pleasure or pain, sympathy or antipathy, love or hatred).

Whoever is wont to let (in a more or less conscious way) his subjective reactions condition his thinking (a thing that always happens when one opines) will end, therefore, by convincing himself that what holds for feeling holds also for thinking, and by finding himself, for that very reason, enrolled in the fan club of the Kantians.

Steiner writes: "Of a human truth that is not subjective (that is, springing from or expressed by the subject – ed.) one cannot even speak. For truth is the introducing of subjective experiences into the objective connection of the phenomena. These subjective experiences can even assume an altogether individual (personal – ed.) character, and are nevertheless the expression of the intimate essence of the things. One cannot introduce into the things anything other than what one has experienced in oneself. Therefore every man, according to his individual experiences, will introduce into the things elements in a certain sense different. Another, who has not had my same experiences, may not at all comprehend my way of interpreting certain processes of nature. But what matters is not that all men
                                                  254
think the same about the things, but that all, when they think about the things, live in the element of truth" (p. 242).


---


Why does Steiner say that "subjective experiences can even assume an altogether individual character, and are nevertheless the expression of the intimate essence of the things"? Because we can give (representative) form only to the "intimate essence of the things" (to the concept, which has no form). As we know by now, in fact, the representation is nothing other than an illustration (particular) of a concept (universal): that is, a form (conditioned by sensible perception) of what, in itself, has no form (do you remember Hegel? 'Representations in general can be considered as metaphors of thoughts and concepts' – The Scientific Works of Goethe (10) – ed.).

In this regard, we have often given the example of the triangle, calling attention to the fact that its concept is one, whereas its representations can be multiple.

But if multiple representations refer to one concept, to what do multiple concepts refer? It is soon said: to one idea. And multiple ideas? It is not difficult: to one I ("All things were made through him, – the Prologue of the Gospel of John has it – and without him, not one of the things created was made").

We find ourselves, therefore, once again in the presence of that world that Goethe considers a "circle of circles" (The Scientific Works of Goethe (48) – ed.), or that Scaligero considers a "truth of truths"; more than once, in fact, he said to me: "Remember, truths are many, reality is one."

"What matters – Steiner says – is not that all men think the same about the things, but that all, when they think about the things, live in the element of truth": that is to say, in the element of the "truth of truths," and therefore in the element of "reality"; and this element is the spirit. If "God (that is, reality – ed.) does not exist – Dostoevsky wrote precisely – everything is permitted."

The problem is constituted, therefore, by the nature of the spirit that animates and guides us: one thing is, in fact, the "spirit of truth," another the spirit of "falsehood," and yet another that of "illusion." Men animated by the former will have, in fact, no need, in order to feel themselves brothers, to think "the same about the things" (of the so-called "single thought"), but will, on the contrary, be glad that the world has need of all, because, in order to be known, it requires to be observed from the most diverse points of view.

The fact is that truth – as we have said and repeated – is not an object (a thing) that one can have or not have, but a subject (a spirit) in which one can be or not be (Christ says: "I am the way, the truth, and the life" – Jn 14:6).

One who is in truth (or in reality) is, therefore, in love; and it is arduous to imagine that one who is in love be incapable of meeting, fraternally, with those who are different from him, with those who have had experiences different from his, or with those who interest themselves in things that have nothing to do with those in which he interests himself.

Perhaps you know that the "devil" is also called the "ape of God"; woe, therefore, to mimicking fraternity on the plane of form (on that of ordinary representations), for, on this plane, only uniformity or conformism can realize itself: that is to say, the (diabolical) unification or homologation of thoughts, and not the (holy) communion of thinking (and, through thinking, of the I's).

Augustine (354-430) used to say: "Dilige et quod vis fac" ("Love and do what you will"); paraphrasing, we could, therefore, say: "Become the I that you are, and do what you will."

But let us return to ourselves, and take up the third section.


---


Steiner writes: "When an object expresses its own being through the organ of the human spirit, total reality springs forth only from the confluence of the outer objective and the inner subjective.

255
Neither through one-sided observation nor through one-sided thinking does man know reality" (p. 243).

Through "one-sided observation" man knows, in fact (without being aware of it), Ahriman; whereas, through "one-sided thinking" he knows (without being aware of it), Lucifer: that is to say, two parts of reality, and not "total reality."

Steiner continues: "This conception of the world which recognizes in ideas the essence of the things, is not mysticism. But with mysticism it has this in common: that it does not contemplate objective reality as something that exists in the outer world, but as something that one really grasps in the inwardness of man (…) Both the mystic, and the follower of the Goethean conception of the world reject the faith in a beyond (material or spiritual – ed.), and the hypotheses concerning the same, and hold to the real spirituality that expresses itself in man himself. Goethe writes to Jacobi: 'God has punished you with metaphysics and has planted a thorn in your flesh; instead, He has blessed me with physics…I hold to the worship of God of the atheist (Spinoza), and abandon to you all that you call and must call religion. You hold to faith in God, I to the contemplation of Him' (p. 243).

What do you say? Will God still bless those who, through "physics," contemplate or adore – as we saw Steiner assert – "what is purely material" or sub-material (The Scientific Works of Goethe (50) – ed.), and no longer Him? (To one who wished to continue – in Goethe's footsteps – to be "blessed" by physics, we would suggest consulting, of Steiner's, Scientific-Spiritual Impulses for the Development of Physics – Miriadi, Mestre (Ve), 1st vol. 2000, 2nd vol. 2001 – ed.).


---


Steiner continues: "What Goethe wishes to contemplate is the essence of the things expressing itself in his world of ideas. The mystic, too, wishes to know the essence of the things by immersing himself in his own inwardness; but he rejects precisely the world of ideas, 'in itself' clear and transparent, as ineffective for enabling him to reach a higher knowledge. He does not believe he must develop his ideal faculties, but other forces of his inwardness, in order to see the profound substrata of the things. Usually he believes he grasps the essence of the things in indistinct sensations and sentiments. But sentiments and sensations belong only to the subjective being of man; in them nothing expresses itself about the things. Only in ideas do the very things speak. Mysticism is a superficial conception of the world, although the mystics vaunt themselves on their 'depth' before the rationalists. They do not know the nature of sentiments, otherwise they would not deem them manifestations of the universal being; and they do not know the nature of ideas, otherwise they would not deem them something superficial and rationalistic. They have not a glimmer of what men who truly have ideas experience in them" (pp. 243-244).

If they knew the "nature of sentiments" they would know, in fact, that these – as we said just now – are "intransitive" or "narcissistic," and for that very reason manifestations of their particular being, and not "of the universal being."

Steiner says that the mystics "have not a glimmer of what men who truly have ideas experience in them." Which means that the mystics are warm in the soul, but cold in the spirit. It will be well, therefore, to remember – I have repeated it often – that Ahriman is a spirit without soul, whereas Lucifer is a soul without spirit.

We have thus arrived at the fourth section.

Steiner writes: "One who seeks in his own inwardness the essential content of the objective world can also situate the essence of the moral ordering of the world only in human nature itself. One who believes in the existence of a real beyond, behind human reality, must seek in that beyond also the source of morality. For morality in a higher sense can proceed only from the essence of the things. Therefore the believer in the beyond presupposes moral commandments to which man must submit" (p. 244).

The "believer" wills, therefore, the willing of another (duty), and not his own (that of the I, inhabited by the Logos). But, in the Pater noster formulated by Steiner, one reads: "Thy will be wrought by us as Thou hast placed it in our inmost essence." Which stands to signify that, in order to do the will of God, one must make oneself, from ego, I. It is not, therefore, a question of "having to be," but of "willing to be."


---


Steiner writes: "Goethe makes morality arise from the ideal world of man. Neither objective norms, nor simple instincts guide human action; rather, ideas clear in themselves, for which man himself gives himself the direction, and which he follows neither from duty, as he ought to follow objective moral norms, nor from compulsion, as one follows one's own instincts and impulses. He serves them from love; he loves them as one loves a child; he wills their realization and commits himself in them, because they are a part of his own being. The idea is the directive, and love is the motive force of Goethean ethics" (p. 245).

Let us say it plainly: the ego, in the best of cases, concerns itself with observing moral norms or precepts in order to secure tranquility during life (the "quiet life") and a paradisiacal beatitude after death. It behaves, therefore, morally, but it is not moral, for, in order to be so, it would have to die to itself and become an I. Dying, however, frightens it, and it seeks, therefore, to earn paradise with "works" or with "good" actions, without paying too much heed to whether its sentiments, its thoughts, and, above all, its most profound and secret intents are equally "good."

When there was still, in the world, one who understood these things, it was known that the "devil," inasmuch as he can permit himself to have all the virtues, is precisely not virtuous, and therefore capable of loving. Not by chance does Steiner explain that God shares omnipotence with Ahriman and omniscience with Lucifer, but reserves love for Himself alone.

Dear friends, I do not think there is anything else to say. Let me, therefore, conclude this our work, by reading to you what Steiner says again of art, in the fifth section of this last chapter.

"If man had not the faculty of generating formed creations, indeed in the same sense as the works of nature, but bearing that sense to a more perfect manifestation than nature can do, there would be no art in the Goethean sense. The artist's creations are natural objects at a higher degree of perfection (…) Not the matter that the artist takes from nature constitutes the work of art; but only what the artist, drawing it from his own inwardness, imprints upon it. The highest work of art is that which makes us forget that it has as foundation a one-sided matter, and which awakens our interest solely by virtue of what the artist has made of that matter. He creates naturally; but not in the same way as nature creates" (p. 246).

L.R.
27 November 2001

257


---
